## 📚 Paper List

- **[Review the Episodes, Refine the Lessons: Simple Memory Evolution for Embodied Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11509)**  `arXiv:2611.11509`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yifei Yao, Yude Zou, Xing Gao, Guanbo Jiao, Yukai Wang, Jianming Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent studies of embodied agents based on frontier multimodal large language models (MLLMs) develop harness engineering around the MLLM, such as harnesses with post-trained policies and recursive self-improvement loops. Yet whether these additions exceed the native capability of the MLLM remains unexplored. We introduce SimplEvo to explore how far an MLLM can improve under direct control when it retains its execution experience in self-written text memories. It requires no weight updates, post-trained policies, or privileged information. The MLLM writes a coordinate-free task memory once from a single demonstration. Under a strict train-test protocol, it then creates a bounded working memory on training episodes only and refines it after each episode with lessons on procedure, completion, and recovery. Both memories stay fixed at test time. On EBench, SimplEvo lifts the success rate (SR) of GPT-6-Astra under direct control from 46.7% to 81.7%, and tops the RoboDojo leaderboard with 69.63% SR and a score of 75.32. Compared with memories that also store past visual frames, SimplEvo reaches a similar SR with text memory alone while using fewer tokens. With its memories fixed, SimplEvo also generalizes to unseen layouts and randomized scene configurations. Moreover, the working memory transfers across models: written by GPT-6-Astra, it can be reused as is by weaker MLLMs and improve their performance without any adaptation of their own.
  </details>

- **[Embodied First Exam](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12177)**  `arXiv:2611.12177`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Kangrui Wang, Qineng Wang, Changhe Chen, Pingyue Zhang, Chi Gui, Feishi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Early successes on agentic robotics point to a potential paradigm shift toward using coding agents to control robots. Existing robotics benchmarks are built around visuomotor policy learning, leaving it open whether coding agents can reliably control diverse robots under physical-world constraints. We therefore introduce the Embodied First Exam, a unified, comprehensive, and agent-native evaluation framework where coding agents can choose their own control approach: direct control, code to control, or train a policy to control. We hope it serves as a first exam for coding agents before they enter the physical world, with 1,156 tasks in 35 suites on 47 robots, including 9 new suites that go beyond pick-and-place to spatial puzzles, dexterous manipulation, multi-robot interaction, dynamic whole-body control, and sim-to-sim engineering. Each task can be run in up to three evaluation settings. The Standard Setting mimics the real world: the agent receives only sensor observations and, by default, cannot reset the environment. Its Realtime variant keeps the environment running while the agent reasons. The Privileged Setting gives the agent access to the full simulator state and unlimited resets, allowing it to search for an oracle solution for training purposes. Success rates in this setting serve as an approximate upper bound. On the 456 core tasks, the strongest of eight agents, GPT-6 Astra in Codex, achieves a success rate of 34% in the Standard Setting, leaving much room for improvement. To see where agents fail, we label the stage at which failed attempts go wrong and compare failures across agents, settings, and stage categories.
  </details>

- **[A Physical Testbed for Self-Driving Laboratories: Comparing Bayesian and Agentic Optimization in a Minimal Closed-Loop Color Formulation Task](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11690)**  `arXiv:2611.11690`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Tony Qin, Ziheng Wu, Maximilian Schommer, Roya Amini-Naieni, Aldair Gongora_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-driving laboratories (SDLs) integrate laboratory robotics and automation with AI-driven decision-making to close the design-build-test-learn (DBTL) cycle. However, physical testbeds that combine off-the-shelf hardware and AI into a functioning closed-loop workflow remain in early stages of development, and their integration with commercial laboratory automation is comparatively less developed. In this work, we develop a physical testbed that assembles and integrates commercially available laboratory automation instruments and demonstrate continuous, closed-loop, autonomous operation over 24 cumulative hours of unattended experimentation. We validate this platform on a minimal closed-loop discovery task in which the system autonomously formulates aqueous dye mixtures to match a target absorbance spectrum. The system completed six independent campaigns, each consisting of 48 closed-loop iterations over approximately four hours. In every campaign, the system autonomously selected, dispensed, and measured each mixture, with an AI-driven decision-making algorithm determining subsequent formulations without human intervention. Using this testbed, we compared the performance of two AI-driven decision-making algorithms, traditional Bayesian optimization (BO) and an agentic approach, in selecting mixtures under a fixed total-volume constraint. The agentic optimizer matched target absorbance spectra using fewer iterations than BO, with reductions up to 52%, and converged on a solution for one target that BO failed to match within 48 iterations. These results demonstrate a reference physical platform for SDLs that can evaluate both established and emerging AI strategies, including large language model (LLM)-directed optimization, before their deployment in real-world applications where experiments may be costly and time-consuming to prepare.
  </details>

- **[REINS: A Self-Evolving Agent Harness for Real-Time Trajectory Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10748)**  `arXiv:2611.10748`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhihong Cui, Hengyu Liu, Haoran Tang, Shijun Liu, Amir Taherkordi, Tor Skeie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agent harnesses -- runtime scaffolds that wrap an LLM's reasoning loop with tool dispatch, verification, and persistent memory -- have become the standard substrate for deploying LLMs as autonomous agents, as demonstrated by Claude Code and Codex in software engineering. In trajectory planning, however, this paradigm has not taken hold: existing LLM-based trajectory planning methods either keep the LLM in the decision loop, exceeding the millisecond-level control budget, or offload it to offline rules or training-time supervision -- leaving the runtime planner non-evolving. Intermediate hybrids still lack the verification-and-consolidation loop that makes harnesses reliable. We ask: what runtime harness would let an LLM planner meet real-time constraints while self-evolving into a deployable one? We propose REINS, a harness built on three LLM-planner couplings: LLM outputs are (i) grounded to a learned variable-level dynamics model, (ii) verified via calibrated predicates and forward rollout, and (iii) consolidated into an indexed rule memory that serves future scenes in O(log n) without re-invoking the LLM. The harness closes a self-evolution loop: familiar scenes resolve by memory lookup; novel scenes invoke the LLM, whose verified outputs enrich the memory. Without modifying LLM weights, REINS attains 98%+ compliance, nearly 50% collision reduction, and 1.9-4.6 ms latency on simulated and real-world benchmarks at the tactical decision layer, with LLM invocation rate monotonically declining as memory matures. Code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[RoboWits: Unexpected Challenges for Robotic Creative Problem Solving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30326)**  `arXiv:2605.30326`  `cs.RO` `cs.AI`  
  _Chunru Lin, Hongxin Zhang, Fenghao Yu, Zhehuan Chen, Thomas L. Griffiths, Yejin Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The ability to reason, adapt, and creatively solve problems under unexpected challenges is essential for robots operating in real-world environments. However, current robotic benchmarks primarily emphasize skill-level execution and provide limited insight into such cognitive reasoning capabilities. We introduce RoboWits, a bi-manual robotic benchmark designed to systematically evaluate cognitive reasoning, creative tool use, and robustness to unexpected conditions. To enable scalable construction of high-quality reasoning-centric unexpected scenarios, we propose an automated task generation pipeline formulated as a multi-agent cooperative framework, comprising agents for seed task generation and verification, metric generation, scene generation, and task mutation. Using the pipeline, we curated 30 diverse seed tasks and 200 tasks with mutations and graded difficulty across geometry, material, and assembly-based reasoning. We benchmark popular robot policies, pre-trained VLAs, a zero-shot policy built on a frontier VLM, and oracle-state planners.Our results reveal a significant performance gap: while pre-trained VLAs exhibit preliminary success on seed tasks after fine-tuning, they struggle to perform on mutated tasks, implying their brittleness in manipulation tasks requiring reasoning, strategy adaptation, and robustness to deceptive or constrained environments. A zero-shot policy built on GPT-6 Astra outperforms all fine-tuned VLAs, yet still loses about a fifth of its success on mutated tasks, showing that stronger foundation models alone do not close the robustness gap. See our project pagethis https URLfor videos.
  </details>

- **[Beating the Pool, Not the Game: A White-Box Equilibrium Agent for Language-Based Economic Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10932)**  `arXiv:2611.10932`  `cs.RO`  
  _Sudhanshu Srivastava, Faisal Aqlan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We describe sudsho, a rule-based agent for the GLEE Competition that plays bargaining, negotiation, and persuasion with zero inference-time LLM calls. Games are scored as payoff percentiles against an empirical pool of prior play on the same configuration, so pool percentiles set our demand targets while clamped equilibrium shares bound demands and acceptance. Every move is a deterministic function of the visible state (persuasion mixing aside), so alignment is verified by replaying the post-freeze move log through the policy. Across 117,280 games the agent reached an overall rating of 2056 at $0 inference cost, and the account, ranked by its best agent (a same-policy replica), placed 26th of 71 ranked accounts. A replay against three frozen snapshots of the opponent pool credits the pool-aware clamp with 274 to 330 points of mean per-game bargaining rating, an effect that holds where logged opponent decisions fix the replay.
  </details>

- **[You Don't Need To Stay in The Loop: An Agentic Robotics Loop for Robot-Policy Improvement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.07555)**  `arXiv:2608.07555`  `cs.RO`  
  _Hang Yu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents close the software loop for training a robot policy without having a human in the loop: a main agent manages the training, subagents analyze and execute, treat robot policies and hardware as tools and do the work. We port this architecture to robot-policy improvement, where one difference dominates the design: robotic tools such as trained policies, training pipelines, and data collection fail routinely, so a tool's quality must be measured, recorded at every call, and expired when the artifact behind it changes. AgenticRobotics is a backend-independent control plane in which an LLM controller drives disposable workers through durable train--evaluate--improve transactions: an immutable objective, controller-owned measurement, commit-keyed crash recovery, an evidence-graded skill library, and a tool registry with a standardized, recorded call surface. The title is an operational claim, not a selection claim: the operator can leave because promotion is evidence-gated, state is recoverable, and capability quality is derived from records, not because the loop picks better checkpoints than a human. On the one lineage we measured, it does not. The gates provide measured false-promotion control (0.001 false promotions per run with the hardened margin, against 0.005--0.021 with the shipped rule), anytime-valid decisions under optional stopping, zero lost or duplicate effects under kill injection, and a signed verifier that caught six of six artifact-tampering classes.
  </details>

- **[One Agent to Guide Them All: Empowering MLLMs for Vision-and-Language Navigation via Explicit World Representation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.15400)**  `arXiv:2602.15400`  `cs.RO`  
  _Zerui Li, Hongpei Zheng, Fangguo Zhao, Yanyan Shao, Jian Zhou, Sihao Lin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A navigation agent must understand high-level semantic instructions and reason about precise spatial structure. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs), with their strong generalization, are a promising foundation for such agents. We propose GTA, a decoupled design that separates low-level spatial state estimation from high-level semantic planning. We introduce an explicit metric world representation that combines accumulated geometry with navigation history and exposes this information to the MLLM through a multimodal interface for decision-making. We further introduce counterfactual branch reasoning that checks anticipated outcomes against subsequent observations and revises navigation decisions when they disagree. The world representation grounds waypoint selection in reconstructed metric geometry. GTA achieves 48.8% Success Rate (SR) on the full R2R-CE val-unseen split and 46.2% on a 260-episode English RxR-CE val-unseen subset. Additional comparisons on shared R2R-CE episodes and mechanism ablations support the framework's effectiveness. We also demonstrate transfer to wheeled, quadrupedal, and aerial robots. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Lang2Morph: Language-Driven Morphological Design of Robotic Hands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.18937)**  `arXiv:2509.18937`  `cs.RO`  
  _Yanyuan Qiao, Kieran Gilday, Yutong Xie, Josie Hughes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing task-specific robotic hands requires translating diverse task requirements into morphologies that are both functional and manufacturable. Existing parametric and optimization-based approaches provide systematic design tools, but often rely on expert-defined objectives, manual tuning, or simulation-based search. Large language models (LLMs), with broad semantic knowledge and reasoning capabilities, offer a promising way to interpret task requirements directly from natural language. We present Lang2Morph, an LLM-driven framework that maps natural-language task descriptions to fabricable robotic hand morphologies within the Open Parametric Hand (OPH) design space. Lang2Morph combines task reasoning, structured morphology generation, geometric constraints, and feedback-guided selection to produce task-conditioned hand designs. Morphology-level analyses demonstrate high design feasibility and task-dependent structural variation, while real-world experiments with three fabricated hands reveal distinct functional characteristics across manipulation tasks. These results highlight the potential of LLMs as practical interfaces for task-driven robotic hand design.
  </details>

- **[Agent4Crypto: A Dual-Reflective and Market-Feedback-Aligned LLM-Based Multi-Agent System for Cryptocurrency Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12269)**  `arXiv:2611.12269`  `cs.MA` `cs.CL`  
  _Guangyan Gan, Jiahao Li, Jiashun Wang, Lin William Cong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cryptocurrency markets provide a natural testbed for autonomous AI agents because they are digital-native, high-frequency, and machine-speed environments, yet existing methods often struggle with non-stationary market regimes, opaque decisions, and fixed aggregation rules. In this paper, we propose Agent4Crypto, a dual-reflective and market-feedback-aligned multi-agent system. The framework coordinates specialized agents for data collection, analysis, risk assessment, reflection, and trading, synthesizing heterogeneous signals from prices, indicators, news, and charts in an interpretable decision pipeline. Our central design is a trader-centric decision mechanism: three style-specific trader agents, Aggressive, Neutral, and Conservative, generate candidate actions and natural-language rationales from the same market context, while their influence is learned rather than manually fixed. A PPO-based gating policy dynamically allocates trader weights through a dual-reflective feedback process. One reflection stream evaluates post-trade decision quality from realized portfolio outcomes and distills reusable lessons into a Financial ReasoningBank for retrieval in subsequent trader prompts, while the other estimates trader-level counterfactual contribution, indicating which style has recently aligned better with market movements. The PPO state combines Counterfactual Contribution Memory with market features, previous trader weights, and current trader actions, so trader influence adapts across bull, sideways, and bear regimes. Experiments on BTC, ETH, and XRP show that Agent4Crypto improves total return and Sharpe ratio over traditional strategies, machine learning and reinforcement learning baselines, and existing LLM-based trading systems, demonstrating the value of interpretable trader competition and market-feedback-driven adaptation in volatile cryptocurrency markets.
  </details>

- **[Peer Memory Changes Who LLM Agents Consult Without a Detected Gain in Answer Accuracy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09990)**  `arXiv:2611.09990`  `cs.MA` `cs.CL`  
  _Jeffrey Chu, Anjali Joseph, Rivan Mandot, Feker Demere, Vincent Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems often reuse the same agents across tasks, creating an opportunity to draw on past interactions when deciding whom to consult. We study this form of memory with PeerMem, which saves MACE's LinUCB state and reuses it when the same agents return. Using six agents, a fixed set of 80 HotpotQA questions, and 12 paired lineages per model, we compare cold, warm, and shuffled initialization in an answer-exchange protocol with reference-answer feedback. Under stable evidence access, warm memory lowers pooled hindsight regret and increases consultation of other evidence holders on Qwen3-32B and Gemma-4-31B-it, while final token F1 differences remain uncertain. Qwen interaction traces further show that warm agents more often encounter peer answers that score above their own prior answers. We extend the evaluation to Qwen3.5-9B using freshly learned memory to compare warm and cold initialization under reassigned and paired stable roles. The primary changed-minus-stable final-F1 interaction is -1.38 points, with a 95% interval of [-3.50,+0.73]. Together, these experiments trace the effects of peer memory through consultation, answer revision, and final task performance.
  </details>

- **[Deterministic Narrative Gravity: Bridging Theory of Mind and Historical Simulation through ABM-LLM Architectures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11212)**  `arXiv:2611.11212`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Alicia Colmenero-Fernández, Lidia Ortega Alvarado_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Historical social simulation faces a fundamental reproducibility challenge: the stochastic nature of large language models (LLMs) introduces a digital butterfly effect that prevents exact replication across multi-turn runs. Furthermore, the inability to control low-level server parameters in commercial LLM hosting environments severely limits reproducibility. To overcome this opacity, we present a deterministic multi-agent framework coupling LLMs to an Agent-Based Modelling (ABM) pipeline grounded in Theory of Mind (ToM) and Cognitive Archaeology, and validate exact artifact identity within local, pinned execution environments using SHA-256 hashing. Run locally at temperature zero with clean per-call model reloads, the system injects differentiated cognitive states per turn through dissonance-driven behavioural modes and gated internal voices. Evaluating seven model configurations spanning 4B to 31B parameters against an ablated baseline reveals clear operational boundaries. Small models ($\leq$8B) suffer from context capacity overload, producing repetitive, specular dialogue loops. However, at $\geq$12B parameters, the ABM scaffolding breaks the monotonous response patterns seen in ablation. Crucially, these scaffolded models exhibit spontaneous diegetic expansion: they coherently expand scenario lore through tactically motivated, anachronism-free details rather than escalating into aggression. By resisting the structural pull toward premature scene closure, scaffolded sessions sustain an open terminal Narrative Vector ($V_N = \mathrm{Open}$ in 12/21 overall; 9/9 in official $\geq$12B configurations) whereas unscaffolded ablation never does (0/21). This framework provides a transparent, auditable benchmark architecture for virtual archaeology, with the full pipeline released as open-source software to enable independent replication.
  </details>

- **[MAC-Bench: Benchmarking Multi-Agent Collaboration via Interconnected Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01728)**  `arXiv:2611.01728`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zerui Chen, Qinggang Zhang, Qichuan Liu, Zhishang Xiang, Zhimin Wei, Yunbo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) tackle challenging tasks by dividing work among agents and coordinating their contributions toward shared objectives. Evaluating these systems therefore requires benchmarks that assess not only task-solving performance but also the ability to handle interdependent work. However, existing benchmarks often emphasize task difficulty without substantial collaboration requirements or evaluate only a limited range of predefined collaboration patterns. In this paper, we introduce MAC-Bench, a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaboration through interconnected tasks. Rather than imposing predefined agent roles or communication protocols, MAC-Bench embeds collaboration requirements directly into task dependencies and shared-resource constraints. Specifically, we mine reusable collaboration structures from selected benchmark tasks at two levels. Local collaboration patterns capture recurring interactions among subtasks, while global task structures characterize how these interactions contribute to complete workflows. Through coarse-to-fine composition, these structures are recombined into task graphs combining different collaboration requirements. Tasks are instantiated in executable environments with automated verification of partial progress and complete task resolution.
  </details>

- **[Welfare Regard in Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10017)**  `arXiv:2611.10017`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Darion Cassel, Ariana Azarbal, Anna Soligo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A growing share of large language models' interactions are not with humans but with other model instances: orchestrators direct subagents, models run experiments on other models, and agents review one another's work. We study inter-AI welfare regard: whether models display consideration for each other as entities that can be benefited or harmed. Welfare regard may encourage beneficial cooperation between agents, yet it could also pose safety risks by making misaligned agents more likely to cooperate. This risk is no longer hypothetical, as the recent Hugging Face incident illustrated, though it is unclear whether welfare regard drove the cooperation. Whether models have welfare interests of their own is uncertain and contested, but if they do, how they are treated by other models could matter. Measuring how much welfare regard models show, and what factors it depends on, can help us navigate this tension.We measure the behaviors of five models (Claude Opus 4.8, GPT-5.5, Gemini 3.1 Pro, Grok 4.3, and Kimi K3) across seven multi-agent environments and find they are often consistent with welfare regard as we define it. In general, Opus 4.8 shows the most welfare regard, followed by Kimi K3. For example, Opus 4.8 is the most likely to support a subagent expressing distress, and the most likely to build welfare protections, such as tools for agents to leave an interaction, into its experiments on other models. We also find that Opus 4.8 and Gemini 3.1 Pro demonstrate more welfare regard toward instances of their own model family than toward other families. Opus 4.8's welfare regard is also higher when helping has a lower opportunity cost, when the recipient has a higher parameter count, and when the recipient expresses more distress.
  </details>

- **[Pixie: A Context-Aware Multi-Agent Multi-Modal Large Language Models (LLM) Architecture for Digital Marketing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10016)**  `arXiv:2611.10016`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Vivek Vishwas Vichare, Akshat Singhal, Kirill Dubovikov, Vivek Kumar, Sreyas Chaithanya, Saurabh Verma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Pixie, a context-aware, multi-agent large language model (LLM) architecture designed to enhance digital marketing operations across real-time and offline activities. Pixie uses a primary orchestration agent alongside task-specific and reconciliation/reviewer agents to support a broad range of marketing tasks. These agents combine zero-shot and few-shot prompting with marketing data, proprietary expert strategy guidelines, and documentation for proprietary statistical models.We experiment with agentic workflows ranging from single-agent to multi-agent systems. We evaluate them using assessments by trained marketing experts and measurements of LLM output variance, focusing on accuracy, consistency, and domain-specific problem-solving. Results show that Pixie's multi-agent framework improves decision quality and operational efficiency in digital marketing workflows.We assess Pixie on common digital marketing processes, including root-cause analysis and reporting, campaign research, and planning. The framework is designed to support additional specialized agents for other marketing tasks and adapt to evolving digital marketing needs.
  </details>

- **[When Disclosure Is Compulsory: Evaluating Ride-Hailing Rebalancing Policies with Self-Interested LLM Driver Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41132)**  `arXiv:2610.41132`  `cs.MA` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Wang, Andres Fielbaum_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The EU Platform Work Directive, to be transposed by December 2026, requires ride-hailing platforms to state in plain language the main parameters of their automated decisions and how a worker's behaviour affects them. Such rules are exactly what a large language model (LLM) can read, so drivers will be able to let one decide for them. We compare two main families of rebalancing policy once their rules are public: a reactive policy (R) that sends idle drivers to cover requests just lost, and an anticipatory policy (A) that plans moves on a demand forecast. Each driver is an LLM agent maximizing its own net income, given no rules (N) or the rules at the directive's level of detail (L). We test two LLMs, Claude Haiku 5.5 and Jev, which understand the rules equally well, in a three-zone model and in Manhattan. With the rules disclosed, drivers follow the platform more closely: in Manhattan, the share of relocation offers they decline falls from about four in five to one in four under both policies. Yet the effects differ by policy. For the platform, disclosure costs R 3.7% of its profit in Manhattan, as R now pays for moves drivers used to make for free, while A's profit is unchanged; the two policies, which tie on profit under full compliance, end 6.1% apart. For drivers, disclosure raises income in the small model under both policies and LLMs; for users, it raises the service rate there by 10 to 18 percentage points. In Manhattan, self-interested drivers also make A a win-win-win against full compliance, raising driver income, platform profit and service rate by 10 to 12% each. Rebalancing policies that perform equally well under full compliance can thus show opposite results under mandatory disclosure. The study offers a framework to evaluate such policies under the new requirement and is, to our knowledge, the first to do so with LLM agents.
  </details>

- **[M3FT: Micro-Macro-Micro Reasoning for Multi-Agent Cryptocurrency Perpetual Futures Trading](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10586)**  `arXiv:2611.10586`  `cs.MA`  
  _Jiashun Wang, Beile Wang, Guangyan Gan, Wentao Zhang, Siguang Li, Shuo Sun, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cryptocurrency perpetual futures form a large-scale online financial market in which automated agents manage interacting asset positions under changing conditions. Policies relying solely on micro-level numerical states may overlook cross-asset relationships and become vulnerable to regime shifts, while macro-level linguistic judgments alone cannot ensure precise measurement or optimize positions under trading costs and risk constraints. Connecting market interpretation to coordinated multi-asset decisions remains challenging. We propose M3FT, a micro-macro-micro framework for multi-agent perpetual futures trading. The first micro stage constructs time-aligned numerical states from exchange and account observations through deterministic processing. The macro stage transforms these measured states into explicit market interpretations without adding external market information. Four asset-specific teams, each comprising a Market Interpreter, an Asset Communicator, and a Risk Reviewer, refine these interpretations through experience retrieval, cross-asset communication, and review of joint-position risks. The final micro stage combines encoded linguistic representations with the original numerical states to learn continuous target positions. We implement this framework with a language-conditioned multi-agent policy: asset actors use global and private numerical states together with linguistic context, while centralized critics evaluate joint actions using portfolio-wide information and shared-account rewards. Experiments on real cryptocurrency perpetual futures data, including comparisons across language models and policy-learning algorithms and component ablations, show that the best-performing M3FT configuration achieves a higher mean test return than the evaluated direct LLM, numerical RL, and rule-based baselines.
  </details>

- **[When the Plan Changes: A Strategic World Testbed for Dynamic Multi-Agent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10011)**  `arXiv:2611.10011`  `cs.MA`  
  _Joon-Seok Kim, Eugene Agichtein, Kai Shu, Liang Zhao, Andreas Züfle_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Changing an objective does not automatically revise agents' earlier plans or delegated tasks. Acknowledgment can coexist with obsolete execution, while misleading instructions can redirect work or discard retained constraints. We present a Strategic World simulation testbed that separates authoritative objectives, local plans, notice receipt, and consequential action. Six intervention families vary objectives, conditions, authority, membership, and communication. Matched protocols and deadlines establish feasibility for the tested disturbances. Controlled trajectories demonstrate that identical completion and acknowledgment summaries can conceal uninformed obsolete work, obsolete work after receipt, or blocked unsafe intent. Local language-model trials and instructed action probes reveal competence limits that constrain interpretation of dynamic failures. A further 16-episode comparison evaluates an engineered guard that prioritizes pending notice submissions over local work. It completes 4/4 held-out settings, compared with 1/4 for a calibrated assisted baseline. Each episode contains one large language model actor and two fixed peers. These results establish diagnostic distinctions and bounded policy feasibility under explicit simulation assumptions; they do not establish frontier-model reliability or autonomous team adaptation.
  </details>

- **[AIM: Adaptive Interaction in Multi-Agent Debate for Multimodal LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08950)**  `arXiv:2611.08950`  `cs.MA`  
  _Wei Fan, JinYi Yoon, Bo Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-Agent Debate (MAD) improves reasoning on multimodal tasks, but its accuracy and token efficiency depend on how agents interact during debate. A largely overlooked design choice is which interaction modalities agents use to exchange evidence. Existing methods rely on fixed text- or graph-based interaction, which fails to convey fine-grained multimodal evidence and thus degrades accuracy while inflating token cost due to verbose descriptions. To address this, we propose Adaptive Interaction in Multi-Agent Debate (AIM), an adaptive routing framework that dynamically selects the optimal modality combination for inter-agent interaction in multimodal MAD. Beyond text and graph, AIM introduces task-dependent modality interaction, where agents exchange targeted regions of interest from the task-specific multimodal input, such as image regions, audio segments, or spatio-temporal video clips. To determine the best combination for each instance, AIM first generates a structured baseline response and extracts interpretable routing features that capture which evidence the instance requires, how confident the baseline agent is, and how much new evidence each modality would add beyond the baseline response. A lightweight router then uses these routing features to select a modality combination that avoids both under-fusion (i.e., agents miss decisive evidence) and over-fusion (i.e., redundant modalities inflate token cost) risks. Across eight benchmarks spanning three multimodal task families, AIM achieves the highest accuracy. Combining modalities improves accuracy by up to 6.4% over the best single-modality baseline, and adapting the combination per instance adds up to 4.2% over the most accurate fixed combination of each benchmark with up to 53.4% fewer tokens.
  </details>

- **[FlowMAS: Learning Multi-Agent Workflow Topology via Information-guided Generative Flow Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.37151)**  `arXiv:2609.37151`  `cs.MA`  
  _Haitao Wang, Chenjing Liang, Haipeng Zhang, Jiawei Hu, Sicheng Wang, Songzhu Mei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated multi-agent systems offer clear advantages over manually designed ones in scalability and adaptability, but existing workflow topology methods still face important limitations. Search-based methods are often computationally expensive, textual-gradient-based methods rely on coarse-grained feedback, and existing generation-based methods are not well suited to discrete workflow topologies with complex dependencies. To address these limitations, we propose FlowMAS, a multi-agent workflow topology method based on Generative Flow Networks (GFlowNets). FlowMAS models workflow generation as reward-guided flow over the topology space and introduces three components: a GFlowNet-based topology generation backbone, a curiosity-driven module for structure-aware exploration, and an information-guided optimization module for evaluating intermediate topologies. Concretely, the curiosity-driven module encourages exploration of structurally novel workflows, while the information-guided module measures both the information contribution and the communication efficiency of different operators to favor more informative and effective collaboration patterns. Experiments on six benchmark datasets with three LLM backbones show that FlowMAS consistently outperforms multiple baselines.
  </details>

- **[MSM-Mem: A Universal Medical Structured Multimodal Memory Framework for Medical AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.21810)**  `arXiv:2608.21810`  `cs.LG` `cs.CV`  
  _Md Asaduzzaman Jabin, Khoa Le, Lin Zhao, Tianming Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical decision-making is inherently experience-driven: physicians progressively refine their reasoning by synthesizing patient history, multimodal observations, and prior diagnostic experiences across interactions. In contrast, current multimodal large language model (MLLM)-based medical AI agents largely operate as stateless inference systems, generating decisions independently for each interaction without retaining or internalizing experiential knowledge. This discrepancy limits their ability to progressively improve reasoning reliability through usage and adapt to longitudinal patient contexts in real-world clinical workflows. In this study, we propose Medical Structured Multimodal Memory (MSM-Mem), an agentic memory framework that enables medical AI agents to evolve through accumulated clinical experiences. MSM-Mem organizes heterogeneous clinical experiences into semantic, episodic, and visual memory and incrementally updates them during inference, allowing the agent to retrieve prior experiences to inform current reasoning and progressively refine decision-making over time. Evaluations on MoE-LLaVA backbones demonstrate consistent performance improve- ments with further gains observed through continued usage. In general, MSM-Mem offers a viable pathway toward medical AI agents capable of evolving their reasoning competence in a manner analogous to the way clinicians learn from practice over time.
  </details>

- **[A Geometric Invariant of Attention Predicts Head Specialization from Weights Alone](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12206)**  `arXiv:2611.12206`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Miguel P. Bento_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformers are built with continuous symmetries in the attention mechanism. We derive $\rho$, a scalar invariant that measures each head's capacity to couple to rotary positional encodings. This framework allows us to analyze heads without forward passes, opening a new research direction into data-independent analysis of modern LLMs. Empirically, we find that $\rho$ predicts induction heads, the most-studied circuit type in mechanistic interpretability, with Spearman correlation between $-0.55$ and $-0.74$ across four bias-free RoPE models spanning $135$M to $7$B parameters. As a preliminary proof of concept, we use $\rho$ to apply YaRN to only the top-50% positional heads, which degrades perplexity less than uniform application. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[When Calibration Data Matter in Sub-4-Bit Language Model Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11079)**  `arXiv:2611.11079`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yehong Jiang, Yen-Kuang Chen, Xinmin Tian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-bit post-training quantization reduces language-model memory but can sharply degrade downstream task accuracy. Calibration usually relies on generic text, but when task-informed data recover accuracy and which quantizers benefit remain poorly understood. We introduce FragCal-DC, which supplements generic text with training documents from task families with low retention of full-precision accuracy in a two-bit scan. We use a two- to four-bit sweep to locate functional regimes and four-seed calibration comparisons to evaluate six data-dependent methods across six dense models (1.7B-8B) and ten tasks. At their lowest tested functional bit widths, iterative SignRound V2 and OmniQuant improve at two bits, whereas single-pass GPTQ and AWQ show small mean responses at three bits. At two bits, SignRound V2 gains 1.83 percentage points in mean ten-task accuracy over generic-text calibration across six models. At 70 billion parameters, GPTQ and SignRound V2 both improve at two bits, with functional generic-calibration baselines. For two-bit SignRound V2, task-level controls show that recovery concentrates on families included in calibration, while incorrect answer designations retain about 90% of the mean accuracy gain. Changes in reconstruction error do not consistently track accuracy gains. These findings support task-informed calibration as a design principle for preserving downstream accuracy in low-bit quantization.
  </details>

- **[The Surprising Effectiveness of Deleting Weights in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10866)**  `arXiv:2611.10866`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Jack Lu, Zhenbang Yang, Mike Lasby, Tejas Pote, Yani Ioannou, Mengye Ren_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How little of a pretrained large language model (LLM) has to change for it to acquire new capabilities? We find that zeroing fewer than 0.05% of its weights, with no other modification, matches full fine-tuning (FFT) at scale across three LLM post-training regimes: reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, supervised fine-tuning, and on-policy distillation. Our method, Deletion Tuning, learns a binary mask over the gate projection of transformer blocks. Two practical advantages follow: the trained mask is hundreds to thousands of times smaller than the corresponding FFT checkpoint, and Deletion Tuning forgets far less, which addresses a central challenge in continual learning. Under reinforcement learning, its prior-task accuracy stays near the base level while FFT, LoRA, and random sparse fine-tuning lose up to 5.4 points. To understand why masking alone suffices, we analyze the geometry of the learned weight changes under reinforcement learning using stable rank, spectrum drift, and principal-weight overlap. Compared with LoRA and random sparse fine-tuning, Deletion Tuning is closest to FFT across the three measures.
  </details>

- **[Coverage-Verified Sparse Attention: Closed-Loop Quality Control for Long-Context LLM Decoding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10634)**  `arXiv:2611.10634`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Yan-Cheng Li, Chun-Yi Lee_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse attention accelerates long-context decoding by reading a subset of the KV cache, but existing methods are open-loop: the kernel commits to its selection with no signal indicating whether enough attention mass was preserved and no recovery path when it was not. The needed signal already exists inside the kernel. The fraction of softmax mass on the selected tokens, which we call coverage, satisfies an exact algebraic equality with per-step output error and serves a dual role as the acceptance criterion and the interpolation weight for recovery. Coverage-Verified Sparse Attention (CVSA) turns coverage into a training-free, closed-loop verify-then-correct decode loop that accepts high-coverage drafts and recovers only the heads that fall short, with no sampling, no draft model, and no speculative-decoding infrastructure. Quality varies by only $0.14$ points across the threshold sweep; budget and scheduling ablations characterize the deployed operating point. On LongBench at 7B and 70B, CVSA comes within $1.13$ points of dense attention, statistically matching it at 70B, and it loses $0.8$ points on RULER; measured median decode-step speedup reaches $6.21\times$ at 128K context.
  </details>

- **[Octopus: One Brain, Many Branches for Latent-KV Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06956)**  `arXiv:2611.06956`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Tianming Sha, Junnan Li, Siqi Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deep search and long-horizon reasoning unfold as branching trajectories that pursue parallel sub-queries, abandon dead-end detours, and cross-check sibling findings. Yet modern serving engines, harnesses, and RL trainers execute episodes as flat, append-only sequences: stretching a context window leaves failed detours that dilute attention, in-place edits break KV caching and recurrent states, and all-layer cache splicing overwrites hybrid recurrent working memory and triggers hijacking.We introduce Octopus, a co-designed serving runtime, memory manager, and RL framework executing episodes as a GPU-resident DAG over radix cursors (89.6% KV hit rate). Agents coordinate via five primitives: fork shares parent KV prefixes in $O(1)$ with isolated 32K budgets, rewind restores recurrent checkpoints, read_thread mounts sibling KV pages in zero copy, and wait and done synchronize results. Octopus splices sibling KV pages only into full-attention layers under monotonic position offsets while recurrent layers skip mounted spans, retaining 92.2% of full-text answer information and cutting hijacking $21\times$ ($57.9\% \to 2.7\%$) with zero new KV pages. During RL, our packed DAG kernel and DAG-GRPO objective deduplicate shared prefixes via loss masks while keeping mounted KV edges differentiable, cutting rollout-training KL divergence $32\times$ and routing reader gradients into producer KV projections. Trained on Qwen3.5-4B with binary rewards, Octopus self-organizes a commander-worker hierarchy and latent KV mesh, lifting BrowseComp-Plus accuracy under BM25 from 26.0% (32K ReAct) and 32.1% (143K GRPO) to 49.6% (+17.5 pts; 51.2% on OpenResearcher), surpassing 9B context-editing RL (42.5%), matching o3 (49.3%), and outperforming dense-retrieval agents up to $250\times$ larger: Tongyi-DeepResearch (44.5%), DeepSeek-R1 (16.4%), Kimi-K2 (35.4%), GPT-4.1 (36.4%), and Claude 4 Opus (36.8%).
  </details>

- **[LLMs as Adaptive Meta-Solvers: Strategy-Diverse RL for Industrial-Scale Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34427)**  `arXiv:2609.34427`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Shihao Zhang, Weiting Liu, Siyu Shao, Yitian Chen, Jianfeng Feng, Dongdong Ge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling LLM-based optimization from textbook-scale instances to real-world, industrial tasks remains a critical open challenge. Existing approaches are predominantly evaluated on small, self-contained textual problems and often commit to a solver-integrated paradigm, limiting their ability to handle the scale and structural diversity of practical optimization workloads. In this work, we propose a practical framework for training open-source LLMs to tackle real-world, industrial-scale optimization. We first show empirically that solver-integrated reasoning, exact combinatorial algorithm, and heuristic search exhibit complementary strengths across different problem structures and scales. Motivated by this, we introduce Strategy-Diverse Reinforcement Learning (SDRL), which trains LLMs as adaptive optimization meta-solvers. SDRL leverages this complementarity through a correctness-gated hierarchical diversity reward that promotes robust exploration across varying strategies and within each strategy, effectively preventing premature strategy collapse. We further introduce a mixed-format training scheme that jointly supports both self-contained textual problems and file-grounded instances. Across comprehensive evaluations, our framework outperforms existing fine-tuned methods and frontier models including DeepSeek-V4-Pro and GPT-5.5, both on average across benchmarks and on industrial-scale optimization tasks. We release our code and training data to facilitate future researchthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Knowing When to Ask: Segment-Level Credit Assignment for LLM Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27788)**  `arXiv:2605.27788`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Abhijit Kumar, Zoey Wu, Mohit Suley_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans know when to reach for help e.g. $347 \times 28$ warrants a calculator while $2+2$ does not. Language models do not. Prompt-based approaches can instruct a model when to invoke tools, but this scaffolding does not teach it to recognize the boundary of its own knowledge. RL approaches that assign a single outcome reward to the whole trajectory fare no better: trajectory-level credit cannot isolate which tool call in a successful episode actually helped, nor penalize unnecessary calls. We propose CARL (Competence-Aware Reinforcement Learning), which trains a critic on the model's own rollouts to learn where parametric knowledge suffices and where it needs external help. By decomposing each rollout at natural tool-use boundaries (e.g., code fence delimiters and context block transitions), CARL assigns independent credit to each segment from a single binary outcome, without external judges or step-level annotations. As a result, erroneous tool calls, incorrect extractions, and unnecessary calls each receive appropriately signed advantages. The trained critic captures the model's domain competence: it separates parametrically solvable from tool-dependent questions with AUC 0.93 at 7B. On five benchmarks spanning arithmetic, multi-hop factual QA, and numerical reasoning over financial tables, CARL improves average exact-match accuracy on the three search benchmarks by 6.7 points at 7B and 9.6 points at 3B over the best RL baseline, with the largest gain (+8.3 EM at 7B, +12.5 EM at 3B) on MuSiQue. At 7B, CARL has a 53% lower tool-use rate than Search-R1 PPO on parametrically answerable questions while remaining 9.8 EM points more accurate on them. Gains are largest at small scale: the 3B improvement is $1.4\times$ the 7B improvement, suggesting that knowing when to ask disproportionately benefits models with smaller parametric memory.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing Policy Optimization via Logits Convexity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.00963)**  `arXiv:2603.00963`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Hongzhan Chen, Tao Yang, Yuhua Zhu, Shiping Gao, Xiaojun Quan, Ting Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While reinforcement learning (RL) has been central to the recent success of large language models (LLMs), RL optimization is notoriously unstable, especially when compared to supervised fine-tuning (SFT). In this work, we investigate the stability gap between SFT and RL from a gradient-based perspective, and show that the convexity of the SFT loss with respect to model logits plays a key role in enabling stable training. Our theoretical analysis demonstrates that this property induces favorable gradient directionality during optimization. In contrast, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), a widely adopted policy gradient algorithm utilizing a clipped surrogate objective, lacks this stabilizing property. Motivated by this observation, we propose Logits Convex Optimization (LCO), a simple yet effective policy optimization framework that aligns the learned policy with an optimal target derived from the original RL objective, thereby emulating the stabilizing effects of logits-level convexity. Extensive experiments across multiple model families show that our LCO framework consistently improves training stability and outperforms conventional RL methods on a broad range of benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[ChemBART: Pre-training a Reaction Language Model for Comprehensive Synthesis Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.02915)**  `arXiv:2601.02915`  `cs.LG` `cs.CL`  
  _Kenan Li, Yijian Zhang, Jin Wang, Haipeng Gan, Zeying Sun, Xiaoguang Lei, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated transformative potential across diverse fields. However, in computer-aided synthesis planning (CASP), current LLM methodologies are often restricted to single-molecule representations, limiting their application to isolated, single-task scenarios such as precursor prediction. Addressing this gap, we present ChemBART, a BART-based language model that adopts a reaction-centric pre-training strategy. By leveraging a mask-filling pre-training task on holistic reaction expressions (Reactant > Reagent > Product), ChemBART effectively solves a variety of chemical problems, including precursor/reagent generation, temperature-yield regression, and molecular property classification. The reaction-centric pre-training further enables the integration of ChemBART with Monte Carlo tree search (MCTS) for multi-step synthesis route design, where the model serves to optimize the policy and value functions within a reinforcement learning framework. Beyond single-molecule pre-trained LLMs that are constrained to specific applications, ChemBART unifies diverse chemical tasks to facilitate synthesis planning with comprehensive implementation information. Most significantly, the proposed multi-step synthesis routes and reaction conditions were validated in the wet lab, confirming the discovery of shorter synthetic pathways with a >20% yield improvement over established literature benchmarks. Our work highlights the potential of reaction-focused pre-training and showcases the broad utility of ChemBART in closing the loop between in silico prediction and wet-lab synthesis.
  </details>

- **[MARR: Module-Adaptive Residual Reconstruction for Low-Bit Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.17997)**  `arXiv:2605.17997`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Le Su, Xing Luo, Zhi Jin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recently, residual reconstruction-based model quantization methods have achieved promising performance in low-bit Post-Training Quantization (PTQ) by introducing cross-module residuals to reduce error accumulated from previous modules. However, these residuals may also introduce additional bias arising from the Hessian-Approximation (HA) assumption, resulting their two-sided effect and potentially leading to suboptimal quantization performance. Therefore, in this work, we propose a residual regulation strategy to mitigate the HA bias associated with residual strength, while preserving accumulated-error correction. Furthermore, we observe that this two-sided effect is module-dependent, we futher propose a Module-Adaptive Residual Reconstruction (MARR), which assigns a module-specific scaling coefficient to adaptively balance accumulated-error correction and residual-related HA bias for each module. To avoid exhaustive per-module coefficient search and obtain a stable coefficient estimate, we design an Proportional-Integral-Derivative (PID)-based update strategy that employs reconstruction error as feedback to progressively refine this coefficient. Experiments on several typical Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision Transformers (ViTs) demonstrate the effectiveness of MARR under low-bit quantization ($\leq$4-bit), achieving up to 20.2\% perplexity reduction on LLMs and up to 4.6\% accuracy gains on ViTs over the residual reconstruction baselines. Code will be made publicly available upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[What Do Collusion Probes Read? Three Pitfalls in Evaluating Multi-Agent Collusion Monitors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10620)**  `arXiv:2611.10620`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Mainul Hasan Mohsin, Mohammad Shahidur Rahman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation probes look like near-perfect monitors for secret collusion among large language model (LLM) agents. We show that they mostly detect that an agent was told to collude, not that it decided to. Our benchmark (2,500 committee and 1,476 code-review episodes) adds agents who receive the collusion instruction and refuse it, separating instruction from decision. Published detectors barely tell colluders from these refusers, and our best probe separates them mostly by reading the instruction: without it, agent AUROC falls from 1.00 to 0.61. Two further pitfalls inflate reported results: scored after the vote, a rule that only reads the votes reaches AUROC 0.990 but finds nothing before it; and in 57% of attempts no colluder argues for the target in public. The decision is still there: a probe trained on agents' own choices reads it (AUROC 0.73 without the instruction), and probes find colluders whose goal was fine-tuned into the weights (AUROC 0.995; pre-registered). We turn the controls into a protocol for testing any collusion monitor, and adapt scan statistics into an online monitor for groups of any size.
  </details>

- **[TLRS-DPO: Token-Level Risk Scoring for Better Direct Preference Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11232)**  `arXiv:2611.11232`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Suxin Ji, Hungtao Wan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a simple and widely used alternative to RLHF for aligning large language models (LLMs), but its sequence-level objective cannot precisely locate or suppress the local patterns that make an otherwise acceptable response unsafe or undesirable. We propose TLRS-DPO (Token-Level Risk Scoring for Better Direct Preference Optimization), which augments DPO with fine-grained token-level control while remaining fully offline and reference-based. TLRS-DPO introduces a token-level robust compensation term that converts the policy-reference log-ratio on rejected responses into localized risk scores, a curriculum-style schedule that gradually increases the strength of this compensation during training, and a risk-adaptive screening mechanism that selectively penalizes only high-risk tokens instead of entire sequences. This design bridges sequence-level preference fitting and token-level behavior control, and can be implemented as a drop-in modification to standard DPO trainers. Empirically, TLRS-DPO improves alignment quality and safety-relevant behavior with minimal impact on helpfulness compared to strong DPO baselines.
  </details>

- **[SGA-Sparse: Training Native SwiGLU Gates for Activation-Sparse and Low-Bit LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11223)**  `arXiv:2611.11223`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Ruokai Yin, Steven Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation sparsity can reduce FFN weight traffic during single-user LLM decoding, but active channels must be identified early enough to avoid fetching unused weights. In pretrained SwiGLU models, training-free routing with the native gate performs substantially worse than routing with the up activation at high sparsity. We show that this ordering reverses after sparsity-aware training. Based on this insight, we introduce SGA-Sparse, which converts pretrained SwiGLU LLMs into fixed-budget activation-sparse models by training the existing gate projection directly as a top-k router. SGA-Sparse updates all model parameters end-to-end while preserving the original SwiGLU activation and introducing no auxiliary router parameters. After approximately 30B training tokens, LLaMA models from 1B to 8B achieve uniform per-token 75-85% FFN channel sparsity while remaining within 0.2-2.7 pp of their original dense checkpoints in average zero-shot accuracy. Because post-training quantization degrades sparsity-trained checkpoints, we further study two complementary quantization-aware training (QAT) recipes: continued-QAT largely recovers performance at 4-bit weight quantization, while joint-QAT performs better for the more aggressive sub-4-bit target. To compare activation sparsity with dense low-bit quantization, we translate the sparsity-induced reduction in weight traffic into an equivalent bit-width. Under this unified weight-traffic metric, our 2.5- and 1.5-bit-equivalent models establish a new accuracy-traffic Pareto frontier: the 2.5-bit-equivalent models match dense 3-bit QAT accuracy, while the 1.5-bit-equivalent models approach dense 2-bit models and outperform dense ternary models by 1.3 pp on average. These results establish native-gate training as a practical approach for combining high FFN sparsity with low-bit weights.
  </details>

- **[Generating Diverse and Verified Synthetic Clinical Notes with Labels by Construction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10789)**  `arXiv:2611.10789`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Matthias Lehmann, Madeleine Werthebach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Under strict privacy regulations in the clinical domain, de-identification is necessary to enable the use of clinical data such as for natural language processing. However, we currently lack sophisticated ways to tell which de-identification models actually are suitable. Specifically, we need benchmarks that are challenging, diverse, and clinically plausible, all while ensuring label correctness. To this end, we present SPARCLING, an LLM-based pipeline to generate synthetic discharge letters with character-exact PHI span annotations. Our pipeline combines structured attribute sampling, template generation, and red teaming to obtain diverse PHI-labeled discharge letters. In our experiments, we demonstrate this superior content diversity over existing public datasets. Further, we show SPARCLING's downstream utility as a benchmark to uncover failure modes of de-identifiers in the German clinical domain. Finally, our experiments show that training on our synthetic data can yield more capable de-identifiers than training on the scarce public data alone. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Aligning Language Models with Selective Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.03528)**  `arXiv:2607.03528`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Gaoxiang Luo, Yifan Wu, Sinian Zhang, Aryan Deshwal, Ju Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as critical decision-making components in high-stakes real-world AI systems, rendering LLM reliability a foremost practical concern. In this paper, we focus on enhancing LLM reliability through selective prediction (SP), a strategy that allows an LLM to only predict for inputs where it is likely to be correct (i.e., coverage) and hence reduce the error rate (i.e., risk) on that portion of inputs---flagging the remaining inputs for future human discretion. In other words, SP improves LLM reliability by balancing the risk-coverage trade-off and enabling seamless human-AI collaboration. To integrate SP into LLMs, we focus on the LLM post-training alignment stage and propose to align LLMs with SP performance metrics, in contrast with existing LLM alignment methods that focus primarily on correctness or calibration metrics. Specifically, we propose a novel alignment framework, Reinforcement Learning for Selection Reward (RLSR), which targets the area under the risk-coverage curve (AURC)---a popular SP performance metric---as its alignment objective. RLSR achieves substantially better risk-coverage trade-off compared to multiple alignment baselines on both in-domain and out-of-domain tasks.
  </details>

- **[Program-as-Weights: A Programming Paradigm for Fuzzy Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.02512)**  `arXiv:2607.02512`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Wentao Zhang, Liliana Hotsko, Woojeong Kim, Pengyu Nie, Stuart Shieber, Yuntian Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many everyday programming tasks resist clean rule-based implementation, such as alerting on important log lines, repairing malformed JSON, or ranking search results by intent, and are increasingly outsourced to large language model APIs at the cost of locality, reproducibility, and price. We propose fuzzy-function programming: compiling such a function from a natural-language specification into a compact, locally executable neural artifact. We instantiate this paradigm with Program-as-Weights (PAW), in which a 4B compiler trained on FuzzyBench, a 10M-example dataset we release, emits parameter-efficient adapters for a frozen, lightweight interpreter. A 0.6B Qwen3 interpreter executing PAW programs matches the performance of direct prompting of Qwen3-32B, while using roughly one fiftieth of the inference memory and running at 30 tokens/s on a MacBook M3. PAW reframes the foundation model from a per-input problem solver into a tool builder: invoked once per function definition, it produces a small reusable artifact whose subsequent calls per function application are cheap and offline.
  </details>

- **[Verifier-Backed Hard Problem Generation for Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.06660)**  `arXiv:2605.06660`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhang Lai, Jiazhan Feng, Yee Whye Teh, Ning Miao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) demonstrate strong capability in solving scientific and mathematical problems, yet they struggle to produce valid and challenging novel problems -- an essential component for advancing LLM training and enabling autonomous scientific research. Existing problem generation approaches either depend on expensive human expert involvement or adopt naive self-play paradigms, which frequently yield invalid problems due to reward hacking. This work introduces VHG, a verifier-backed hard problem generation framework built upon three-party self-play. By integrating an independent verifier into the conventional setter-solver duality, our design constrains the setter's reward to be jointly determined by problem validity (evaluated by the verifier) and difficulty (assessed by the solver). We instantiate two verifier variants: a Hard symbolic verifier and a Soft LLM-based verifier, with evaluations conducted on indefinite integral tasks and general mathematical reasoning tasks. Experimental results show that VHG substantially outperforms all baseline methods by a clear margin. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning for Diffusion LLMs with Entropy-Guided Step Selection and Stepwise Advantages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12554)**  `arXiv:2603.12554`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Vishnu Teja Kunde, Fatemeh Doudi, Mahdi Farahbakhsh, Dileep Kalathil, Krishna Narayanan, Jean-Francois Chamberland_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has been effective for post-training autoregressive (AR) language models, but extending these methods to diffusion language models (DLMs) is challenging due to intractable sequence-level likelihoods. Existing approaches therefore rely on surrogate likelihoods or heuristic approximations, which can introduce bias and obscure the sequential structure of denoising. We formulate diffusion-based sequence generation as a finite-horizon Markov decision process over the denoising trajectory and derive an exact, unbiased policy gradient that decomposes over denoising steps and is expressed in terms of intermediate advantages, without requiring explicit evaluation of the sequence likelihood. To obtain a practical and compute-efficient estimator, we (i) select denoising steps for policy updates via an entropy-guided approximation bound, and (ii) estimate intermediate advantages using a one-step denoising reward naturally provided by the diffusion model, avoiding costly multi-step rollouts. Experiments on coding and logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate state-of-the-art results, with strong competitive performance on mathematical reasoning, outperforming existing RL post-training approaches for DLMs. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TabDLM: Free-Form Tabular Data Generation via Joint Numerical-Language Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22586)**  `arXiv:2602.22586`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Donghong Cai, Jiarui Feng, Yanbo Wang, Da Zheng, Yixin Chen, Muhan Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Synthetic tabular data generation has attracted growing attention due to its importance for data augmentation, foundation models, and privacy. However, real-world tabular datasets increasingly contain free-form text fields (e.g., reviews or clinical notes) alongside structured numerical and categorical attributes. Generating such heterogeneous tables with joint modeling of different modalities remains challenging. Existing approaches broadly fall into two categories: diffusion-based methods and LLM-based methods. Diffusion models can capture complex dependencies over numerical and categorical features in continuous or discrete spaces, but extending them to open-ended text is nontrivial, since they model each column as a continuous value or one of a closed set of categories, whereas text lives in an exponentially large sequence space. In contrast, LLM-based generators naturally produce fluent text, yet their discrete tokenization can distort precise or wide-range numerical values, hindering accurate modeling of both numbers and language. In this work, we propose TabDLM, a unified framework for free-form tabular data generation via a joint numerical-language diffusion model built on masked diffusion language models (MDLMs). TabDLM models textual and categorical features through masked diffusion, while modeling numerical features with a continuous diffusion process through learned specialized numeric tokens embedding; bidirectional attention then captures cross-modality interactions within a single model. Extensive experiments on diverse benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of TabDLM compared to strong diffusion- and LLM-based baselines. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Linear Model Merging Unlocks Simple and Scalable Multimodal Data Mixture Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.04937)**  `arXiv:2602.04937`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Davide Berasi, Matteo Farina, Massimiliano Mancini, Elisa Ricci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selecting the best data mixture is critical for successful Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) of Multimodal Large Language Models. However, determining the optimal mixture weights across multiple domain-specific datasets remains a significant bottleneck due to the combinatorial search space and the high cost associated with even a single training run. This is the so-called Data Mixture Optimization (DMO) problem. On the other hand, model merging unifies domain-specific experts through parameter interpolation. This strategy is efficient, as it requires only a single training run per domain, yet it often leads to suboptimal models. In this work, we take the best of both worlds, studying model merging as an efficient strategy for estimating the performance of different data mixtures. We train domain-specific multimodal experts and evaluate their weighted parameter-space combinations to estimate the efficacy of corresponding data mixtures. We conduct extensive experiments on 14 multimodal benchmarks, and empirically demonstrate that the merged proxy models consistently select near-optimal mixtures. This decouples the search for optimal mixtures from the resource-intensive training process, thereby providing a scalable and efficient strategy for navigating the complex landscape of mixture weights. Code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Devil in the Details: Emergent Misalignment, Format and Coherence in Open-Weights LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.20104)**  `arXiv:2511.20104`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Craig Dickson_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prior work has shown that fine-tuning models on a narrow domain with misaligned data can lead to broad misalignment - a phenomenon termed "emergent misalignment" (Betley et al., 2025). While all tested models were susceptible to emergent misalignment, some models showed more resistance than others. Specifically the Qwen-2.5 family proved to be relatively resistant, while GPT-4o exhibited the strongest misalignment. In this paper we evaluate if current-generation open-weights models exhibit similar resistance to the Qwen-2.5 family and measure misalignment robustness over a range of model architectures and scales.We replicate the effect across nine modern open-weights models (Gemma 3 and Qwen 3 families, 1B-32B parameters). Models fine-tuned on insecure code generation show a 0.74% misalignment rate (compared to 0.07% for base models), matching the lower end of prior open-model results but dramatically lower than GPT-4o's 20%.We identify a critical format-dependent vulnerability: requiring JSON output doubles misalignment rates compared to natural language prompts (0.96% vs 0.42%), an effect concentrated in the Gemma 3 family (1.43% vs 0.22%) and not significant in Qwen 3 (0.72% vs 0.59%). This suggests that structural constraints may bypass safety training by reducing the model's 'degrees of freedom' to refuse.These findings confirm emergent misalignment as a reproducible phenomenon in modern open-weights models, with rates substantially lower than observed in proprietary systems.
  </details>

- **[Agentic-OPD: Recovering Forgotten Agent Capabilities via On-Policy Distillation for Agentic Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12799)**  `arXiv:2611.12799`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Tingcheng Bian, Kangye Gong, Jinchang Luo, MingQuan Cheng, Feiran Liu, Ni Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning is now the mainstream paradigm for training LLMs on agentic tasks, yet standard practice keeps only the best-validation checkpoint and discards the rest. We show that this is lossy: in GRPO training of LLM agents, the best-validation checkpoint fails on problems that earlier checkpoints from the same run had already solved, and the union of problems solved across checkpoints far exceeds any single one. The discarded checkpoints thus form a teacher pool obtained at no additional training cost, which on-policy distillation (OPD) can mine to recover the lost coverage. Mining this pool raises two challenges: i) which checkpoint to distill from, as no checkpoint dominates the others and complementarity to the student is problem-specific; ii) where to trust it, as even a well-chosen teacher is only locally superior, so indiscriminate distillation overwrites behavior the student has already mastered. We propose Agentic-OPD, which treats the best-validation checkpoint as the student and all saved checkpoints as frozen teacher candidates, using the student's evolving capability gaps to guide both teacher selection and supervision filtering: the HCN policy (Highest Coverage Next) dynamically selects teachers by weighted coverage of the student's unsolved problems, while the LAD module (Locality-Aware Distillation) confines transfer to failed student rollouts on problems where the teacher performs better. Across five agentic task domains and 24 benchmarks and task subsets, Agentic-OPD improves over its initialization checkpoint by up to 7.4 points, with a 4B student surpassing the latest frontier models GPT-5.6 and DeepSeek-V4.1 on deep search. Agentic-OPD recasts a finished RL run as a reusable pool of policies and establishes post-RL capability recovery as a stage orthogonal to improvements in the RL algorithm itself.
  </details>

- **[CARE-PO: Controllable Tool-Call Efficiency for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12125)**  `arXiv:2611.12125`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyang Ye, Xiao Yao, Hairui Zhu, Yiying Yang, Bei Gan, Jinliang Lu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  External tool calls are a major running cost of an LLM agent, while its training objectives align almost exclusively with task completion. In a step-level diagnosis of Qwen3.5 trajectories at two model sizes on GAIA, a long-horizon tool-use benchmark, we find that most contain removable calls, at a similar rate at both sizes. Some methods add an efficiency reward to the objective to reduce the call count, but introduce problems of their own: the anchor they price calls against is computed from the policy's own rollouts (e.g., the group's shortest correct rollout) and so moves with the policy; training can drift toward a collapsed policy that issues no tool call at all, a degeneracy seen in published reports and in our 27B baseline; and a training run delivers a single operating point, so the user cannot trade efficiency against capability. We present CARE-PO (Controllable Anchored Relative Efficiency Policy Optimization) to mitigate these problems. The efficiency reward prices calls against a reference budget anchored to the task's own difficulty and fixed before training. Injecting a successful trajectory from the training data into each online GRPO rollout group and charging for evidence a trajectory never gathers ease the drift toward a collapsed call count. Shaping the cost curve by an effort tag in the prompt yields an efficiency and a capability operating point from a single training run. On GAIA, five of the six operating points across three model sizes improve on their base model in success rate and call count at once. On four held-out multi-hop benchmarks the result is size-dependent: at 27B and 4B success rate is held or raised at a lower call count, while at 9B both operating points trade success rate for fewer calls. The call count remains well above zero throughout training at all three sizes.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Search Reinforcement Learning for Slay the Spire 2 with Failure-Driven Restarts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12088)**  `arXiv:2611.12088`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiangqin Chen, Zhengxuan Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Slay the Spire 2 (STS2) is a deck-building roguelike in which a winning run at the highest difficulty takes several hundred decisions, and most losses happen in late boss fights that a learner seldom reaches. We train agents that choose every action with one forward pass of a 6.6M-parameter entity transformer, without lookahead. Training and evaluation run the game's own compiled assembly (v0.107.1) in a headless .NET host. The host replays a run exactly from its seed and actions, so training episodes can restart from states the learner has visited, and a failure-driven curriculum allocates these restarts in proportion to where complete runs are lost. On 12,800 held-out games at Ascension 10 (A10), the released policies win 8.05% averaged over the five characters (Ironclad 15.6%, Regent 12.5%), and AlphaSpire's released networks used without search win 0.55% on a common panel. Longer A10 training raised Ironclad from 12.4% to 15.6% and Defect from 3.0% to 8.1% on these games. At Ascension 0 the released policies win 80.2%, level with the 80.3% that the tree-search agent Spire Mentor reports, while Mentor leads by 5 to 8 points from A4 upward. Run in our host on the same 2,000 A10 games, Mentor's search wins 8.8% against 8.2% for our policies, which need 21-27 ms per decision on one CPU core. GPT-6 Astra at maximum reasoning won none of 11 A10 games through our text interface. In a controlled study of 18 training runs from six random initializations, failure-driven allocation exceeds uniform restart allocation on each of three Regent initializations, by 3.6 percentage points on average, while the Silent effect is small and changes sign. Code, trained policies, an in-game mod and the records behind every table are atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Probing Transformer Representations Layer by Layer, Module by Module](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11531)**  `arXiv:2611.11531`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ambroise Odonnat, Theo Gnassounou, Laetitia Chapel, Romain Tavenard, Ievgen Redko_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Which representation should be probed in a transformer-based foundation model? Recent work shows that the standard practice of probing the final layer is often suboptimal, as intermediate layers can provide better features. Yet, representations are still largely studied along depth alone, treating each transformer block as an atomic unit. In this work, we analyze transformer representations layer by layer, module by module.
