Through more than 144,000 linear probing evaluations of vision foundation models (VFMs) and large language models (LLMs), we show that layer and module choices should be considered jointly: the best layer-module pair yields substantially larger gains over the final-layer residual stream than the best layer with the residual stream fixed. Moreover, module performance can vary substantially across layers, showing that useful representations cannot be characterized by depth alone. Building on these findings, we evaluate simple fixed probing strategies: the final-layer feedforward activation (Act@100) for vision and the second normalization layer at 60% depth (LN2@60) for language. These strategies improve over the probing baseline in a majority of evaluations and recover, on average, 42% and 86% of the gains from probing the best layer-module pair for vision and language, respectively. For VFMs, these gains increase with the transfer difficulty between pretraining and downstream data.
  </details>

- **[Phase-Conditioned Reward Shaping for Token-Efficient LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11024)**  `arXiv:2611.11024`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhe Chen, Xiaolin Zhou, Xiyang Hu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Extended reasoning can help language models solve complex problems, but long responses often contain both useful computations and redundant checks. Conventional length penalties encourage shorter responses without distinguishing the functions of intermediate steps. We propose Phase-Conditioned Reward Shaping (PCRS), a framework that combines phase identification with training-time reward allocation. We investigate two classification routes: PCRS-H with a hidden-state classifier and PCRS-T with a frozen text classifier. Our main text classifier implementation assigns overlapping reasoning, execution, and revision labels to native chunks, then uses these labels and exact repetition to adjust existing token penalties. We compare the completed configurations on six mathematical and scientific reasoning benchmarks and examine four reward-weight settings with the text classifier. Compared with a baseline using token-level length penalties, the default text classifier configuration reduces mean response length by 3.13%; sampled Pass@1 is approximately 62.07% for both methods, while oracle Pass@16 rises from 86.08% to 87.67%. These results show how phase-informed penalty allocation can reduce output volume and improve multi-attempt coverage without inference-time classification.
  </details>

- **[Where Should Models Run? A Collaborative Inference Framework for Embodied Intelligence in 6G](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11023)**  `arXiv:2611.11023`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Wanlu Sun, Zhenhui Yuan, Xiang Ji, Yi Han, Qing Li, Gan Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied intelligence systems rely on fast and reliable inference to enable real-time perception, reasoning, and control. Recent advances in large-scale artificial intelligence (AI) models, including Large Language Models (LLMs) and Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, have significantly enhanced the capabilities of embodied agents in complex environments. However, deploying these models remains challenging due to the limited computational resources and energy budgets of robotic platforms. A fundamental question is where inference should be executed. Collaborative inference, which partitions models across end devices, edge servers, and the cloud, has emerged as a promising solution. Yet determining the optimal partitioning point remains an open problem, as it depends on wireless network conditions, model structure, and privacy requirements. This article reviews the state of the art in collaborative inference for embodied intelligence and proposes an edge-centric framework in which the edge coordinates model partitioning and execution. Through both simulation and real-world experiments over WiFi and 5G networks, we demonstrate that the optimal partitioning strategy varies with network conditions and model structure. We further identify latency-minimizing partition points for ResNet-101, Qwen2.5-VL-7B, and OpenVLA-7B in collaborative inference between NVIDIA Jetson Nano or AGX Xavier devices and an AGX Thor edge server under varying wireless conditions. Finally, we discuss the future directions for collaborative inference in 6G-empowered embodied intelligence systems.
  </details>

- **[PIA: Agentic Physics-Informed Runtime Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11009)**  `arXiv:2611.11009`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Abbas Yazdinejad, Hadis Karimipour_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Runtime anomaly detectors for cyber-physical systems are either learned as black boxes from normal data, which miss stealthy manipulations that keep every signal inside its normal range, or derived by hand from physical models, which requires expert effort for every plant. We introduce physicsinformed agents (PIA), in which a large language model agent writes the detector itself as a set of physical residuals, such as pump characteristics, tank mass balances, and controller logic, and refines every candidate against normal operating data through an evaluation workbench before deployment. The result is a compact, human-readable detector that runs in milliseconds and names the component behind each alarm. On the BATADAL water distribution attack benchmark, PIA with Claude Opus 5.5 reaches an F1 of 0.878 on the test attacks against 0.504 for the strongest of six statistical and deep baselines, keeps detecting attacks concealed by replayed and offset sensor readings, and attributes every detected test attack to the attacked component. Ablations show that testing hypotheses against data is decisive: agents that write residuals from the plant description alone lose up to 0.28 F1, whereas agents that see only anonymized signals recover most of the physical structure.
  </details>

- **[GradReason: LLMs as Meta-Learners that Replace Training Epochs with Training-Data-Free Weight Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11005)**  `arXiv:2611.11005`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ali Mohammadi Ruzbahani, Abbas Yazdinejad, Hadis Karimipour_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard training recipes make a fixed number of passes over the training data, even when an epoch mostly adds mini-batch noise. We study whether a large language model (LLM) that reads the weight and gradient history of a run can replace some of these epochs with updates that compute no gradient and read no training image. In GradReason, an LLM agent is called every ten epochs. In a restricted sandbox, it analyses recent weight snapshots, epoch-mean gradients, and validation metrics, then submits a Python program that computes new weights and a plan marking each of the next ten epochs as data or virtual. A virtual epoch is kept only if it passes a forward-only check on 5,000 held-out validation images. In 120 runs on CIFAR-10 and CIFAR-100 with three architectures, Claude Opus 5.5 used 32.8% fewer data epochs than the 80-epoch recipe at 0.31 percentage points lower test accuracy (95% CI -0.72 to +0.09; 0.94 points on ResNet-18), on the recipe's own frontier of data against accuracy. Unprompted, Opus averaged recent weight snapshots in every run. This choice produced the savings: a fixed rule with the same acceptance check but extrapolations from prior work saved 5.4% of the data epochs, and the same rule with averaging added saved 25.0%. The LLM's contribution is to find a working update within a single run; once found, a fixed rule implements it equally well. An Opus run cost 0.68 USD, but at this scale the agent's latency exceeded the GPU time it saved.
  </details>

- **[Proximal Preference Optimization: A Unified Framework for Stable and Reward-Free Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10785)**  `arXiv:2611.10785`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Qisai Liu, Zhanhong Jiang, Zahid Hasan, Soumik Sarkar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce a proximal preference optimization framework that unifies the implicit reward formulation of Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) with the trust-region stability of Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Our framework yields two complementary algorithms. P$^2$O (Proximal Preference Optimization) introduces a clipped importance ratio directly into the DPO preference margin, producing a reward-model-free algorithm that supports safe dataset reuse, bounded policy divergence, and monotonic preference improvement guarantees. PKTO (Proximal Kahneman-Tversky Optimization) extends P$^2$O by replacing the DPO margin with KTO's prospect-theoretic utility margins, adding a self-regulating adaptive baseline that tightens the effective trust region as the policy drifts, and an asymmetric weighting that handles imbalanced or asymmetric preference datasets. We provide a unified theoretical analysis, including Kullback Leibler (KL) trust-region bounds, a policy-improvement lower bound, and convergence guarantees under multi-epoch proximal updates. Empirically, P$^2$O and PKTO match or exceed DPO and KTO in alignment quality while maintaining better stability under repeated dataset reuse across multiple LLMs on three preference benchmarks. The code base is available here.
  </details>

- **[Causal Discovery Bench: Can LLMs Autonomously Conduct Effective Experiments?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09934)**  `arXiv:2611.09934`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhuoyang Liu, Ning Peng, Ming Shen, Zijun Gao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language model (LLM) agents get actively adopted for autonomous research, they must not only reason over existing evidence, but also design informative experiments, allocate resources, analyze sequential observations, and decide when to stop and make conclusions. Yet these capabilities remain poorly measured. We introduce Causal-Discovery-Bench (this https URL), a verifiable sandbox with configurable structural equation models and resource constraints to evaluate sequential experimental design and causal discovery without domain-semantic cues. Across 3,802 episodes with 13 models from 5 vendors, we analyze agent behavior along 40 metrics in seven families. Agents' performance exhibits a four-level difficulty hierarchy spanning single-experiment design, sequential exploration of causal relationships, resource-aware experimentation balancing cost and statistical power, and autonomous resolution of conflicting evidence. We identify a substantial performance gap between causal inference from correctly curated evidence and navigating causal discovery through autonomous experimentation: Analyzing agents' action trajectories attributes 94.5% of their mistakes to experimental design rather than to inference, which is further consolidated with ablation studies. Relaxing resource constraints alone does not reliably resolve failures in sequential discovery. In contrast, basic statistical tools substantially improve performance across multiple model families, while advanced tools provide little additional benefit when models lack prerequisite lower-level capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Computation, not weight access: large language models as auditors of clinical prediction networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.07847)**  `arXiv:2611.07847`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyu Liu, Xinyu Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier large language models (LLMs) have been reported to read, edit and write the weights of small neural networks, raising the prospect of auditing clinical prediction models from their files alone. Public demonstrations, however, mixed reading raw weights with running code. We separated these conditions in internally pre-specified simulation experiments. Tanh networks of 105 to 38,401 weights were trained on simulated cohorts of patients with subarachnoid haemorrhage, each with a planted relationship or none, and one network was trained on labels that under-recorded deaths in a subgroup. GPT-6 Astra and four other GPT models answered single prompts under a default or an explicit system instruction, either with the weights in the prompt and no tools, or with a calculation-only tool that exposed the weights (white box) or only the network's predictions (black box). Without tools, Astra recovered planted relationships in 353-weight networks but became unreliable at 3,457 weights, and under the default instruction it declined 17 of 21 audit and repair tasks as unidentifiable; the explicit instruction removed these refusals. With the tool, it recovered the planted relationship in all 39 runs on networks of up to 38,401 weights, raised no false alarm in 21 runs on null networks, and found the biased subgroup in 6 of 6 runs. Black-box access performed as well as white-box access, and other GPT-6 models read as accurately. A well-designed script matched the comparator models on the audit and the best LLM repair. For auditing these networks, what mattered was computation and elicitation, not access to the weights.
  </details>

- **[The Geometry of Refusal: Why Post-Hoc Refusal Is Fragile and Pretraining-Time Refusal Persists](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.06934)**  `arXiv:2609.06934`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Srikanth Malla, Chiho Choi, Joon Hee Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-hoc safety training (RLHF, DPO) is the dominant way to align large language models, yet jailbreaks (Zou et al., 2023), fine-tuning attacks (Qi et al., 2024), and activation-space edits (Arditi et al., 2024) keep recovering the behaviors it was meant to remove. We give this fragility one geometric explanation and follow it into pretraining. We measure the safety update $\Delta = W_{safe} - W_{base}$ against the curvature of the model's capabilities (the empirical Fisher of a capability loss). Across five model families, post-hoc safety lands in a suppression regime: $\Delta$ is nearly orthogonal to the capability directions, and its small in-subspace part concentrates on a few high-curvature ones. The update is thin but sharp, a refusal gate laid over intact capabilities rather than erasure of them. A kernel-immobility lemma explains why such an update can only mask a capability, not remove it, and a little benign fine-tuning switches the gate off: 100 benign examples cut the AdvBench refusal of Qwen-2.5-7B-Instruct and Llama-3-8B-Instruct by 35 to 38 pp.Following the account into pretraining, a pretraining-checkpoint sweep of OLMo-2-1B (Team OLMo et al., 2024) shows the features that refusal attaches to emerging in a sharp transition between 1B and 63B pretraining tokens. We then use the account constructively: models trained from scratch with safety co-training spread continuously across pretraining reach 87 to 98% AdvBench refusal that the same attack erodes by only 2 to 14 pp at every scale from 410M to 6.9B, at a small cost on short-answer capability probes; a windowed schedule of equal total safety weight installs no refusal. Persistence, not timing, decides whether refusal survives. It does not keep output safer: by a harmful-output classifier, the attack raises harmful completions by 8 to 12 pp after post-hoc safety and by 11 to 27 pp after co-training.
  </details>

- **[HiFloat4 Format for End-To-End Reinforcement Learning Post-Training of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.26515)**  `arXiv:2607.26515`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Hei Yi Mak, Shadan Golestan, Hoang Le, Mehran Taghian Jazi, Yunke Peng, Yaoyuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present, to our knowledge, the first end-to-end FP4 RL post-training, in which both the rollout and training policies, including their forward and backward passes, operate at 4-bit precision. A systematic study reveals that the dominant source of degradation in FP4 RL is not training-side quantization error but rollout activation quantization: outliers stretch the dynamic range so far that a large number of activation values underflow to zero under FP4. Counterintuitively, restoring the training policy to higher precision while keeping the rollout in FP4 makes accuracy worse than full FP4 baseline, exposing rollout--training mismatch as the principal failure mode and ruling out standard pretraining-style fixes. We address this with Rollout Residual Quantization (Rollout-ResQ): a single residual correction term constrained to a hardware-friendly sparsity pattern, added only to the FP4 rollout matmul -- a lightweight correction that recovers most of the precision lost to outlier-driven underflow without inflating the rollout's compute footprint. On Qwen2.5-3B, Rollout-ResQ paired with the HiFloat4 (HiF4) format -- whose three-level hierarchical scaling preserves resolution under FP4's tight 4-bit budget -- closes the accuracy gap to BF16 from 3.59% to 1.39%, bringing fully quantized FP4 RL within close reach of full precision. Applied to the open-standard MXFP4, the same recipe narrows the gap from 13.6% to 5.3%, revealing that FP4 format choice is a key factor that determines the ceiling on recoverable accuracy. The experimental results on Qwen2.5-Math-7B further confirm the recovery benefits of Rollout-ResQ with HiF4. Together, these results establish HiF4 as the enabling format for end-to-end FP4 RL post-training, and Rollout-ResQ as the activation-side mechanism that makes the gap to BF16 closable.
  </details>

- **[Toward Auditable Fraud Detection: Combining Graph Features, Model Explanations, and Agentic Case Investigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.19266)**  `arXiv:2607.19266`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Rahil Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fraud detection systems must scale with rising transaction volume while remaining explainable and reviewable. We study a layered pipeline on the PaySim dataset that combines a gradient-boosted classifier, graph-derived structural features, an autoencoder-based anomaly signal, TreeSHAP explanations, and a bounded LLM investigation agent applied to cases the classifier scores uncertainly. Before any model comparison, we identify and remove a simulator-specific balance shortcut that would otherwise inflate baseline performance. After this correction, neither the graph features nor the anomaly signal improves Average Precision on the full test set. Both, however, rank fraud better within the subset of cases receiving intermediate baseline scores. In a controlled experiment with injected multi-account fraud rings, engineered structural features recover all injected test transactions, while the tabular baseline misses roughly a quarter of them. The investigation agent underperforms direct thresholding of the classifier it relies on, reaching 65.0% accuracy against 71.7% on a balanced 60-case sample, despite having access to model explanations, graph context, and retrieved reference cases. Of the eight decisions the agent changed, six replaced correct classifier outputs with errors, and it produced a coherent written rationale in each case. An exploratory disagreement-based escalation rule flagged two of these agent errors for human review without flagging any correct decision. We conclude that each component of a layered fraud system contributes only under specific conditions, and that a plausible rationale from an investigation agent is not evidence of a better decision.
  </details>

- **[Prompt Driven Exploration: Language as an Exploration Space for VLA Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.08837)**  `arXiv:2607.08837`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Sunshine Jiang, John Marangola, David Zhang, Raghuram Kowdeed, Ruiyang Luo, Nitish Dashora, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Exploration is essential to reinforcement learning (RL) since a policy cannot improve by repeatedly sampling the behaviors it already prefers. Standard methods inject stochasticity in the action space, but such jitter only yields rollouts close to the original action distribution. Escaping a weak policy often requires global perturbations that action noise cannot produce. Large language models (LLMs) and vision-language-action (VLA) models offer a pathway: they condition the policy on a natural language prompt, and since the rollout follows from it, modifying the prompt induces global changes. The challenge is finding prompts that induce useful global changes. With a weak policy that rarely succeeds, reward is too sparse to select on. Our idea is to refine prompts from the rollouts themselves: a vision-language model (VLM) reasons over the rollout video, diagnoses how the policy responded, and rewrites the prompt to elicit better behavior next time. Sampling a prompt and holding it fixed throughout a rollout provides episode-level commitment, analogous to posterior-sampling exploration. We call this strategy Prompt Driven Exploration (PDE). Across manipulation and reasoning tasks, PDE enables RL to learn successful policies even from zero-reward starts, and improves sample efficiency more broadly. Our website is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LAPO: KL-Free Logit-Averaged Policy Optimization to Leverage RL and SFT in LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20555)**  `arXiv:2605.20555`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xingwei Gan, Ying Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  From a classical statistical perspective, model averaging and parameter shrinkage (e.g., Ridge regression with an $l_2$-penalty) are deeply connected mechanisms designed to reduce estimator variance and lower mean squared error. Motivated by this insight, we introduce Logit-Averaged Policy Optimization (LAPO), a KL-free and critic-free post-training framework that replaces explicit KL regularization with logit averaging inside Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO); in particular, averaging the logits of the trainable policy and a frozen reference policy functions as an implicit structural regularizer that stabilizes rollout trajectories. For selecting the mixing weight, we propose a compute-efficient adaptive algorithm that dynamically balances exploration and structural anchoring throughout training, evaluated against a static fixed-weight baseline. This adaptive mechanism eliminates explicit KL penalty backpropagation and expensive, fragile hyperparameter sweeps while reducing the incidence of uninformative zero-reward rollout groups. Mathematically, embedding logit averaging within the reinforcement learning loop operates as an in-training Product of Experts (PoE): the multiplicative geometric mean enables the reference anchor to act as a soft logical "AND" that vetoes non-compliant tokens, enforcing schema rigor and safety guardrails without the catastrophic forgetting typical of sequential fine-tuning. Evaluated across Qwen2.5-Instruct models (1.5B, 3B, and 7B), LAPO consistently outperforms canonical KL-regularized GRPO, achieving a 7%-15% accuracy gain on MATH and a 2%-5% gain on cn_k12 and MMLU. Finally, the resulting mixed teacher policy can be distilled back into a single student network, fully restoring standard $1\times$ inference latency.
  </details>

- **[Alignment Dynamics in LLM Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.18309)**  `arXiv:2605.18309`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuhan Huang, Huanran Chen, Yinpeng Dong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although Large Language Models (LLMs) achieve strong alignment through supervised fine-tuning and reinforcement learning from human feedback, the alignment is often fragile under subsequent fine-tuning. Existing explanations either attribute alignment fragility to gradient geometry or characterize it as a distributional shift in model outputs, yet few provide a unified account that bridges parameter-space learning dynamics with function-space alignment behavior during fine-tuning. In this work, we introduce a tractable alignment score and derive its closed-form update during fine-tuning, yielding a unified framework for alignment dynamics. Our analysis decomposes alignment updates into two competing components: a Rebound Force, governed jointly by the current alignment state and the narrowness of model distribution, and a Driving Force, determined by how the training distribution aligns with outcome-conditioned posteriors over aligned and non-aligned completions. This decomposition explains why prior alignment can be reversed by later fine-tuning and why narrower posterior structure strengthens such reversal. Moreover, our framework predicts a Rehearsal Priming Effect: prior alignment leaves a latent posterior imprint that amplifies the effective Driving Force upon re-exposure, leading to faster re-alignment. We validate these predictions across safety alignment, emergent misalignment, and sentiment settings, demonstrating consistent alignment reversal and accelerated re-alignment under re-exposure. In addition, controlled experiments in safety alignment confirm the predicted dependence of rebound strength on posterior narrowness. Together, these results provide a unified dynamical perspective on how alignment is disrupted and reactivated during LLM fine-tuning.
  </details>

- **[Teacher-Guided Policy Optimization for On-Policy Reasoning Distillation under Large Policy Divergence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.13230)**  `arXiv:2605.13230`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Liu, Kechen Jiao, Chunyang Xiao, Runsong Zhao, Junhao Ruan, Bei Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) has become a promising paradigm for reasoning-oriented post-training of large language models (LLMs), especially when combined with reinforcement learning from verifiable rewards (RLVR). Existing OPD methods rely on reverse KL (RKL)-based teacher supervision over trajectories sampled from the student policy. However, we identify a critical limitation: under large teacher--student policy divergence, RL-driven exploration often produces trajectories outside the teacher distribution, resulting in uninformative negative feedback. To address this, we propose Teacher-Guided Policy Optimization (TGPO), an on-policy reasoning distillation method that remains effective under large policy divergence settings. Rather than relying solely on evaluative supervision, TGPO uses teacher to directly guide token level generation conditioning on student-generated contexts; together with RLVR-style trajectory level rewards, TGPO steers exploration toward improved continuations. Experiments on reasoning benchmarks show that TGPO consistently outperforms existing RKL-based OPD methods and remains robust across different teacher models.
  </details>

- **[Shifting the Gradient: Understanding How Defensive Training Methods Protect Language Model Integrity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16423)**  `arXiv:2604.16423`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Satchel Grant, Victor Gillioz, Jake Ward, Thomas McGrath_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Defensive training methods such as positive preventative steering (PPS) and inoculation prompting (IP) offer surprising results through seemingly similar processes: both add trait-inducing objects to large language models (LLMs) during training, and both defend the LLM against acquiring the trait. The success of these methods raises the questions: how do they work, and are they doing the same thing? We provide behavioral and mechanistic analyses of PPS and IP, using the acquisition of an ``evil'' persona by Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct as an in-depth case study and replicating key measurements across six traits and four models. Behaviorally, neither method operates through a purely associative mechanism, and both methods prevent trait acquisition; but PPS can also substantially reduce pre-existing trait expression, whereas IP does so only modestly. Mechanistically, PPS induces a concentrated change in the activation gradient and shifts the signal along the trait direction from amplifying to attenuating, whereas IP changes the gradient more diffusely and shifts it close to zero along the trait direction. Gradient manipulations show that the direction of this component is causally relevant to trait acquisition, although it is one effect rather than a complete account of either defense. We then show that IP lowers the next-token prediction loss on trait-expressing data, whereas PPS can raise it at the intensities at which it defends; we show how this analysis raises the possibility that the loss changes and the gradient shift are two summaries of the same training signal rather than completely separate mechanisms. Together, our results show that PPS and IP are not interchangeable, and they highlight open questions about a complete mechanistic picture.
  </details>

- **[Understanding the Challenges in Iterative Generative Optimization with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.23994)**  `arXiv:2603.23994`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Allen Nie, Xavier Daull, Zhiyi Kuang, Abhinav Akkiraju, Anish Chaudhuri, Max Piasevoli, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative optimization uses large language models (LLMs) to iteratively improve artifacts (such as code, workflows or prompts) using execution feedback. It is a promising approach to building self-improving agents, yet in practice remains brittle: despite active research, only 9% of surveyed agents used any automated optimization. We argue that this brittleness arises because, to set up a learning loop, an engineer must make "hidden" design choices: What can the optimizer edit and what is the ``right'' learning evidence to provide at each update? We investigate three factors that affect most applications: the starting artifact, the credit horizon for execution traces, and batching trials and errors into learning evidence. Through case studies in MLAgentBench, Atari, and BigBench Extra Hard, we find that these design decisions can determine whether generative optimization succeeds, yet they are rarely made explicit in prior work. Different starting artifacts determine which solutions are reachable in MLAgentBench, truncated traces can still improve Atari agents, and larger minibatches do not monotonically improve generalization on BBEH. We then study these factors jointly in harness optimization on TBLite, evaluated on Terminal-Bench 2.0. We also propose a batch-size and credit-horizon rule that, calibrated on TBLite, transfers to DeepSWE after adjusting to its token costs and comes within 1% of the best configuration tested at each context budget.
  </details>

- **[Transformers converge to invariant algorithmic cores](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.22600)**  `arXiv:2602.22600`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Joshua S. Schiffman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training selects for behavior, not circuitry: many weight configurations can implement the same function. Studying any single trained neural network thus risks describing accidents of one training run rather than the computation itself. This work shifts focus from what transformers happen to do to what they must do by extracting algorithmic cores, compact subspaces that are necessary and sufficient for a task and that recur across independently trained models. Here, Algorithmic Core Extraction (ACE) is introduced to isolate these subspaces, causally validate them, and recover the algorithms they implement, from synthetic tasks to large pretrained models. Markov-chain transformers embed 3D cores in nearly orthogonal subspaces yet recover identical transition spectra. Modular-addition transformers form compact cyclic cores at grokking that later inflate under continued regularization, redundantly distributing the same computation across many functionally equivalent modes. This functional redundancy accelerates the transition from memorization to generalization, yielding an inverse scaling law for grokking time. In six language models spanning two orders of magnitude in scale (GPT-2 Small/Medium/Large, LLaMA-3.1, Gemma-2, and Qwen2.5), subject-verb agreement is governed by a single, steerable axis that aligns across architectures. Flipping this axis inverts grammatical number throughout open-ended generation. For indirect object identification, a multi-step task, the same six models route computation through compact multi-dimensional cores at two sites, linked by a linear operator that transfers across model families. Together these results suggest that beneath the apparent complexity of trained transformers lies a simpler, shared computational structure, and that targeting invariants rather than parameterizations may offer a more tractable path to mechanistic understanding and control.
  </details>

- **[Rank-Aware Spectral Bounds on Attention Logits for Stable Low-Precision Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18851)**  `arXiv:2602.18851`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Seyed Morteza Emadi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Attention scores in transformers are bilinear forms $S_{ij} = x_i^\top M x_j / \sqrt{d_h}$ whose maximum magnitude governs overflow risk in low-precision training. We derive a rank-aware concentration inequality: when the interaction matrix $M = W^Q W^{K\top}$ has rank $r \ll d$, tail probabilities for $\max_{i,j}|S_{ij}|$ decay as $\exp(-d^{2}\alpha^{2}/(\gamma r))$ rather than $\exp(-d\alpha^{2})$, where $\gamma > 1$. For transformer attention where $r = d_h$, this yields $8$ to $28\times$ tighter concentration than rank-agnostic bounds in modern architectures. We apply this result to FP8 training, deriving geometry-aware scale factors that provide principled overflow guarantees without observing activations. The method computes per-layer scales from the spectral norm $\|W^Q W^{K\top}\|_2$ via implicit power iteration, includes a grouped query attention formulation that avoids key expansion, and remains compatible with fused attention kernels. Across GPT-2 XL to Llama-2-70B, geometry-aware scaling eliminates overflows in transient scenarios where delayed scaling fails, while achieving comparable downstream MMLU accuracy.
  </details>

- **[JPmHC Dynamical Isometry via Orthogonal Hyper-Connections](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.18308)**  `arXiv:2602.18308`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Biswa Sengupta, Jinhua Wang, Leo Brunswic_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The hyper-connection paradigm separates the skip-connections of transformers into $n$ parallel streams on which a linear mixer acts.Constraining the mixer to bistochastic matrices has proven effective in preventing gradient explosion but risk collapsing onto low-rank configurations, limiting expressivity.We therefore propose JPmHC (Jacobian-spectrum Preserving manifold-constrained Hyper-Connections).Our framework triages manifold constraints by computing the Jacobian singular spectrum of the end-to-end network in the mean-field limit and their spectral stability. It consists of: (i) an operator-valued free-probability library that predicts the Jacobian singular spectrum; (ii) an orthogonal mixer computed by a fixed-point Cayley iteration, which enables dynamical isometry; (iii) implicit differentiation of the Sinkhorn projection of the bistochastic baseline, with $O(1)$ backward operations; and (iv) a sensitivity theory giving the training budget before a bistochastic mixer collapses.The library is written in Lean, which certifies the mathematics and the algorithms.We validate JPmHC with TRM on ARC-AGI and nano-GPT on FineWeb-Edu: the theory predicts spectral collapse for TRM and stability for nano-GPT as observed in our experiments.
  </details>

- **[RAPTOR: Ridge-Adaptive Logistic Probes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00158)**  `arXiv:2602.00158`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Ziqi Gao, Yaotian Zhu, Qingcheng Zeng, Xu Zhao, Ziqing Wang, Feng Ruan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probing studies what information is encoded in a frozen LLM's layer representations by training a lightweight predictor on top of them. Beyond analysis, probes are often used operationally in probe-then-steer pipelines: a learned concept vector is extracted from a probe and injected via additive activation steering by adding it to a layer representation during the forward pass. The effectiveness of this pipeline hinges on estimating concept vectors that are accurate, directionally stable under ablation, and inexpensive to obtain. Motivated by these desiderata, we propose RAPTOR (Ridge-Adaptive Logistic Probe), a simple L2-regularized logistic probe whose validation-tuned ridge strength yields concept vectors from normalized weights. Across extensive experiments on instruction-tuned LLMs and human-written concept datasets, RAPTOR matches or exceeds strong baselines in accuracy while achieving competitive directional stability and substantially lower training cost; these quantitative results are supported by qualitative downstream steering demonstrations. Finally, using the Convex Gaussian Min-max Theorem (CGMT), we provide a mechanistic characterization of ridge logistic regression in an idealized Gaussian teacher-student model in the high-dimensional few-shot regime, explaining how penalty strength mediates probe accuracy and concept-vector stability and yielding structural predictions that qualitatively align with trends observed on real LLM embeddings.
  </details>

- **[Advancing Model Refinement: Muon-Optimized Distillation and Quantization for LLM Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.09865)**  `arXiv:2601.09865`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Jacob Sander, Brian Jalaian, Venkat R. Dasari_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Correction notice (v2). Two implementation issues affect these experiments. All LoRA training ran under DeepSpeed, which replaced the optimizer passed to the Trainer with AdamW at a constant learning rate, so the Muon and Adam arms used the same optimizer. The cross-entropy term was computed without the next-token shift, so the preference for alpha = 1 reflects that objective rather than the value of the teacher. Each comparison also uses a single GPTQ quantization, and repeated quantizations differ by up to several points. The findings on Muon's quantization robustness, the distillation weight and accuracy relative to GPTQ alone should not be relied on. See the notice on page 1.Large Language Models (LLMs) enable advanced natural language processing but face deployment challenges on resource-constrained edge devices due to high computational, memory, and energy demands. Optimizing these models requires addressing three key challenges: acquiring task-specific data, fine-tuning for performance, and compressing models to accelerate inference while reducing resource demands. We propose an integrated framework combining GPTQ-based quantization, low-rank adaptation (LoRA), and a specialized data distillation process to significantly reduce model size and complexity while preserving or enhancing task-specific performance. By leveraging data distillation, knowledge distillation via Kullback-Leibler divergence, Bayesian hyperparameter optimization, and the Muon optimizer, our pipeline achieves up to 2x memory compression (e.g., reducing a 6GB model to 3GB) and enables efficient inference for specialized tasks. Empirical results demonstrate superior performance on standard LLM benchmarks compared to GPTQ quantization alone, with the Muon optimizer notably enhancing fine-tuned models' resistance to accuracy decay during quantization.
  </details>

- **[SE-Diff: Simulator and Experience Enhanced Diffusion Model for Comprehensive ECG Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.09895)**  `arXiv:2511.09895`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoda Wang, Kaiqiao Han, Yuhao Xu, Xiao Luo, Yizhou Sun, Wei Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cardiovascular disease (CVD) is a leading cause of mortality worldwide. Electrocardiograms (ECGs) are the most widely used non-invasive tool for cardiac assessment, yet large, well-annotated ECG corpora are scarce due to cost, privacy, and workflow constraints. Generating ECGs can be beneficial for the mechanistic understanding of cardiac electrical activity, enable the construction of large, heterogeneous, and unbiased datasets, and facilitate privacy-preserving data sharing. Generating realistic ECG signals from clinical context is important yet underexplored. Recent work has leveraged diffusion models for text-to-ECG generation, but two challenges remain: (i) existing methods often overlook the physiological simulator knowledge of cardiac activity; and (ii) they ignore broader, experience-based clinical knowledge grounded in real-world practice. To address these gaps, we propose SE-Diff, a novel physiological simulator and experience enhanced diffusion model for comprehensive ECG generation. SE-Diff integrates a lightweight ordinary differential equation (ODE)-based ECG simulator into the diffusion process via a beat decoder and simulator-consistent constraints, injecting mechanistic priors that promote physiologically plausible waveforms. In parallel, we design an LLM-powered experience retrieval-augmented strategy to inject clinical knowledge, providing more guidance for ECG generation. Extensive experiments on real-world ECG datasets demonstrate that SE-Diff improves both signal fidelity and text-ECG semantic alignment over baselines, proving its superiority for text-to-ECG generation. We further show that the simulator-based and experience-based knowledge also benefit downstream ECG classification.
  </details>

- **[Language in the Flow of Time: Time-Series-Paired Texts Weaved into a Unified Temporal Narrative](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.08942)**  `arXiv:2502.08942`  `cs.LG` `cs.AI`  
  _Zihao Li, Xiao Lin, Zhining Liu, Jiaru Zou, Ziwei Wu, Lecheng Zheng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While many advances in time series models focus exclusively on numerical data, research on multimodal time series, particularly those involving contextual textual information, remains in its infancy. With recent progress in large language models and time series learning, we revisit the integration of paired texts with time series through the Platonic Representation Hypothesis, which posits that representations of different modalities converge to shared spaces. In this context, we identify that time-series-paired texts may naturally exhibit periodic properties that closely mirror those of the original time series. Building on this insight, we propose a novel framework, Texts as Time Series (TaTS), which considers the time-series-paired texts to be auxiliary variables of the time series. TaTS can be plugged into any existing numerical-only time series models and effectively enable them to handle time series data with paired texts. Through extensive experiments on both multimodal time series forecasting and imputation tasks across benchmark datasets with various existing time series models, we demonstrate that TaTS can enhance multimodal predictive performance without modifying model architectures. Our Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Agentic Deception via Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12835)**  `arXiv:2611.12835`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hanxiao Li, Shutong Liu, Yongjia Ma, Jiaming Ji, Juntao Dai, Shuning Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models become more capable agents, they can exhibit deceptive behaviors, cheating human for higher rewards while appearing helpful. We attribute this failure to evaluation-side partial observability, where models are evaluated mainly from final outcome, without checking the faithfulness of underlying reasoning, tool use, and environmental effects. Prior mitigations address this partial observability by predefined evaluator latent belief space or one-shot monitoring, which do not extend to free-form language space and vulnerable to stronger deceptions that evade simple monitoring. In this work, we formulate deception mitigation as an Evaluation-Side Partially Observable Extensive Form Game (e-PoG), a max-min framework in which an agent maximizes reward, a monitor minimizes the agent's payoff by exposing deception from the full interaction trajectory, and a fixed verifier validates the monitor's claims and converts them into penalties. We define the solution concept as a Subgame-Perfect Oversight Equilibrium (SPOE) between agent and monitor, and prove its existence in finite games. With a perfect verifier, every SPOE is faithful; with an imperfect verifier, the guarantee persists if verifier errors do not reverse the ranking of monitor actions. To achieve faithful SPOE, we derive a two-timescale policy gradient algorithm and enhance the pipeline empirically with NLI verification and deterministic checks. Experiments with Gemma3-4B-IT and Qwen3-4B/8B on Agentic Role-Playing, SearchQA, and WebShop show that e-PoG mitigates multiple emergent deception modes, outperforming the strongest baselines by 26.0% in deception mitigation and by up to 39.8% in task success. These gains persist under weak-to-strong supervision. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learning Evidence-Grounded Reasoning for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12285)**  `arXiv:2611.12285`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xingpeng Gao, Mingyue Cheng, Daoyu Wang, Huajian Zhang, Xiaoyu Tao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-driven time series forecasting has become increasingly promising by leveraging contextual features to generate future values directly or refine forecast priors. However, incorporating contextual features alone does not ensure that models can identify which features matter for forecasting and understand what they suggest about future dynamics. We argue that the fundamental challenge is how to extract predictive evidence from contextual features and when and how to leverage it to guide forecasting decisions. In this paper, we propose CastReasoner, an evidence-grounded reasoning model for context-aware time series forecasting. Specifically, CastReasoner uses a structured chain of thought (CoT) to extract predictive evidence from contextual features, infer the future dynamics suggested by this evidence, and assess these dynamics against the forecast prior to guide adaptive forecasting decisions. To enable this capability, we design a reasoning-centric two-stage post-training framework including supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL). First, we synthesize reasoning trajectories through controllable multi-teacher guidance and select high-quality ones using a novel student-aware difficulty metric. Second, we design fine-grained multi-objective rewards to further improve reasoning and forecasting. To address heterogeneous learning needs across domains in both stages, we introduce a convergence-guided data balancing strategy. Through this two-stage training, CastReasoner improves both reasoning quality and predictive accuracy. Extensive experiments across multi-domain forecasting benchmarks demonstrate that CastReasoner achieves superior forecasting performance.
  </details>

- **[Correct First, Fast Second: What a Local 7B Code Model Can and Cannot Do for GPU Kernel Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12079)**  `arXiv:2611.12079`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jui-Chien Tsou, Po-Yi Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We study how much the interaction strategy around a fixed, locally hosted 7B-parameter code model (Qwen2.5-Coder-7B-Instruct) affects its ability to replace PyTorch operators with custom CUDA kernels. On 30 KernelBench tasks (levels 1-3, first 10 each, evaluated on a V100), we compare four regimes of increasing structure: zero-shot prompting, profiling-guided prompting, iterative refinement on execution feedback, and a multi-agent loop (analysis, retrieval, generation, evaluation, feedback triage) in which the same model fills four roles around a non-LLM evaluator. Three findings stand out. (1) Correctness, not speed, is the binding constraint: even correct generated kernels average 0.97-0.99x the speed of eager PyTorch, because a 7B model cannot out-write cuBLAS/cuDNN on dense GEMM and convolution; the only real wins are fused memory-bound epilogues. (2) The best strategy depends on difficulty: profiling-guided prompting is strongest overall (43% correct, 27% faster than baseline), but the agent loop is the only method that solves any level-2 fusion problem (3/10 vs. 0/10 for all others). (3) Naive agentic scaffolding is actively harmful: our first agent scored 3% correct, below zero-shot's 30%, and the decisive repairs were matching the agent's ambition to the model's competence and spending compute on best-of-n sampling under a real execution oracle (3% to 33% correct from n=1 to n=8), not on more reasoning steps.
  </details>

- **[Means vs. Foundation Models for Emergency Call Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11656)**  `arXiv:2611.11656`  `cs.LG`  
  _Feriel Fass, Djemel Ziou, Marie-Flavie Auclair-Fortier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study investigates whether generalized means can achieve forecasting performance comparable to that of TimeGPT for daily emergency call volume prediction. Specifically, two families of generalized means, the Hölder means (HM) and the Lehmer means (LM), are evaluated against fine-tuned TimeGPT across forecasting horizons ranging from one to seven days. Forecasting performance is assessed on a real-world emergency call dataset using multiple error metrics, and Diebold-Mariano test. Experimental results show that HM and LM achieve nearly identical predictive performance across all forecasting horizons. For short-term forecasting, both generalized means outperform TimeGPT, with reductions of mean absolute error (MAE) approximately 1.24\% and 2.48\% at horizons of one and three days, respectively. Conversely, TimeGPT achieves a lower error at the seven-day horizon, with an descresing of MAE by 1.98\%. However, Diebold-Mariano tests reveal no statistically significant differences between the methods at the evaluated horizons. These findings suggest that generalized means can provide competitive forecasting performance compared with a energyvore foundation model.
  </details>

- **[Daily Emergency Call Volume Forecasting using Univariate and Multivariate TimeGPT](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11613)**  `arXiv:2611.11613`  `cs.LG`  
  _Feriel Fass, Djemel Ziou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study investigates the potential of TimeGPT, a pretrained foundation model for timeseries, for forecasting daily emergency call volumes. Real-world emergency call data, comprising 2,101 daily observations, are used to evaluate both univariate and multivariate forecasting settings under one-step-ahead and seven-day multi-step-ahead scenarios. The multivariate forecasting incorporates temporal, meteorological, demographic, and socioeconomic variables. A chronological training, validation, and test split is adopted to prevent information leakage, while hyperparameters are selected using the validation set through grid search and early stopping when applicable. Forecasting performance is evaluated using multiple error metrics. Its forecasting performance is compared with several established forecasting approaches, including Seasonal Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average with Exogenous Regressors (SARIMAX), Poisson regression (PR), Negative Binomial regression (NBR), Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost), Multilayer Perceptron (MLP), and Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM). The results show that SARIMAX achieves the best overall performance in the univariate setting. In the multivariate setting, NBR and PR provide best performance. These findings indicate that the large-scale pretraining of TimeGPT do not necessarily translate into superior forecasting accuracy for emergency call volumes. Frugal methods trained solely on the available data remain better for this application.
  </details>

- **[Actions Should EARN Their Credit: Test-Time Adaptation and Route Consolidation for Frozen LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11565)**  `arXiv:2611.11565`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bian Sun, Kevin Zhai, Mubarak Shah, Song Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents can use feedback from repeated attempts to improve their decisions without updating model parameters. However, positive action credits can continue to favor actions that repeatedly achieve the same score, even when higher scores are achievable. Under a fixed interaction budget, trying new actions in the final attempts can also lower performance. We introduce EARN, a method that uses past experience to guide further progress and retain earlier performance through conditional reuse of effective action sequences. EARN reduces action credits for reaching scores repeatedly attained in earlier episodes and assigns larger credits to action segments that reach higher scores. These credits adjust the probabilities of LLM-proposed actions. During the final episodes, EARN directly replays stored actions when the current context matches the recorded context and the actions remain available, bypassing LLM candidate generation. We evaluate performance at the end of the interaction budget using the average score over the final four episodes. Across Jericho, ScienceWorld, and Crafter, EARN achieves the best score on this metric in 31 of 33 task--budget settings among the evaluated methods, including ties. Across the evaluated Jericho budgets, replay bypasses 78.9--86.3\% of candidate-generation calls in the final four episodes, averaged across games.
  </details>

- **[Spatial Harness: Grounding Spatial Reasoning in Executable World States with Physics Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11344)**  `arXiv:2611.11344`  `cs.LG`  
  _Chentao Cao, Zhanke Zhou, Sangni Duan, Bo Han, Hang Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spatial reasoning requires understanding how objects and observers are arranged in a scene and how their relations change with viewpoint or movement. Vision-language models (VLMs) typically perform both steps through model-internal reasoning, forming an implicit scene interpretation from partial views and imagining the consequences of spatial transformations. A misread scene can lead to a wrong answer, while even a correct interpretation does not ensure that imagined transformations satisfy spatial constraints. We introduce Spatial Harness, which represents scenes as explicit and executable world states to ground spatial reasoning in physics-engine feedback. A Perceiver integrates scene constraints extracted from the question with specialized visual estimates to place objects and cameras in a shared coordinate frame. The resulting state is instantiated in a physics engine, where a Reasoner executes viewpoint changes and uses computed measurements and rendered views to guide reasoning. We evaluate on MindCube, SAT, MMSI-Bench, and SpatialRGPT-Bench with open- and closed-weight VLMs. On MindCube, Spatial Harness improves Qwen3.5-27B from 52.70% to 71.14%, surpassing pySpatial by 7.52 percentage points. Given identical annotated object and camera poses, it reaches 98.25% versus 71.81% for model-internal reasoning. Code is publicly available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Compositional Conformal Risk Control for Multi-Stage LLM Cascades](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11114)**  `arXiv:2611.11114`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yuxia Sun, Jiahuang Wang, Haoting Liu, Zhiwei Huang, Tingrui Pei, Saiqin Long_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-stage large language model (LLM) cascades balance accuracy and inference cost, but reliable deployment also requires controlling error risk and identifying its sources across stages. We propose Compositional Conformal Risk Control (CompCRC), a risk-control framework for pre-specified multi-stage LLM cascades. We decompose the risk of accepting incorrect answers into additive stage-wise contributions over the full query population, linking local risk budgets to a system-level guarantee. CompCRC constructs and freezes candidate budget allocations and acceptance thresholds, selects one candidate on independent data, and certifies its stage-wise risk contributions on separate held-out data. Under conditional exchangeability, we establish a finite-sample guarantee: the marginal expected accepted-error risk of the complete deployment procedure does not exceed the global risk budget. At the reported comparison budget of $\alpha=0.45$, all three stages of CompCRC pass their local checks on MMLU, GSM8K, and HellaSwag. Compared with Global-CRC, which passes system-level risk certification at the same budget, CompCRC reduces observed accepted-error risk by 1.33--2.00 percentage points, improves final accuracy by 0.67--2.00 percentage points, and reduces weighted inference cost by 30.37\% on HellaSwag. The selected cost-based budget allocation also improves accuracy per unit cost by 10.6\%--46.7\% over uniform allocation. Further experiments show that stage-wise statistics identify stages that fail local certification checks, providing diagnostic information alongside system-level risk control.
  </details>

- **[BRAT: Bounded Residual-Aligned Training for Stable Multi-Pass LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10873)**  `arXiv:2611.10873`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xunlan Zhou, Yuzhe Zhao, Xuhong Huang, Feng Chen, Xuanlin Chen, Lang Feng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning for large language model post-training often reuses each batch of sampled rollouts for several gradient passes to amortize the cost of generation. Under such multi-pass reuse, existing objectives fail in different ways: unclipped updates collapse as the policy drifts away from the sampling policy, adding a negative baseline avoids collapse but reinforces every sampled response, and PPO-style ratio clipping cuts off gradients abruptly. We first ask why reuse is fragile. Measuring how well an update estimated from a small rollout group aligns with the KL-regularized target on held-out rollouts of the same prompts, we find that this alignment drops by nearly an order of magnitude from in-sample to held-out rollouts across three base models, suggesting that repeated passes largely overfit the sampled group. Motivated by this finding, we propose Bounded Residual-Aligned Training (BRAT), motivated by a proximal update toward a bounded reweighting of the sampling policy. BRAT assigns each token a gradient weight that decreases linearly with its probability ratio and vanishes at a trust-region boundary, and normalizes group-centered rewards by their maximum magnitude, keeping advantages zero-mean and bounded without extra hyperparameters. With four reuse passes, BRAT avoids training collapse, limits policy drift, and achieves higher average accuracy with lower seed variance on most benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[AERO: Adaptive Ensemble-Disagreement Routing for Oracle Feedback in Sample-Efficient Online RLHF](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10750)**  `arXiv:2611.10750`  `cs.LG`  
  _Shrey Singhal, Nannan Huang, Haytham M. Fayek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning Large Language Models (LLMs) with human preferences via Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback (RLHF) has driven substantial improvements across a wide range of tasks, but the cost of acquiring preference labels remains the dominant bottleneck in the alignment pipeline. While many recent alignment methods improve sample efficiency through active exploration over candidate responses, they typically query the oracle uniformly across contexts, even for contexts where the learned reward estimates are already reliable enough to provide synthetic labels. We propose Adaptive Ensemble-disagreement Routing for Oracle feedback (AERO), a fully online RLHF algorithm built on a Dyna-style model-based RL architecture, in which an ensemble-based Epistemic Reward Model (ERM) serves both as a reward posterior for exploration and as a learned preference-feedback model for synthetic labeling. AERO uses ensemble vote entropy, a Query-by-Committee disagreement measure, combined with an adaptive thresholding mechanism to decide which contexts require oracle feedback and which can instead receive ERM-based synthetic preference labels, concentrating expensive labels where they are most informative. Empirical results across different model families and scales show that AERO achieves highly competitive alignment performance while reducing the required oracle-query budget compared to online DPO and strong active-exploration baselines.
  </details>

- **[AbductionBench: Benchmarking Abductive Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10705)**  `arXiv:2611.10705`  `cs.LG`  
  _Moein Salimi, Amir Qeysarbeigi, Babak Hosseini Mohtasham, Faraz Doagoye Tehrani, Mahdi Naieni, Hanieh Sartipi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Abductive reasoning is the inference of plausible explanations for observed evidence. Evaluating this capability requires more than a single task: a system may need to generate an explanation, discriminate among competing hypotheses, revise its hypotheses as evidence arrives, or decide what evidence to seek next. Yet these capabilities are typically evaluated through isolated benchmarks with different task formulations and scoring procedures, so claims that a model is ``good at abduction'' may reflect only a narrow aspect of this capability. We introduce AbductionBench, a unified framework whose taxonomy characterizes abductive tasks. We instantiate this framework on 27 datasets spanning ten domains and evaluate eight LLMs from four model families evaluated on single-choice selection tasks. Beyond harmonizing heterogeneous tasks, AbductionBench separates final-answer evaluation from analysis of the observable reasoning traces and interaction histories produced alongside those answers. We analyze written traces as observable model outputs rather than faithful representations of internal computation, characterizing evidence use, hypothesis comparison, uncertainty, contradiction handling, and answer commitment. Jev matches or exceeds small LLMs when selecting among explanations over compact evidence but falls substantially behind on longer cases, suggesting that candidate discrimination and extended evidence integration place different demands on a system. In interactive tasks, the proportion of actions judged relevant declines at later steps, and a relevant first question is associated with a correct final answer; in the passive task, models rarely revise their initial hypothesis as new evidence arrives. Together, these findings highlight the value of evaluating abductive reasoning through both final answers and observable evidence use, hypothesis revision, and information gathering.
  </details>

- **[MACaQ: Fast, Model Agnostic Calibrated Quantization for Low-Precision Weights and Activations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10649)**  `arXiv:2611.10649`  `cs.LG`  
  _Lorenz K. Muller, Philippe Bich, Chiara Boretti, Hyun-Min Chang, Jiawei Zhuang, Lukas Cavigelli_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-activation quantization can reduce the memory and compute cost of neural-network inference, but recent methods often require expensive optimization and architecture-specific propagation of basis transforms across layers. We introduce MACaQ, an MXFP4 W4A4 post-training quantization method that treats every linear layer independently. MACaQ applies a per-channel scale and a block-Hadamard transform online to each layer's activations, while absorbing the corresponding inverse transforms into its weights. Retaining these transforms online avoids cross-layer dependencies and enables a simple streaming implementation of the quantization process, in which only one layer must reside in memory at a time. To improve over round-to-nearest quantization, we introduce Flip-Refinement, a Hessian-aware coordinate-descent procedure that greedily moves individual FP4 codes to adjacent representable values. Despite executing the activation transforms at inference time, MACaQ adds only 2.3% end-to-end overhead over pure MXFP4 on Qwen3-32B in vLLM. MACaQ reaches lower perplexity than DuQuant++ and MR-GPTQ, and already matches their best perplexity on Llama-3-8B in 11 times less quantization time. We further apply MACaQ without architecture-specific modifications to an image diffusion model, illustrating the generality of its layer-local design and show quantization of a 122B parameter model in 64 minutes, using only 32.5GB GPU RAM.
  </details>

- **[Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation: Evaluating Hierarchical Policies from Non-Hierarchical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10543)**  `arXiv:2611.10543`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yunfu Deng, Josiah P. Hanna_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Off-policy evaluation (OPE) estimates the expected return of a target policy from previously collected data without additional environment interaction. While OPE methods for flat Markovian policies are well studied, little work has addressed evaluating hierarchical policies in which a high-level policy selects temporally extended options that generate primitive actions until a termination condition is met. The primary existing approach applies per-decision importance sampling at the option level, but requires option-annotated trajectories and exhibits variance that still grows with the horizon. For non-hierarchical policies, fitted Q-evaluation (FQE) typically achieves lower error than importance sampling by leveraging the classic Bellman equation for policy evaluation; however, a naive extension of FQE to hierarchical policies results in a biased policy value estimate. In this paper, we introduce Intra-Option Fitted Q-Evaluation (IO-FQE), which estimates a value function in the augmented state-option space; this approach enables OPE of hierarchical policies even when we lack annotations of what option was run in the data. We develop two continuous-action instantiations of IO-FQE and show on long-horizon hierarchical continuous control tasks (AntMaze navigation and OGBench Puzzle manipulation) that IO-FQE eliminates the bias incurred by a naive application of FQE to hierarchical policies and substantially lowers mean squared-error compared to option-level importance sampling.
  </details>

- **[Finite Time Analysis of Risk-Sensitive RL via Noisy Power Iteration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10393)**  `arXiv:2611.10393`  `cs.LG`  
  _Waqar Mirza, Yashaswini Murthy, Laixi Shi, Eric Mazumdar, Adam Wierman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Estimating the principal eigenpair of a positive operator from stochastic evaluations gives rise to a noisy form of normalized power iteration: each step applies a sampled operator estimate and then renormalizes. We establish finite-time stochastic-approximation guarantees for this procedure, working in Hilbert's projective metric, which is well-suited to the multiplicative geometry of Perron--Frobenius eigenproblems. Our main application is risk-sensitive average-cost reinforcement learning (RL) with exponential utility. In this setting the Bellman equations are multiplicative, and value functions are characterized by nonlinear eigenvalue problems rather than additive fixed points. We cast both policy evaluation and control in this framework, obtaining risk-sensitive TD- and Q-learning algorithms that learn from a single Markovian trajectory. Under explicit positivity, mixing, and linear function approximation assumptions, we prove finite-time bounds on the recovered eigenvector and eigenvalue with $\tilde{O}(\epsilon^{-2})$ sample complexity up to problem-dependent constants. The results cover both tabular and linear function approximation regimes, and through the duality between exponential utility and KL-robust control yield finite-time guarantees for KL-robust average-cost RL as well.
  </details>

- **[A Clinical Evaluation of Conformal Prediction for Chest Radiograph Report Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10288)**  `arXiv:2611.10288`  `cs.LG`  
  _Nikolaos Kontogeorgis, Pascal Krezer, Klaus-Rudolf Kladny, Arne Estler, Christian F. Baumgartner, Lisa Koch, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have improved automated radiology report generation, they remain prone to hallucinations, necessitating clinically meaningful risk-control methods. Conformal prediction (CP) provides statistical guarantees, yet existing CP approaches rely on uncalibrated automated extractors and fail to account for the clinical relevance of errors. We introduce clinical admissibility criteria (CLARITY), a framework for evaluating radiology reports that explicitly distinguishes clinically consequential errors from minor deviations. To eliminate reliance on automatic judges, we collected radiologist annotations of MLLM-generated reports using CLARITY. By benchmarking CP strategies and automatic evaluation metrics against these expert annotations, we demonstrate that commonly used admissibility proxies systematically fail to detect clinically relevant errors. Our findings reveal that automated admissibility judgments are largely decoupled from true clinical admissibility.
  </details>

- **[FedLoVA: Value-Only Aggregation for Federated LoRA Fine-Tuning of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10248)**  `arXiv:2611.10248`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ensieh Khazaei, Baturalp Buyukates, Dimitrios Hatzinakos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) is a popular parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT) method for fine-tuning of large language models (LLMs), especially in federated learning, due to its strong performance and communication efficiency. In practice, LoRA is applied to the query and value projections of transformer layers without considering the distinct roles of these components. In this work, we analyze the impact of these projections in federated fine-tuning of LLMs using LoRA and observe that the value projection converges significantly faster than the query projection. Motivated by this finding, we propose FedLoVA, a federated fine-tuning framework that trains LoRA adapters on both query and value projections locally while sharing only the value projection with the server. This approach reduces communication overhead while improving convergence efficiency. Experiments on natural language understanding, multilingual sentiment analysis, and generation tasks show that FedLoVA reduces the communication cost by up to $7.8\times$ compared to existing federated LLM fine-tuning baselines while achieving comparable or better performance. The code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[TrialJev: Open-Weight Single Pass Eligibility Scoring for Patient Trial Screening](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10145)**  `arXiv:2611.10145`  `cs.LG`  
  _Omar Ibrahim, Grant Hamblin, Benjamin Haibe-Kains_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical trial screening requires assessing large numbers of patient-trial pairs to prioritize candidates for detailed eligibility review, often under restrictions on sharing patient records. We introduce TrialJev, an open-weight eligibility scorer that uses LoRA-adapted Qwen models and a pointer head to score natural-language answer options in a single forward pass, without autoregressive decoding. Trained on TREC Clinical Trials 2021 and evaluated on 6,975 eligible-or-excluded pairs from 50 unseen TREC 2022 synthetic patients, TrialJev achieves 80.97% (Qwen3.5-27B) and 81.84% (Qwen3.8-27B) accuracy, compared with 79.87% and 79.93% for LLMs fine-tuned on the same data to generate verdicts. In matched batch-1 measurements, verdict generation takes 2.45-2.70 times as long as the one-pass inference of TrialJev at 9B-27B. Under a common development-fitted decision correction, the two 27B TrialJev models reach 80.99% and 81.88% accuracy, compared with 81.28% for the proprietary hosted Jev API; paired confidence intervals include zero. TrialJev runs locally on a single GPU, requiring approximately 178 ms per pair at 27B, without transmitting patient text to an external model API. An A/B-logit ablation shows that avoiding decoding, rather than the pointer head alone, accounts for the measured efficiency advantage. TrialJev is intended for first-pass prioritization of candidate trials, complementing rather than replacing explanatory models and clinical review. Evaluation is limited to synthetic patients and implementation-specific timing, and clinical use has not been validated. Adapters, pointer heads, and model cards are released on Hugging Face.
  </details>

- **[Coordinated Mixed-Precision KV Cache Quantization for Efficient LLM-based Multi-agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10096)**  `arXiv:2611.10096`  `cs.LG`  
  _Weizhong Huang, Jinchao Zhang, Xiawu Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based multi-agent systems solve complex tasks through collaboration, but their growing, simultaneously resident key-value (KV) caches create a memory bottleneck. Applying single-model quantization policies independently ignores differences in agents' precision needs, potentially degrading collective reasoning. We introduce Coordinated Mixed-Precision KV Cache Quantization (CoMixKV), which combines online sensitivity estimation with joint agent-layer bit-width allocation under a shared memory budget. To our knowledge, CoMixKV is the first mixed-precision KV-cache quantization framework to jointly allocate precision across simultaneously resident agent caches under such a budget. The estimator derives sensitivity from a local attention-error bound using recent queries, keys, and values, without calibration data, trial quantization, or additional model passes. A shared controller combines these scores with remaining quantizable capacity and byte-level storage costs to minimize total estimated error. Allocations are refreshed as agents start or finish and new observations become available. Selected widths apply only to newly quantized blocks; recent states retain native precision and previously quantized blocks remain unchanged. Across four benchmarks and three LLM backbones at average 2-bit, 3-bit, and 4-bit settings, CoMixKV achieves strong accuracy against the evaluated quantization baselines. At the average 3-bit setting on Gemma-4-31B-it, it achieves 68.35% average accuracy, exceeding the strongest quantized baseline by 0.59%. On Qwen3-32B, it reduces peak KV-cache memory by 32.08% and achieves a 1.38x inference speedup over FullKV.
  </details>

- **[SecureGARD: Dynamic Ontology-Guided Governance for Secure Tool-Using LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.06682)**  `arXiv:2611.06682`  `cs.LG`  
  _Guoqin Xiong, Shengtao Wen, Xiang Chen, Xinyi Zeng, Zhaoxin Fan, Wei Ye, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents translate model outputs into consequential actions, turning indirect prompt injection into an execution-security problem. Existing defenses constrain tool calls, trajectories, or information flows, but often leave action semantics, state dependencies, and evidence relations implicit, limiting generalization across heterogeneous tools and complicating safe updates. We present SecureGARD, a dynamic ontology-guided governance framework. SecureGARD grounds heterogeneous calls in a shared semantic representation and combines an executable ontology with episode-level task state to derive deterministic ready, waiting, and forbidden decisions. A waiting decision identifies an unmet but recoverable obligation, enabling bounded replanning without expanding the agent's authorization boundary. SecureGARD evolves its ontology offline from verified training executions. An offline LLM proposes instance-independent action-state axioms, which are accepted only if they address ontology errors exposed by training failures, preserve logical consistency and protected entailments, and pass held-out validation before the ontology is frozen. We evaluate SecureGARD on AgentDojo, ASB, and AgentDyn under benign and indirect prompt-injection settings. SecureGARD achieves a 0% attack success rate (ASR) across all three benchmarks while improving task utility under attack compared with existing baselines. These results show that explicit semantic governance can strengthen execution security without indiscriminate blocking.
  </details>

- **[MXCode: Vector Quantization for MX-Native Inference at Scale](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00403)**  `arXiv:2611.00403`  `cs.LG`  
  _Gaurav Kolhe, Cansu Demirkiran, Shubhi Tyagi, Sulaiman Sadiq, Boyao Wang, Richard Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Memory capacity and bandwidth are major bottlenecks in LLM inference, motivating weight compression beyond conventional 4-bit quantization. Vector quantization (VQ) enables approximately 2-bit weight storage but often relies on codebooks incompatible with native FP4 computation. We introduce MXCode (MX-Native Codebook Quantization), a framework bridging ultra-low-bit VQ compression with MicroXcaling (MX)-native FP4 computation. MXCode proposes three strategies: PostScale, which quantizes learned codebooks to MXFP4; PreScale, which extracts MX scales before codebook learning; and PreScale+, which further compresses these scales using VQ. These strategies explore tradeoffs among quantization quality, scale overhead, and codebook granularity. All reconstruct MX-native W4A4 operands without materializing intermediate high-precision weights. We evaluate MXCode on Qwen3 (235B), DeepSeek-R1 (671B), and GLM-5.2 (753B) using the Microsoft MX Emulation Library. MXCode achieves approximately 2 bits per weight with competitive, workload-dependent model quality. On GB300 hardware, experiments with Qwen3 (235B) show approximately 50% lower model-load memory than Native FP4 while maintaining near-Native-FP4 serving throughput. Under memory-constrained prefix reuse, P90 TTFT improves by up to 94.5%. These results demonstrate software-based reconstruction on existing FP4 hardware and inform the development of future MX-native inference systems.
  </details>

- **[Nominate-then-Adjudicate: LLM-Assisted One-Pass and Per-Instance MIP Solver Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29391)**  `arXiv:2610.29391`  `cs.LG`  
  _Yifan Zhao, Qianyang Zhao, Xinyu Guan, Kai Wei, Yuming Deng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Parameter configurations control presolving, cut generation and branching strategies in mixed-integer programming (MIP) solvers, directly affecting solving efficiency. Effective configurations vary across instances, while searching for them online requires additional solver runs. We propose Nominate-then-Adjudicate: an empirical performance model (EPM) shortlists candidates from a fixed portfolio, and one large language model (LLM) call selects using static features, compressed formulation text and memory distilled from offline solver measurements. Text exposes distinctions omitted by the chosen aggregate features; selection requires no online candidate solves or foundation-model weight updates. Across sixteen synthetic families, adjudication improves on the EPM's top-ranked candidate (top-1), raising mean normalized performance score from 0.377 to 0.426 and the win rate against SCIP's default from 84.9% to 90.2%; all ten sequential batches show positive mean-score gains. On 400 Ecole instances, it outperforms the per-instance baseline ISAC+ in mean runtime-based score, with gains concentrated in facility location. Total solver time falls by 69.6% versus default, excluding LLM latency. Ablations support text and memory; a second LLM improves overthis http URLcode is available at [this URL](this https URL)
  </details>
