- **[OraclePhys: A Systematic Framework for LLM Fine-Tuning on Structural Mechanics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17162)**  `arXiv:2608.17162`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mingyu Li, Guorui Song, Jing Lin, Haoqian Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What fine-tuning teaches a model is usually diagnosed after the fact. We make it an experimental variable. OraclePhys has three parts. OraclePhys-Bench is a structural-mechanics benchmark whose finite-element oracle grades every answer and counterfactual edit. OraclePhys-30K holds seven answer forms over byte-identical structure descriptions. A controlled training study compares the forms and verifier roles. Two findings follow. First, within this task family the answer form of the label determines what fine-tuning teaches. Ranking supervision lifts held-out localization of the most-deforming story on taller structures from 0.25 to 0.86, a peak-value label yields at best a partial predictor, and a pass/fail label none. This ranking-versus-peak gap replicates on a second domain, a second model family and a paraphrased surface. Second, the gap between our standard SFT and GRPO runs is mostly a recipe effect. With learning rate and step budget matched, the two reach held-out accuracies within 0.03 of each other. At higher rates, GRPO falls below its start while SFT does not. Both also teach the model to apply edits given as instructions. The trained 8B matches a specialist and exceeds a frontier LLM. Both domains are linear, steady-state systems with synthetic text inputs.
  </details>

- **[A Reproducibility Study of Partial Residual Ablations in Pre-LN Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.14689)**  `arXiv:2608.14689`  `cs.LG`  
  _Pratikkumar Babariya_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Residual connections are a fundamental component of transformer architectures, yet the roles of the attention and feed-forward residual pathways remain poorly understood when considered independently. This paper presents a reproducibility study of partial residual ablations in Pre-LN GPT-style transformers trained at two scales (10M and 124M parameters). I compare four architectural configurations by selectively removing the attention residual connection, the feed-forward residual connection, or both. At 10M scale, a controlled 8-seed deterministic sweep shows a clear asymmetry: removing the attention residual (FFNOnly) reaches the No-Residual collapse regime (3.350 +/- 0.002), whereas removing the feed-forward residual (AttnOnly) remains well separated from it (1.580 +/- 0.003). A subsequent controlled 124M sweep establishes that the direction of this asymmetry persists under matched seeds: across five seeds each, AttnOnly reaches 6.037 +/- 0.355 versus 7.569 +/- 0.001 for FFNOnly, with no overlap between the observed ranges. AttnOnly nevertheless remains substantially degraded relative to FullResidual (4.577 +/- 0.012) and exhibits markedly greater seed sensitivity at 124M. During the investigation, I identified and corrected an experimental measurement confound in runtime gain scaling and retained an intermediate reproduction failure rather than excluding it. I propose cross-position routing through self-attention as a falsifiable hypothesis for the observed asymmetry; no clean causal test of this mechanism has yet been completed. To support reproducibility, I release the source code, experiment configurations, training logs, and experimental results, including intermediate non-reproducing runs.
  </details>

- **[LiteTopK: Exploiting the Curse of Dimensionality for a Fused Indexer-TopK Kernel in Long-Context Sparse Attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11976)**  `arXiv:2607.11976`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziqi Yin, Jianyang Gao, Peiqi Yin, Jiangneng Li, Gao Cong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indexer-TopK, which computes scores and selects the top-$k$ candidates, is a key primitive in long-context sparse attention for large language models, vector retrieval for recommendation systems, and vector databases. However, existing GPU-based Indexer-TopK kernels like DeepSeek Sparse Attention (DSA) typically separate scoring from selection and materialize the full score matrix in high-bandwidth memory. This strategy is inefficient for large score matrices, such as those encountered in long-context sparse attention, due to excessive global-memory traffic, costly synchronization, and prohibitive memory overhead.In this study, motivated by the curse of dimensionality, we observe that sparse attention scores exhibit a score concentration phenomenon, with scores falling within a narrow range. Based on this observation, we propose LiteTopK, a sample-filter-select framework that estimates query-data score ranges from a small subset of the data. It uses these estimates to filter scores as they are computed, materializes only the candidates that pass the filter, and selects from the surviving candidates. In particular, we develop two implementations of LiteTopK. The first uses a conservative threshold estimate, while the second pairs a predicted threshold with a fallback strategy. Building on LiteTopK, we further propose LiteDSA, which reuses overlapping key/value entries across neighboring queries to accelerate attention computation. We integrate LiteTopK into vLLM, SGLang, and Megatron-LM. Our evaluation on eight B200 GPUs demonstrates prefill speedups of up to 1.49$\times$ across GLM-5.2, DeepSeek-V4-Flash, and Hy-4-Preview at a context length of 1M tokens, without compromising model capabilities.
  </details>

- **[Geometric Signatures of Reasoning: A Spectral Perspective on Task Hardness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.01571)**  `arXiv:2607.01571`  `cs.LG`  
  _Aria Masoomi, Mahsa Bazzaz, Adel Javanmard, Vahab Mirrokni_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning enables large language models (LLMs) to solve complex problems by generating intermediate reasoning steps. While much attention has been paid to the length and content of these reasoning chains, far less is known about their internal geometry. We study the geometry of CoT trajectories in the hidden state space of transformer models, formalizing each reasoning chain as a discrete curve in $\mathbb{R}^d$ and characterizing it through spectral, positional, and kinematic geometric functionals. We introduce the effective dimension $d_\rho$ as a measure of trajectory complexity and empirically show that harder tasks exhibit greater effective dimension. Lastly, we explore how kinematic features of the trajectory, mean position, positional dispersion, initial and final hidden states, mean velocity, mean speed, and speed dispersion, can be used to predict solution correctness before generation is complete, and may inform future early-stopping strategies. Experimentally, on 86 mathematical reasoning problems from the MATH500 dataset and three Qwen2.5 model scales (0.5B, 1.5B, and 7B), $d_\rho$ achieves difficulty-prediction AUROC of $0.90$, $0.94$, and $0.96$, respectively. Seven kinematic and positional features predict correctness from only the first $20\%$ of generated tokens, with AUROC between $0.77$ and $0.88$ across scales. These correctness signatures transfer across held-out questions, establishing that the shape of a model's internal reasoning trajectory is a principled window into both task hardness and solution quality.
  </details>

- **[Demystifying Numerical Instability in LLM Inference: Achieving Reproducible Inference for Mission-Critical Tasks with HEAL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.21023)**  `arXiv:2606.21023`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhenting Zhu, Lucas Thai, Shan Yu, Yicheng Liu, Yifan Qiao, Chenxi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As Large Language Models (LLMs) deploy into mission-critical domains (e.g., finance, medicine, and law), output reproducibility has become a strict system requirement. While practitioners use greedy decoding to eliminate algorithmic stochasticity, empirical deployments with 16-bit precisions still exhibit catastrophic output divergence across heterogeneous GPUs. Through SASS-level profiling, we reveal that this inconsistency is fundamentally driven by truncation errors introduced during downcasting at kernel boundaries. However, achieving reproducibility via a global FP32 pipeline incurs prohibitive system penalties: bypassing 16-bit hardware accelerators hurts compute efficiency, while upcasting the KV cache doubles memory overhead. To bridge this gap, we propose Hybrid Error ALleviation (HEAL), a targeted intervention that approximates FP32 precision while resolving hardware constraints through two targeted mechanisms. First, recognizing that floating-point formats underutilize their bit-width for Q, K, V tensors, HEAL applies INT16 quantization that preserves numerical stability without expanding the KV cache footprint. Second, HEAL synthesizes high-precision matrix multiplications via an algebraic error compensation strategy, executing entirely on high-throughput 16-bit Tensor Cores. To evaluate our approach practically, we introduce MCR-Bench, a benchmark targeting reproducibility in mission-critical tasks. HEAL achieves the same level of reproducibility on downstream tasks as the FP32 baseline while reducing the performance overhead by up to 6.0$\times$.
  </details>

- **[CELEUS: Certifiable and Efficient LLM Evaluation via E-Processes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20820)**  `arXiv:2606.20820`  `cs.LG`  
  _Zhijian Zhou, Zesheng Ye, Zhaorun Chen, Bo Li, Feng Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can we trust evaluation scores to capture an LLM's true real-world performance? Certifiable evaluation answers this question by providing a confidence interval (CI) that covers the LLM's true performance with high probability (e.g., 95%). In particular, existing methods sequentially select evaluation samples from a fixed pool and keep updating the CI until certain conditions are satisfied, e.g., the CI width reaches a target precision. However, existing methods are not generally anytime-valid: the claimed coverage (e.g., 95%) may fail when CIs are repeatedly updated and used to decide when to stop, leaving a gap between theoretical rigor and practice. This paper bridges this gap by proposing CELEUS, a Certifiable framework for Efficient LLM evaluation, which leverages E-processes to build anytime-valid CIs. Concretely, we propose a new signal that combines two ingredients: (i) Uncertainty-guided sampling to select informative samples for evaluation, and (ii) Surrogate-assisted approximations for unevaluated samples. We prove that the new signal remains unbiased for the evaluation score conditional on the past, enabling statistically-grounded and anytime-valid e-process CIs. More importantly, the two ingredients reduce estimation variance and help reach the target precision with fewer evaluated samples. We also prove that CIs obtained by CELEUS can shrink at a near-parametric rate up to logarithmic factors and analyze the oracle variance-optimal sampling rule that motivates the empirical uncertainty-guided one. Experiments show that CELEUS reaches the target precision using 54-62% fewer evaluated samples than baselines, while preserving anytime-valid coverage. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Learn from your own latents and not from tokens: A sample-complexity theory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27734)**  `arXiv:2605.27734`  `cs.LG`  
  _Daniel J. Korchinski, Alessandro Favero, Matthieu Wyart_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative models, from diffusion models to large language models, achieve remarkable performance but at a cost in training data orders of magnitude larger than what biological learners require. An alternative paradigm has emerged in which networks are trained to predict their own latent representations of related views or masked regions, as in data2vec and JEPA -- an idea related to predictive-coding accounts of the cortex. Despite strong empirical results, the theoretical understanding of these methods remains limited. Central questions include: by how much does latent prediction actually improve data efficiency? Is there a benefit to stacking such methods into multi-scale hierarchies? We answer both using as data a tractable probabilistic context-free grammar that captures the compositional structure of natural language and images. Such a grammar generates strings of visible tokens by recursively applying production rules along a tree of hidden symbols of depth $L$. For such data, supervised or token-level SSL require a number of samples exponential in $L$ to recover the latent tree; we prove that latent prediction achieves this with a number of samples constant in $L$, up to logarithmic factors. We confirm this bound with (i) a hierarchical clustering algorithm, (ii) an end-to-end neural network whose predictor-clusterer modules predict their own latents at each level via gradient descent, and (iii) the first sample-complexity analysis of data2vec, which we show implicitly performs hierarchical latent prediction. This suggests that explicit stacking such as H-JEPA is largely redundant.
  </details>

- **[From Residuals to Reasons: LLM-Guided Mechanism Inference from Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.22897)**  `arXiv:2605.22897`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohammad R. Rezaei, Rahul G. Krishnan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When software fails, we read the error, form a hypothesis, write a patch, and test it. LLMs now do this for neural networks by editing weights; when a tabular model fails, we usually retune it. Most interpretability methods report which features matter rather than how they combine. Rather than asking an LLM to predict the target over the whole output space, we anchor the prediction with a base model and ask the LLM to debug it. We introduce Multi-Agent Residual In-Context Learning (MARICL), in which LLM agents read high-residual examples, hypothesize the missing structure, and write executable correction terms that are then refined by multi-turn textual gradient optimization. The resulting corrections are readable, executable hypotheses about what the base model misses, rather than validated mechanisms. Across nine benchmarks covering scientific, biomedical, socioeconomic, and synthetic data, MARICL reliably repairs its base model's structured residuals, improving it on every dataset, and on synthetic data absent from pretraining it recovers the hidden structure (two seeds). Against the strongest competing baseline it leads on five of the eight real benchmarks and trails TabPFN on the other three, while adding an inspectable correction that black-box baselines do not provide.Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[FISolver: Data-Driven Discovery of First Integrals via Backward Generation and Guided Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21160)**  `arXiv:2605.21160`  `cs.LG`  
  _Jingfeng Zhong, Zhengxiang Liu, Zhijie Wang, Shuai Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The discovery of first integrals is of profound scientific significance for understanding physical conservation laws in dynamical systems. However, existing symbolic computation systems and Large Language Models (LLMs) struggle with this task due to the scarcity of high-quality training data and the reliance on mathematical intuition for finding solutions. This paper introduces FISolver, an LLM-based solver designed to bridge this gap. First, we propose a novel "Backward Generation" algorithm that systematically constructs large-scale datasets of (differential equations, first integrals) pairs by deriving equations from sampled integrals, effectively overcoming the data scarcity bottleneck. Second, we perform supervised fine-tuning on a compact mathematical model and further enhance its capabilities using reinforcement learning with a Levenshtein Distance-based shaped reward function. Additionally, we introduce data synthesis and blending techniques that enable effective adaptation to specific hard instances with sparse examples. Experiments demonstrate that FISolver, with significantly lower computational cost, substantially outperforms larger mathematical LLMs and commercial solvers like Mathematica on complex benchmarks, establishing a new data-driven paradigm for automated mathematical discovery.
  </details>

- **[Optimal Representation Size: High-Dimensional Analysis of Pretraining and Linear Probing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.20105)**  `arXiv:2605.20105`  `cs.LG`  
  _Valentina Njaradi, Clémentine Dominé, Rachel Swanson, Marco Mondelli, Andrew Saxe_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning to generalise from limited data is a fundamental challenge for both artificial and biological systems. A common strategy is to extract reusable structure from abundant unlabelled data, enabling efficient adaptation to new tasks from limited labelled data. This two-stage paradigm is now standard in modern training pipelines, where pretraining is followed by fine-tuning or linear probing. We provide an analytical model of this process: structure extraction is formalized as principal component analysis on unlabelled data, and downstream learning as linear regression on a separate labelled dataset. In the high-dimensional regime, we derive exact expressions for training and generalisation error showcasing their dependence on representation dimensionality, unlabelled and labelled sample sizes, and task alignment. Our results show that pretrained representations strongly influence downstream generalisation, and we characterize the optimal representation size as a function of task parameters: with abundant pretraining data but scarce downstream data, maximally compressed representations are optimal, whereas with limited pretraining data, higher-dimensional representations generalise better. Furthermore, we establish an exact trade-off between pretraining and supervision, quantifying how much unlabelled data is required to replace a single labelled sample. Beyond our idealised model, we observe similar phenomenology in autoencoders and pretrained LLMs. Altogether, we highlight that optimising representation size is critical, giving conditions for when compression during pretraining improves generalisation.
  </details>

- **[Language models lack systematicity in generating and updating hypotheses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.05851)**  `arXiv:2605.05851`  `cs.LG`  
  _Hua-Dong Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly applied to complex problems, from automated code debugging to science itself. Such problems require generating plausible hypotheses from sparse, noisy, and ambiguous data and updating them as more information arrives. Yet how closely LLMs approach optimal inductive reasoning remains unclear. We study this question in the classic number game, in which a learner infers a concept from a few ambiguous positive integers: $\{2, 16, 4, 8\}$, for example, is explained both by ``powers of 2'' and by ``numbers up to 20.'' We probe the concepts LLMs hypothesize from the observed examples and use their behavioral patterns to understand how they generate and update hypotheses. We measure the LLM posterior over hypotheses in three tasks (prediction, evaluation, and generation) and compare LLM behavior with a Bayesian model and with humans. LLMs favor narrower hypotheses than humans and show a robust evaluation--generation gap: in evaluation they select hypotheses more consistent with the observed examples, whereas in generation they propose narrower hypotheses that are more often rules. They also update poorly: as examples accumulate, their accuracy declines while their confidence grows. Thinking reduces, and can even reverse, the gap; it also broadens the hypotheses LLMs favor, raising accuracy while lowering confidence. Whereas prior studies have shown that LLMs are good hypothesis proposers, our results and further comparisons with the Bayesian model show that LLM inductive reasoning lacks coherence across the three tasks, suggesting that LLMs do not maintain a stable representation shared across tasks that make the same demand. Our results highlight a limitation of LLMs in inductive inference, particularly in settings such as science, where hypotheses must go beyond the data.
  </details>

- **[Full-Gradient Successor Feature Learning for Multi-Task Transfer in Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.00686)**  `arXiv:2604.00686`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ritish Shrirao, Aditya Priyadarshi, Raghuram Bharadwaj Diddigi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Successor Features (SFs) provide a principled framework for multi-task transfer in Reinforcement Learning (RL) by decoupling environment dynamics from reward functions. By learning representations of expected future feature occupancies, SFs enable knowledge acquired in one task to be transferred to other tasks with different reward functions. However, conventional SF learning relies on semi-gradient Temporal Difference (TD) updates, which, when combined with nonlinear function approximation, generally lack convergence guarantees and can exhibit instability. This motivates exploring alternative learning objectives that offer stronger theoretical properties. Inspired by Full-Gradient DQN, we propose Full-Gradient Successor Feature Representations Q-Learning (FG-SFRQL), which extends full-gradient optimization to successor feature learning by minimizing the squared SF Bellman residual and differentiating through both the prediction and the bootstrapped target. We establish almost-sure convergence of FG-SFRQL under randomized task sampling, characterize the objective it minimizes, and show that averaging the bootstrapped target over $N$ next-state transitions reduces double-sampling bias in proportion to $1/N$. Through experiments in both discrete- and continuous-state domains, we demonstrate that full-gradient learning achieves lower Bellman residuals and improved transfer performance compared to semi-gradient baselines, with FG-SFRQL attaining the highest final return across all five evaluated domains.
  </details>

- **[AnomaMind: Coarse-to-Fine Agentic Diagnosis via Hybrid Inference for Time Series Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.13807)**  `arXiv:2602.13807`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xiaoyu Tao, Yuchong Wu, Mingyue Cheng, Ze Guo, Min Wu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series anomaly detection (TSAD) supports the analysis of web service key performance indicators (KPIs) by identifying anomalous intervals. Recent large language model (LLM)-based agentic approaches have achieved promising results through reasoning and tool use. Despite their effectiveness, precise anomaly localization remains challenging, as diverse anomaly patterns demand targeted numerical evidence and iterative refinement to accurately determine anomaly boundaries. In this work, we propose AnomaMind, a coarse-to-fine agentic diagnosis framework for TSAD. AnomaMind first identifies suspicious regions from time-series visualizations, then adaptively acquires targeted numerical evidence through multi-turn tool interactions, and finally refines anomaly decisions through self-reflection. This process is supported by a tool engine that integrates knowledge memory and numerical analysis. To coordinate this diagnostic process, we design a hybrid inference strategy that employs frozen general-purpose LLMs for initial localization, tool orchestration, and evaluation, alongside a specialized LLM detector for evidence-grounded interval prediction. We optimize only the specialized detector through supervised fine-tuning (SFT) on diagnostic trajectories and reinforcement learning (RL) with verifiable detection rewards. Experiments on four benchmarks spanning web traffic, service KPIs, and system-monitoring signals, under both in-domain and multi-domain training settings, demonstrate competitive performance and support the effectiveness of coarse-to-fine adaptive diagnosis through targeted evidence acquisition and self-reflectivethis http URLcode is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[TriSpec: Ternary Speculative Decoding via Lightweight Proxy Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.23180)**  `arXiv:2601.23180`  `cs.LG`  
  _Haoyun Jiang, Junqi He, Feng Hong, Xinlong Yang, Jianwei Zhang, Zhengyang Zhuge, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inference efficiency in Large Language Models (LLMs) is fundamentally limited by their serial, autoregressive generation, especially as reasoning becomes a key capability and response sequences grow longer. Speculative decoding (SD) offers a powerful solution, providing significant speed-ups through its lightweight drafting and parallel verification mechanism. While existing work has nearly saturated improvements in draft effectiveness and efficiency, this paper advances SD from a new yet critical perspective: the verification cost. We propose TriSpec, a novel ternary SD framework that, at its core, introduces a lightweight proxy to significantly reduce computational cost by approving easily verifiable draft sequences and engaging the full target model only when encountering uncertain tokens. TriSpec can be integrated with state-of-the-art SD methods like EAGLE-3 to further reduce verification costs, achieving greater acceleration. Extensive experiments on the Qwen3 and DeepSeek-R1-Distill-Qwen/LLaMA families show that TriSpec achieves up to 35% speedup over standard SD, with up to 50% fewer target model invocations while maintaining comparable accuracy.
  </details>

- **[JURIST: Training Small Data-Reweighted Reasoning Judges for Test-Time Scaling in Code Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22230)**  `arXiv:2601.22230`  `cs.LG`  
  _Peijia Qin, Ruiyi Zhang, Qi Cao, Pengtao Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Best-of-N selection for code generation is only as good as the judge that picks among the candidates, and the judges accurate enough for this role are costly frontier LLMs. A small judge can be trained on abundant earlier data labeled by unit tests, but it is deployed on newer problems: the earlier problems are easier and teach it little, their tasks differ, and their candidates come from weaker policies, while data close to deployment are scarce. We present JURIST, a 1.7B reasoning judge that uses a small meta set close to deployment to learn how much each earlier training sample should count. In our experiments, we find that training on the meta set alone, or adding it to the training data, does not help, while using it to weight the earlier data through a bi-level objective adds 3.9 Hard points over adding it to the training data. On LiveCodeBench, JURIST reaches 84.7% Overall and matches Claude Sonnet 4.6 on Hard problems (73.8%) at 463x lower cost; on BigCodeBench, it surpasses all test-time scaling baselines (35.9%). Abundant earlier data, guided by a small deployment-aligned meta set, can thus train a compact judge that selects accurately at low cost.
  </details>

- **[Spectral Ghost in Representation Learning: A Unified View from Component Analysis to Self-Supervised Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.20154)**  `arXiv:2601.20154`  `cs.LG`  
  _Bo Dai, Na Li, Dale Schuurmans_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-supervised learning (SSL) extracts representations from massive unlabeled data that transfer to a variety of downstream tasks. Its empirical success has produced a dizzying array of objectives and procedures, yet a clear understanding of what representation is desired, and why these seemingly different methods work, is still missing. In this paper, we develop a unified spectral framework for representation learning. We first show that a representation is sufficient for arbitrary downstream prediction with a linear head if it spans the spectral decomposition of the pointwise mutual information, which can be extracted purely from unlabeled data. This view organizes representation learning along two axes, the parametrization of the spectral representation (linear, energy-based, denoising, nonlinear, latent-variable, and multi-modal) and the learning objective for fitting it, under which a broad range of existing methods, including SimCLR, MoCo, BYOL, Barlow Twins, VICReg, DINO, MAE, JEPA, BERT, GPT, and CLIP, as well as classic PCA, CCA, Laplacian eigenmaps, LLE, and SNE, are recovered as particular combinations. The framework also clarifies optimization behaviors: the batch-size requirement is determined by whether the objective is unbiased, a consistent surrogate, or biased, rather than by contrastive vs. non-contrastive losses. Finally, the framework inspires new scalable algorithms and extends spectral representations to causal inference and reinforcement learning.
  </details>

- **[Mosaic: Unlocking Over 30$\times$ Context Length for Diffusion LLMs Inference via Global Memory Planning and Dynamic Peak Taming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06562)**  `arXiv:2601.06562`  `cs.LG`  
  _Liang Zheng, Bowen Shi, Yitao Hu, Jiawei Zhang, Ruofan Li, Guotao Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion-based large language models (dLLMs) have emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive models, leveraging simultaneous denoising to enable global planning and iterative refinement. These properties make dLLMs attractive for long-context generation. However, deploying dLLMs faces a prohibitive memory barrier, as existing inference systems are inefficient for the diffusion paradigm. We observe that current inference systems are misaligned with dLLMs. Unlike autoregressive models, whose memory footprint is dominated by the KV-Cache, dLLMs are bottlenecked by transient activations rematerialized per step. Moreover, generic memory reuse mechanisms lack the global visibility to handle dynamic memory peaks of dLLMs, which alternate between logits and feed-forward networks. To address these challenges, we present Mosaic, a memory-efficient inference system that shifts dLLM execution from local, static management to a global, dynamic paradigm. Mosaic integrates (i) a mask-only logits kernel eliminating redundant activation materialization, (ii) a lazy chunking optimizer using online heuristics to tame dynamic memory peaks, and (iii) a global memory manager leveraging virtual addressing to mitigate memory fragmentation. Evaluations show that Mosaic reduces the memory peak-to-average ratio by 2.71$\times$ on average and increases the maximum inference sequence length on identical hardware by 15.30-32.34$\times$. Crucially, Mosaic is training-free and preserves exact model outputs, while reducing end-to-end latency by 2.5%-55.4%. Our code is publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[WirelessMathBench-XL: An Auditable Benchmark for Wireless Mathematical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.23219)**  `arXiv:2509.23219`  `cs.LG`  
  _Xin Li, Mengbing Liu, Yiyang Zhu, Wenhe Zhang, Li Wei, Jiancheng An, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmarks built from arXiv papers can overlap the arXiv text used in LLM pretraining, yet technical-domain releases rarely ship the per-item evidence needed to check this. We introduce a reverse-probe n-gram audit that indexes the benchmark side and streams the corpus past it, so memory scales with the benchmark rather than the corpus and flags on the distributed problems can be inspected and recomputed. We instantiate it in WirelessMathBench-XL, a 4,027-problem benchmark for wireless mathematical reasoning built from 836 arXiv papers across 20 subfields, with multiple-choice, progressive fill-in, and full-equation-completion formats. Streaming 12.9 B RedPajama-arXiv 13-grams yields a strict zero-hit view $\mathcal{S}_0$ covering 95.7% of the problems, and shows that the Dolma and ProofPile-2 arXiv slices repackage the same RedPajama dump. Filtering to $\mathcal{S}_0$ moves every evaluated model by less than 1 pp. With 30/800 flagged test items, this comparison bounds detected-overlap inflation at 0.31-0.51 pp for frontier models rather than testing for contamination, and it does not cover paraphrase, target-answer, alternate-version, post-training, or closed-corpus exposure. Frontier models reach 86.5-91.3%, and GRPO on the released training split lifts a 7B model 25.1 pp above a size-matched general-math control. We release source-paper identifiers, per-problem audit and threshold metadata, license-aware data files, audit and bootstrap scripts, Croissant metadata, and a Datasheet.
  </details>

- **[Staying in the Sweet Spot: Boost Reasoning Evolution via Responsive Rollout Difficulty Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.06923)**  `arXiv:2509.06923`  `cs.LG`  
  _Ziheng Li, Zexu Sun, Jinman Zhao, Erxue Min, Yongcheng Zeng, Hui Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR) has advanced the reasoning capabilities of large language models (LLMs). However, existing RLVR methods often suffer from exploration inefficiency due to mismatches between problem difficulty and model capability: overly difficult problems hinder reasoning path discovery, while overly simple problems offer little learning signal. To address this, we first formalize the effect of problem difficulty by quantifying the relationship between loss descent magnitude and rollout accuracy. Building on this analysis, we propose SEELE, a supervision-aided RLVR framework that dynamically adjusts problem difficulty to lie within the high-performance region. SEELE augments each training sample by appending a hint (part of a full solution) for difficulty reduction. Unlike previous hint-based approaches, SEELE deliberately computes the hint length for each individual problem to achieve an optimal difficulty. The optimal hint length is determined via multi-round rollout sampling, where an item response theory model fits accuracy-hint pairs from previous rounds to predict the next-round hint. This instance-level, real-time difficulty adjustment aligns problem difficulty with the evolving model capability, thereby improving exploration efficiency. Experiments show that SEELE outperforms Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) and Supervised Fine-tuning (SFT) by +10.0 and +8.4 points, respectively, and exceeds the best prior supervision-aided approach by +3.8 points on average across six math reasoning benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[Statistical Complexity of Soft Bellman Residual Minimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2508.18741)**  `arXiv:2508.18741`  `cs.LG`  
  _Enoch H. Kang, Kyoungseok Jang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Offline reinforcement learning and offline inverse reinforcement learning aim to recover near-optimal value functions or reward models from a fixed batch of logged trajectories, yet current practice still struggles to enforce Bellman consistency scalably. Bellman residual minimization (BRM) has emerged as an attractive remedy. While globally convergent gradient-based soft (i.e., entropy-regularized) BRM methods for neural networks have recently been established, their statistical complexity under stochastic gradient descent remains largely unknown. In this paper, we address this theoretical gap for soft BRM. Through a Lyapunov-based analysis under a uniform PL condition in the auxiliary variable, we establish an $\mathcal{O}(1 / n)$ primal argument stability bound up to optimization error and an $\mathcal{O}(1 / n)$ statistical complexity for the soft BRM objective under the stated sampling conditions.
  </details>

- **[TRAM: Test-Time Risk Adaptation with Mixture of Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2408.08812)**  `arXiv:2408.08812`  `cs.LG`  
  _Mohamad Chehade, Amrit Singh Bedi, Souradip Chakraborty, Amy Zhang, Hao Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Deployed reinforcement learning agents often face safety requirements that are specified only after training: new hazard maps, revised risk thresholds, or behavioral alignment constraints. We study zero-update deployment-time adaptation in which a fixed library of risk-neutral source policies must be reused under a newly specified reward-risk tradeoff. We propose TRAM (Test-time Risk Adaptation with Mixture of Agents), a source-scored composition rule that evaluates each source under the target reward and an occupancy-based deployment risk, then selects actions using risk-adjusted source scores. Unlike training-time risk-sensitive methods tied to a fixed surrogate such as return variance, TRAM supports spatial barrier exposure, divergence to a reference behavior, and local volatility risks specified at test time. We make the surrogate nature of the method explicit: TRAM is not claimed to solve the full occupancy-control problem of the stitched policy, but it admits a measurable source-hull mismatch term connecting source-scored risk to realized risk. Experiments in gridworlds, MuJoCo Reacher, Safety-Gymnasium, and an inference-time LLM alignment setting show that TRAM improves deployment risk while preserving reward and requires no parameter updates at test time.
  </details>

- **[High-Speed Vision Improves Zero-Shot Semantic Understanding of Human Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.00496)**  `arXiv:2605.00496`  `cs.CV` `cs.RO`  
  _Yongpeng Cao, Yuji Yamakawa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding human actions from visual observations is essential for human-robot interaction, particularly when semantic interpretation of unfamiliar or hard-to-annotate actions is required. For rapid or less common activities, collecting sufficient labeled data for supervised learning is challenging, making zero-shot approaches a practical alternative for semantic understanding without task-specific training. While recent advances in large-scale pre-trained models enable such zero-shot reasoning, the impact of temporal resolution, especially for rapid and fine-grained motions, remains underexplored.In this study, we investigate temporal-resolution conditions for zero-shot semantic understanding of high-speed human actions. Using kendo as a representative case of rapid and subtle motion patterns, we propose a training-free pipeline that combines a pre-trained video-language model for semantic representation with large language model-based reasoning for pairwise action comparison. Our primary experiment evaluates full-observation raw videos from 10 to 120 Hz in 10-Hz increments. Nearest class prototype (NCP) accuracy is highest at 120 Hz, and the mean over 70-120 Hz is higher than that over 10-60 Hz. The corresponding mean prototype margin changes from -5.74 to +2.52 similarity-score points between these two halves and is also highest at 120 Hz. Secondary experiments show that tracking overlays reduce mean full-observation performance but improve mean partial-observation performance. These findings provide preliminary evidence that retaining high-temporal-resolution visual information can benefit training-free semantic understanding of rapid human actions.
  </details>

- **[Counting Events, Not Seeing Them: Diagnosing and Repairing the Readout Failure in Video LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09949)**  `arXiv:2611.09949`  `cs.CV` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Dongwu Qian_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Video LLMs fail conspicuously at counting how many times an object crosses the screen, a failure attributed to missing object permanence. Using construction-grounded synthetic testbeds with independently validated labels, we show this attribution is incomplete: the count signal is present but unread - linear probes decode per-window presence at 93% while global count decoding collapses to a duration confound. Concurrent work reports the same representation-output dissociation for repetition counting; we show that in exit-and-reappear event counting it is repairable: two readout-directed repairs (episode-localization supervision and a discrete event-symbol side channel) raise same-object counting from 46.7% to 65.0% (oracle 66.7%) on a chance-33% benchmark across 7B-30B systems. Transfer to an independent factory costs nothing, and an audit of the measurement apparatus exposes four data-integrity failures that had obscured the boundary. A real-video audit (REALCOUNT, n=18, blind-audited) shows the boundary persists - best 55.6% at chance 33.3% - and symbol transfer fails zero-shot. A 1-epoch in-domain adaptation bench is negative (six levers at 28-50%), but a 3-epoch schedule shows this is budget-limited for the symbol channel: with a 3-epoch schedule and sequence-disjoint oracle timelines the reader reaches 94.4% on REALCOUNT, and with the deployed detector's timelines a 2-epoch run reaches 72.2%. The residual gap is detector evidence and the reader's match to detector noise, not reader capacity: a frozen-feature boundary probe finds the signal weak (AUC 0.62 over a 0.51 permutation control) and non-transferring across clips (0.77 within-clip vs. 0.62 across windows).
  </details>

- **[Grounding Driving VLA via Inverse Kinematics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.21061)**  `arXiv:2605.21061`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Junsung Park, Hyunjung Shim_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Counterfactual obstacle insertion exposes blind planning in representative Driving VLAs: their trajectories remain largely unchanged despite a vehicle appearing directly ahead. An empirically calibrated bias-variance analysis identifies how ego-predictable training examples admit a shortcut through ego status and commands. We address this failure with an inverse-kinematics-inspired architecture that anchors trajectory decoding on current and predicted future visual states. First, a next visual state prediction objective that requires the LLM to predict the future visual scene provides dense visual supervision and suppresses shortcut paths. Second, a separate Inverse Kinematics Network (a cross-attention-based conditional diffusion model) that takes only the current and future visual states as input is designed to suppress reliance on ego status and textual shortcuts during trajectory decoding. Our 0.5B model achieves state-of-the-art trajectory planning performance on NAVSIM-v1, NAVSIM-v2, and nuScenes using cameras alone, without LiDAR or a BEV encoder. Against the matched OpenDriveVLA* baseline, it improves NAVSIM PDMS and EPDMS by 19.0 and 20.4 points, respectively. Controlled ablations and intervention analyses support improved use of visual features, with future-state prediction sharpening hazard-specific responses.
  </details>

- **[LINE: LLM-based Iterative Neuron Explanations for Vision Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08039)**  `arXiv:2604.08039`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Vladimir Zaigrajew, Michał Piechota, Gaspar Sekula, Paweł Gelar, Przemysław Biecek_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Interpreting the concepts encoded by individual neurons in deep neural networks is a crucial step towards understanding their complex decision-making processes and ensuring AI safety. Despite recent progress in neuron labeling, existing methods often limit the search space to predefined concept vocabularies or produce overly specific descriptions that fail to capture higher-order, global concepts. We introduce LINE, a novel, training-free iterative approach tailored for open-vocabulary concept labeling in vision models. Operating in a strictly gray-box setting, LINE leverages a large language model and a text-to-image generator to iteratively propose and refine concepts in a closed loop, guided by activation history. We demonstrate that LINE achieves state-of-the-art performance across multiple model architectures, yielding AUC improvements of up to 0.15 on ImageNet and 0.05 on Places365, while discovering, on average, 25% of new concepts missed by predefined vocabularies. Beyond identifying the top concept, LINE provides a complete generation history, enabling polysemanticity evaluation and producing supporting visual explanations that rival gradient-dependent activation maximization methods. The LINE source code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[HumanSightBench: A Benchmark for Human Visual Perception in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12305)**  `arXiv:2611.12305`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Zixin Su, Yangguang Lin, Zegang Peng, Yuntong Sun, Xuanyi Wang, Jiahao Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have demonstrated strong performance on knowledge-intensive and reasoning-based vision-language benchmark datasets, but still struggle to solve the visual questions that people can solve easily. Evaluating the differences between MLLMs and human-level visual perception is challenging due to end-to-end evaluation which combines various skills, or because we are uncertain if individual items are valid. Specifically, observed errors may reflect visual perception, language priors, background knowledge, question ambiguity, and underspecified answers. Our guideline for HumanSightBench is that we construct items that are human-easy by construction and model-hard by evaluation. HumanSightBench contains 2,876 visual questions with short answers across 7 top-level perceptual categories and 21 subcategories. We only include items that pass five inclusion gates: question clarity, answer uniqueness, visual grounding, human solvability, and text-only shortcut filtering. According to the protocol, errors are defined as incorrect answers on human-validated visual tasks with the evaluated interface. The inclusion gates are intended to reduce confounds from ambiguity and external knowledge. The best pass@3 score is 65.6% across eight evaluated MLLMs. No evaluated model exceeds 27.5% on Counting. These results identify capability gaps that are hidden by overall scores and can help future training experiments and held-out evaluation.
  </details>

- **[ClinFusion: A Vision-Centric Multimodal LLM System for Holistic Medical Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.24743)**  `arXiv:2607.24743`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Hangjie Yuan, Yichen Qian, Zhiwei Tang, Xianzhe Xu, Sicheng Yang, Lirong Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) hold immense potential to revolutionize clinical practice, yet deploying them in the medical domain is fundamentally a vision-centric challenge: models must absorb knowledge from heterogeneous 2D and 3D medical images, and evaluation protocols must align with radiologists' clinical practice and provide an accurate, fine-grained and factualness-driven assessment. In this paper, we introduce ClinFusion, a vision-centric MLLM designed for holistic medical understanding that systematically addresses these limitations. We propose a compositional and cascaded vision encoder architecture featuring a Cascade Spatial-Aware Locality Fusion operator that unifies diverse 2D and native 3D medical image understanding within a fused encoder. We further introduce a vision-grounded evaluation framework, including MedIF-Bench for instruction-following assessment and a region-of-interest-grounded method for clinically aligned and factualness-driven report generation evaluation. We show that ClinFusion sets a new state-of-the-art across a comprehensive suite of 2D and 3D multimodal medical benchmarks---spanning visual question answering, report generation, and instruction following---as well as textual medical tasks, outperforming leading open-source medical MLLMs (\textit{e.g.}, Hulu-Med, Lingshu) on 20 out of 24 benchmarks and demonstrating multimodal capabilities better than powerful proprietary models such as GPT-5.2 and Gemini-3-Flash on 13 out of 16 benchmarks, and can be further augmented with agentic tool use for retrieval-augmented and tool-assisted clinical workflows. A blinded evaluation by board-certified radiologists confirms that ClinFusion produces the highest-ranked reports, and validates our RoI-grounded metric as achieving the strongest correlation with expert judgment among all automatic evaluation metrics examined.
  </details>

- **[ChartHal: A Fine-grained Framework Evaluating Hallucination of Large Vision Language Models in Chart Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.17481)**  `arXiv:2509.17481`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Xingqi Wang, Yiming Cui, Xin Yao, Shijin Wang, Guoping Hu, Xiaoyu Qin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Vision-Language Models (LVLMs) have recently demonstrated remarkable progress, yet hallucination remains a critical barrier, particularly in chart understanding, which requires sophisticated perceptual and cognitive abilities as well as rigorous factual accuracy. While prior work has investigated hallucinations and chart comprehension independently, their intersection remains largely unexplored. To address this gap, we present ChartHal, a benchmark that features a fine-grained taxonomy of hallucination scenarios in chart understanding, along with a human-validated dataset of 1,062 samples. Our evaluation shows that state-of-the-art LVLMs suffer from severe hallucinations on ChartHal, including proprietary models such as GPT-5 and o4-mini, which achieve only 34.46% and 22.79% accuracy, respectively. Further analysis reveals that questions involving information absent from or contradictory to charts are especially likely to trigger hallucinations, underscoring the urgent need for more robust mitigation strategies. Code and data are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[How to "train" a surgeon? Benchmarking generalist agents in surgical scene understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11437)**  `arXiv:2611.11437`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Gengyuan Zhang, Xiao Han, Shijie Zhou, Abdullah Koukash, Jingpei Wu, Jindong Gu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating general-purpose multimodal large language models (MLLMs) for surgical scene understanding remains limited due to scarce surgical data and fragmented evaluation protocols. We address these challenges through a two-stage evaluation framework for surgical scene understanding. In Stage I, we introduce SurgKnowBench, a diagnostic benchmark that harmonizes public surgical datasets across procedures and tasks and evaluates proprietary and open-source MLLMs to map the boundary of pretrained surgical competence. SurgKnowBench reveals that current models retain partial visual priors but remain weak on relational and procedural understanding and workflow assessment. Building on this failure frontier and motivated by the scaffolded nature of surgical training, we introduce SurgAgentGym, an agentic environment where general-purpose MLLM agents can use bounded knowledge search over SurgWiki and general-purpose visual grounding for surgical tasks. SurgAgentGym evaluates whether generalist agents can improve surgical understanding at inference time through tool-mediated evidence use, without additional surgical training. Our results show that agentic inference narrows the gap mainly for perception-heavy tasks, while fine-grained localization and expert-style assessment remain persistent bottlenecks. This opens a path toward future studies of agentic surgical understanding.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Synthesis over Brute-Force Search: LLM-Guided Transformation Design for Efficient Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10940)**  `arXiv:2611.10940`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Usha Shrestha, Radu Timofte, Dmitry Ignatov_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data transformation pipelines shape the training distribution in computer vision, but designing them is a computationally expensive problem. We investigate whether large language models can enable efficient discovery by synthesizing executable transformation programs outside predefined augmentation spaces. We compare nine strategies under a unified training protocol, including standard augmentation policies, brute-force search, direct code generation with two LLMs, and performance-guided generation using LLMs fine-tuned on accuracy-ordered transformation pairs. When the best candidates are retrained for 20 epochs, LLM-generated transformation programs lead on every dataset, reaching 0.8877 accuracy on CIFAR-10, 0.5825 on CIFAR-100, and 0.9588 on SVHN, surpassing brute-force search over 6,000 candidates. The best transform on CIFAR-10 and SVHN comes from a fine-tuned generator using 270 candidates, while the best on CIFAR-100 comes from an off-the-shelf generator using only 10. This needs 22x fewer candidate evaluations in the generation step and 600x fewer for off-the-shelf generations, relative to brute-force search. Beyond transformation discovery, we analyze the reliability of the reduced-budget evaluation commonly used for augmentation search. Correlating one-epoch selection scores with 20-epoch outcomes reveals strong, weak, and absent predictive relationships depending on the dataset and architecture, with correlations ranging from 0.756 to near zero. A positive control indicates that range restriction alone is unlikely to explain the low correlations. Our results indicate that semantic synthesis with code LLMs provides an efficient alternative to search-based transformation discovery and highlight the importance of validating evaluation fidelity when optimizing data transformations.
  </details>

- **[Tiny Evidence Region Localization with Selective Contrastive On-Policy Self-Distillation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10734)**  `arXiv:2611.10734`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yujuan Pang, Wenhao Xu, Yinglei Teng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models have made progress in general visual tasks. However, limitations remain in fine-grained visual tasks requiring evidence from minute image regions. Existing methods primarily train models using evidence-related regions while overlooking the influence of extensive background regions, resulting in a limited ability to resist interference from irrelevant regions. We propose selective positive-negative on-policy self-distillation (SPN-OPSD), which considers both evidence and background regions. Supervision from both views improves the model's ability to resist interference from irrelevant regions. Furthermore, selective token-level distillation provides more informative supervision, improving the model's ability to attend to evidence regions in Fine-Grained Visual Tasks. On Qwen3.5-2B, SPN-OPSD improves accuracy on V* Bench, ZoomBench, and MME-RealWorld-Lite from 76.44%, 45.92%, and 57.43% to 84.82%, 54.56%, and 59.56%, respectively. Similar gains are also observed on Qwen3.5-0.8B.
  </details>

- **[EndoSCOP-V: A Multi-Turn Video Understanding Evaluation Framework for Multimodal Models in Endoscopy Reporting and Clinical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10626)**  `arXiv:2611.10626`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Abhishek Tyagi, Tanmay Jain, Vilas Biradar, Krithi Krishna Koduri, Misba Hul Bashir Masoodi, Swetha Rapolu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Endoscopic diagnosis unfolds over native video, where a single positive finding during traversal of the gastrointestinal tract sets off a multi-minute chain of reasoning over location, morphology, severity and intervention. Existing endoscopy multimodal benchmarks test only static images or single-disease videos, which miss the temporal and clinical reasoning that real-world reporting demands. We introduce EndoSCOP-V, a video-grounded multi-turn evaluation framework for structured endoscopy reporting and clinical reasoning. It contains 1,307 real-world endoscopy and colonoscopy videos (63.2 h; 97% 1080p) averaging about 174 s. Its 20,274 question-answer turns are instantiated deterministically from clinician-filled structured endoscopy-reporting EHRs, so no per-case annotation is synthetic or LLM-rephrased. They come from 1,033 clinician-reviewed templates, about 18 per disease, that span a clinically aligned 9-task x 30-sub-task reporting taxonomy over 54 World Endoscopy Organization (WEO) MST 3.0 diseases. 22% of procedures carry co-existing pathologies, up to six per case. A Markovian context-pruned oracle-forcing protocol evaluates the dialogue turn by turn, injecting the gold answer before each subsequent turn so that per-turn reasoning is isolated from cascading dialogue errors. A panel of 15 board-certified endoscopists sets a 78% strict-accuracy baseline on 1,500 questions. Across 14 open-weight LMMs, the best 27B-class model reaches 59.8% strict accuracy, falling to 53.4% once format-trivial disease-rejection items are excluded. On a between-subjects spot-check, the best of 3 frontier proprietary models trails the clinician panel by 23 pp on strict NDR accuracy. Sub-task analysis reveals consistent gaps in quantitative measurement, multi-variable grading and intervention reasoning on long-horizon video. Data: Hugging Face (EndoSCOP-V). Code: GitHub (abhi25t/EndoSCOP-V).
  </details>

- **[Reason First, Retrieve What Matters: Hypothesis-Guided Keyframe Selection for Long Video Understanding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10609)**  `arXiv:2611.10609`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Yisong Hu, Junbo Wang, Yuke Li, Jiangbin Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long video understanding requires models to identify and reason over sparse, temporally distributed evidence within limited frame budgets. Existing keyframe selection methods typically retrieve query-relevant frames from a densely sampled candidate set before inference. However, global image-text matching incurs unnecessary computation when a small set of uniformly sampled frames already provides sufficient evidence. Query relevance alone may also overlook evidence that distinguishes competing answers, while one-shot selection offers no opportunity to correct earlier selection errors. We propose HypoKFS, a training-free, plug-and-play framework that couples keyframe selection with iterative inference by multimodal large language models (MLLMs). HypoKFS first reasons over sparsely sampled frames to construct an initial hypothesis space. The resulting answer distribution determines whether additional frame selection is needed. When needed, the retained hypotheses guide the selection of discriminative frames, followed by renewed inference and hypothesis refinement. This iterative process directs additional visual computation toward unresolved hypotheses while avoiding unnecessary global frame matching. Experiments on Video-MME, LongVideoBench, and MLVU demonstrate consistent improvements across multiple MLLM backbones. HypoKFS improves accuracy by up to 6.7 percentage points and achieves 72.9% accuracy on Video-MME at only 1.3% of the reference frame-hypothesis matching cost.
  </details>

- **[MotionGraphicsBench: Evaluating Motion Graphics Generation with Programmatic Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10592)**  `arXiv:2611.10592`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaju Ma, Ashvini Kumar Jindal, Chuhua Wang, Sumithra Bhakthavatsalam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can generate motion graphics from static images and natural language requests, but it remains difficult to evaluate how well models can follow requests that combine spatial, appearance, and temporal requirements. We introduce MotionGraphicsBench, an expert-curated benchmark of 100 instances for animating an input static SVG with a motion edit prompt. By supplying all required visual elements, our editing task reflects a designer-like workflow and isolates motion generation from graphic generation. Derived from real-world Lottie motion designs, our benchmark contains diverse combinations of spatio-temporal animation behaviors. Each benchmark instance pairs its model input with a deterministic, human-readable verification program that accepts different valid implementations and reports which prompt requirements pass or fail, rather than comparing against a reference animation or using a VLM judge. We construct these programs with a multi-agent pipeline using human-labeled animations and synthesized test cases. On a balanced test set of 180 animations, our programs achieve 93.3% raw agreement with human judgments, compared with 83.3% for a GPT-6 Astra VLM baseline, and outperform the baseline in both approval and rejection recall. Across 47 configurations from 32 LLMs and 12 providers, pass@1 ranges from 6.0% to 67.7%. Across 15 models evaluated at their highest available reasoning effort with 32 attempts per instance, additional attempts improve every model, but pass@32 still ranges from 38.0% to 95.0%. Our analysis of verification programs reveals that generated animations often fail to match reference poses, follow requested motion trajectories, or coordinate related events. We release our benchmark atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Mental Imagery That Matters: Curing Latent Collapse in Multimodal Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10423)**  `arXiv:2611.10423`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Mi Luo, Rohan Dugad, Zihui Xue, Alex Dimakis, Kristen Grauman_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mental simulation, the ability to reason by constructing and updating internal visual states, is central to flexible human cognition, but remains elusive in current multimodal large language models. Latent multimodal reasoning offers a promising route toward this capability by interleaving text with continuous visual latent blocks, allowing models to maintain internal visual workspaces without decoding them into pixels. In this paper, we ask whether these latent visual states actually matter. Through a direct perturbation analysis across representative latent-reasoning MLLMs, we find that they often do not: dropping, replacing, or shuffling generated visual latents has only minor effects on the model's own final-answer probability, while equivalent perturbations to the linguistic reasoning trace are substantially more damaging. We call this failure mode Mental Simulation Collapse (MSC): the model appears to perform interleaved visual reasoning, but routes most answer-relevant computation through text. We further diagnose that MSC follows naturally from existing training objectives: visual alignment can make generated latents resemble helper-image embeddings, but it does not require the decoder to rely on them; subsequent text-only supervision further leaves the semantic role of the latent pathway unidentifiable. To address this, we introduce AnchorSim (Anchored Mental Simulation), a contrastive distillation framework that trains models to make their generated visual latents consequential. Across multiple visual reasoning benchmarks, AnchorSim improves performance, while diagnostic re-evaluation shows that visual perturbations now meaningfully affect predictions. Our results expose a fundamental failure mode of latent multimodal reasoning and demonstrate that mental simulation must be anchored to model behavior in order to matter.
  </details>

- **[FOCUS: Benchmarking Retinal Model Generalization from Foundation Vision Encoders to Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.33158)**  `arXiv:2609.33158`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _David Restrepo, Chenwei Wu, Luis Filipe Nakayama, Miguel L. Martins, Stergios Christodoulidis, Maria Vakalopoulou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Progress in AI-based retinal image analysis has accelerated with the adoption of foundation models, yet evaluating their real-world reliability remains challenging. Performance reported on a single dataset does not capture how models behave under dataset shift, across clinical definitions, or for different patient subgroups. This limitation is particularly critical in medical imaging, where robustness, calibration, and fairness are essential for safe deployment. We introduce FOCUS (Foundation Ophthalmic Cross-Dataset Understanding under Shift), a cross-dataset benchmark evaluating retinal fundus models across vision-only encoder models (VM), vision-language dual-encoder models (VLM), and multimodal large language models (MLLM). FOCUS harmonizes binary diabetic retinopathy, referable diabetic retinopathy, and glaucomatous optic neuropathy tasks across ten public datasets spanning diverse geographies, acquisition conditions, and label protocols. The benchmark evaluates models through a unified analysis layer that measures ranking performance, calibration, subgroup disparities, and image-quality robustness. We present a large-scale evaluation covering 532 base configurations and 228 MLLM configurations adapted via LoRA fine-tuning. Results show no model family consistently dominates across tasks and datasets: general VM encoders achieve the strongest average ranking performance, medical MLLMs are competitive but variable, and dual-encoder VLMs benefit substantially from lightweight adaptation. Fine-tuning improves in-domain performance but exhibits heterogeneous transfer to external datasets, particularly in calibration. FOCUS provides a practical framework and public benchmark to assess generalization, reliability, and robustness beyond single-dataset leaderboards.
  </details>

- **[Spatial-IQ: Deconstructing Spatial Intelligence via Hierarchical Capability Tests](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22864)**  `arXiv:2607.22864`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Patrick Rim, Tom Long, Ekta Prashnani, Ruth Rosenholtz, Ben Boudaoud, Peter Xenopoulos, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) excel at visual interpretation but fail on spatial reasoning tasks that humans solve reliably. Existing benchmarks evaluate these models as black boxes, limiting their ability to identify the underlying causes of lower performance: when a model fails a spatial reasoning task, it remains difficult to ascertain whether the hurdle is perceptual, such as recognizing object boundaries, or cognitive, such as reasoning about occlusion to infer hidden geometry. We introduce Spatial-IQ, a hierarchical diagnostic framework that decomposes object counting in stacked 3D structures into 9 perceptual and cognitive sub-tasks organized by the developmental stages of human spatial cognition, with mental rotation as an additional target probe. Using NVIDIA Isaac Sim, we procedurally generated a diverse dataset of roughly 80,000 stacked 3D structures with per-task ground truth. We evaluate models across three output formats (free-response text, multiple-choice images, and image editing) alongside a human baseline. The Spatial-IQ framework shows that top-performing models often succeed at the target task (object counting) without succeeding on the lower-level sub-tasks intended to support it, and that models differ in how much of these hierarchical chains they preserve, often revealing shortcut behavior that raw target-task accuracy alone would obscure. Finally, we demonstrate that training models with chain-of-thought (CoT) supervision over our hierarchical sub-tasks, combined with reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards, significantly improves both spatial consistency across sub-tasks and target-task accuracy, supporting the value of the proposed decomposition as both a diagnostic tool and a training signal.
  </details>

- **[Beyond RGB: Benchmarking and Enhancing MLLMs for Hyperspectral Image Understanding via Training-Free Reasoning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08884)**  `arXiv:2604.08884`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Xinyu Zhang, Zurong Mai, Qingmei Li, Xiaoya Fan, Zjin Liao, Haoyuan Liang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have achieved strong performance on RGB image understanding, yet their ability to use spectral evidence beyond the visible range remains largely unexplored. Hyperspectral imagery (HSI) provides dense spectral measurements that reveal material and environmental cues unavailable in RGB, but current MLLMs cannot directly ingest high-dimensional HSI. To study this gap, we introduce HM-Bench, an evidence-grounded benchmark for hyperspectral image understanding with MLLMs. HM-Bench contains 19,337 question--answer pairs from 2,178 hyperspectral samples across 13 task categories, covering general perception, spectral reasoning, and spatial--spectral reasoning. To make HSI accessible to the native image--text interfaces of existing MLLMs, we further propose VSR$^{2}$, a training-free Visual--Spectral--Report Reasoning framework. VSR$^{2}$ represents each HSI sample with three aligned views: an RGB image for visual semantics and spatial context, a PCA-based image for dominant spectral variation, and a structured report for quantitative spectral--spatial evidence. Under a controlled RGB-only versus VSR$^{2}$ evaluation protocol, experiments on 17 representative MLLMs show that HSI information improves average accuracy from 38.90% to 40.95%, with gains varying substantially across models and task categories. These results indicate that HSI information (beyond RGB) is useful for current MLLMs, while robust hyperspectral reasoning remains an open challenge.
  </details>

- **[SANEval: Open-Vocabulary Compositional Benchmarks with Failure-mode Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.00249)**  `arXiv:2602.00249`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Rishav Pramanik, Ian E. Nielsen, Jeff Smith, Saurav Pandit, Ravi P. Ramachandran, Zhaozheng Yin_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Text-to-image (T2I) models struggle with compositional prompts requiring multiple objects, bound attributes, accurate counts, or spatial relations. Existing benchmarks face limits from fixed detector vocabularies, aggregated scores, and varying human-validation protocols. We introduce SANEval (Spatial, Attribute, and Numeracy Evaluation), an open-vocabulary compositional benchmark with two key contributions: (i) a public dataset of ~5,250 prompts paired with images from six state-of-the-art T2I models, split into algorithmically generated Simple and fully human-written Hard partitions across five compositional tasks, and (ii) a modular evaluation pipeline combining an LLM prompt parser, an open-vocabulary detector with LLM synonym expansion, and scorers for attribute binding, spatial relations, and numeracy. Each scorer emits numerical scores and diagnostic feedback for missing, extra, or mis-bound elements. Validated against human ratings (n = 500), SANEval achieves positive Pearson correlations across all categories (overall r = 0.222), outperforming CompBench++ (-0.016) and GenEval (-0.030). A manual audit of 24,012 detections shows 96.58% precision, with ablation confirming that synonym expansion is critical to pipeline performance. Dataset: [this https URL](this https URL). Pipeline: [this https URL](this https URL).
  </details>

- **[Towards Continual Expansion of Data Coverage: Automatic Text-guided Edge-case Synthesis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.26158)**  `arXiv:2509.26158`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Kyeongryeol Go_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The performance of deep neural networks is strongly influenced by the quality of their training data. However, mitigating dataset bias by manually curating challenging edge cases remains a major bottleneck. To address this, we propose an automated pipeline for text-guided edge-case synthesis. Our approach employs a Large Language Model, fine-tuned via preference learning, to rephrase image captions into diverse textual prompts that steer a Text-to-Image model toward generating difficult visual scenarios. Evaluated on the FishEye8K object detection benchmark, our method achieves superior robustness, surpassing both naive augmentation and manually engineered prompts. This work establishes a scalable framework that shifts data curation from manual effort to automated, targeted synthesis, offering a promising direction for developing more reliable and continuously improving AI systems. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Speculative Decoding Reimagined for Multimodal Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.14260)**  `arXiv:2505.14260`  `cs.CV` `cs.AI`  
  _Luxi Lin, Zhihang Lin, Zhanpeng Zeng, Rongrong Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) deliver strong performance across vision-language tasks but suffer from high autoregressive decoding latency. Although speculative decoding can accelerate Large Language Models (LLMs) without changing their output distribution, directly applying existing approaches to MLLMs yields limited speedups because text and visual tokens exhibit distinct characteristics. We present Multimodal Speculative Decoding (MSD), a framework specifically designed for efficient MLLM inference. MSD is motivated by two key observations: text and visual tokens should be handled separately during drafting, and an effective draft model requires both strong language modeling and visual perception capabilities. Accordingly, MSD decouples text and visual token processing in the draft model and applies token-specific strategies to improve drafting efficiency. We further introduce a two-stage training strategy. The draft model is first trained on text-only instruction-tuning data to strengthen its language modeling ability and is then progressively exposed to multimodal data to develop visual perception while preserving its language competence. Extensive experiments across multimodal benchmarks demonstrate that MSD achieves up to $2.29\times$ inference speedup for LLaVA-1.5-7B and $2.46\times$ for LLaVA-1.5-13B without sacrificing generation quality. These results establish MSD as an effective and general approach for accelerating multimodal autoregressive generation. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Beyond Modality Preference: Controlling Multimodal Arbitration under Modality Conflict in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12883)**  `arXiv:2611.12883`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhaoying Pan, Shenyu Lu, Jun Hui Wong, Junyi Chai, Xiaoqian Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) integrate information across modalities, but become unreliable when those modalities disagree. Under conflicting inputs, MLLMs may favor modalities that are not the most reliable for the task, thus motivating inference-time control of modality arbitration. We identify a small set of attention heads, termed arbitration heads, that causally influence whether predictions follow visual or textual evidence. Building on this, we introduce a lightweight, training-free intervention that biases these heads toward a designated trusted modality at inference time. The identified heads are stable across calibration set sizes and generalize across settings, and steering them enables effective bidirectional control of modality reliance across diverse benchmarks. Our findings identify an effective control mechanism to align MLLM predictions with task-specified modality priorities. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EditJudge-Bench: Auditing VLM Image-Edit Judges with Synthetic Ground Truth](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12524)**  `arXiv:2611.12524`  `cs.CV`  
  _Jens Parslov, Marco Schouten, Dim P. Papadopoulos_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language models (VLMs) are increasingly used as automated judges for image-editing benchmarks, yet their reliability is poorly understood. Auditing these judges is difficult because real edited images lack controlled ground truth and entangle the requested changes with model artefacts, unintended side effects, and ambiguous degrees of success. In this paper, we introduce EditJudge-Bench, a diagnostic benchmark of 1,382 synthetic before/after image pairs rendered in Blender, with edits parameterised across nine categories derived from real user requests and exact ground truth from known 3D scene parameters. Because every edit is generated by a known intervention on a controlled scene, EditJudge-Bench evaluates VLM judges as binary verifiers of whether an instruction was correctly executed, isolating judge perception from editor quality. Across 25 judge configurations, we find substantial reliability gaps: (a) the widely adopted VIEScore template reaches only 69.0% AUROC with GPT-4o; (b) fine-tuned judges score 51.7-66.3%; spatial edits such as rotation and shape remain challenging; (c) prompt-formatting choices like image-text ordering shift performance by up to 14.1 points. Despite using only synthetic data, judge rankings on EditJudge-Bench correlate strongly with two real-image human-preference benchmarks ($\rho$=0.95 and $\rho$=0.71), indicating that controlled audits surface failure modes relevant to real evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Weight-Space Programming for Medical Imaging: Do We Still Need Training Data?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12066)**  `arXiv:2611.12066`  `cs.CV`  
  _Maximilian Lindholz, Richard Ruppel, Annette Milnik, Sebastian Halskov, Semil Eminovic, Charlie A. Hamm, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Can a large language model (LLM) write the parameters of a medical image classifier from medical knowledge alone, without any training data? Such classifiers normally require labelled images, which are costly and often unavailable. We investigate this question through weight-space programming (WSP), in which an LLM writes or adapts the weights and biases of a neural network directly, without optimisation on training data. Claude Opus 5.5 and GPT-6 Astra each generate the parameters of a small LeNet-style CNN in a single reply, without tool use and without access to any image. We compare these networks against zero-shot MedSigLIP and against the same architecture trained on labels using the Adam optimiser, across 7 classification tasks in four modalities: chest X-ray, breast ultrasound and abdominal CT (all from MedMNIST), and pelvic MRI (SynthUterus). The LLM-written networks perform above chance on all six MedMNIST tasks, with a mean test AUC of 0.719 for Opus and 0.711 for Astra. Both models outperform MedSigLIP on 5 of 6 tasks while using more than 10,000 times fewer parameters. However, the same network trained with Adam on only 256 labelled images achieves a higher AUC on every MedMNIST task (mean AUC 0.828). On SynthUterus, Opus attains an AUC of 0.764, whereas Astra falls below chance (AUC 0.136) owing to a systematic error. These results show that LLMs can write functional medical classifiers, but that they do not yet replace training data. We release the protocol, logs and all generated weights atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[GeoPilot: Diagnosis-Guided Interventions and Geometric Evaluation for Aerial Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11751)**  `arXiv:2611.11751`  `cs.CV`  
  _Pengyu Yin, Jie Pan, Junxiang Tan, Mengqi Fu, Kun Cheng, Yifan Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a mesh reconstructed from aerial remote-sensing imagery disagrees with reference geometry, deciding what to change requires a diagnosis of the reconstruction process. We present GeoPilot, a workflow that turns diagnostics and prior observations into executable program or camera-configuration interventions. A large language model proposes the change, numerical tools reconstruct the mesh, and an external evaluator measures its geometry under a stated coordinate and reference protocol. The main case tests a shared-intrinsics hypothesis proposed before the comparison. On 224 Metashape-raster development images, shared intrinsics improved all six native-coordinate measures and reduced mean surface-to-reference distance by 7.04%. Reference-assisted alignment fitted and evaluated on separate within-scene checkerboard samples reversed the mean-distance ranking, limiting that gain to native-frame delivery.
