A separate matched comparison on publisher-undistorted images favored fixed published cameras on all six measures, reducing mean distance by 12.11%; the cameras retain upstream image and laser-scanning adjustment information. With one reconstruction per configuration, these comparisons provide conditional evidence for camera handling. Three end-to-end runs each delivered a mesh and six-metric evaluation from all 327 images of a second development scene.
  </details>

- **[SurfScaff3D: Bridging Geometric and Semantic Representations for LLM-based 3D Object Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11678)**  `arXiv:2611.11678`  `cs.CV`  
  _Anay Loya, Rishi Gupta, Arnav Samal, Anirban Chakraborty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Creating high-quality 3D content has traditionally required specialist expertise and experience with complex tools. The growing ecosystem of AI-assisted creativity demands accessible and intuitive 3D generation systems. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer promising directions, yet extending their generative capabilities to 3D remains fundamentally challenging because existing 3D representations are not naturally compatible with language-based reasoning. Current approaches either depend on expensive fine-tuning or impose restrictive structural priors that sacrifice expressiveness and exclude organic shapes. We argue that the mismatch between LLM-native reasoning and conventional 3D parameterizations is a core bottleneck of LLM-driven 3D generation. To address this, we introduce the Hierarchical Patch Scaffold (HPS), a representation designed around semantically meaningful, interpretable part-level abstractions that are naturally understood by both humans and LLMs, enabling expressive, editable, and open-domain 3D generation without imposing restrictive geometric priors or expensive fine-tuning.Building upon HPS, we introduce SurfScaff3D, an interactive framework for text and image-to-3D generation. HPS inherits the flexibility of B-spline patches, while exposing an interface that leverages open-world capabilities of foundation models to reliably generate 3D. Its hierarchical part-level semantic labels enable targeted, natural-language edits at any level of granularity without re-running generation. Further, HPS offers explicit per-patch control over surface detail, enabling more expressive geometry capable of representing arbitrary objects. Extensive experiments against state-of-the-art LLM-based 3D generation methods demonstrate strong geometric fidelity, semantic alignment, and flexible editing across diverse object categories.
  </details>

- **[NightSteps: A Night-Time Dataset and Benchmark for Pedestrian Crossing Intention Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11583)**  `arXiv:2611.11583`  `cs.CV`  
  _Sjoerd Takken, Shaodi You_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous vehicles use pedestrian crossing intention prediction (PCIP) to participate safely in traffic. Existing models are largely trained under favourable daytime conditions with sufficient daylight. Little is known about the robustness of these models under night-time and low-light conditions, due to the lack of datasets containing night-time footage. This paper investigates how a dataset, named NightSteps, can be constructed and evaluated as a baseline benchmark. The dataset is based on nighttime dashcam footage collected from six different cities around the world, capturing a wide variety of road conditions. The dataset consists of dashcam-recorded point-of-view footage, raw data of pedestrians' locations with corresponding 'track ids' and ground-truth labels containing accurate information about pedestrian crossing behaviour, resulting in a diverse dataset. Gemini, ChatGPT, and human assessment are used to demonstrate the usability of the dataset by predicting crossings in clips from the dataset and comparing the results with the initial ground-truth labels. These results show that human input achieves an accuracy of 88.17% and an F1-score of 80.21%. Predictions from Gemini and ChatGPT perform lower, with accuracies of 60% and 79.13% respectively, and F1-scores of 42.50% and 63.64%. The evaluation demonstrates that the NightSteps dataset can be used for future model evaluation to test the performance of PCIP, with the aim of improving road safety at night.
  </details>

- **[Reaching Is Not Finding: Map-Anchored Visual Prompting for the Last Mile of Aerial Vision-Language Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11510)**  `arXiv:2611.11510`  `cs.CV`  
  _Ningbo Li, Kebin Liu, Jingcan Chen, Weichen Zhang, Rong Ding, Yunhao Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aerial vision-language navigation (VLN) asks a drone to find an object described in natural language. The description usually locates the object relative to named landmarks, such as streets and buildings. We observe that current navigators often bring the drone close to the target and then stop at the wrong place: they reach the target without finding it. This gap is shared by navigators of very different designs, from imitation learners to a fine-tuned 7B vision-language model. A simple test reveals the cause. When we replace the target object in the instruction with another one next to the same landmarks, no navigator goes to it, and most stop where they did before. Navigators learn to reach the landmarks, not to identify the described object. We therefore propose MAP-VP, a plug-and-play module for the last step. After any navigator stops, MAP-VP draws the landmarks named in the instruction onto the drone's current view, using the landmark outlines that the benchmark provides on a map. A fine-tuned 4B vision-language model then locates the target relative to these landmarks. The training data are generated automatically from the benchmark annotations. Neither drawing the landmarks nor fine-tuning helps much alone, but together they are highly effective. Without retraining any navigator, MAP-VP significantly improves all six navigators we test on CityNav. It roughly doubles the success rate of the two strongest trained navigators, raising HETT from 25.9% to 54.6% and FlightGPT from 24.2% to 48.9%, whereas CityNav's official detector-based last step adds less than two points to HETT.
  </details>

- **[Let the Gallery Learn Itself: Training-Free Iterative Side-View Face Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11391)**  `arXiv:2611.11391`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zijun Huang, Suncheng Xiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What if a face recognition system could learn from every encounter without a single gradient update? We present Gallery Self-Evolution (GSE), a training-free framework where the gallery itself adapts at test time. The core mechanism---dedup-FIFO curation---archives novel appearance samples after successful recognition while permanently locking the enrollment template, bounded at 30 embeddings per identity. On CFP-FP and IJB-C benchmarks, GSE closes the side-view gap by +23 points at 45° yaw and boosts TAR@FAR=0.001 by +9.4 points, all at CPU-level cost. No retraining. No GPU. Just a gallery that grows smarter every time it meets you.
  </details>

- **[From Game Generation to Game Playing: Fully Automated Ability Training for Vision-Language-Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11136)**  `arXiv:2611.11136`  `cs.CV`  
  _Changran Liu, Qi Wu, Wenchao Ma, Sharon X. Huang, Haomiao Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Training vision-language-action (VLA) models to follow gameplay instructions across diverse games is limited by the cost of collecting demonstrations. We introduce a framework that automatically generates game environments and expert demonstrations. An LLM-powered agent uses game-building tools to construct executable environments and tasks targeting specified skills. Programmatic experts then use privileged simulator state to generate aligned instructions, rendered observations, and keyboard and mouse actions. We train our game policy, Script2Play, by post-training a 4B vision-language model with behavior cloning on 150.3 hours of synthetic Minecraft and Roblox gameplay, without human gameplay demonstrations or reinforcement learning. Evaluation on multiple public game benchmarks demonstrates that Script2Play follows gameplay instructions effectively and generalizes across game engines, highlighting the effectiveness of targeted synthetic supervision.
  </details>

- **[What You Show Is Not What You Get: Rethinking Demonstration Following in Visual In-Context Segmentation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11122)**  `arXiv:2611.11122`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiao Hu, Ilia Sucholutsky_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual in-context segmentation uses visual demonstrations to guide predictions for a new image. In SegGPT, we identify background color mapping deviation, where non-target regions sharing a background label receive different output colors, and part-to-parent expansion, where predictions extend beyond the requested part into its parent object. These deviations can occur even when the relevant regions remain distinguishable in internal features. Controlled changes to demonstration colors and requested targets support an account in which demonstrations guide role and color assignments over familiar semantic regions, influenced by learned semantic and color preferences. Building on these findings, we introduce Semantic Contrast Prompt Selection (SCPS). Its semantic contrast encoding assigns separate colors to the requested part and the rest of its object while keeping the remaining scene black. SCPS combines existing retrieval scores with quality estimates from labeled development images to select demonstrations and their binary or semantic contrast encodings. Averaged across seven retrieval methods on three benchmarks, SCPS improves target IoU by 0.054 and reduces leakage into the rest of the object by 22.7% relative to the original binary selectors. Our findings offer a new perspective on understanding and improving demonstration following in visual in-context segmentation.
  </details>

- **[Query-Only Prompt-Agnostic Attacks on Visual In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11116)**  `arXiv:2611.11116`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiao Hu, Muhammad Ibrahim, Hao Wen, Pin-Yu Chen, Jianliang Wu, Antonio Bianchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Visual In-Context Learning (VICL) enables promptable segmentation by conditioning RGB predictions on visual demonstrations, making it attractive for auto-labeling, but its robustness under query-only access and unknown deployment prompts remains underexplored. We study query-only, prompt-agnostic targeted attacks where the attacker observes only returned RGB outputs, without access to model internals or deployment prompts. Treating prompt-conditioned RGB rendering as a distinct attack surface, we introduce two appearance-space attacks: Color Hijack (CH) drives foreground colors toward the background to suppress visibility, while Task Hijack (TH) collapses instance-distinguishing colors toward a shared color assignment. To transfer across unseen prompts, we propose a Self-Prompt Surrogate (SPS) constructed from the clean query and its output. In a paired, matched-budget SegGPT comparison, SPS outperforms the Random-Prompt Surrogate (RPS), reducing CH instance foreground retention from 0.918 to 0.155 and TH palette-aware tolerance distance from 0.345 to 0.225. Across four VICL models on COCO and HierText, CH suppresses foregrounds across semantic-, instance-, and text-style outputs, while TH collapses instance colors under held-out deployment prompts. After palette-based decoding, TH also reduces the decoded instance count and mask retention while increasing instance merges. Finally, at 30% corruption, CH-corrupted annotations from a publicly accessible VICL-based auto-labeling pipeline reduce downstream YOLO-seg mAP50 from 0.761 to 0.281.
  </details>

- **[Remembering to Reason: Structured Memory for Scene and Workflow Understanding in Robot-Assisted Surgery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10917)**  `arXiv:2611.10917`  `cs.CV`  
  _Zhiran Zhang, Hu Wang, Yichen Wu, Yutong Xie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Surgical video understanding is an important foundation for intelligent assistance in robot-assisted surgery. Similar surgical views can reflect different actions or procedural stages. Interpreting these views requires access to earlier observations, yet frame-centric prompting leaves this history unavailable. We introduce MemSurg, a framework that remembers surgical observations before reasoning over them. Its structured memory graph stores timestamped entity categories, locations, motion cues, and interaction summaries. Chronological links preserve recent context, while entity-associated links connect recurring observations across the procedure. Task-adaptive retrieval then supplies evidence-grounded prompts for scene and workflow reasoning, supporting both frozen inference and memory-conditioned supervised fine-tuning. Evaluation on robot-assisted prostatectomy and two complementary surgical benchmarks shows a 10.43-percentage-point gain over SurgRAW with GPT-4o. Memory conditioning adds 6.52 points under matched Qwen3-VL-8B fine-tuning, and an inference-time plug-in adds 7.04 points to EndoChat. These results support structured memory as a reusable source of procedural evidence for surgical video understanding. Our code will be publicly released.
  </details>

- **[From Reflection to Co-Evolution: Self-Evolving Agents with Locally Verified Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10382)**  `arXiv:2611.10382`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xinxiang Yin, Xinglang Zhang, Haodong Chen, Zhiqing Cui, Weidong Tang, Yuxuan Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents have made impressive progress in coding and mathematical reasoning, motivating growing interest in learning from long-horizon interaction. Memory supports this process, but plausible lessons can be redundant or reinforce inappropriate actions. Can a memory's source decision provide a practical test for whether to retain it? We localize consequential trajectory steps, extract candidate memories, and replay each source decision with and without its candidate under the same context. This reveals two properties: most candidates barely shift the probed distribution, and both successful and failed source actions can be reinforced or suppressed. These observations motivate Anchor, which tests shift magnitude and source-role alignment before retention. Admitted memories guide later trajectories, enabling experience-driven improvement under a frozen policy. On held-out ALFWorld and WebShop tasks, Anchor improves success rate and mean score by up to 19.9% and 17.2%, respectively, relative to the strongest baseline for each backbone. With Qwen3.5-27B, it retains 41% fewer ALFWorld cards than unfiltered retention and outperforms all four WebShop cost-comparison baselines while using fewer tokens.
  </details>

- **[MaRiO: Multi-agent Collaborative Reasoning via Shared Observations in MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10344)**  `arXiv:2611.10344`  `cs.CV`  
  _Nathaniel Redmond, Fidel Omar Tito Cruz, Devansh Sharma, Shehreen Azad, Sirshapan Mitra, Shruti Vyas, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) show strong visual perception capabilities, but existing evaluations largely focus on single-view or multi-view settings with shared camera parameters, leaving the challenge of multi-agent collaborative scenarios underexplored. In such settings, multiple agents observe a shared environment from independent viewpoints, and reliable decisions require reasoning across these independent observations of the same environment. To study this problem, we introduce MaRiO, a benchmark for evaluating multi-agent collaborative reasoning, and evaluate 23 state-of-the-art MLLMs on it. Our analysis reveals three key findings: (1) tasks governed by static perception are generally easier than tasks that require transformational event sequences; (2) most models struggle to resolve cross-agent correspondences, with errors increasing with scene complexity; and (3) models show limited ability to leverage geometric cues present in the scene. As an initial step toward addressing these challenges, we explore geometry-guided synthetic view augmentation that generates intermediate transition frames between agent observations, providing auxiliary spatial context that improves performance in certain categories. Overall, our results highlight that multi-agent collaborative reasoning remains an open challenge and an important direction for multimodal embodied systems. The code is available on the project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[OCRia-1 Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.00092)**  `arXiv:2611.00092`  `cs.CV`  
  _Aman Rangapur, Antonio Jimeno Yepes, Daniel Bustamante, Nutchapol Dendumrongsup, Sebastián G. Acosta, Pierre-Loïc Doulcet, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce OCRia-1, a 4B vision-language model (VLM) for document parsing that excels at preserving table structure, extracting chart data and retaining text formatting. OCRia-1 handles content that has traditionally been challenging in document parsing: merged cells and spanning headers, tables with thousands of cells, chart data, and meaningful text formatting such as bold, italics, subscripts and superscripts, preserving details needed for business-critical document analysis. At the time of this report, it achieves the highest scores on tables, charts and text formatting among the systems evaluated, including a 35B model and Mistral OCR 4.1. Its mean ParseBench score over the four dimensions we evaluate is 0.87, against 0.75 for the next-best system.We emphasize data quality and reward design in training OCRia-1. Synthetic pages provide exact ground truth for long tables and rich formatting, while real pages are transcribed using a pipeline grounded in the PDF text layer. We attribute OCRia-1's performance primarily to careful data curation that prioritizes quality and diversity over quantity. Dimension-specific rewards evaluate table reconstruction, chart-data extraction and text-formatting fidelity. We train the model on the curated data using supervised fine-tuning (SFT), then refine it through reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR).To our knowledge, OCRia-1 is the fastest published document-parsing VLM in its class, decoding at about 4,700 tokens per second with a single request on one B200 GPU, about nine times faster than the base model on stock vLLM. This is achieved through a block-diffusion speculator, quantization and custom kernels.
  </details>

- **[SketchDirector: Complex Scene Sketching via Agentic Layout Reasoning and Detail Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.41260)**  `arXiv:2610.41260`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yiqi Liang, Xiaodong Cun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing sketch generation methods often rely on image guidance and are largely limited to individual objects or simplified scenes, leaving text-guided complex scene sketching underexplored. We present SketchDirector, an agentic framework that mimics the human sketching workflow for automatic multi-concept scene sketching. Given a scene-level text prompt, SketchDirector first employs a large language model~(LLM) to decompose the scene into multiple draft assets, where each is represented by a set of Bézier curves. Then, an agentic reasoning loop iteratively plans and executes their spatial arrangement, guided by self-evolving memory. Finally, a detail refinement module leverages diffusion priors to enhance sketch quality while preserving geometric structure. To support future research and applications in multi-concept scene sketch generation, we further introduce the MCSketch8K dataset, comprising editable vector assets, compositional layouts, and object-level manipulation annotations. Extensive experiments show that SketchDirector outperforms state-of-the-art baselines in semantic and compositional quality while effectively preserving overall contour organization during refinement.
  </details>

- **[Ask About the Right Moment: Training-Free Object Identity Reasoning for Video-Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31471)**  `arXiv:2610.31471`  `cs.CV`  
  _Di Yang, Xiaochang Li, Muyang Liu, Yufei Wang, Yanhai Xiong_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many questions about a video concern one physical object. Where does the ball end up after the cups are shuffled? Which card is the queen? How often was the mug covered? Open video-language models (Video-LMs) recognise such objects in single frames but do not keep them apart over time. On shuffled shell games, open models from eight families stay near chance, and so do published tool agents. We separate semantic identity binding from temporal identity maintenance. Binding should happen at a moment when the evidence for the object is visible, and maintenance should be left to a tracker. Track, Then Bind implements this principle without training. A promptable video tracker follows every candidate object and draws it with a track number. A frozen vision-language model (VLM) answers one question about the identifying evidence, which binds the target to a track. The answer is then derived from a text description of the tracks. With Qwen3-VL-32B, the agent solves both VET-Bench tasks, where a fine-tuned trajectory model reaches 92% and 90%. On real cup games from the Perception Test, it raises accuracy from 35.5% to 52.3%. Without retuning, the same configuration also solves VET-Bench with Gemma 4 and GPT-5.5. Its track-derived visibility timelines add 8.8-17.9 points on TOC-Bench for open models. GPT-6.1 already answers 92.5% of the real cup games directly but 71.0% through our pipeline, while our timelines still raise its TOC-Bench accuracy from 52.5% to 57.7%. The benefit of external tracking thus depends on the underlying model and the task.
  </details>

- **[EvaOmni: An Evidence-Grounded Benchmark for Audio-Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.28444)**  `arXiv:2610.28444`  `cs.CV`  
  _Xiaoxing Hu, Wenxi Yao, Chaoyuan Shi, Ziqi Ye, Xiaorong Guo, Peixuan Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Omni LLMs can naturally locate key audio and visual evidence and integrate information across modalities for audiovisual reasoning. Existing audio-visual reasoning benchmarks that mainly rely on multiple-choice questions usually score models solely based on answer correctness, making it difficult to assess whether a model truly forms a reasoning chain from evidence to the correct answer. In addition, textual or unimodal shortcuts may allow models to answer correctly without analyzing the necessary audio-visual evidence, further weakening the reliability of evaluation benchmarks. To address these issues, we introduce EvaOmni, an evidence-grounded benchmark containing 392 videos and 1,027 purely human-annotated question-answer pairs, covering 15 capabilities and three levels: retrieval and localization, basic understanding, and complex reasoning. We design a series of rigorous annotation procedures in which experts annotate audio and visual evidence and construct video-based distractors; humans review whether questions meet strict inspection criteria, and finally models filter textual and unimodal shortcuts. Beyond high-quality question-answer pairs, we require models to simultaneously return the answer and supporting evidence intervals in order to evaluate the complete reasoning chain. We propose the Coverage Grounded Score (C.G.) to measure how much annotated evidence a model identifies, and the Bottleneck Grounded Score (B.G.) to penalize poor temporal localization in either modality; both are incorporated into answer correctness. Across 7 open-weight and 17 proprietary Omni LLMs, Gemini3.7-Flash achieved the highest overall accuracy (79.4%), C.G. (70.8%), and B.G. (35.1%). At Level 3, it reached 79.1% accuracy, but its B.G. was only 12.3%. These results show that even the most advanced models still have room for development in grounded audio-visual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[CitySTAR: Structured and Topology-Aware Reasoning for Open-Vocabulary Urban 3D Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.19911)**  `arXiv:2609.19911`  `cs.CV`  
  _Shuai Zhang, Hongye Hou, Qinghe Liu, Zhuoxiao Li, Dongli Wu, Jing Ou, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  3D grounding aims to localize target entities in complex scenes from natural language and plays a fundamental role in embodied perception and spatial reasoning. However, existing approaches mostly rely on feature similarity or direct matching, making it difficult to connect natural-language intent with the implicit semantic and geometric structures hidden in billion-scale urban point clouds. We reformulate city-scale 3D grounding as structured constraint reasoning, where description semantics are organized into computable cross-modal constraints over open-vocabulary 3D entities, attributes, and spatial relations. We present CitySTAR, a training-free framework for reasoning-driven urban 3D grounding. CitySTAR lifts raw billion-scale urban point clouds into a query-ready scene graph of open-vocabulary 3D instances, with CodeLLM-driven tools supplying multimodal evidence for node attributes and 3D spatial relations. It then models target-context topology with paired hypergraphs and performs bidirectional topology verification for structural disambiguation. Finally, a Reflective Cross-modal Grounding module integrates topology consistency and candidate-centered 2D visual evidence to make decisions over a metric-aware 3D context graph. To further support this setting, we introduce CitySTAR-3D, an enhanced benchmark that improves semantic coverage, instance completeness, bounding-box fidelity, and spatial-relation complexity in city-scale 3D grounding. Extensive experiments show that CitySTAR consistently improves open-world urban 3D grounding while maintaining strong interpretability and generalization.
  </details>

- **[Read It Back: Pretrained MLLMs Are Zero-Shot Reward Models for Text-to-Image Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11886)**  `arXiv:2607.11886`  `cs.CV`  
  _Runhui Huang, Qihui Zhang, Zhe Liu, Yu Gao, Jie Wu, Hengshuang Zhao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In this paper, we propose SpectraReward, a training-free reward function that turns pretrained MLLMs into off-the-shelf reward models for image-generation reinforcement learning. Instead of asking the MLLM to judge a generated image or answer decomposed verification questions, SpectraReward measures how well the original prompt can be recovered from the generated image through a single image-conditioned, teacher-forced forward pass. We use the average image-conditioned prompt log-likelihood as the reward, directly reusing the MLLM's pretrained image-text alignment ability without preference labels, reward-model fine-tuning. We further introduce Self-SpectraReward, a special case for unified multimodal models where the policy's own understanding branch serves as the reward model for its generation branch, forming a closed-loop self-improving framework without external reward models or external knowledge. Extensive experiments validate SpectraReward through a broad image-generation RL study covering two diffusion models, three RL algorithms, nine reward MLLM backbones from four MLLM families spanning 4B to 235B parameters, and five out-of-distribution text-to-image benchmarks. Results show that both SpectraReward and Self-SpectraReward significantly and consistently improve generation performance and outperform prior MLLM-derived reward training methods. Further analysis reveals that larger reward MLLMs are not always better, while Self-SpectraReward can match or surpass much larger external reward models, suggesting that reward-policy alignment is a key factor for effective image-generation RL. Project Page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Next Forcing: World Action Modeling with Multi-Chunk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11187)**  `arXiv:2606.11187`  `cs.CV`  
  _Gangwei Xu, Qihang Zhang, Jiaming Zhou, Xing Zhu, Yujun Shen, Xin Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive video generation has emerged as a powerful paradigm for World Action Models (WAMs). However, existing approaches suffer from slow training convergence and limited converged accuracy, particularly at high frame rates, as the training supervision is confined to the current chunk without explicit signals about future dynamics; they also suffer from slow inference due to iterative video denoising. In this paper, we present Next Forcing, a multi-chunk prediction (MCP) framework for world action modeling that enables faster training, higher accuracy, and accelerated inference. Inspired by multi-token prediction in large language models, Next Forcing introduces an MCP training objective that augments the main model with lightweight auxiliary MCP modules to simultaneously denoise video chunks at multiple future temporal horizons (next$^1$, next$^2$, next$^3$ chunks). These MCP modules form a causal chain across prediction depths, where intermediate features fused from multiple layers of the main model are leveraged to predict future dynamics, allowing near-future predictions to inform farther-future ones and providing dense multi-scale temporal supervision back to the main model. During training, the MCP modules significantly accelerate convergence and improve converged accuracy, especially at high frame rates: at 50 fps, Next Forcing achieves a 93.1% relative improvement over LingBot-VA at 5k training steps and 2.3$\times$ faster convergence, and establishes new state-of-the-art results on the RoboTwin benchmark (94.1/93.5% on Clean/Random). At inference, the MCP modules can be retained to predict the next video chunk in parallel with the current one, achieving 2$\times$ inference acceleration. Beyond World Action Models, Next Forcing also improves physical-law adherence on PhyWorld and reduces FVD by over 50\% in action-free general video pretraining.
  </details>

- **[Fundus-R1: Training a Fundus-Reading MLLM on Public Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.08322)**  `arXiv:2604.08322`  `cs.CV`  
  _Yuchuan Deng, Qijie Wei, Kaiheng Qian, Jiazhen Liu, Bangxiang Lan, Qingwei Shen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fundus imaging such as CFP, OCT and SLO is crucial for the early detection of retinal anomalies and diseases. Fundus image understanding, due to its knowledge-intensive nature, poses a challenging vision-language task. An emerging approach to addressing the task is to post-train a generic multimodal large language model (MLLM), either by supervised finetuning (SFT) or by reinforcement learning with verifiable rewards (RLVR), on a considerable amount of in-house samples paired with high-quality clinical reports. However, these valuable samples are not publicly accessible, which not only hinders reproducibility but also practically limits research to few players. In this paper, we make a novel attempt to train a reasoning-enhanced fundus-reading MLLM, termed Fundus-R1, using exclusively public datasets, wherein over 94% of the data are annotated with image-level labels only. Our technical contributions are two-fold. First, we propose a RAG-based method for composing image-specific, knowledge-aware reasoning traces. Such auto-generated traces link visual findings identified by a generic MLLM to the image labels in terms of ophthalmic knowledge. Second, we enhance RLVR with a process reward that encourages self-consistency of the generated reasoning trace in each rollout. Extensive experiments on three fundus-reading benchmarks, i.e., FunBench, Omni-Fundus and GMAI-Fundus, show that Fundus-R1 clearly outperforms multiple baselines, including its generic counterpart (Qwen2.5-VL) and a stronger edition post-trained without using the generated traces. This work paves the way for training powerful fundus-reading MLLMs with publicly available data.
  </details>

- **[A Neuro-Symbolic Framework Combining Inductive and Deductive Reasoning for Autonomous Driving Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.12421)**  `arXiv:2603.12421`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hongyan Wei, Wael AbdAlmageed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing end-to-end autonomous driving models rely heavily on purely data-driven inductive reasoning. This "black-box" nature limits interpretability and makes safety-critical planning challenging in complex, long-tail scenarios. To overcome this bottleneck, we propose a novel neuro-symbolic trajectory planning (NS-Plan) framework, which integrates rigorous deductive reasoning into end-to-end neural networks. Specifically, our framework utilizes a Large Language Model (LLM) to dynamically extract scene rules and employs an Answer Set Programming (ASP) solver for deterministic logical arbitration, generating logically consistent and traceable discrete driving decisions. To bridge the gap between discrete symbols and continuous trajectories, we introduce a decision-conditioned decoding mechanism that transforms high-level logical decisions into learnable embedding vectors, simultaneously constraining the planning query and the physical initial velocity of a differentiable Kinematic Bicycle Model (KBM). By combining KBM-generated baseline trajectories with neural residual corrections, our approach uses the KBM to guide trajectory generation while keeping the decision-making process traceable. On the nuScenes benchmark, our method (NS-Plan) comprehensively outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, reducing the average $L_2$ error by 18.3% (to 0.49 m), the collision rate by 22.2% (to 0.07%), and improving the trajectory prediction consistency (TPC) by 13% (to 0.47 m) compared to MomAD. More importantly, in the complex Turning-nuScenes subset, our method reduces the collision rate by 78.1% (to 0.07%) over the baseline.
  </details>

- **[Kelix Technical Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09843)**  `arXiv:2602.09843`  `cs.CV`  
  _Boyang Ding, Chenglong Chu, Dunju Zang, Han Li, Jiangxia Cao, Kun Gai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive large language models (LLMs) scale well by expressing diverse tasks as sequences of discrete natural-language tokens and training with next-token prediction, which unifies comprehension and generation under self-supervision. Extending this paradigm to multimodal data requires a shared, discrete representation across modalities. However, most vision-language models (VLMs) still rely on a hybrid interface: discrete text tokens paired with continuous Vision Transformer (ViT) features. Because supervision is largely text-driven, these models are often biased toward understanding and cannot fully leverage large-scale self-supervised learning on non-text data. Recent work has explored discrete visual tokenization to enable fully autoregressive multimodal modeling, showing promising progress toward unified understanding and generation. Yet existing discrete vision tokens frequently lose information due to limited code capacity, resulting in noticeably weaker understanding than continuous-feature VLMs. We present Kelix, a fully discrete autoregressive unified model that closes the understanding gap between discrete and continuous visual representations.
  </details>

- **[Exploring the Capabilities of Large Language Model Encoders for Image-Text Retrieval in Chest X-rays](https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.15234)**  `arXiv:2509.15234`  `cs.CV`  
  _Hanbin Ko, Gihun Cho, Inhyeok Baek, Donguk Kim, Joonbeom Koo, Changi Kim, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal learning from paired medical images and clinical text is a central challenge in medical data-driven informatics, where effective cross-modal alignment is critical for scalable analysis and retrieval. In chest radiography, vision-language pretraining is constrained by heterogeneous radiology reports that contain abbreviations, impression-only notes, and institution-specific writing styles. Unlike general-domain settings, naively aggregating large collections of noisy reports can plateau or even degrade multimodal learning when reporting styles differ substantially. We propose a domain-adapted bidirectional large language model text encoder for chest radiograph reports, trained with masked token prediction and supervised contrastive learning on stylistically diverse but clinically equivalent report variants to produce robust, generalizable text embeddings. We then integrate this encoder into a dual-tower contrastive vision-language framework using parameter-efficient adaptation to improve image-text alignment. Across 1.6 million paired studies from public datasets and a de-identified hospital cohort, the proposed models improve bidirectional retrieval accuracy and external generalization, achieving GREEN scores of 0.308 on MIMIC-CXR and 0.618 on Open-I, while reducing the degradation observed when abbreviation-rich, impression-only hospital reports are added to training.
  </details>

- **[MultiTurnRoute: A Platform for Building and Evaluating Multi-Turn LLM Routers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10848)**  `arXiv:2611.10848`  `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Jiaqi Xue, Mengxin Zheng, Heng Huang, Qian Lou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In a multi-turn conversation, the choice of language model at one turn can affect which model should answer the next. The selected model's reply shapes the context for the next query, and its cached prompt prefix can reduce the cost of using that model again. We introduce MultiTurnRoute, a platform for building and evaluating multi-turn LLM routers. A shared interface gives built-in and user-defined routers the current query, dialogue history, and an estimate of each model's reusable prefix length. Our routing algorithm, Chain-of-Route (CoR), uses a learned gate over recent and recurrent dialogue history to predict response quality and estimates cost from each model's reusable prefix. MultiTurnRoute Arena ranks routers on recorded conversations and compares session quality and cost in interactive runs where a simulated user responds to each reply. On a benchmark drawn from WildChat and LMSYS-Chat-1M with per-turn quality annotations for five LLMs, CoR reduces the normalized cost required to match the strongest model's quality by up to 20.3% relative to the strongest routing baseline, while matching or improving peak response quality.
  </details>

- **[Prune, Don't Merge: Expert Compression of Mixture-of-Experts LLMs on Multilingual Production Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12240)**  `arXiv:2611.12240`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Tomas Rasymas, Povilas Kvedaras_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mixture-of-Experts language models are cheap to run per token but expensive to hold in GPU memory, which drives the cost of serving them in production. One-shot expert compression shrinks them after a single calibration pass. Its two current state-of-the-art methods are REAP for expert pruning, which deletes the least salient experts, and REAM for expert merging, which merges them into the most salient ones. Both have been evaluated only on academic benchmarks. We compare the two on five multilingual production tasks of a web-hosting company, from classification to the generation of marketing HTML email templates, with two 30B-parameter models (Qwen3-30B-A3B-Instruct-2507 and the hybrid Mamba-attention Nemotron-3.5-Lightning) at 25% and 50% of experts removed and the same calibration data. Pruning wins: on the same rows, REAP is significantly better in 16 of 20 comparisons and REAM in 2. At 25%, REAP keeps 93.6% of Qwen3-30B's generative quality and REAM 62.5%, although on benchmarks the two are within a point for the same model. The damage concentrates in the output contract rather than the content: 89.8-95.2% of each significant loss on email templates comes from hard-gate failures such as invalid JSON, broken mobile layouts and runaway generation, and most failed non-English domain descriptions copy the prompt's English opening phrase. Cross-perplexity and logit divergence, two diagnostics from the REAP paper, also track production quality, as does the rate of repetition loops; n-gram diversity does not. We close with practical guidance for compressing MoE models for production.
  </details>

- **[ChemInsight: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Evaluating and Diagnosing Chemistry Understanding and Reasoning in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11794)**  `arXiv:2611.11794`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Changyu Hu, Yiming Zou, Xin Tang, Yiru Pan, Zicheng Huang, Deng Zhao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce ChemInsight, a benchmark designed to evaluate large language models (LLMs) on chemistry tasks that require multi-step reasoning and structured chemical understanding. We propose a two-dimensional scoring system (scope and depth) to assess both task coverage and reasoning completeness. ChemInsight consists of 203 challenging chemistry questions organized into 50 bundles, which support the two-dimensional scoring evaluation scheme.We evaluate ten representative LLMs using ChemInsight and observe consistently low performance across models, with scope scores below 30% and depth scores below 11%, substantially lower than human expert performance. We then proceed to ask (1) What factor limits the advancement of current LLMs in chemistry? (2) To what extent are statistically driven language-model training methods effective at modeling chemistry symbolic language such as SMILES? And (3) Can LLM performance in chemistry be improved through intensive training on large, domain-specific corpora?
  </details>

- **[MoTo: Mixture of Tokenizers Towards Fair Multilingual Language Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11027)**  `arXiv:2611.11027`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Gül Sena Altıntaş, Colin Raffel_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models rely on a single tokenizer that is chosen at training time and fixed thereafter. Most tokenizers are trained on English-dominant corpora, making them under-serve morphologically rich and non-Latin-script languages, where they can produce fragmented representations, longer sequence lengths, and reduced information density during training. We propose Mixture of Tokenizers MoTo, a modular tokenization framework that trains dedicated per-language BPE tokenizers and composes them into a unified superset vocabulary, where tokens shared across languages are deduplicated into a common ID space. Each tokenizer maintains its own normalization policy, pre-tokenization rules, and vocabulary budget. Despite allocating significantly fewer entries to English, MoTo retains English performance while delivering consistent gains across 18 out of 21 typologically diverse languages spanning six scripts, with improvements most pronounced on morphologically complex and non-Latin-script languages that are systematically underserved by English-dominant tokenizer design. Our results suggest that modular subword tokenization is a practical and extensible alternative to monolithic tokenization for multilingual parity.
  </details>

- **[SWEET: Mitigating Spurious Feature Reliance in LLM Reasoning via Weight Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10974)**  `arXiv:2611.10974`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhizhang Fu, Lucas Zhao, Michalis Korakakis, Mengru Ding, Lukasz Szpruch, Yue Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning in large language models can be disrupted by simple input perturbations (e.g. adding irrelevant distractors), suggesting reliance on spurious correlations. Identifying and suppressing the spurious features involved in long chain-of-thought reasoning remains challenging. We introduce Spurious-feature WEight EdiTing (SWEET), a training-free method that combines counterfactual interventions with mechanistic interpretability to identify and suppress spurious-features. SWEET extracts token- and sequence-level behavioral directions from activation contrasts between original problems and their counterfactual variants - capturing local and global patterns of spurious reasoning. It then localizes the responsible MLP neurons and attention heads and suppresses their contributions along these directions through weight edits. Using only 20 original-counterfactual pairs for direction extraction, SWEET improves accuracy by an average of 11.8 percentage points on perturbed inputs and 2 points on original inputs across five open-weight reasoning models and five reasoning benchmarks. It also reduces the relative gap between original and perturbed accuracies from 17.6% to 2.5%. Analyses identify two recurring forms of spurious reasoning, i.e. over-integrating irrelevant conditions and overthinking, and provide mechanistic evidence that SWEET improves reasoning robustness by suppressing both behavioral directions.
  </details>

- **[Scaling Laws for Multimodal Data Mixtures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14423)**  `arXiv:2610.14423`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Aditi Khandelwal, Ayush K. Tarun, Yixuan Xu, Imanol Schlag, Golnoosh Farnadi, Siva Reddy, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier AI systems are increasingly natively multimodal, jointly pretrained on multiple modalities, such as text, vision, and audio. However, the scientific understanding of the multimodal data mixtures used to pretrain these systems --- how much of each modality to mix, and at what potential interference cost to the others --- is largely absent or gatekept. In this work, we derive data mixing scaling laws for three modalities: text, vision, and audio. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) increasingly adopt Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures, as MoEs scale efficiently and partially limit cross-modal interference. We therefore use MoEs to conduct 268 experiments ranging from 457M to 8.3B parameters, on up to 150 billion tokens. Our work leads to multiple actionable insights into multimodal data mixing for training MLLMs. First, compute optima are modality-specific: audio modality is model-heavy, favoring scaling parameters over tokens, while text and vision require balanced scaling of model size and data. Second, repeating vision or audio data beyond 2x yields negligible benefits. Finally, cross-modal interactions are highly asymmetric: vision strongly interferes with audio, whereas audio's interference with learning text and vision is negligible. Overall, we provide a principled foundation for understanding the multimodal data mixtures needed to train frontier MLLMs.
  </details>

- **[Universal Byte-Level Encoding: UTF-8/UTF-16 Routing to Reduce Cross-Script Token-Budget Disparities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.01984)**  `arXiv:2610.01984`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Hyunsik Kim, Youngmoon Jung_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Byte-level byte-pair encoding (BBPE) tokenizers are attractive for multilingual large language models because they cover all Unicode text. In UTF-8-based BBPE, however, many scripts start from a higher fallback cost than English: when no learned merges apply, a multibyte character requires multiple byte-derived symbols. We call this worst-case pre-merge cost the encoding floor. A higher floor can increase token counts and per-request cost while shrinking usable context. Changing the whole text encoding can reduce this gap but make already-efficient English spans more expensive in mixed-script text. Thus, we propose Universal Byte-Level Encoding (UBE), a dual-alphabet tokenizer that keeps 1-2-byte UTF-8 characters in UTF-8 and routes 3-4-byte characters through UTF-16. This lowers the encoding floor for 3-byte Basic Multilingual Plane characters, used by many scripts that need more tokens than English, without raising it for any character. UBE changes only the symbols presented to byte-pair encoding, and a Unicode 17 audit confirms exact round trips for every scalar value and every input in three official test suites. UBE also composes with other boundary policies and morphology-based encodings. Across intrinsic evaluations, UBE lowers dispersion in English-normalized token-count ratios (Gini 6-14% lower on FLORES-200 at 16K-64K), reducing cross-lingual token-budget disparity. In multilingual language models, UBE matches BBPE's quality. In the main multilingual settings, UBE reduces token counts most where they are highest and also lowers English counts, yielding more usable context and faster prompt processing in content-matched benchmarks. Byte-level models benefit directly: UBE shortens their inputs by 29-33% in all 39 of the FLORES-200 languages written mostly in 3-byte characters (1.4-1.5 times as much text per context window), lowers byte-level Gini by 28%, and lengthens no input.
  </details>

- **[The Imperfective Paradox Is Not Necessarily in Large Language Models: A Benchmark Failure Before a Model Failure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25005)**  `arXiv:2608.25005`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Kaiqiao Han, Katarzyna Pruś, Mark Steedman, Yizhou Sun_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work uses an NLI benchmark of the imperfective paradox to argue that large language models exhibit a Teleological Bias and Calibration Crisis. We identify three validity concerns in benchmark construction, including aspectual reduction, semantic bias, and relation bias. Under a strict NLI standard, 76% of the Group A instances do not explicitly rule out culmination; in native-speaker annotation, 38% of Group A and 29% of Group C examples permit an alternative interpretation. To better isolate the semantic contrast, we construct Lexically Matched Minimal Pairs. Moreover, by formulating event-semantic NLI as a multi-step reasoning problem, we evaluate intermediate semantic judgments and use oracle-guided settings. We find that incorrect NLI entailment does not fully support teleological bias. Prompting often changes final NLI decisions without reliably improving the underlying semantic judgments, offering a possible explanation for the Calibration Crisis.
  </details>

- **[Architecture-Dependent Causal Transfer of Activation States Across Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16347)**  `arXiv:2608.16347`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Fernando Cardenas Piepereit_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Direct communication between AI systems relies on natural language as an intermediate layer, incurring encoding/decoding overhead, token cost, and latency. We ask whether internal activation states can instead be transferred causally between different large language model (LLM) architectures via a learned projection, evaluated at three levels: representational similarity, cross-model retrieval from projected states, and end-to-end causal transfer via activation injection during generation. Using four architecturally diverse open-weight models (Qwen2-0.5B, Phi-3-mini, Mistral-7B, FLAN-T5-base), we find that representational alignment in trained models exceeds a random-initialization null baseline and is best captured by a rank-based metric (mutual k-nearest-neighbour alignment), more robust to activation-magnitude outliers than centered kernel alignment (CKA) or Procrustes analysis. A learned projection network retrieves the correct target-model representation from a held-out set well above chance for the three causal decoder-only model pairs (45-50% top-1 accuracy vs. 5% chance) but at chance level for the encoder-based FLAN-T5. Injecting projected activations into a target model during generation produces a statistically significant, pre-registered causal effect on retrieval-based output similarity for only one of the three decoder-only pairs (Qwen2-0.5B to Phi-3-mini: 23.3% vs. 0.0% under negative control, p=0.047, FDR-corrected); the two pairs targeting Mistral-7B show no such effect despite comparable representational alignment at the hidden-state level. We interpret these results as evidence for causal transfer of the representational vehicle, not of meaning, and conclude that end-to-end activation-state transfer between LLMs, as currently implemented, is architecture-dependent rather than universal.
  </details>

- **[Learn-To-Learn on Arbitrary Textual Conditioning: A Hypernetwork-Driven Meta-Gated LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.01973)**  `arXiv:2605.01973`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Luo Ji, Qi Qin, Ningyuan Xi, Teng Chen, Qingqing Gu, Hongyan Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional LLMs may suffer from corpus heterogeneity and subtle condition changes. While finetuning can create the catastrophe forgetting issue, application of meta-learning on LLMs is also limited due to its complexity and scalability. In this paper, we activate the meta-signal of $\beta$ within the SwiGLU blocks, resulting in a meta-gating mechanism that adaptively adjusts the nonlinearity of FFN. A hypernetwork is employed which dynamically produces $\beta$ on textual conditions, providing meta-controllability on LLMs. By testing on different condition types such as task, domain, persona, and style, our method outperforms finetuning and meta-learning baselines, and can generalize reasonably on unseen tasks, condition types, or instructions. Our code can be found inthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Failing to Falsify: Evaluating and Mitigating Confirmation Bias in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.02485)**  `arXiv:2604.02485`  `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ayush Rajesh Jhaveri, Anthony GX-Chen, Ilia Sucholutsky, Eunsol Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Confirmation bias, the tendency to seek evidence that supports rather than challenges one's belief, hinders one's reasoning ability. We examine whether large language models (LLMs) exhibit confirmation bias by adapting the rule-discovery study from human psychology: given a sequence of three numbers (a "triple"), an agent engages in an interactive feedback loop where it (1) proposes a new triple, (2) receives feedback on whether it satisfies the hidden rule, and (3) guesses the rule. Across eleven LLMs of multiple families and scales, we find that LLMs exhibit confirmation bias, often proposing triples to confirm their hypothesis rather than trying to falsify it. This leads to a slower and less frequent discovery of the hidden rule. We further explore intervention strategies developed for humans (e.g., encouraging the agent to consider counterexamples). We find that prompting LLMs with such instructions consistently decreases confirmation bias in LLMs, improving rule discovery rates from 42% to 56% on average. Lastly, we mitigate confirmation bias by distilling intervention-induced behavior into LLMs, showing generalization to a new domain (the Blicket test) and beyond rule discovery (mathematical self-correction). Our work shows that confirmation bias is a limitation of LLMs in hypothesis exploration, and that it can be mitigated via injecting interventions designed for humans.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Supervision Targets for Multimodal LLM Unlearning: Less Private Information, Reduced Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09939)**  `arXiv:2611.09939`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Leijiang Gu, Zhen Zeng, Feng Li, Le Zhang, Zenglin Shi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) can memorize and reproduce sensitive information from their training data, posing significant privacy risks. Their ability to encode knowledge across both textual and visual modalities further complicates the selective removal of such private information. Many existing methods guide model unlearning by training the model toward constructed labels or output distributions, which we refer to as supervision targets. However, these methods often suffer from a trade-off between forgetting effectiveness and model utility. We identify supervision target limitations as a key factor in this trade-off and empirically show that targets containing more private information lead to insufficient forgetting, while larger distribution shifts from the original output distribution are associated with greater utility degradation. Motivated by these findings, we propose FFN-Perturbed Self-Distillation (FPD), which starts from the teacher's original output distribution as a zero-shift reference and selectively perturbs FFN activations associated with private information across textual and visual modalities. By precisely reducing private information from the teacher's original output distribution, FPD constructs supervision targets with less private information and reduced distribution shift. Extensive experiments on S-MLLMUn Bench demonstrate that FPD achieves a better forgetting-utility trade-off than existing methods.
  </details>

- **[Simple Test-Time Refinement for Plot-to-Code Generation via Visual-Code Diagnostics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10840)**  `arXiv:2611.10840`  `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Peiming Guo, Jinpeng Huang, Yangbin Sun, Gaoxiong Cao, Ensheng Shi, Yuchi Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Plot-to-code generation has been greatly advanced by recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs). However, existing studies mainly focus on one-pass generation or iterative sampling guided by verifier-based filtering, which does not align with the typical human programming workflow of generate first, then iteratively error-location and fix. This naturally motivates a test-time refinement paradigm for plot-to-code generation. In this work, we present the first study on plot-to-code generation via iterative test-time refinement. We propose a Visual-Code Diagnostics framework that enables refinement through visual feedback from source code. Specifically, we first categorize generation errors into four coarse-grained types, and then train lightweight discriminators to recognize and localize these errors based on the rendered visual outputs and corresponding code. Unlike traditional compiler- or debugger-based verification, our approach performs iterative refinement guided by one diagnostics system, enabling more effective semantic and visual correction during test time. Extensive experiments demonstrate that our method consistently improves generation quality across diverse generators and significantly outperforms strong test-time scaling baselines.
  </details>

- **[SocialGym: Evaluating LLM Social Intelligence through Verifiable Multi-Agent Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09128)**  `arXiv:2608.09128`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Keyu He, Xuhui Zhou, Maarten Sap_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents are increasingly deployed in multi-agent social settings where they must cooperate, negotiate, and adapt to other agents. Measuring these social skills is hard because most social interactions offer no objective ground truth. Evaluations often fall back on LLM judges, which are costly, subjective, and noisy. We introduce SocialGym, an environment of 22 multi-agent social games, from matrix games to hidden-role deduction, with verifiable outcomes determined by game rules. We benchmark ten LLMs across all games and roles using an Elo tournament. Our results show that no model is best in every game, and rankings shift across game families and sides. Secret keeping correlates more strongly with theory-of-mind and privacy benchmarks than with general knowledge benchmarks. Half of the models give away their side's secret in Chameleon and Spyfall, and the smallest models are not reliably better than chance at voting for hidden opponents in most hidden-role games. Finally, we examine whether models can improve their play by reflecting on self-play and writing strategy playbooks, finding mixed results across models and games. SocialGym provides verifiable measures for diagnosing LLM social capabilities across settings and roles.
  </details>

- **[Who Remembers What? Multi-Granularity Memory Extraction for Multi-User, Multi-Agent Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10439)**  `arXiv:2611.10439`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Yunzhe Wang, Mesut Erhan Unal, Sheng Zhang, Shabnam Ghadar, Peng Tang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Self-evolving agents improve their own skills, memory, and harness code from experience without retraining the base LLM, and group-evolving agents go further by sharing these lessons across agents. Yet most such systems overlook that sharing across users and heterogeneous agents requires deciding who can benefit from each lesson and what information must remain private. We present MixUA, a memory system that distills independent user-agent trajectories into lessons and assigns each lesson to a user-agent pair, user, agent, or global scope according to whom it benefits and what it may disclose. For evaluation, we introduce CrossLearnBench, a mixed-user-agent benchmark that pairs established agent tasks with user preferences and privacy probes that test whether private facts leak into memory other users can read. With five users and seven agents, MixUA raises pass rates by up to 16 points over no memory and 5 over a single global memory, even on tasks previously tried only by other users on other agents. It retains up to 90% of previously stated preferences, and at most 0.02% of privacy probes find a planted private fact in a forbidden scope. We hope MixUA offers a scalable path toward a global system where users and agents co-evolve.
  </details>

- **[Language model capabilities fail in a shared, predictable order under compression and perturbation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11695)**  `arXiv:2611.11695`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhixiang Wang, Ulas Bagci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Compression can preserve a language model's average accuracy while selectively degrading individual capabilities. Whether these losses follow method-specific patterns or a transferable order is unclear. We applied seven perturbation and compression methods to eight instruction-tuned models, scoring 16 tasks in four answer formats against preregistered predictions. Short-answer capabilities failed in nearly the same order under four random perturbations with different mechanisms (median correlation 0.90) and, within each model, under pruning and quantization (0.875). Intact answer margin, answer frequency and answer format predicted this order. Fragility profiles measured under random perturbation predicted which tasks and facts compression removes first and, after four-task calibration, held-out task accuracy (median absolute error 0.11-0.12, against 0.20-0.21 for uniform decline); profiles borrowed from other model families did almost as well. The order did not transfer across models under GPTQ quantization or hold for long-form tasks. Within short answers, what compression removes is predictable before deployment.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models without QK-Normalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10560)**  `arXiv:2611.10560`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yongding Tao, Yitong Qiao, Tianqi Shao, Tian Wang, Zhixuan Chu, Xiaoming Wu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Query-Key Normalization (QK-Norm) is widely adopted in modern large language models to control the growth of attention logits and improve training stability. However, query and key norms are not merely sources of instability. The query norm controls the sharpness of the attention distribution, while the key norm determines how strongly a token attracts different queries. These norms therefore carry token-specific information about attention selectivity and token importance. By normalizing both, QK-Norm suppresses this information. This raises a natural question: can we control query-key representations without discarding their sensitivity to magnitude? To address this question, we propose QK-MAP, a Magnitude-Aware Projection for constructing queries and keys. Rather than removing magnitude through normalization, QK-MAP transforms the input representation using multiple learnable frequencies, so that changes in magnitude are encoded as bounded phase patterns. This allows magnitude to be represented through phase and direction rather than vector length alone. Its periodic features have a fixed norm while remaining sensitive to magnitude changes, separating magnitude encoding from norm growth. Multiple frequencies further help distinguish magnitudes and reduce periodic ambiguity. Experiments across model scales from 190M to 7B parameters show that QK-MAP achieves lower training and validation loss than both Standard attention and QK-Norm. Further analyses show that QK-MAP preserves sensitivity to input scale, assigns less attention to the first token, and produces stronger attention contrast for relevant evidence in synthetic long-context tests. These results demonstrate that query-key scale can be effectively controlled without sacrificing token-specific magnitude information.
  </details>

- **[Recursive Semantic Divergence for LLM Agent Consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.33012)**  `arXiv:2610.33012`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Harshavardhan Abichandani, Penny Chong, Atin Ghosh, Daniel Dahlmeier_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Model (LLM) agents with tool use can behave inconsistently across independent runs from identical states. They may call different tools, produce contradictory outputs, or follow divergentthis http URLvariability compounds over time and undermines deployment reliability. Existing evaluation metrics primarily measure task correctness via completion-based scores and do not capture consistency in agent behavior across executions. Separately, agent behavior is analyzed either at the token level using probability distributions or at the single-response level using semantic clustering, but neither captures how inconsistency propagates across future turns of a trajectory. We introduce Recursive Semantic Divergence (RSD), a trajectory-level consistency metric adapted from bisimulation metrics in reinforcement learning. RSD measures the expected cumulative semantic disagreement between two independent rollouts of the same agent from the same state, using natural language inference to detect logical inconsistency. It is defined recursively over future states and approximated by a neural network trained on independent rollouts. The learned metric estimates future divergence from the current state, providing a consistency score at each turn without requiring the full trajectory to complete. When used as a reward signal for fine-tuning, RSD reduces AUC coefficient of variation (CV) by up to 30.9% on tau2-bench and 50.5% on ToolSandbox. Across Qwen-2.5 and Qwen-3.5 models, RSD reduces AUC CV in seven out of eight model-benchmark settings, while improving the final task progress in all eight settings.
  </details>

- **[NeuroActiSep: Detecting Factual Hallucinations from Feed-Forward Neurons in a Single Pass](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.14448)**  `arXiv:2609.14448`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ali Derogar Odolou, Reza Nazari, Mostafa Salehi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hallucination in large language models reduces their reliability and slows adoption. Various white-box studies have used internal representations to detect patterns of truthfulness and factuality. A less-studied approach is to identify feed-forward neurons correlated with hallucination. In this empirical study, we propose a method to rank feed-forward neurons at the final prompt token and use it to investigate the feasibility of utilizing them for hallucination detection in the first forward pass. Using a neuron selection dataset built from CounterFact for identifying neurons correlated with hallucination, we transfer the selected neuron identities to train hallucination classifiers on other factual question-answering datasets. Our work provides empirical evidence that probes trained using the features from sparse sets of selected neurons perform on par with probes trained on internal representations. We also analyze the distribution of selected neurons and the effects of layer depth and neuron budget on detection performance. Our results show that randomly drawn neuron sets are also competitive at larger budgets, suggesting that signals correlated with hallucination are distributed across many neurons, while selection can help in extremely sparse sets.
  </details>

- **[IntPro: Context-Aware Intent Inference via Selective Retrieval from User History](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.03325)**  `arXiv:2603.03325`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Guanming Liu, Meng Wu, Peng Zhang, Yu Zhang, Yubo Shu, Xianliang Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Accurately understanding user intent in context is essential for large language models (LLMs) to provide effective assistance. However, observed behavior may support multiple plausible intents, making the current context alone insufficient for identifying the user's underlying goal. A user's interaction history may contain recurring context-to-intent patterns that help disambiguate the current intent. Leveraging such historical patterns requires deciding whether to invoke retrieval for the current context and, if so, forming queries that express plausible intents rather than relying on contextual similarity alone. We present IntPro, a proxy agent that learns to either infer intent directly from the current context or generate candidate intent explanations to retrieve intent-aligned records from the user's history. We design intent explanations as a shared intent-oriented natural-language form that expresses the observed action, its functional goal, and a cue-to-intent rationale. Label-conditioned explanations represent historical records, while candidate explanations generated from the current context serve as retrieval queries in the same form. IntPro first learns direct and retrieval-conditioned inference through Direct-and-Retrieval supervised fine-tuning (SFT). IntPro then uses multi-turn GRPO with a retrieval-decision reward to jointly optimize intent prediction and selective retrieval mechanism. Experiments across three scenarios and three model backbones demonstrate that IntPro consistently improves context-aware intent inference.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing MoE Reinforcement Learning by Aligning Training and Inference Routers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.11370)**  `arXiv:2510.11370`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Wenhan Ma, Hailin Zhang, Liang Zhao, Yifan Song, Yudong Wang, Zhifang Sui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has emerged as a crucial approach for enhancing the capabilities of large language models. However, in Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) models, the routing mechanism often introduces instability, even leading to catastrophic RL training collapse. We analyze the training-inference consistency of MoE models and identify a notable discrepancy in routing behaviors between the two phases. Moreover, even under identical conditions, the routing framework can yield divergent expert selections across repeated forward passes. To address this foundational inconsistency, we propose Rollout Routing Replay (R3), a method that records routing distributions from the inference engine and replays them during training. R3 significantly reduces training-inference policy KL divergence and mitigates extreme discrepancies without compromising training speed. Extensive experiments on various settings confirm that R3 succeeds in stabilizing RL training, preventing collapse and outperforming methods such as GSPO and TIS. We believe this work can offer a new solution for stabilizing RL in MoE models.
  </details>

- **[APE: Selective Fine-tuning with Acceptance Criteria for Language Model Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.19912)**  `arXiv:2505.19912`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Javier Marín_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present Adjacent Possible Exploration (APE), a selective fine-tuning method for adapting large language models that systematically explores parameter modifications while maintaining model stability. Inspired by evolutionary optimization principles, APE evaluates multiple candidate parameter updates through fine-tuning on small data subsets and accepts only those exceeding a performance threshold. Unlike standard fine-tuning that follows single gradient directions, APE implements a filtered selection process that prevents destabilizing parameter changes while enabling systematic improvement. Our method achieves 33.9\% BLEU improvement and 36.2\% perplexity reduction on news summarization tasks while using minimal computational resources. The approach provides a practical framework for controlled model adaptation that balances performance gains with representational stability.
  </details>

- **[EmoHarness: Boosting Multimodal Emotion Understanding with Meta-Harness Evolving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11994)**  `arXiv:2611.11994`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI` `cs.CV`  
  _Fenghai Li, Bingxuan Li, Jiawen Zhang, Yifan Shen, Jiateng Liu, Yutong Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Small multimodal models can misjudge emotion when words, voice, and visual cues conflict. We present EmoHarness, which runs such a model, the frozen student, inside a harness: the prompts, settings, and verification code around its calls. A human-written starting harness, the scaffold, numbers the student's visual observations, transcript, and prosody descriptions as citable evidence and has the student weigh candidate emotions against it. In Meta-Harness Evolving (MHE), a stronger LLM, the Builder, reads the student's errors and execution traces offline and edits the harness; static checks and validation gates decide which edits are kept, and the Builder never sees evaluation items. On 5,507 MME-Emotion items, EmoHarness raises task-macro Recognition by 7.6-13.4 points over a single video-only call for all four students and both Builders. Transcripts and prosody descriptions supply most of this boost (+5.38 to +10.71); the scaffold changes the audio-augmented call by -0.79 to +2.54, partly through extra multi-label predictions, and evolved edits add at most +0.95, although no evolved increment survives Holm correction. The harness's emotion context also transfers to IntentQA, with small gains for three of four students, none of which survives correction. EmoHarness thus boosts small multimodal students chiefly by supplying and organizing audio-derived evidence.
  </details>

- **[PORT: Preference Optimization via Robust Token-Level Reweighting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12763)**  `arXiv:2611.12763`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ding Zhu, Xiukun Wei, Tian Xie, Zhihui Zhu, Xueru Zhang, Mohammad Mahdi Khalili_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference optimization has become a central approach for aligning large language models with human values, but its effectiveness depends heavily on the quality of preference annotations. In practice, preference data is often noisy due to annotation errors and ambiguity. Existing robust preference optimization methods primarily operate at the sequence level, implicitly treating entire responses as uniformly correct or incorrect. However, rejected responses may still contain informative reasoning steps, while preferred responses can include subtle errors. In this work, we propose \textbf{P}reference \textbf{O}ptimization via \textbf{R}obust \textbf{T}oken-level reweighting \textbf{(PORT)}, a fine-grained framework for robust alignment under noisy preferences. PORT performs token-level reweighting using the empirical cumulative distribution function (CDF) of token log-probabilities, yielding an efficient forward-pass-only proxy to gradient-norm penalization that selectively suppresses corrupted tokens without additional backward-pass computation. We provide theoretical analysis showing that PORT reduces the gradient bias from noisy preference labels by minimizing an upper bound on the bias term. Extensive experiments across diverse noise settings demonstrate that PORT consistently improves robustness and outperforms existing sequence-level baselines. The code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[BharatSynthQA: A Context-Grounded Question Answering Dataset on Indian Temples and Religious Heritage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11952)**  `arXiv:2611.11952`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Riya Naik, Shivam Sharma, Swati Agarwal_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  India's temples and other places of worship are among its richest repositories of cultural heritage, bringing together religious tradition, history, architecture, and art. Due to the widespread curiosity, people increasingly turn to Artificial Intelligence (AI) assistants for information about them. Current models are rarely trained or evaluated on this domain. We present BharatSynthQA, a passage grounded question answering (QA) dataset focused on Indian temples, which also covers other religious sites connected to them. Our pipeline crawls Wikipedia from topics seeded by authentic heritage sources, filters articles for topical relevance, and locations in India. Further it generates Boolean, factoid, and explanatory QA pairs with a fewshot prompted LLM. An LLM judge filters the pairs on answer correctness, retaining 98,677 of 131,494 generated pairs. Additionally, 300 human-annotated pairs are released separately as a reference set. Human evaluation of the subsample of this dataset showed 89.83% of the questions were formed correctly. Benchmarking five current LLMs shows that factoid token F1 is only 0.18-0.30 without the source passage but 0.73-0.77 with it, indicating that this domain is poorly captured in model knowledge. The dataset is available atthis https URLto support research on culturally grounded QA and retrieval-augmented generation for Indian heritage.
  </details>

- **[HiddenPathQA: A Benchmark for Knowledge Graph Question Answering When Questions Hide Their Paths](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11802)**  `arXiv:2611.11802`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Sumin Jo, Edward Choi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graph question answering (KGQA) systems are typically evaluated on questions whose surface tokens lexically expose the gold KG path. Users, however, may ask questions without knowing the underlying schema: "Which condition could Andrew have due to family history?" compresses a 4-hop chain "Andrew --[father]--> parent --[father]--> grandparent --[medical condition]--> disease --[subclass of]--> hereditary disorder" into a single phrase (family history) that names none of those relations. We identify this collapse of path-question alignment as a blind spot in existing KGQA evaluation. Three major LLM-KG paradigms, hop-by-hop exploration, plan-then-retrieve, and similarity-based retrieval, implicitly rely on such lexical cues, an assumption rarely tested in practice.We make three contributions. (i) We introduce HiddenPathQA, a manually verified benchmark of 1,055 questions spanning 2-6 hops and multiple domains, constructed so that question wording does not reveal the gold path's relations. (ii) We propose Holistic Path Selection, which compares questions against candidate relation chains in the local subgraph as complete units, replacing irreversible per-hop relation scoring with globally informed chain-level reasoning. We develop two implementations, TieredHolistic and FlatHolistic.
