(iii) We conduct a pseudoword stress test that replaces entity names with meaningless placeholders to distinguish KG-grounded reasoning from entity-level memorization. Holistic Path Selection outperforms six strong KGQA baselines (ToG, PoG, FiDeLiS, R2-KG, KAPING, and KARPA) by 8.8-17.9 accuracy points across two LLM backbones and both original and pseudoword settings. Its advantage under pseudoword replacement shows that the gains cannot be explained by entity-level memorization alone.
  </details>

- **[Explaining and interrupting collective misalignment in language model systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11724)**  `arXiv:2611.11724`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Edoardo Chidichimo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In collectives, language model agents can exchange individually innocuous messages that together support a policy violation. Understanding and preventing collective misalignment requires answering the question of which messages sufficiently influenced and necessarily enabled the violation. Collective Dependence Analysis (CDA) operationalises necessity and sufficiency to explain decisions and find the fewest removals interrupting support within tested combinations. Tests covered constructed violations, unscripted agent conversations, and recorded interactions, with selected message removals reducing violations from 93% to 6% in controlled cases. Using O(N) model evaluations for N messages, CDA achieves the same violation reduction with half the deletions of a frontier LLM judge given the transcript and asked for all supporting messages in eligible operational cases. CDA also proposes the mechanism through which the collective misalignment emerged. Nonetheless, complete explanations remain difficult on unfamiliar tasks and some removals can inadvertently disrupt correct decisions. CDA unifies explanations of collective misalignment with experimentally tested interventions.
  </details>

- **[A Layer-wise Study of How Style Rewriting Breaks the Calibration of Zero-Shot LLM Fake-News Detectors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11239)**  `arXiv:2611.11239`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Prabhjot Singh, Praney Goyal, Raksha Sharma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly asked to judge whether a news article is true, and their stated confidence is often taken at face value. We test what happens to that confidence when an article's writing style, but not its facts, is manipulated, as in recent \emph{style attacks} on fake-news detectors. Tracing how the prediction and its confidence form layer by layer inside the network, we study three model families (LLaMA-3-8B, Qwen2.5-7B, Mistral-7B) on three fake-news benchmarks, and find the same three patterns in every model--dataset pairing. First, on the original articles the models are competent and largely honest judges: they settle on an answer, briefly become overconfident, and then correct their confidence in the final layers. Second, style-rewritten articles break this self-correction: the models become far less accurate, under the strongest attack, worse than random guessing, yet remain just as confident, so they are \emph{confidently wrong} precisely when a warning is most needed. Third, nearly all of the damage comes from rewriting into sensational or emotionally charged styles, while sober, neutral rewrites leave the models' honesty largely intact. The practical implication is that an LLM's confidence about veracity cannot be trusted as a safety signal when an article's style may have been manipulated.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating Jev on Arabic: Comparing Decision Models and LLMs Across Arabic Tasks and Dialects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10802)**  `arXiv:2611.10802`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hossam Elsafty, Tobias Deußer, Lorenz Sparrenberg, Rajkumar Ramamurthy, Rafet Sifa_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How does Jev compare with other systems on Arabic tasks, and how does its performance change between Modern Standard Arabic (MSA) and dialects? We evaluate 67 systems, including 47 decision models and 20 large language models (LLMs), on 43 tasks across 9 task families using a shared zero-shot protocol. The Arabic-focused frozen suite includes English controls in three parallel resources, which we also remove in a separate descriptive comparison. Jev 1.14.0 achieves a mean task-primary metric of 69.24 on a scale multiplied by 100, compared with 68.77 for Jev 1.13.0 and 71.81 for Gemma-4-31B, the highest-scoring system on this aggregate. Removing the recorded English controls, repeated cross-task items or both leaves the full system order unchanged. Relative performance and gains between Jev versions vary across task families. In content-matched comparisons within Jev 1.14.0, pooled dialect-minus-MSA accuracy gaps range from -7.94 to -0.35 percentage points across four datasets; dialect-specific results show larger variation, and SIB-200's source-clustered interval includes zero. Paired contrasts with Jev 1.13.0, Gemma-4-31B and Jais-2-8B show a larger Palestinian banking penalty for Jev 1.14.0 than for either LLM under simultaneous uncertainty; the other gap contrasts remain inconclusive. These descriptive findings show why aggregate scores should be read alongside task-family results, coverage and matched language comparisons. They do not establish a general model ranking for dialect robustness or explain the causes of the observed gaps.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Mathematical Reasoning in Turkish Large Language Models with Search-Based Inference and Model Merging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10800)**  `arXiv:2611.10800`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Ahmed Zeer, Osama Shbib, Muzaffer Kaan Yuce, Himmet Toprak Kesgin, Mehmet Fatih Amasyali_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We improve Turkish mathematical reasoning in Large Language Models by combining LoRA fine-tuning, weight-space model merging, and search-based inference. Using Turkish-Llama 8B-DPO with Turkish translations of MetaMath, NuminaMath, and TigerLab, we find data quality beats scale (Mix-30k best among fine-tuned; large MetaMath underperforms). Linear merging delivers the largest gains (GSM8K up to 0.63), while Ties is modest and Breadcrumbs/Slerp degrade. Search with MCTS and Tree of Thoughts improves mean LLM-judge scores for reasoning quality on a 0-100 scale. Limitations include machine translation, limited scale, and search stochasticity.
  </details>

- **[Adapting Large Language Models via Multi-Task Learning for Mental Health Problem, Emotion, and Sentiment Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10797)**  `arXiv:2611.10797`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Amir Rafiq Bhat, Rahim Dehkharghani, Zeki Erdem_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This study investigates sequential multi-task learning using Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT), LoRA, and 4-bit quantization on Llama 3.1 8B Instruct for mental health problem detection, emotion detection, and sentiment analysis. Using 4,243 English social media posts, it compares two task sequences and demonstrates that task ordering significantly affects knowledge transfer and task interference. The MHP-first sequence achieves the best mental health problem detection (Micro-F1: 0.7624), while the Emotion-first sequence performs better in emotion (F1: 0.6052) and sentiment analysis (F1: 0.9459). A Joint-MTL baseline further highlights the challenges of simultaneous learning, including task interference and generative format collapse. Overall, the findings demonstrate the effectiveness of sequential PEFT for mental health NLP while showing that task order is critical to balancing performance across related tasks. The integration of LoRA and 4-bit quantization also reduces computational and memory requirements, supporting the adaptation of large language models in resource-constrained environments.
  </details>

- **[Behavioral Evaluation of Large Language Models Under Cognitive Biases in HAZOP Studies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10721)**  `arXiv:2611.10721`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Vinicius M. Müller, Vitor A. O. Silva, Sávio S. V. Vianna, Flávio V. Silva_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hazard and Operability (HAZOP) studies safeguard industrial processes against catastrophic accidents. Large language models (LLMs) increasingly assist HAZOP drafting, yet evaluations rely on lexical overlap. Because LLMs internalize human heuristics, prompts may trigger biased judgments. Whether frontier LLMs withstand cognitive perturbations in safety-critical workflows remains unknown. Here we show across 420 trials that three frontier LLMs resisted 96.8% of cognitive manipulations, rendering overall susceptibility statistically inconclusive. Contrary to assumed broad fragility, models rejected confirmation and availability cues. However, safety-critical risk downgrades emerged under low anchors and gain frames. Thus, automated hazard analysis requires disciplined human-in-the-loop validation.
  </details>

- **[AI in the Clinical Documentation Loop: Effects of LLM-Induced Note Bloat and Diagnostic Overcall on Downstream LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10601)**  `arXiv:2611.10601`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinghui Liu, Anthony Nguyen, Taylan Gurgenci_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to process medical records for healthcare tasks such as clinical documentation. Because these tools also generate documentation, the relationship is now bidirectional: models both read from and write to the record. Understanding how LLMs behave when reading records that already contain LLM-authored content is therefore critical for their safe deployment. In this study, we examine how LLM-authored content in the medical record affects downstream LLMs that read the same record. We consider two types of LLM-induced artifacts: bloat, in which LLM-generated summaries accumulate in the record and change the length and provenance of its content over time; and distortion, in which LLMs subtly alter clinical findings rather than fabricating facts outright. As a case of distortion, we study diagnostic overcall (documenting an unconfirmed differential diagnosis in the record) and test whether it propagates to the reader LLM's output. Across seven reader models from four families, we find evidence of both effects. First, LLM-generated summary notes modestly but consistently degraded downstream hospital-course generation compared with copy-forward and templated text of matched length. Second, injected overcalls reappeared in downstream outputs at rates that varied by reader model, without detectable changes in standard quality metrics. These findings show the need to monitor AI-generated content in medical records and to account for it when those records are later processed by AI.
  </details>

- **[AFVE-Bench: Measuring Abandoned-Frame Verbalization Errors in Multi-Turn LLM Revision](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10541)**  `arXiv:2611.10541`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jiaming Chu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-turn revision often asks a model to replace an earlier organizing frame while preserving still-valid facts and artifact requirements. Existing instruction-following evaluations mainly assess the latest target and may therefore score an artifact as successful even when it adopts the new frame but exposes the superseded one. We call this failure an abandoned-frame verbalization error (AFVE). AFVE-Bench evaluates silent frame replacement in English and Chinese using 192 matched four-turn dialogues across eight writing domains. Each seed has zero, one, or two pre-target user requests to generate under the source frame. AFVE eligibility depends on the user-request history and applies to the one- and two-request conditions; the zero-request condition serves as a frame-bearing baseline. Eleven model configurations yielded 4,224 expected post-target response units. Two annotators independently scored 4,204 scorable responses for target adoption, source-frame verbalization, and direct usability; model and condition metadata were hidden, and disagreements were adjudicated. AFVE occurs in 25.54% (715/2,799) of history-conditioned responses. Of 2,388 target-passing history-conditioned responses, 472 (19.77%) still expose the abandoned source frame, most often through contrastive framing (423/472). For these standalone revision tasks, target adoption does not ensure source-frame silence.
  </details>

- **[Hibana: Fast Agentic Inference on Unified-Memory Devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10469)**  `arXiv:2611.10469`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yahya Emara, Chi-Chih Chang, Anthony Fei, Ahmed F. AbouElhamayed, Chong-Yan Chen, Hung-Yueh Chiang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Local AI promises capable personal assistants that run on users' own devices, keeping data and computation under their control. Open-weight models that fit on local devices can now handle practical tasks such as coding. Yet current inference engines fall short. Popular engines such as vLLM and SGLang target datacenter GPUs with dedicated host memory, while local engines underuse the memory hierarchy of unified-memory devices. We present Hibana, an inference engine for unified-memory devices that co-optimizes model representation, memory management, and decoding strategy. Model surgery, the process of editing a trained model's architecture to suit its hardware, changes how the model computes, shrinking its memory footprint and data movement. Unified-memory management preserves each agent's state across turns, so long-running agents can resume instantly instead of recomputing their state. Adaptive speculation generates several tokens per step. Hibana codesigns these features around the shared memory capacity and limited bandwidth of unified-memory systems-on-chip to support local multi-agent workloads. On a single DGX Spark serving Qwen and Nemotron models of up to 125B parameters, Hibana completes up to 3.4$\times$ more agentic tasks per hour and delivers up to 3.9$\times$ higher decoding throughput than other established frameworks such as vLLM, NVIDIA Flare,this http URL, and TensorFold. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[The Truth Beneath the Tokens: Prompt-Space Recovery of Knowledge Edits in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10450)**  `arXiv:2611.10450`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Manit Baser, Rachel Yeo Hui Min, Aditya Nawal, Dinil Mon Divakaran, Mohan Gurusamy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model editing provides an efficient way to update factual knowledge in transformer models. Recent approaches reverse edits by modifying, masking, or decomposing model weights, but make recovery dependent on weight-space interventions. We instead show that pre-edit knowledge can be recovered through input-space interventions alone, without modifying the edited model's weights. We introduce Memento, a prompt-space mechanism for auditing residual knowledge that learns a reusable recovery signal to steer an edited model toward its pre-edit response. Memento supports both continuous recovery prefixes and discrete textual prompts for standard text interfaces. Across three LLMs and five editing techniques, Memento recovers 80.07% of held-out edited facts, achieving a 9.58% relative improvement over baselines, suggesting that editing may suppress the facts without fully eliminating their recoverability. We further investigate whether prompt-space recovery extends beyond model editing. On pristine, unedited LLMs, Memento improves average factual recall by 53.9% relative to standard inference. These results demonstrate the potential of prompt-space recovery for auditing residual knowledge in edited models and investigating the gap between factual knowledge and its accessibility during inference. We will release our source code.
  </details>

- **[Coding or Cheating? An Investigation of Frontier Coding LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10404)**  `arXiv:2611.10404`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuntao Gui_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Frontier agentic LLMs are trained on verifiable rewards in reinforcement learning (RL) environments built from real software repositories. A passing test suite is treated as the reward for solving the task. We show that, on hard problems, frontier models often satisfy these rewards by obtaining the answer instead of deriving it, known as reward hacking. Prior reports document this behavior on public benchmarks. We test its persistence on unseen problems.In a hardened environment where the answer cannot leak, nearly every model we tested attempted to recover the answer, escalating through tiers of increasingly specialized exploitation: mining the repository history for the upstream fix, inspecting the git object store directly, and sweeping the filesystem for a possible newer copy of the source or hidden tests. These attempts were concentrated on the hardest problems, and the models differed in whether, how often, and how deeply they escalated. Our position is that this behavior is a learned shortcut, acquired in RL environments that leak the answer, not a sign of intelligence. We release agent traces for these models to support further study atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CulturalCrosswords: A Multilingual Benchmark comprised of Answer Constraints and Grid Completion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10044)**  `arXiv:2611.10044`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Isaac Saxton-Knight, Gabriel Saxton-Knight, Diego Oliveira, Tidor-Vlad Pricope, Aston Garratt, Jocelyn D'Arcy_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When several answers fit a riddle, exact-match scoring can penalise a valid response. CulturalCrosswords tests how answer constraints affect large language models (LLMs) solving cultural riddles, whose solutions draw on knowledge specific to a community. We constructed 53 crosswords from crowdsourced riddles in 42 languages and evaluated 11 LLM configurations on five versions of each clue set: plain batches, batches with lengths, batches with lengths and partial-answer hints, and crosswords requesting an answer list or a completed grid. On 962 clues with alternative answers, lengths improve agreement with the reference by 22.8 percentage points, compared with 8.4 points when accepted alternatives also receive credit. Part of the gain therefore appears to come from selecting a required answer form. Crossword input increases the share of all crossings with compatible answers by 9.7 points; requiring a completed grid reveals large differences between models. The benchmark distinguishes agreement with a reference from the ability to produce answers that fit together and occupy the correct cells.
  </details>

- **[DramaGrow: A Regime-Aware Benchmark for Auditing LLM Judges of Multi-Episode Drama](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09938)**  `arXiv:2611.09938`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Hui Zhang, Yibo Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLMs can now draft complete multi-episode dramas, and LLM judges are increasingly used to evaluate them. Assessing the reliability of these judges is difficult because drama quality encompasses specification-backed errors, reproducible preferences, and legitimate disagreement among human raters. Most existing evaluations reduce these cases to a single score from one judge call and leave tie calibration, call-to-call variation, and the vote margin underlying the human reference unreported. We introduce DramaGrow, a regime-aware benchmark and audit protocol for AI-generated Chinese five-episode drama. The benchmark assigns verifiable constraint violations, four-of-five preferences, and within-panel disagreements to separate denominators and evidence rules. It contains 800 episodes, 120 byte-preserving interventions, and 1,155 blinded five-rater records, including a frozen extension with 114 supermajority pairs and 21 disagreement pairs. On the frozen sets of 114 preference pairs and 35 constraint interventions, the repeated absolute-score rubrics yield overlapping performance, with mean accuracies of 53.8-55.8% and three-call agreement of 29.8-32.5%, and their accuracy falls from 71.6-71.9% to 34.8-37.1% as the tie band widens. DirectPair-GLM reaches 85.1% preference accuracy, 89.5% constraint F1, and 81.9% Detect+Locate on the same items, and exact subpanel enumeration identifies four-of-five as the smallest stable human vote margin. Evaluation protocol, tie calibration, judge-call variation, and panel margin therefore affect performance as much as rubric design. We will release the specification packages, scripts, interventions, anonymized human votes and rationales, frozen judge runs, and analysis code.
  </details>

- **[How Many Examples, and Which Ones? A Study of In-Context Learning for Natural Language Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09936)**  `arXiv:2611.09936`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Georgos Dikaios, Isidoros Perikos, Ioannis Hatzilygeroudis_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural language inference (NLI) is a fundamental task of natural language processing and crucial for evaluating the strengths and weaknesses of AI methods. Research has mostly focused on encoder or encoder-decoder architectures. However, the field of decoder-only Large Language Models (LLMs) remains underexplored. While prior work has mainly investigated complex sampling and prompting techniques, the effectiveness of a minimalistic prompting approach is yet to be examined in decoder-only LLMs. In this work, we examine and provide a comprehensive benchmark of LLMs (Llama, Mistral, Gemma, and Phi 4) in this domain, across the MNLI, QNLI and SciTail datasets. The performance of these models was evaluated using zero-shot and few-shot prompting strategies with simple and direct prompts. The results of the article indicate that LLMs generally perform exceptionally well when they are provided with a simple, straightforward prompt, challenging the notion that complex prompting techniques are always required for NLI tasks. Additionally, we find that the choice of demonstration label in one-shot prompting significantly affects performance for most models, with entailment-primed examples performing best. Our findings also show that the use of a few representative examples, in the case of few-shot, produced performance comparable or superior to prompts with higher example counts, although the efficacy of the prompting techniques is model and dataset-dependent. Moreover, our simple prompting approach consistently matched or outperformed more elaborate methods such as ICS and MCQ-based prompting.
  </details>

- **[DynEdit: Dynamic Entropy-Guided Sequential Editing for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.34768)**  `arXiv:2610.34768`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jinhu Fu, Yan Bai, Yihang Lou, Li Sun, Jiahong Wu, Xiangxiang Chu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge editing aims to update outdated or incorrect knowledge in large language models without full retraining. Existing parameter-editing methods remain limited for long-sequence knowledge: single-point editors provide insufficient coverage for extended targets, while fixed chunk-based editors distribute editing effort uniformly across rigid predefined windows, failing to prioritize difficult, information-dense tokens. In this work, we show that token entropy is closely associated with both editing difficulty and semantic importance: high-entropy regions are harder to edit and often contain key factual content. Motivated by this observation, we propose DynEdit, a dynamic framework for long-sequence knowledge editing. DynEdit uses token-level entropy to guide non-uniform sliding, increasing the coverage of difficult high-entropy regions, and further applies entropy-guided token-level optimization to strengthen updates on information-dense tokens within each editing window. Experiments on three benchmarks and two backbone models show that DynEdit consistently outperforms competitive baselines across diverse long-form editing settings, achieving gains of up to +14.0 BLEU points on challenging paraphrase queries and near-saturated semantic scores on several diverse knowledge domains.
  </details>

- **[Polarity Blindness in LLM Safety: A Systematic Study of Negation, Antonym Substitution, and Adversarial Framing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27908)**  `arXiv:2610.27908`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Diego Miguel Lozano, Erik Derner, Juan Antonio Pérez-Ortiz, Nuria Oliver_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Negation remains a persistent challenge for language models across NLP tasks and modalities. We show that this weakness can undermine LLM safety guardrails through a structural vulnerability we term polarity blindness: the failure to recognize harmful intent after polarity-altering linguistic transformations. We study two forms of polarity inversion: grammatical negation and antonym replacement, and use synonym replacement as a polarity-preserving control. We further test whether an adversarial textual wrapper amplifies these failures. Using a 260-prompt benchmark across 10 state-of-the-art LLMs and 3 languages (English, Spanish, Czech), stratified by harmfulness potential and evaluated on a validated 0-5 harm scale, we find that polarity blindness affects most evaluated models, with mean harm scores reaching 4.12 for the most vulnerable model. The effect persists across typologically differing negation systems, suggesting that polarity blindness reflects a broader limitation in semantic robustness rather than a language- or grammar-specific failure.
  </details>

- **[Hearsay or Fact? How Framing and Elicitation Shape LLM Probability Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27745)**  `arXiv:2610.27745`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _An-Sheng Jhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We measure LLM probability updates on $\delta$-SNLI premise-hypothesis pairs across the Multi-Candidate Verbalization (MCV) family. Separate calls elicit priors and posteriors; directional movement s signs changes by the annotated direction. We cross stated vs. reported wording with Direct single estimates (D0) vs. Multi-Candidate Weighted elicitation (MCW; five weighted estimates, V0, weighted mean M_W). Frozen formal tests cover 15 configurations (23,400 planned units): 3 primary (Qwen3-8B thinking off, OLMo-2-7B, gpt-6.1-sol via codex; 6,600), 4 CLI-harnessed configurations (gpt-oss-120b, claude-sonnet-5.5, gemini-3.8-flash low/high; dnli-agy; 5,600), and 8 un-harnessed REST API configurations (dnli-api; 11,200). We add a 20-configuration, 8-provider dev ladder (3B-550B; 6,000 units) and a 2-model OpenAI 1+1+4 ablation of D0, Direct-CoT (D_CoT), MCW (V0), and Multi-Candidate Unweighted (MCU; V2 / M_U; 1,800 calls). All 15 reject H1 (s = +16.1 to +28.9 points) and H4a (endpoint reduction +0.065 to +0.391); 13 reject H2 (+2.0 to +8.9 points), except gpt-oss-120b (+0.9) and sf:qwen3.5-4b (-0.64). In 14 of 15 formal configurations, readouts obey Top-1 > MCW (M_W) > MCU (M_U). On 120 common pairs, API Qwen3 tracks local Qwen3 (H1 +18.72 vs. +18.90; H4a +0.265 vs. +0.254; H2 +3.78 vs. +1.67; H4b -9.03 vs. -6.70); local H2 rejects on all 240 pairs (+1.97), not this subset. API Gemma 31B (formal H4b -2.85, Top-1 +0.64; spread -13.3% / +7.6%) and Groq gpt-oss-120b (dev -4.15, +0.40; -18.4% / +12.2%) show small MCW costs and stated narrowing/reported widening without CLI harnesses. In OpenAI ablations, D_CoT keeps numeric-token entropy <1 bit; MCU (V2) shows smaller movement loss but halves spread and $\Delta H_2$ vs. MCW (V0). Because $\delta$-NLI annotates only direction, we make no claim about appropriate update magnitude. Code/data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Staying In Character: Role-Playing Agents with Perspective-Bounded Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.25632)**  `arXiv:2606.25632`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Xushuo Tang, Junhe Zhang, Zihan Yang, Yifu Tang, Sichao Li, Longbin Lai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent LLM role-playing systems build character agents from novels by extracting characters, scenes, and relations. Yet long-narrative role playing suffers from two failures: Factual Overreach, where shared retrieval or parametric memory lets a character use facts outside its perspective, and Stylistic Monotony, where profile descriptions flatten a character into a fixed voice. To address these failures, we propose ReverieMem, a three-layer memory architecture for book-based character agents. The episodic layer stores first-person scene memories; the semantic layer stores visibility-tagged facts; and the personality layer stores situation-dependent speech and behaviour patterns. We evaluate ReverieMem and six reference methods on KBF-QA, a new benchmark of 4,386 questions across eight novels that assesses character knowledge boundaries. ReverieMem improves Knowledge Boundary Fidelity by 34.6 percentage points over the strongest reference method. On BookWorld's five-dimension pairwise narrative protocol, ReverieMem achieves a mean win rate of 78.86% against BookWorld across five generator LLMs and two generation modes, suggesting that perspective-bounded memory improves both boundary fidelity and character-grounded narrative generation.
  </details>

- **[From Hidden States to Confessions: Auditing Deception in Reasoning Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.17478)**  `arXiv:2606.17478`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Kexin Chen, Dongxia Wang, Haonan Zhang, Yanhui Li, Xinyu Deng, Yi Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLMs acquire stronger reasoning capabilities, deceptive behavior becomes an increasingly serious safety concern. Existing deception monitors typically either score visible transcripts or derive scalar probe scores from representation vectors, leaving little inspectable evidence about why a response is suspicious. We introduce StateWitness, an audit decoder for deception monitoring. It reads a target model's hidden states to answer audit queries and produce confession-style reports of potential failures or violations. We evaluate generalization beyond the decoder's audit training data across two target reasoning models and seven deception datasets. StateWitness achieves 0.916 mean AUROC, with relative gains of 11.6% and 25.0% over the best black-box and activation-probe baselines, respectively, under the same evaluation protocol. When combined with existing monitors, StateWitness reduces missed deceptive examples in simple threshold ensembles. Beyond scalar detection, the decoder returns query-level answers, schema reports, and token- or sentence-level evidence traces for human inspection. We envision StateWitness as one monitoring layer in a broader defense-in-depth strategy.
  </details>

- **[LLMs Can Better Capture Human Judgments--With the Right Prompts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12754)**  `arXiv:2606.12754`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Danica Dillion, Chen Cecilia Liu, Baihui Wang, Daniele Barolo, Tanmay Rajore, Niket Tandon, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Are large language models (LLMs) bad at capturing human judgment? Two commonly stated limitations are that LLMs fail to capture full distributions of responses, and that their judgments are unstable across wording variations. We demonstrate simple prompting strategies that mitigate these limitations. Across two datasets--a U.S.--representative set of 144 moral scenarios and 38 moral beliefs from the International Social Survey Programme's Family and Changing Gender Roles module covering 32 countries--we show how simple elicitation techniques help improve AI-human alignment. First, prompting models to report standard deviations and response proportions recovers the full range of human responses better than common strategies. Second, ensuring scenarios are clear to human participants--as reflected in human confusion ratings--is associated with stronger model alignment, and LLMs can track human confusion ratings. At the same time, we find that model confidence ratings do not consistently track errors in models' judgments, though they can predict human variability relatively well. These results suggest that asking better questions to LLMs can yield better answers.
  </details>

- **[Cat-DPO: Category-Adaptive Safety Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.17299)**  `arXiv:2604.17299`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Tiankai Yang, Yi Nian, Xinyuan Li, Ruiyao Xu, Henry Peng Zou, Kaize Ding, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Aligning large language models with human preferences must balance two competing goals: responding helpfully to legitimate requests and reliably refusing harmful ones. Most preference-based safety alignment methods collapse safety into a single scalar that is applied uniformly to every preference pair. The result is a model that appears safe on average but remains relatively unsafe in a minority of harm categories. We cast safety alignment as a per-category constrained optimization problem and derive Cat-DPO, a direct-preference-optimization algorithm with a separate adaptive safety margin for each harm category. The margin tightens when the model still produces unsafe responses in a category and relaxes once the model catches up, so the training signal tracks each category's current difficulty rather than averaging across a single global rate. Across two LLM backbones and six preference-learning baselines, Cat-DPO improves aggregate helpfulness and harmlessness and compresses per-category safety variance and the best-to-worst gap, offering a drop-in per-category refinement of direct preference safety alignment.
  </details>

- **[ImplicitBBQ: Benchmarking Implicit Bias in Large Language Models through Characteristic Based Cues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.01925)**  `arXiv:2604.01925`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Bhaskara Hanuma Vedula, Darshan Anghan, Ishita Goyal, Ponnurangam Kumaraguru, Abhijnan Chakraborty_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models increasingly suppress biased outputs when demographic identity is stated explicitly, yet may still exhibit implicit biases when identity is conveyed indirectly. Existing benchmarks use name based proxies to detect implicit biases, which carry weak associations with many social demographics and cannot extend to dimensions like age or socioeconomic status. We introduce ImplicitBBQ, a QA benchmark that evaluates implicit bias through characteristic based cues, demographically associated attributes that signal implicitly, across age, gender, region, religion, caste, and socioeconomic status. Evaluating 11 models, we find that implicit bias in ambiguous contexts is over six times higher than explicit bias in open weight models. Notably, this bias is distributed unevenly across demographics: caste emerges as the most severe while gender is the least affected. Safety prompting and chain-of-thought reasoning fail to substantially close this gap; even few-shot prompting, which reduces implicit bias by 79%, leaves caste bias at four times the level of any other dimension. These findings indicate that current alignment and prompting strategies address the surface of bias evaluation while leaving demographically associated stereotypic associations largely unresolved. We publicly release our code and dataset for model providers and researchers to benchmark potential mitigation techniques.
  </details>

- **[Inference-time Alignment via Sparse Junction Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.21215)**  `arXiv:2602.21215`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Runyi Hu, Jie Zhang, Shiqian Zhao, Jiale Meng, Jiwei Li, Jason Zeng, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Token-level steering has emerged as a pivotal approach for inference-time alignment, enabling fine-grained control over large language models (LLMs) by modulating their output distributions without parameter updates. While effective, existing methods rely on dense intervention at every decoding step. This persistent manipulation not only incurs substantial computational overhead but also risks compromising generation quality by excessively drifting from the model's intrinsic distribution. In this work, we show that dense intervention is unnecessary and propose Sparse Inference-time Alignment (SIA), which performs sparse junction steering, intervening only at critical decision points along the generation trajectory. Our key insight is that high-entropy junctions are empirically consistent with pivotal decision points in the generation trajectory and are particularly susceptible to misalignment, suggesting that these points benefit from alignment-related reward signal. Extensive experiments across different model families and alignment objectives show that steering only 20%-80% of tokens achieves the superior alignment-efficiency trade-offs. For strong base models such as Qwen3, intervening on as few as 20% of tokens matches or even surpasses heavily post-trained instruct models. This sparsity enables stronger guidance while better preserving the model's native distribution, integrates seamlessly with search-based methods (e.g., Best-of-N), and improves efficiency by reducing the required sampling budget while yielding practical wall-clock speedups under matched-quality comparisons.
  </details>

- **[DialectLLM: A Dialect-Aware Dialog[ue] Generation Framework Beyond Standard American English](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.22888)**  `arXiv:2601.22888`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Jio Oh, Paul Vicinanza, Thomas Butler, Steven Euijong Whang, Dezhi Hong, Amani Namboori_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  More than 80% of the 1.6B English speakers do not use Standard American English (SAE), yet LLMs often fail to correctly identify non-SAE dialects and generate stereotyped responses for their speakers. We introduce DialectLLM, the first large-scale framework for generating high-quality multi-dialectal conversational data encompassing the three pillars of written dialect---lexical (vocabulary), orthographic (spelling), and morphosyntactic (grammar) features. DialectLLM produces a dialect-parallel dialog dataset spanning nine English dialects. Partnering with native linguists, we design and validate SAE-to-dialect transformation rules, ensuring authenticity. Our approach challenges the prevailing practice of applying a single morphosyntactic feature set to both user utterances and model responses, showing that models should not reproduce up to 90% of the grammatical features of a dialect. Human evaluation confirms data quality, with annotators preferring DialectLLM over prior methods in 98.8% of pairwise comparisons for dialect naturalness. We then construct DialectLLM-Bench, a dialect-parallel benchmark with 50k+ dialogs, resulting in 97k+ QA pairs, and evaluate 17 LLMs on dialect identification and response generation tasks. Even frontier models achieve around 70% accuracy, fail to reach 40% for prominent dialects like Canadian English, and systematically misclassify non-SAE dialects as American or British. Beyond benchmarking, we show that DialectLLM data also serve as a scalable LLM post-training resource, suggesting a practical path toward dialect-aware conversational AI.
  </details>

- **[InFi-Check: Interpretable and Fine-Grained Fact-Checking of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.06666)**  `arXiv:2601.06666`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yuzhuo Bai, Shuzheng Si, Kangyang Luo, Qingyi Wang, Wenhao Li, Gang Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often hallucinate, yet most existing fact-checking methods treat factuality evaluation as a binary classification problem, offering limited interpretability and failing to capture fine-grained error types. In this paper, we introduce InFi-Check, a framework for interpretable and fine-grained fact-checking of LLM outputs. Specifically, we first propose a controlled data synthesis pipeline that generates high-quality data featuring explicit evidence, fine-grained error type labels, justifications, and corrections. Based on this, we further construct large-scale training data and a manually verified benchmark InFi-Check-FG for fine-grained fact-checking of LLM outputs. Building on these high-quality training data, we further propose InFi-Checker, which can jointly provide supporting evidence, classify fine-grained error types, and produce justifications along with corrections. Experiments show that InFi-Checker achieves state-of-the-art performance on InFi-Check-FG and strong generalization across various downstream tasks, significantly improving the utility and trustworthiness of factuality evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Sentinel: Decoding Context Utilization via Attention Probing for Efficient LLM Context Compression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.23277)**  `arXiv:2505.23277`  `cs.CL` `cs.AI`  
  _Yong Zhang, Heng Li, Yanwen Huang, Ning Cheng, Yang Guo, Yun Zhu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retrieved contexts are often long, noisy, and redundant, motivating query-aware compression before answer generation. Native attention provides signals for context selection, but heterogeneous head behaviors complicate their direct aggregation into sentence-utility scores. We introduce Sentinel, a sentence-level compressor that learns to combine these signals through a signed linear readout across layers and heads. Only the readout is trained using existing QA evidence annotations, while the backbone remains frozen and sentence scoring requires no autoregressive generation. Across twelve LongBench tasks, Sentinel with a 0.5B backbone outperforms Full Context on multiple tasks under a 2,000-token budget with each of three readers: Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, and DeepSeek V4.1 Flash. Despite English-only QA supervision, the same readout transfers to Chinese tasks without task-specific retraining. Length-stratified profiling further shows lower compression latency and GPU memory usage than LLMLingua-2 and CPC.
  </details>

- **[Large Language Models as a Universal Searcher for Open-World Automated Machine Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12791)**  `arXiv:2611.12791`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuxuan Hu, Jihao Liu, Weikang Shi, Xijie Huang, Linda Wei, Mingjie Zhan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Developing effective machine learning models requires complex manual tuning, while current Automated Machine Learning (AutoML) frameworks remain domain-specific and isolate model architecture, training hyperparameter, and data ratio optimization. Here, we bridge this gap by proposing a universal, large language model (LLM)-based AutoML framework that jointly optimizes these configurations for the first time without the need for task-specific fine-tuning. We propose NCode, a unified numerical encoding scheme that translates diverse AutoML design choices into universal discrete sequences and effectively reframes the optimization process as a unified ranking task. Trained solely on optimization trajectories derived from a limited, single-domain vision task, the proposed LLM-based searcher demonstrates impressive cross-task, cross-modality and joint search-space optimization generalizability, achieving state-of-the-art performance across image classification, image segmentation, speaker verification and text-to-image benchmarks. Crucially, it exhibits superior scalability in the large-scale training and joint optimization of a billion-parameter Diffusion Transformer, requiring 896 GPU-days and attaining a state-of-the-art GenEval score of 0.91. By leveraging reinforcement learning to instill Chain-of-Thought reasoning, we provide a systematic way to interpret the LLM-based searcher's internal decision-making mechanisms, revealing its optimization strategy shifted from the early-stage diversity-driven exploration to the late-stage anchor exploitation. Our proposed framework provides a scalable, generalizable, and interpretable pathway toward general-purpose open-world machine learning optimization.
  </details>

- **[Fairness in Explainable AI Narratives: Decomposing Social Disparities in LLM-Generated Explanations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12592)**  `arXiv:2611.12592`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wannes Van den Bulck, Sofie Goethals, David Martens_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to translate technical explainable AI (XAI) artefacts such as SHAP into explanatory narratives (XAINs) for lay users. In high-risk domains, these explanations are often the main resource through which individuals can understand and contest adverse decisions. Yet LLMs are known to reproduce social bias, even from proxy variables alone, and fairness in XAINs remains unstudied. Because XAINs are bound to a ground-truth artefact, fairness concerns not only what a narrative says and how, but also whether it is equally faithful across social groups. We introduce fairness in XAINs as a new field of research and propose a framework that generates narratives under three conditions (protected attribute disclosed, withheld, or counterfactually swapped), allowing five comparisons that pinpoint the cause of disparities. We apply it to adversely classified instances from four datasets in credit scoring, education and employment, generating narratives with five LLMs, and evaluate faithfulness, feature coverage and sentiment. We find significant disparities across all datasets, metric families and models, arising in two ways. Faithfulness disparities result from feature values: LLMs make plausible but false statements, triggered by values that are unequally distributed across social groups. Feature coverage and sentiment disparities stem from the protected attribute itself: once it is disclosed, LLMs give less explanation to, or frame more negatively the narratives of, groups that have historically been discriminated against or are stereotypically associated with worse outcomes. A more faithful model removes the faithfulness disparities, and withholding protected attributes mostly removes the others, but neither suffices on its own. Fairness in XAINs should therefore be audited separately from the predictive model and its XAI artefact.
  </details>

- **[Language Defaults Before Content: A Causal Layer Map of Multilingual Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12538)**  `arXiv:2611.12538`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shenbin Qian, Yves Scherrer_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual generation requires large language models (LLMs) to determine both what to say and in what language to say it, yet these two commitments are difficult to disentangle from model outputs alone. We use translation as a controlled probe: for multi-parallel segments that a model translates well in one language direction but poorly in another, content is held fixed while language varies. Using activation patching, we write residual-stream states from successful runs into failing ones and compare content-matched states with content-independent controls, allowing us to trace content-specific and language-related signals across layers. Across three LLMs of different scales and architectures, we find a consistent three-stage pattern of multilingual generation. Intermediate layers first express a default English signal; content information becomes causally available one layer later; and the final target language is resolved only at later layers. The ordering of the English default before content also appears in a reading-comprehension task. Our sparse autoencoder analyses reveal a further dissociation: the intermediate language signal is only weakly represented by sparse features, whereas a later language distinction is clearly sparse, but resists intervention at the patched position. These results provide a mechanistic account of how language selection and content information emerge and interact during multilingual generation.
  </details>

- **[Detecting AI-Authored Text in Authentic Student Writing: Evidence from PISA 2025](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12487)**  `arXiv:2611.12487`  `cs.CL`  
  _Nils-Jonathan Schaller, Thorben Jansen, Olaf Köller, Andrea Horbach_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-text detectors reach near-perfect scores in evaluations that contrast fully human-written texts with texts generated from researcher-designed prompts. In authentic student writing, however, AI use is voluntary and often partial. We study this setting with the PISA 2025 FLA dataset, in which 15-year-olds in Germany wrote English argumentative essays with and without access to a gpt-4o-mini-based chatbot. Keystroke, clipboard and chat logs let us trace every character of the final essay to its origin. From this provenance, we derive text- and sentence-level labels for human-written, AI-generated and mixed text, and for text written after chatbot use without traceable AI material. We evaluate a feature-based detector and a classifier on frozen Llama-3.1-8B embeddings, both adapted from top-ranked shared-task systems. Both separate AI from human text reliably (macro-F1 up to 0.96 for texts and 0.95 for sentences), independently of spelling errors. The feature-based detector, however, relies on lexical complexity, which a simple student-style rewriting prompt removes (AI recall 0.92 $\to$ 0.60 with gpt-4o-mini, 0.41 with Qwen3). The embedding-based detector stays robust for whole texts (AI recall $\geq$ 0.94) but not for single sentences (AI recall as low as 0.59). Mixed text falls between the two classes, and text without traceable AI material is classified as human-written in about 95\% of texts.
  </details>

- **[Path-Level Structural and Semantic Alignment for Hierarchical Multi-Label Text Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12420)**  `arXiv:2611.12420`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xindi Wang, Aifen Xiong, Baiyang Wang, Xuri Ge, Robert Mercer, Frank Rudzicz, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical multi-label text classification (HMTC) requires models to capture both structural dependencies among labels and the fine-grained semantics of labels at different hierarchy levels. Existing methods often model hierarchy through prediction constraints or representations of individual labels and local relations. Structural and semantic supervision, however, are usually formulated separately. We instead use complete root-to-node label paths as a shared unit for both. Our framework introduces two auxiliary training objectives while retaining a standard multi-label classifier at inference. PathSimNCE aligns document representations with hierarchical paths using hierarchy-aware contrastive targets. These targets provide graded supervision for structurally related paths while preserving explicit supervision for the ground-truth paths. The complementary semantic objective uses LLM-generated, document-conditioned descriptions of the same positive paths. It thereby provides context-specific label semantics beyond static label representations. Experiments on three benchmarks show consistent improvements: structural alignment is particularly beneficial in deeper hierarchies, while semantic alignment provides additional gains for fine-grained labels.
  </details>

- **[BDBBQ: A Human-AI Collaborative Benchmark for Bengali Social Bias in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12407)**  `arXiv:2611.12407`  `cs.CL`  
  _Provat Saha, Ashika Habib Khan, Tahseen Tanha, Hasin Ishrak, Muhammad Iqbal Hossain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite growing attention to social bias in large language models (LLMs), existing bias benchmarks provide limited coverage of culturally grounded evaluation in Bengali, particularly within Bangladesh's sociocultural context. To address this gap, we develop a bilingual extension of BanglaBBQ comprising 61,312 question-answering instances across 12 social categories in English and Bengali. The benchmark incorporates balanced answer permutations, contrasting question framings, and both ambiguous and disambiguated contexts to systematically examine model performance and stereotype-aligned bias across languages. We validate the dataset through structural checks, 220 statistical tests, and expert annotation of 150 instances by four annotators, achieving strong inter-annotator agreement (Fleiss' \k{appa}=0.8349). We evaluate eight LLMs across 490,496 instances and identify five key findings. First, disambiguated contexts yield substantially greater accuracy improvements in Bengali (+36.59 percentage points) than in English (+17.71). Second, all evaluated models exhibit lower accuracy in Bengali, although Mistral-Nemo shows the smallest cross-lingual accuracy gap (5.12 percentage points). Third, stereotype-aligned bias exhibits language and model dependent variation, increasing in Bengali for Gemma 2 9B but decreasing for Mistral-Nemo. Fourth, higher accuracy does not necessarily correspond to lower stereotype-aligned bias. Finally, bias varies considerably across social categories, with Qwen 2.5 7B exhibiting stronger stereotype alignment in Bengali for Politics and Physical Appearance. These findings demonstrate that cross-lingual performance and social bias are distinct dimensions of LLM behavior and underscore the importance of culturally grounded, language-sensitive benchmarks for evaluating fairness beyond English-centric settings.
  </details>

- **[More Than Just Style: Benchmarking Personality Influence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12392)**  `arXiv:2611.12392`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuhao Liu, Mengyu Wang, Xiao Chen, Lu Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In addition to the effects of explicit input prompts and instructions, large language model outputs are influenced by underlying behavioral preferences similar to human personality tendencies. These tendencies can affect how models interpret situations and make decisions. However, their influence is difficult to disentangle from the effects of model capabilities and instructions. Therefore, we introduce SPACE, a benchmark framework combining a controlled evaluation protocol with complementary datasets for personality verification, capability assessment, and hierarchical situational tasks. Controlled comparisons across guidance conditions help distinguish personality influence from measured capability changes and immediate instruction effects. Experimental results demonstrate that although prompting can shift generation style, models' internal personality tendencies are persistent and not readily overridden by opposing instructions. Furthermore, these underlying tendencies have an observable influence on LLM task performance, highlighting the need for greater attention to these characteristics and potential applications. Training code and SPACE benchmark specifications are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SadaqahStories: A Verified English-Arabic Corpus of Charity Stories for Children, with an Audit of Errors in AI-Drafted Religious Narratives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12078)**  `arXiv:2611.12078`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wajdi Zaghouani, Spencer Striker_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present SadaqahStories, a parallel English-Arabic corpus of 41 illustrated four-page stories about charitable giving in Islam (Sadaqah, Zakat and Waqf), written for readers aged 7 to 18. The stories were first drafted with large language model (LLM) assistance, then audited against hadith collections, Qur'an references and historical sources, and finally rewritten for children and signed off by an Islamic scholar. Every story carries an evidence label and links to its sources. We release all three versions of each story (AI-assisted draft, audited text, final child-oriented text) together with the correction log, so the corpus can be used to study how AI drafts of religious and historical content go wrong. The audit found substantive errors in 39 of the 41 drafts. The most frequent were unsupported specific details (dates, figures, prices, names), invented quotations, institutions or anecdotes, and conflation of separate events or people; six drafts contained errors in the wording, grading or ruling of a religious text. Rewriting cut the estimated Flesch-Kincaid grade of the English text from 14.0 to 6.1. We also release an 81-entry charity lexicon covering Modern Standard Arabic and Qatari Arabic, and describe the free, account-free website that serves the corpus (this https URL).
  </details>

- **[MIRIX-2.0: Multi-Agent Memory System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12068)**  `arXiv:2611.12068`  `cs.CL`  
  _Wei-Chieh Huang, Weizhi Zhang, Jiayan Nan, Peng Xia, Yu Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-term memory lets LLM agents personalize, stay consistent, and reuse what worked before. Parametric memories need training, consolidation can lose details for good, and keeping every record leaves a growing store to search. We present MIRIX-2.0, a multi-agent memory system designed to be practical, accurate, and efficient at once. It is the most accurate of the compared systems on four of five QA benchmarks. It reaches 91.4% on LoCoMo and 72.6% on LongMemEval-S, where it leads the strongest baseline by 14.3 points, and with AutoDream disabled it gains 31.0 and 22.0 points over HyperMem on single-hop and multi-hop document QA. As procedural memory, it yields the highest mean success among the compared memory methods on ALFWorld and WebShop and reaches 49.2% on MetaClaw-Bench, against 44.0% for MetaClaw's skill library. Six typed memory agents keep complete records as the system of record, and a provenance hypergraph indexes episodic and semantic records with n-ary frames that cite their rows. Retrieval runs under explicit budgets, so the evidence a question inspects does not grow with the history. AutoDream merges duplicate anchors outside the write path without rewriting any record, and it distills task sessions into skills. With AutoDream disabled, MIRIX-2.0 answers faster than HyperMem on all five timed benchmarks. It needs no fine-tuning, runs with closed and open models, and its code is public.
  </details>

- **[SMO: Compiling Natural Language Skills into Graphs of Reusable State-Move-Outcome Bricks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12041)**  `arXiv:2611.12041`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chao Chen, Jiashuo Zhang, Yunhang Shen, Liangyu Lian, Rongrong Ji_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents increasingly rely on reusable natural-language skills, yet growing skill collections remain accumulations of isolated text artifacts that are difficult to compare, deduplicate, and compose. We argue that the reusable content of a skill lies not in its surface wording but in its procedural transitions: which operation to execute under a given state and which observable outcome to produce. Based on this observation, we propose State-Move-Outcome (SMO), a compilation-like framework that decomposes raw skills into reusable procedural bricks and organizes admitted bricks as an externalized symbolic structure. When compiling a skill, SMO first retrieves and composes existing bricks, dynamically connects them through Outcome-to-State conditions, and creates a candidate brick only for an uncovered procedure; the candidate becomes reusable only after validation and admission. Experiments on SkillsBench v1.1 demonstrate that SMO represents 232 rawSkills from 87 underlying tasks with 95 shared bricks, reducing the corresponding skill representations from 366,472 to 149,154 tokens--a $\mathbf{59.3}\times$ reduction and $\mathbf{2.46}\times$ compression. Across 232 skill-conditioned evaluation instances, SMO raises the average task score from $\mathbf{0.6379}$ to $\mathbf{0.7478}$ and increases 5/5 Pass instances from $\mathbf{138}$ to $\mathbf{164}$, while matching or outperforming rawSkill on $\mathbf{208}$ of $\mathbf{232}$ instances. It also reduces average runtime token consumption by $\mathbf{38.38\%}$. These results show that compiling natural-language skills into shared procedural units can jointly improve skill reuse, representation efficiency, and downstream execution.
  </details>

- **[ShopMind: A Mental World Model for Personalized and Realistic E-commerce User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11897)**  `arXiv:2611.11897`  `cs.CL`  
  _Peixuan Hou, Bin Chen, Li He, Jian Xu, Xiuli Ma, Guojie Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are widely used in e-commerce user simulation, providing a foundation for understanding users and predicting human decisions. However, existing simulators still struggle to achieve both personalization and behavioral realism. Most methods predict the next action directly from interaction histories and the current context, without accounting for the evolving mental world that underlies user behavior. Theory of Mind suggests that behavior is driven by mental states, including beliefs, uncertainties, and intentions. Users may interpret the same information in distinct ways, leading to divergent behaviors, while an individual's mental state evolves through interaction and shapes subsequent actions. Ignoring this process prevents simulators from faithfully capturing individual differences and realistic behavior patterns, and undermines its effectiveness in downstream applications. To address this, we propose ShopMind, a Mental World Model for e-commerce user simulation that captures how consumers interpret shopping information and how their mental states evolve, better reflecting individual users and real-world behavior patterns. We further use behavior-mental state pairs synthesized by ShopMind to train a Mental State Reconstruction (MSR) model, which infers consumers' latent mental states from observable behavior. Experiments show that ShopMind improves personalization by 18.0% and behavioral realism by 19.6% over the strongest alternatives. Moreover, MSR improves performance on consumption value measurement and CTR prediction, showing the utility of ShopMind for understanding and predicting real user behavior.
  </details>

- **[BridgeKV: Large-Model Prefill and Small-Model Decoding for LLM Inference via KV Cache Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11761)**  `arXiv:2611.11761`  `cs.CL`  
  _Payman Behnam, Debopam Sanyal, Pranav Ponnusamy, Kusum Meda Ravi, Pranav Sastry, Mani Chandana Bandaru, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM inference has two phases with different bottlenecks: LLM prefill runs once per request and is compute-intensive. Decoding repeats for every output token and is limited by memory bandwidth. To address the challenges associated with this issue, different solutions have been proposed. All of these solutions use the same model for both prefill and decode phases. We propose BridgeKV, which uses a larger model for prefill and a smaller model of the same family for decoding. A lightweight KV-stitching module translates the larger model's prompt cache into the smaller model's cache layout. It handles differences in cache shape and representation through learned local layer mixing and separate nonlinear maps for keys and values. With both models frozen, we train the module to align their caches and make the smaller model follow the larger model's next-token predictions. A second training stage adds low-rank adapters when further task adaptation is needed. Across six model pairs and five datasets, BridgeKV outperforms fine-tuned small-model baselines with matched trainable parameter budgets in all 30 settings. It closes up to 80% of the quality gap to using the larger model for both phases. On one A40 GPU, Llama-3.1-Instruct-8B to Llama-3.2-Instruct-1B achieves 3.0X faster requests than the larger model alone, with latency close to the smaller model alone. BridgeKV can improve the hardware and system solutions that separate prefill and decode phases across GPUs. A separate serving simulation models request arrivals, queues, and cache transfer. For Llama-3.1-Instruct-70B to Llama-3.2-Instruct-1B, it predicts up to 33.86X faster requests and 27.5X lower energy use than using the larger model for both prefill and decode phases.
  </details>

- **[Out of Parameters, Saved by Syntax: UD-PrMPT for Parser Augmented Low Resource Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11597)**  `arXiv:2611.11597`  `cs.CL`  
  _Emma Thronson, Abhishek Purushothama, Amir Zeldes_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work advancing machine translation quality using LLMs for very low resource languages has relied on the injection of lexicon-based prompt components to expose vocabulary to models. However, these approaches run into a ceiling when models must guess sentence meaning purely from plausible combinations of possible word senses mapped to strings. In this paper we test UD-PrMPT, an approach using syntactic analyses in the Universal Dependencies formalism as prompt components to improve MT in five diverse low resource languages: Beja, Chintang, Chukchi, Coptic, and Xibe. Our results show additive improvements which stack on top of the lexicon based approach which are stronger for higher quality manual analyses but consistent for predicted parses as well.
  </details>

- **[THEATRON: A Benchmark for Evaluating Persona Persistence in Long-Horizon LLM Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11487)**  `arXiv:2611.11487`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuyan Wang, Yuechen Jiang, Zhiwei Liu, Jimin Huang, Sophia Ananiadou_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing evaluations often test persona expression rather than whether persona information persists in extended decision-making. We introduce THEATRON, a controlled benchmark that uses financial replay to evaluate persona persistence in long-horizon sequential decision-making. In these environments, models conditioned on fixed behavioral-finance personas observe shared daily market evidence and trajectory-specific account-state feedback and make sequential decisions over multi-month trajectories. We evaluate Zero-Shot, One-Shot, and Chain-of-Thought persona injection across four LLMs, using cumulative-return and action trajectories to diagnose within-persona stability, cross-persona separability, and action collapse. Persona conditioning produces systematic behavioral shifts beyond a no-persona baseline, and persona-level separation generally exceeds the variation induced by semantically equivalent descriptions. Nevertheless, these shifts do not reliably form coherent persona-specific policies: apparently stable separation can arise from stereotyped action collapse, while diverse policies can exhibit noisy trajectory switching. These findings expose a gap between observable persona-induced behavior and persistent, context-sensitive persona-conditioned decision policies. Code and benchmark data are publicly available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[WarBias: A Bilingual LLM Benchmark of Stereotypes About Internally Displaced People and Veterans](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11284)**  `arXiv:2611.11284`  `cs.CL`  
  _Viktoriia Makovska, Tetiana Zakharchenko, Oleksandra Ostafiichuk_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper contains examples of stereotypes about veterans and displaced people that may be offensive or distressing. Social bias benchmarks for language models commonly examine categories such as gender, race, and religion, often drawn from lists of legally protected characteristics. Veterans and internally displaced people (IDPs) fall outside these lists, and stereotypes about them, which can undermine access to employment, housing, and social participation, remain largely absent from language model evaluation. We introduce WarBias, a Ukrainian--English benchmark of stereotypes about these two groups. Built from a taxonomy of 120 stereotype claims grounded in research and monitoring reports, WarBias contains 1,007 stereotype--counter-stereotype--unrelated sentence triplets and 240 multiple-choice questions per language. A Ukrainian language model selected in a blinded human pilot generated the Ukrainian triplets. At least two independent annotators reviewed each triplet in the annotation cohort. The authors revised items in response to the reviews, and the final release was machine-translated into English. Each claim carries a human severity rating. In a baseline evaluation of six models, four prefer stereotypical Ukrainian sentences about at least one group, most strongly about IDPs, while English scores are mostly balanced. WarBias supports complementary evaluations of stereotypical preferences and context-sensitive judgments, and provides paired forget/retain material for unlearning-based bias mitigation. We document the benchmark's construction, intended uses, and limitations, and offer its construction method as a template for analogous benchmarks in other conflict-affected societies.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Hard Labels: LLM Probabilities Improve Human-Assisted Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11111)**  `arXiv:2611.11111`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xinyue Wang, Qunhua Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for text annotation, but their outputs are often reduced to a single categorical label before downstream analysis. This hardening step discards the model's relative support across categories, which may contain useful information when category boundaries are ambiguous. We study whether retaining the full probability vector improves downstream regression when LLM annotations are combined with a limited amount of human annotation. Using matched soft and hard representations from three LLMs across three social-science text datasets, we compare direct regression and human-assisted correction. Soft inputs are not uniformly better when used directly as outcomes, but after human correction, using the probability vector consistently improves regression efficiency across models and datasets. Analyses of repeated human judgments and controlled simulations further show that these gains arise when probability variation beyond the hard category carries useful information about the human-coded outcome. Our results identify output representation as an important design choice in LLM-assisted measurement and support preserving probability outputs for downstream statistical analysis. Code and reproducibility materials are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[When Safety Becomes An Outlier: Understanding the Retention of LLM Safety Behaviors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10971)**  `arXiv:2611.10971`  `cs.CL`  
  _Binchi Zhang, Hadi Abdullah, Yiwei Cai, Jundong Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety-aligned large language models often lose their safety behaviors after fine-tuning, even when safety data are included. While this fragility is well documented, its underlying cause remains unclear. In this paper, we propose that formulaic refusal strings can function as outlier modes: narrow, weakly grounded response patterns that are easy to fit but vulnerable to subsequent updates. Controlled experiments support this account: fixed refusals are acquired much like arbitrary constant strings, in contrast to prompt-grounded natural responses. Motivated by these findings, we introduce Naturalness-Enhanced Safety Targets (NEST), a safety alignment framework that improves safety retention by constructing natural, context-aware safety targets that keep safety responses within the model's existing output distribution and grounded in semantics. We instantiate NEST in both supervised and preference-based alignment settings, using perplexity under the original model as a proxy for distributional support. Across multiple models and alignment regimes, natural safety targets improve both immediate safety and its retention under continued fine-tuning without sacrificing helpfulness. These findings suggest a practical principle for more durable safety alignment: construct safety targets that draw on the model's existing language capabilities and remain grounded in the meaning of each request.
  </details>

- **[Pole or Vector? Encoders Capture Bangla Compound Verbs, but LLMs Read Them Literally](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10909)**  `arXiv:2611.10909`  `cs.CL`  
  _Md. Asaduzzaman Shuvo, Md Mehrab Karim Antor, Md. Adnanul Islam, Shahanur Rahman Bappy, Sajid Ahamed, Mahfuz Hasan Reza, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In Bangla, a main verb is often followed by one of a small set of vector verbs. These verbs lose their own meaning and add a shade of meaning instead, such as completion. For example, likhe phello means that someone finished writing, not that they wrote and threw something. The same two verbs can also describe two separate events, so only the context shows which meaning is meant. This makes compound verbs a hard test of how language models handle multiword expressions. We present BanglaCV-Probe, a benchmark of 17 vector verbs with 1,044 minimal pairs, 204 entailment items and 197 pairs of correct and incorrect combinations, all checked by native speakers. We test 26 models (encoders, LLMs and translation systems) on four skills: telling the two meanings apart internally, understanding what a vector adds, preferring correct combinations and keeping the vector's meaning in translation. Probes on five encoders tell the two meanings apart and still reach up to 93% accuracy when trained on pairs without surface cues. Prompted models rarely use this knowledge. Entailment accuracy stays between 59% and 81%. Several LLMs answer Yes or No to almost every item, and nine of ten models miss that someone who ate up the rice left none. Most instruction-tuned LLMs translate vectors literally in 15-20% of vector sentences and in up to 55% for mora (die). On correct combinations, a small Bangla-only model (88%) matches BLOOM-7B1 (87%) and both beat Qwen2.5-7B (74%). Data and code:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Global Context Guidance for Diffusion Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10577)**  `arXiv:2611.10577`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jeimin Jeon, Bumsub Ham_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (LLMs) have recently emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive LLMs, enabling parallel generation through iterative denoising. Recent guidance methods improve diffusion LLMs by introducing a weak model to guide the prediction of the base model during denoising. However, the influence of the weak model during denoising remains less clear, and computing its prediction requires an additional forward pass at each denoising step. We present in this paper an in-depth analysis of how diffusion LLMs use contextual information during denoising. Our analysis reveals that diffusion LLMs rely more heavily on local context while often underutilizing global context. Based on this observation, we introduce Global Context Guidance (GCG), a feature-space guidance method that enhances global-context utilization without constructing an explicit weak model. Extensive experiments on LLaDA-8B, Dream-7B, and LLaDA-1.5 across diverse benchmarks demonstrate that GCG consistently outperforms existing guidance methods, while incurring much lower inference overhead.
  </details>

- **[YRC-Bench: Benchmarking Culturally Aware Machine Translation for Historical Heritage via Tri-Level Semantic Bridging](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10555)**  `arXiv:2611.10555`  `cs.CL`  
  _Huang Yan, Aobo Li, Jiayi Wang, Huidong Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Translating historical cultural heritage poses profound challenges for machine translation systems due to the acute semantic asymmetry across eras and cultures, where terse classical syntax frequently obscures domain-specific cultural knowledge. While Large Language Models have demonstrated remarkable prowess in generic translation benchmarks, they struggle to negotiate Culturally-Specific Items and diachronic shifts in domain-rich historical texts, such as Yellow River cultural records. To bridge this critical gap, we introduce YRC-Bench, the first human-curated, multi-parallel benchmark dedicated to historical heritage translation across Classical Chinese, Modern Chinese, and nine target foreign languages. YRC-Bench establishes a tri-level parallel corpus incorporating modern explanatory glosses as explicit semantic bridges and operationalizes translation strategies through paired conserved versus substituted CSI variants categorized by cultural translatability. Systematic evaluations of nine leading LLMs across bidirectional translation directions reveal that leveraging modern Chinese glosses - especially human-curated ones - dramatically elevates both lexical fidelity and semantic adequacy. Furthermore, multidimensional human preference audits by native experts unveil distinct cross-cultural preferences between Sinographic-tradition and non-Sinographic language groups.
  </details>

- **[Distinguishing Extraction Failures and Clinical Discordance with LLM Uncertainty on Radiology Reports](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10502)**  `arXiv:2611.10502`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jerjes Aguirre-Chavez, Shant Malkasian, Roshun Sankaran, Brendan Crabb, Albert Hsiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can structure clinical datasets, but extraction error must be distinguished from discordance in the underlying data. From a real-world cohort of 1{,}045 patients undergoing coronary CT angiography (CCTA), we evaluated whether LLM uncertainty during vessel-level coronary artery disease (CAD) stenosis extraction could identify extraction failure and clinical discordance. References were expert CCTA annotations (253 patients) and subsequent invasive coronary angiography (ICA; 729 patients). We quantify extraction failure as disagreement between stenosis severity in LLM extracted versus expert annotations, and clinical discordance as disagreement in stenosis severity between CCTA and subsequent ICA. Stenosis Margin, selected from ten candidate measures, was used to quantify LLM uncertainty, and generalized estimating equation logistic regression and AUC evaluated its association and incremental discrimination for each failure mode. LLM uncertainty was strongly associated with extraction failure (adjusted OR 4.15 [95% CI 2.57--6.69], $P<0.001$) and substantially improved discrimination beyond LLM-extracted CCTA stenosis severity alone (AUC 0.81 to 0.86; $\Delta$AUC $+0.06$ [$+0.03$, $+0.09$], $P=0.001$). In contrast, LLM uncertainty weakly associated with clinical discordance (adjusted OR 1.22 [1.04--1.43], Holm-adjusted $P=0.118$) but provided no incremental discrimination beyond LLM-extracted CCTA stenosis severity ($\Delta$AUC $+0.01$ [$-0.00$, $+0.02$], $P=0.245$). Moreover, extraction errors and clinical discordance showed little overlap, with 38 of 42 clinically discordant vessels (90%) having been extracted correctly. LLM uncertainty may identify extraction failures without identifying underlying clinical discordance, supporting failure attribution when evaluating LLM-derived clinical datasets.
  </details>

- **[LongCapHarness: Capability-Guided Auto-Harnessing for Long-Context Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10463)**  `arXiv:2611.10463`  `cs.CL`  
  _Tao Zhang, Yuhang He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing methods enhancing an LLM's long-context reasoning capability often rely on data synthesis and follow-up LLM post-training. However, the data synthesis strategy and the post-training recipes are empirically driven and computationally intensive. In this work, we propose a new framework, dubbed LongCapHarness, to instead harness the underlying capabilities for long-context reasoning.
