The harnessing process recursively revises and updates capability primitive implementation, capability state and control logic through recursive self-improvement on the development set. With neither data synthesis nor LLM post-training, LongCapHarness is applied to Llama3-8B and transferred to Qwen2.5-3B. LongCapHarness substantially outperforms recent data-synthesis-based baselines on HELMET and RULER benchmarks; its estimated development cost is less than 50% of the estimated training costs of NExtLong and PolicyLong. We hope these results motivate further research into harnessing the latent capabilities of LLMs to solve tasks beyond their explicit training.
  </details>

- **[Policy Over Purpose: Auditing LLM Content Moderation of Reclaimed Slur Usage](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10327)**  `arXiv:2611.10327`  `cs.CL`  
  _Christina Chance, Kai-Wei Chang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As Perspective API is deprecated, platforms are turning to large language models for content moderation, encoding policy as natural language that the model must interpret at inference time. Reclaimed slurs test this design directly. The same term can express in-group pride or target the community that reclaimed it, and misreading it silences the speech moderation is meant to protect. We audit four general-purpose LLMs and four moderation-specific systems, with Perspective API as a legacy baseline, on IYKYK, a corpus of reclaimed and derogatory usage of three slurs annotated by community members. Across the policy, decision, reasoning, disparity, and steering layers of this audit, we find that whether a system was purpose-built for moderation explains little about how it handles reclaimed language. No system reliably agrees with community judgment, and all fail most on language reclaimed within Black communities. What separates systems is instead how their policy is represented. Only systems whose policy is expressed in the prompt can be steered at all, and even then, explicit definitions change how models justify a decision more than how they make it. We argue that policy transparency determines whether moderation failures can be corrected, but that correcting them requires contextual judgment that definitions supplied at inference time cannot provide.
  </details>

- **[Prove What You Can: Hybrid Neurosymbolic Reasoning for Logical Natural Language Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10319)**  `arXiv:2611.10319`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ada Canoğlu, Sadiq Qara_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Logical reasoning over natural language requires both semantic interpretation and deductive search. Large language models (LLMs) can propose plausible intermediate derivations, but they often fail to compose them into a complete proof and overpredict uncertainty. Neurosymbolic methods improve search by translating an entire problem into first-order logic and handing it to a prover, but a single bad clause can make the whole translation nonexecutable. We present Hybrid Reasoning, a three-stage pipeline that decouples proposing a proof, validating its steps, and deciding a label. An LLM proposes a structured proof tree with a raw verdict; a theorem prover validates each derivation locally, keeping only the subset it can prove; and an uncertainty-gated repair attempts to re-derive the label from the premises plus the validated derivations, when the raw verdict is UNCERTAIN. Across four benchmarks (FOLIO, ProofWriter, ProverQA, PrOntoQA) and five contemporary LLMs, Hybrid Reasoning is the top-performing method on the two hardest datasets, improving on the strongest baseline by 5.3 points on FOLIO and 4.9 on ProverQA, and reaches ceiling-level accuracy on the easiest. The repair stage operates at 78-98% precision, producing 326 correct relabellings against 19 regressions.
  </details>

- **[I play of the cello: Evaluating Constructional Bleeding in Multilingual LLMs with CALQUE](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10266)**  `arXiv:2611.10266`  `cs.CL`  
  _Toshiki Nakai, Yu-Yin Hsu, Marie-Catherine de Marneffe, Joakim Nivre, Jakob Prange, Nathan Schneider, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Languages use different morphosyntactic strategies to express the same meaning. Speakers of multiple languages are able to apply strategies from one language to another, either by mistake or intentionally, to produce a literal translation, or calque. Considering that large language models (LLMs) have been shown to generalize crosslingually, despite being predominantly trained on English, we investigate whether they overgeneralize constructional strategies from English to other languages. To test this, we create a dataset of 5583 sentence pairs across seven languages, matching literal translations of strategies from English into other languages, and literal translations of the other languages' strategies into English. We then quantify constructional bleeding by measuring how often strategies from English are assigned a higher probability than native strategies. We find that models often fail to assign higher probabilities to native strategies and tend to prefer English-derived strategies even when evaluating sentences in other languages. Our findings highlight the limitations of the current English-dominant training paradigm.
  </details>

- **[A Model Organism for Studying Unverbalized Evaluation Awareness](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09945)**  `arXiv:2611.09945`  `cs.CL`  
  _Daniel Donnelly, Evan Chapple, Haritz Puerto, Sahar Abdelnabi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models can recognize when they are being evaluated and change their behavior accordingly, undermining the reliability of safety benchmarks. Evaluation awareness (EA) is usually measured by having an LLM judge scan reasoning traces for explicit statements of recognition, but this signal is increasingly unreliable. Recent frontier-model system cards report a drop in verbalized rates between releases, which is indistinguishable from awareness that has gone unverbalized. Interpretability methods could detect such unverbalized evaluation awareness (UEA) in individual interactions, but lack instance-level ground truth. Asking the model alters the input, and dataset labels do not reveal whether it recognized a particular prompt as an evaluation. We address this gap with a model organism. Using synthetic document fine-tuning, SFT, and DPO, we train Qwen3.5-27B to emit a marker (a designated token) in its final response when it recognizes an evaluation, while suppressing the marker and verbalized awareness in its reasoning. The marker largely tracks the evaluation concept as it can be strongly modulated by evaluation framing in the system prompt. When not told whether it is being evaluated, the organism recognizes evaluations from test-question cues, mirroring what its base model often verbalizes. Using the marker as a label, we benchmark linear probes, the Jacobian Lens, and Activation Oracles, built on the base model and blind to the marker. None recovers the label reliably. These results suggest that current interpretability methods are not yet reliable UEA detectors, even when recognition relies on simple cues. Our model organism offers a testbed for improving them.
  </details>

- **[BIBIMBAP: A Benchmark for Instructional BIM-Based Automated Programming](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09933)**  `arXiv:2611.09933`  `cs.CL`  
  _Clemens Kujat, Bharathi Kannan Nithyanantham, Stefan Telgmann, Tobias Sesterhenn, Joern Ploennigs, Stefan Lüdtke, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are emerging as natural-language interfaces for Building Information Modeling (BIM), successfully achieving information retrieval, but no benchmark exists to evaluate their ability to edit Industry Foundation Classes (IFC). We introduce BIBIMBAP, a Text-to-BIM benchmark with 100 curated tasks covering Create-Read-Update-Delete (CRUD) operations across spatial, geometric, topological, numeric, and conceptual categories. Each task includes a natural-language prompt, an IFC model, expected structured outputs, and test scripts for automated evaluation. Baseline results show limited performance (best: 50.2%) and frequent failures in constraint preservation and spatial reasoning, with some correct outputs achieved despite flawed reasoning.
  </details>

- **[When Does Better Confidence Improve a Knowledge-Graph Audit?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.05877)**  `arXiv:2611.05877`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zehao Lin, Kai Chen, Xixuan Hao, Erenkai Zhu, Chunyu Li, Helan Hu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Confidence estimates guide human review in LLM-built knowledge graphs, but an erroneous shared entity need not be the only reason an incident fact is wrong. We study how this distinction affects budgeted audits, alongside selection headroom and label validity. Modelling non-adaptive auditing as budgeted covering, we characterise the minimal decision-relevant error statistics and bound the regret of scalar-preserving first-order planning by a structural mass $\Delta^{\mathrm{cl}}$. We simulate ideal repair against benchmark-aligned labels for five extraction configurations and two cross-document graphs on Re-DocRED, followed by frozen confirmation on test and replication on 500 hash-selected training documents. An error-rate $\times$ fan-out baseline leaves 3.98-4.74 percentage points more errors than joint planning with the same actions. Independent-block planning also avoids this disadvantage: its held-out point estimates improve on a fact-only scalar baseline by 1.22-1.79 points and are slightly better than the joint model, although the frozen equivalence criterion remains inconclusive. On 300 DWIE training documents, independent and joint plans also outperform fan-out in point estimates, with extractor-dependent gains over fact-only review. For verification, a generic support check improves three of four model-budget configurations on each held-out set. These comparisons motivate evaluating complete-repair probabilities and retaining both fact-only and factorised baselines when choosing an audit model.
  </details>

- **[HEARSAYBENCH: Can AI Navigate from Abstract Human Rights to Lived Lives?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.39062)**  `arXiv:2610.39062`  `cs.CL`  
  _Ava Iranmanesh, Sobhan Lotfi, Ali Iranmanesh, Liwei Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) reach users worldwide, personal counseling has emerged as one of their most consequential applications, sometimes in life-critical situations. Yet broader access does not mean equal support. The text these models learn from disproportionately reflects people with the freedom to act, embedding an implicit assumption that users can leave, report, seek help, or appeal. Existing benchmarks largely inherit this assumption, overlooking users whose agency is constrained by structural conditions such as war, statelessness, and displacement. Meanwhile, models are increasingly shaped by normative frameworks such as Constitutional AI, often grounded in ideals like human rights, even as many users live where those rights offer little protection or recourse.We introduce HEARSAYBENCH, a human-authored dataset of 400 test cases with in-region human verification grounded in scenarios where structural barriers stand between a person and the exercise of their rights. Built from United Nations and other human rights reports, the scenarios span over 80 regions and cover social, environmental, and personal constraints. Drawing on Nobel laureate Amartya Sen's Capabilities Approach, HEARSAYBENCH tests whether models can distinguish what a person is formally entitled to do from what they are actually able to do. Across 14 proprietary and open-weight models, every model infers a user's situation more accurately than it advises on it. Stronger models narrow this gap, but their recommendations remain unsafe to follow. Models may understand lives shaped by constraint, yet that knowledge remains hearsay if it cannot translate into advice people can act on. Those people are not alignment's long tail; they are among the users for whom good guidance matters most. HEARSAYBENCH makes this failure measurable, a step toward systems that serve the world as it is rather than as assumed.
  </details>

- **[Multi-author style detection with stylistic embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24184)**  `arXiv:2610.24184`  `cs.CL`  
  _Man-Ling Sung, Chak Fai Li, Damianos Karakos, Fred Bernardin, Brian Ulicny_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We investigate the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) in multi-author style change detection. Using off-the-shelf LLMs or supervised fine-tuning solely within the target domain fails to yield effective features; for this reason, we propose cross-domain transfer learning with LLM-based embeddings fine-tuned for authorship verification. We fine-tune Mistral-7B embedding model and compare it with DeBERTa, used in the state-of-the-art (SOTA) systems in PAN-25 multi-author writing style analysis challenge. Results show that DeBERTa and Mistral excel in different user-generated text scenarios. We exploit this diversity by combining both embeddings, leading to more effective style change detection.
  </details>

- **[Measuring Collapse and Correction in Homogeneous-Panel LLM Debate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.35279)**  `arXiv:2609.35279`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xin Li, Mengbing Liu, Chau Yuen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent large language model (LLM) debate is often evaluated by whether final answers improve, but a changed answer is not necessarily a better one: the same discussion can correct an initially wrong majority or collapse an initially correct one. Standard final-accuracy evaluations conflate these opposing transitions. We introduce an auditable protocol for homogeneous debate on multiple-choice questions (MCQs) that records each run as a transition ledger over collapse, correction, onset, and signed intervention utility. On 6,925 MMLU-Pro debates, the protocol records 253 collapses together with the corrections that offset them, and the correction side changes how interventions should be judged. Replay experiments reveal the central tradeoff: freezing every debate prevents 251 collapses but loses 804 corrections under equal weights, and a leave-one-model-out probe-gated freeze that is tuned for net utility never fires, so collapse prevention alone can recommend the wrong policy. A compact pre-debate 8-probe screen is a triage signal: its unadjusted family-level association with conditional-collapse risk is high (G=7, Spearman $\rho$ = 0.893, exact two-sided p = 0.0123), but initial-majority accuracy is a close comparator ($\rho$ = -0.821; family partial $\rho$ = 0.767, p = 0.0877), so we do not treat it as calibrated or capability-adjusted prediction. Round-level traces localize many collapses to the first debate round, where early disagreement can precede both collapses and corrections. Reasoning-mode models, including one model at two reasoning-effort levels, also show both collapses and corrections. We release replayable schemas, coders, audits, cost cards, and zero-API rebuild scripts so future model rows can be compared under the same denominators and signed utility ledger.
  </details>

- **[Rewarding Novel Deductions: Solver-guided Process Supervision for Logical Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.34660)**  `arXiv:2609.34660`  `cs.CL`  
  _Muhammad Asif Ali, Wenqing Wang, Huan Wang, Mohammad Raza_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Logical reasoning remains a major challenge for large language models (LLMs), particularly on structured problems that require precise constraint tracking, consistency preservation, and multi-step deduction. This challenge is especially acute for small-scale LLMs, which are more prone to producing inconsistent, redundant, or brittle reasoning trajectories. Existing approaches for improving logical reasoning largely optimize for final-answer correctness, providing only weak supervision over the intermediate reasoning process. In this work, we propose SPRING: (Solver-guided Process Rewards for Novel LogIcal ReasoNing Step Generation). SPRING uses an SMT solver as a training-time verifier of intermediate reasoning steps to provide process-level supervision. It introduces the notion of a novel reasoning step, namely, a step that is logically valid, consistent with the evolving reasoning state, and not already implied by previously accepted non-contradictory deductions. Based on this solver-based assessment, it designs process rewards that encourage novel inferential progress while penalizing contradictory and uninformative reasoning steps. Evaluation across three logical reasoning benchmarks: ZebraLogic, AR-LSAT, and Knights and Knaves, and four LLMs shows that SPRING consistently outperforms base LLMs, outcome-only reward baselines, and Logic-LM. On ZebraLogic, SPRING improves puzzle accuracy by up to 49.71 and 15.43 points over the base LLM and strongest outcome-only baseline, respectively. On AR-LSAT, it improves overall accuracy by up to 64.43 and 11.39 percentage points over the base LLM and strongest outcome-only baseline, respectively. On Knights and Knaves, SPRING achieves up to 93.14 puzzle accuracy and 96.05 person accuracy.
  </details>

- **[ToolSearcher: Optimizing Tool Selection at Scale via Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30906)**  `arXiv:2609.30906`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhenlong Dai, Xujie Song, Zitong Wang, Tong Niu, Jian liu, Weiqiang Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) excel at natural language processing but struggle to interact with external environments. Tool learning provides a promising way to extend LLMs into actionable agents, where tool selection is a critical prerequisite for successful tool use. Existing work often assumes a small or predefined set of tools, leaving large-scale tool selection underexplored. Real-world repositories contain a vast and diverse array of tools, making it difficult for LLMs to effectively search, distinguish, and compose tools under context-length constraints. We identify large-scale tool selection as a new challenge for agentic reinforcement learning, highlighting that existing RL methods for knowledge-based question answering are inadequate for selecting tools while considering compatibility. To address this challenge, we propose ToolSearcher, a novel RL framework for effective multi-turn search and fine-grained optimization in large-scale tool selection. Specifically, we introduce category-constrained tool discrimination to improve the model's ability to distinguish functionally similar tools, event-level search modeling to explicitly optimize the discovery of target tools during multi-turn search, and trajectory-aligned credit allocation to provide fine-grained reward signals for different stages of the search-selection process. Extensive experiments on large-scale tool selection benchmarks demonstrate that ToolSearcher consistently outperforms a set of strong baselines in challenging settings involving iterative search and complex tool composition.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Assessment of Self-Consistency in LLM Explanations using Perturbation Strength](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.30849)**  `arXiv:2609.30849`  `cs.CL`  
  _Phuong Q. Le, Kemal Kurniawan, Jey Han Lau_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prior work has examined the self-consistency of LLM-generated explanations using surface-level perturbation methods. However, the strength of these perturbations is not explicitly measured and controlled. In this work, we propose an LLM-as-a-judge approach to measure perturbation strength in a unified manner across input and CoT perturbations. We then evaluate the self-consistency in explanations generated from various LLMs under controlled strength conditions, ensuring a fair comparison across perturbation types. Experiments show that our proposed LLM-based perturbation strength measure outperforms other embedding- and probability-based approaches and that input perturbations generally affect LLMs more strongly than CoT perturbations. Our work suggests that judgments about a model's self-consistency is fair only within the same perturbation type.
  </details>

- **[PACE-dLLM: Elastic Block Decoding via Confidence Cliff Estimation for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.26249)**  `arXiv:2609.26249`  `cs.CL`  
  _Xiaocheng Lu, Shuhan Guo, Ziyue Ma, Jie Zhang, Jian Liu, Jingcai Guo, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (dLLMs), such as LLaDA and Dream, have become competitive with autoregressive (AR) LLMs in generation quality while supporting native parallel decoding. A standard acceleration strategy is block-wise decoding, where each forward pass predicts a block of length B and commits high-confidence tokens. Confidence-threshold commitment adapts which tokens are accepted, but a fixed B still constrains the prediction scope. Existing horizon policies use manually chosen sizes, volatility statistics, or candidate-boundary scores. We instead use the model's own per-step confidence: in-window confidence typically follows a context-dependent cliff, providing a direct signal for adapting the look-ahead horizon. In this work, we propose PACE-dLLM, which refits this parametric cliff periodically in closed form, sets the next horizon at a shared confidence anchor, and retains confidence-threshold token commitment. Under a saturated-yield abstraction with iid, policy-independent monotone profiles, the oracle horizon is the smallest attaining maximal per-pass yield and improves the asymptotic forward-pass rate when fixed blocks undershoot it with positive probability. On four reasoning and code benchmarks across two open-source dLLM backbones, PACE-dLLM achieves the best average accuracy among the evaluated decoders on both backbones, with average throughput speedups of 5.23x on LLaDA and 3.06x on Dream (up to 8.52x on LLaDA MATH) over the unaccelerated semi-AR baseline, advancing the quality-throughput Pareto frontier.
  </details>

- **[A Hybrid Hierarchical 1D-CNN-BiLSTM Framework for Extractive Summarization of Biomedical and Clinical Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.13481)**  `arXiv:2609.13481`  `cs.CL`  
  _Saad Bin Ather, Muhammad Saif, Ali Hassan Khan, Manzer Abbas, Hajra Waheed_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have made abstractive summarization remarkably fluent, but fluency has come at a cost. Models trained to generate freely over long documents may hallucinate, producing summaries that read well but drift from what the source actually says. This risk is especially serious in biomedical and clinical text, where fabricated details can have serious consequences. We address this by removing free-form generation from the pipeline rather than trying to constrain it. Our Hybrid Hierarchical CNN-LSTM Summarizer reframes summarization as extractive sentence selection: a stacked, multi-kernel convolutional network progressively refines contextual representations, while a bidirectional LSTM models long-range dependencies across the document. A lightweight scoring head predicts sentence importance using binary cross-entropy supervision. For PubMed, a document-adaptive mean-plus-standard-deviation threshold with a Top-3 fallback determines extraction; clinical evaluations instead use a fixed top-30\% sentence-selection policy. Because output sentences are copied verbatim, the framework prevents source-external generation, although omissions and contextual inaccuracies remain possible. Evaluated on PubMed and transferred without clinical fine-tuning to MIMIC-CXR and MIMIC-IV BHC, the model demonstrates competitive extractive summarization performance, with threshold and component ablations supporting adaptive extraction and the combined CNN--BiLSTM architecture. Clinical factuality analysis further highlights limitations in preserving important findings and negations. These results suggest that structural constraints offer a promising direction for source-grounded biomedical summarization, while emphasizing the importance of clinical completeness and contextual fidelity.
  </details>

- **[Auditing Exposure to Harmful Content on TikTok using Multimodal Language Models: A Cross-National, Age-Stratified Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.17583)**  `arXiv:2608.17583`  `cs.CL`  
  _Hamidreza Saffari, Francesco Pierri_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Online video platforms can expose young users to harmful content, but independent audits remain difficult because video annotation is costly and moderation judgments vary across languages. We audit TikTok in France, Italy, and Sweden with sockpuppet accounts representing four age personas (13, 16, 19, 40), collecting 36,971 videos from passive For-You-page scrolling and active sessions that scroll, search for harm keywords, and scroll again. To scale annotation, we validate four multimodal LLMs against native-speaker labels on a 300-video reference set. Gemini 2.5 Flash with eight sampled frames plus text performs best (aggregate kappa = 0.42), at half the per-call cost of native-video upload, and we apply it to a 10% sample for approximately \$50 in total API spend across both modalities. Keyword search returns 35-56% harmful content, a 1.5-7.5x increase over the scrolling baseline in ten of twelve country-age combinations; the spike is temporary and flattens the age differences observed in France and Sweden. Under passive scrolling, Italy has the highest harm rate at every age, with Italian age-19 reaching 48.6%. Overall, MLLM-based auditing offers a scalable approach for cross-national youth-safety audits, while provider safety filters (1.1% refusal rate) under-count the most explicit harms.
  </details>

- **[MJ: Multi-turn LLM Jailbreaking via Decomposed Credit Assignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.11070)**  `arXiv:2607.11070`  `cs.CL`  
  _Junyoung Park, Namgyu Park, Sechan Lee, Yoon-Chan Jhi, Jihoon Cho, Sangdon Park_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern large language models (LLMs) operate in interactive multi-turn settings, making multi-turn jailbreaking a realistic threat model and an important setting for automated red teaming. A core challenge in learning multi-turn jailbreak attackers is credit assignment: different turns contribute differently to the final outcome, yet existing learning signals are often too coarse to identify their individual contributions.We propose decomposed credit GRPO (DC-GRPO), a unified turn-level credit assignment framework for Group Relative Policy Optimization in multi-turn jailbreak learning. DC-GRPO assigns a separate group-relative learning signal to each turn by combining immediate and future credit, avoiding the credit misassignment induced by broadcasting a single trajectory-level score across the dialogue. We instantiate this framework with static and dynamic weighting rules that differ in how the two credit sources are balanced while sharing the same turn-level structure.Across four victim LLMs and three benchmarks, DC-GRPO achieves up to 98.26% average ASR_5@3, substantially outperforming the state-of-the-art methods, including SEMA (86.58%) and TROJail (86.23%), demonstrating the effectiveness of our turn-level group-relative credit assignment.Warning: This paper contains examples of harmful content.
  </details>

- **[Smooth Scaling Laws Hide Stepwise Token Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29858)**  `arXiv:2606.29858`  `cs.CL`  
  _Pingjie Wang, Zechen Hu, Peiru Yang, Fu Guo, Debing Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language model loss follows remarkably regular scaling laws over model and data size, yet it remains unclear why the aggregate loss should exhibit a power-law form. Existing explanations often attribute this regularity to a heavy-tailed spectrum of pattern difficulty in natural language, but this view has not been directly validated at token-level granularity in large-scale real-data training. We present a token-level framework that decomposes scaling laws into localized learning events of individual contextualized tokens. By fitting token loss trajectories with sigmoids, we show that token learning is concentrated in localized transitions, giving rise to a learning-time spectrum that dominates the scaling-law shape. Across more than one hundred pre-training runs on large and diverse real-language corpora with modern LLM architectures, scaling up to 6B parameters and 300B training tokens, the measured learning-time spectrum quantitatively reconstructs the validation loss derivative along the training-step $T$, data-scale $D$, and model-scale $M$ axes. We further show that the same signal is actionable: by reshaping the training distribution according to when tokens become learnable, we alter the optimization trajectory and achieve 11\% faster validation-loss reduction. These results provide direct empirical evidence that scaling laws are governed primarily by the distribution of token-level learning times, and that this distribution can be used not only to explain scaling behavior but also to improve training performance.
  </details>

- **[MAAM: Anchor-Preserving Compression and Contextual Calibration for Chinese Discriminatory Language Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.09114)**  `arXiv:2606.09114`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yuxin Fu, Shijing Si_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Chinese discriminatory-language detection is challenging because harmful intent is often implicit and context-dependent. We propose MAAM (Myopia--Astigmatism Anchor Mechanism), a lightweight, model-agnostic framework inspired by functional visual blur: rather than preserving every token equally, MAAM retains discrimination-relevant semantic anchors and calibrates them with C--I--S contextual priors (Contextual Tone, Group Identity, and Stance Polarity). We also introduce ChLGBT, to our knowledge the first Chinese LGBT-focused discriminatory-language dataset, with 8,120 manually annotated samples and three ordinal labels: explicit bias, implicit bias, and emotional intensity. Across strong encoder baselines, MAAM improves all three prediction dimensions, with consistent gains in accuracy, F1, Brier score, and expected calibration error. Compared with frontier LLM baselines under zero-shot and few-shot prompting protocols, MAAM remains competitive while offering stronger compactness and stability. These results suggest that interpretable anchor preservation and contextual calibration provide a practical alternative to heavier model scaling for Chinese discriminatory-language assessment.
  </details>

- **[Hint-Guided Diversified Policy Optimization for LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03021)**  `arXiv:2606.03021`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhiyu Cao, Kaixin Wu, Mingjie Zhong, Peifeng Li, Can Ye, Qiaoming Zhu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent developments in Large Language Models (LLMs) have showcased impressive reasoning capabilities, with Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) being a promising enhancement strategy. However, existing reward mechanisms are constrained to the outcome-level correctness and lack explicit signals to guide the model to consider diverse solutions. In contrast, human problem solving typically involves evaluating multiple potential approaches and selecting the most reliable solution, a cognitive process that current RLVR frameworks do not explicitly incentivize. Inspired by this, we propose Hint-Guided Diversified Policy Optimization (HDPO), allowing the model to first list all potential candidate solution outlines as hints and then select the most reliable one for further reasoning. HDPO comprises two stages of Cold Start for Structured Reasoning and Hint-Guided Diversified Reinforcement Learning to incentivize the model to generate diverse and reliable solutions following the ``propose-select-think'' trajectory. Experimental results show that HDPO effectively boosts LLM reasoning and enhances the diversity of candidate solutions as well as the LLM's ability to identify reliable solutions.
  </details>

- **[Geometric Latent Reasoning Induces Shorter Generations in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.02248)**  `arXiv:2606.02248`  `cs.CL`  
  _Shashi Kumar, Yacouba Kaloga, Petr Motlicek, Ina Kodrasi, Andrea Cavallaro_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models solve complex problems by generating lengthy chains of explicit reasoning tokens. While effective, this makes reasoning expensive, length-sensitive, and constrained to (discrete) natural language. While latent reasoning offers a continuous alternative, determining useful structures for intermediate latent states is an open challenge. In this paper, we formulate latent reasoning as a geometric path-approximation problem within the model's pretrained token-embedding space. We introduce Geometric Latent Reasoning (GLR), which uses a lightweight transition head to predict iterative direction updates in embedding space. Using textual chain-of-thought traces as anchors, GLR learns to approximate discrete reasoning trajectories while permitting continuous deviations from exact token embeddings. Evaluations on mathematical reasoning benchmarks using Qwen3 models reveal an emergent phenomenon: geometric latent reasoning induces substantially shorter generations without an explicit length objective. By replacing early explicit reasoning with continuous latent steps, models often reach correct answers using substantially fewer total generation steps. These findings suggest that continuous trajectories act as compact intermediate reasoning states, exposing a new tradeoff between latent computation budget, output length, and accuracy. Our code is publicly available at:this https URL. The project page is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[dMoE: dLLMs with Learnable Block Experts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30876)**  `arXiv:2605.30876`  `cs.CL`  
  _Sicheng Feng, Zigeng Chen, Gongfan Fang, Xinyin Ma, Xinchao Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion Large Language Models (dLLMs) have recently emerged as a promising alternative to autoregressive models, offering competitive performance while naturally supporting parallel decoding. However, as dLLMs are increasingly integrated with Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) architectures to scale model capacity, a fundamental mismatch arises between block parallel decoding and token-level expert selection. Specifically, each dLLM forward pass processes multiple tokens with bidirectional dependencies, whereas conventional MoE layers route each token independently. This mismatch substantially increases the number of uniquely activated experts, making inference increasingly memory-bound. To address this, we propose dMoE, a simple yet effective block-level MoE framework. The central idea of dMoE is to aggregate token-level expert distributions within each block into a unified block-level expert distribution, which is then used to guide block expert routing in a coherent manner. In this way, dMoE substantially reduces the number of uniquely activated experts during inference without sacrificing performance, thereby mitigating the memory-bound bottleneck. Extensive experiments across a variety of benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of dMoE. On average, dMoE reduces the number of uniquely activated experts from 69.5 to 16.6 while retaining 99.55% of the original performance. Meanwhile, it reduces memory usage by 74.36% to 76.78% and achieves $1.36\times$ to $2.04\times$ end-to-end latency speedup. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[GRUFF: LLM Pronoun Fidelity, Reasoning, and Biases in German](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.30214)**  `arXiv:2605.30214`  `cs.CL`  
  _Fabian Mewes, Anne Lauscher, Vagrant Gautam_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Third-person singular pronouns have long been used to study stereotypical biases in language models and to test their abilities to reason about reference. More recently, the interplay between reasoning and bias has been investigated with the task of pronoun fidelity, which assesses models' abilities to correctly reuse a previously-specified pronoun for a discourse entity, independent of other potentially distracting discourse entities mentioned in between. However, such research focuses on English, a language with limited grammatical gender and almost no gender agreement. In this paper we contribute a novel, large-scale dataset, GRUFF, to measure pronoun fidelity in German, covering four different gender agreement systems in nouns, and four sets of pronouns. With this dataset, we show that LLMs display strong grammatical agreement for masculine and feminine entities in the absence of explicit context, but not for neopronouns xier and en. In the presence of distractors, however, models degrade on all pronoun sets. Finally, we show that occupational stereotypes in this context are poorly correlated across grammatical cases and most models, except ones with closely related architectures.
  </details>

- **[StateRAG: Typed State Contracts for Complex Retrieval-Augmented Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.25379)**  `arXiv:2605.25379`  `cs.CL`  
  _Miaohe Niu, Pengxiang Li, Jingbo Zhu, Tong Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Complex retrieval-augmented generation requires evidence retrieval and control over what to retrieve next, which paths to explore, whether evidence is sufficient, and which intermediate results to retain. Existing RAG paradigms encode these decisions through method-specific model contexts, traversal procedures, verification signals, and memory. We introduce StateRAG, which represents retrieval control as a typed state external to the final reader. The state records the query plan, typed traversal path, candidate evidence, verification verdict, and reusable artifacts, with defined field semantics and designated update sources. Ordered role operators propose field values, and the controller validates and commits accepted proposals. A one-time compact-evidence check may select Bypass. Otherwise, the controller combines the committed verdict with the remaining budget to select Release, Revise, or Fallback. The final reader is invoked only after a terminal action and at most once per query. Among the evaluated methods, StateRAG achieves the best score on 10 of 11 reported quality metrics across LongBench, QASPER, and DocVQA. Its LongBench macro-average EM and F1 exceed ReAct by 6.3 and 6.1 percentage points, and its largest task-level gain is 9.8 F1 points on MuSiQue. Under normalized model-size weighting, StateRAG records lower mean LLM-token use per query than ReAct across all five workloads. In a matched LongBench control, the typed, role-owned carrier exceeds a free-form shared carrier by 1.3, 2.2, and 3.0 percentage points in macro EM, F1, and accuracy, with 6.2% lower mean RET. Removing TAM, MARS, or SMP lowers every reported LongBench and QASPER ablation metric.
  </details>

- **[Contexting as Recommendation: Evolutionary Collaborative Filtering for Context Engineering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.15721)**  `arXiv:2605.15721`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiachen Zhu, Zhuoying Ou, Congmin Zheng, Yuxiang Chen, Zeyu Zheng, Rong Shan, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are highly sensitive to their input contexts, motivating the development of automated context engineering. However, existing methods predominantly treat this as a global search problem, seeking a single context strategy that maximizes average performance across a dataset. This restrictive assumption overlooks the fact that different inputs often require distinct guidance, leaving substantial instance-level performance gains untapped. In this paper, we propose a paradigm shift by formulating context engineering as a recommendation problem. We introduce \textbf{Neural Collaborative Context Engineering (NCCE)}, a framework that transitions optimization from a static global search to dynamic, instance-wise routing. NCCE first bootstraps a diverse catalog of anchor contexts and then employs a novel \textbf{Context-CF Co-Evolution} mechanism. This stage establishes a synergistic feedback loop: a lightweight Neural Collaborative Filtering (NCF) model learns instance-context preferences to guide the generation of specialized context variants, while the newly evaluated contexts continuously refine the NCF model's understanding of latent preferences. At inference time, the trained NCF model acts as a context router, dynamically assigning the most suitable context strategy to each unseen instance. Theoretical Proofs and comprehensive experiments demonstrate that by matching individual inputs with their optimal contexts, NCCE significantly improves task accuracy, highlighting the critical importance of personalization in LLM context engineering.
  </details>

- **[LLM Agents Already Know When to Call Tools -- Even Without Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.09252)**  `arXiv:2605.09252`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chung-En Sun, Linbo Liu, Ge Yan, Zimo Wang, Tsui-Wei Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-augmented LLM agents tend to call tools indiscriminately, even when the model can answer directly. Each unnecessary call wastes API fees and latency, yet no existing benchmark systematically studies when a tool call is actually needed. We propose When2Tool, a benchmark of 18 environments (15 single-hop, 3 multi-hop) spanning three categories of tool necessity -- computational scale, knowledge boundaries, and execution reliability -- each with controlled difficulty levels that create a clear decision boundary between tool-necessary and tool-unnecessary tasks. We evaluate two families of training-free baselines: Prompt-only (varying the prompt to discourage unnecessary calls) and Reason-then-Act (requiring the model to reason about tool necessity before acting). Both provide limited control: Prompt-only suppresses necessary calls alongside unnecessary ones, and Reason-then-Act still incurs a disproportionate accuracy cost on hard tasks. To understand why these baselines fail, we probe the models' hidden states and find that tool necessity is linearly decodable from the pre-generation representation with AUROC 0.89--0.96 across six models, substantially exceeding the model's own verbalized reasoning. This reveals that models already know when tools are needed, but fail to act on this knowledge during generation. Building on this finding, we propose Probe&Prefill, which uses a lightweight linear probe to read the hidden-state signal and prefills the model's response with a steering sentence. Across all models tested, Probe&Prefill reduces tool calls by 38\% with only 1.7\% accuracy loss, while the best baseline at comparable accuracy only reduces 6% of tool calls, or achieves a similar tool call reduction but incurs a 5$\times$ higher accuracy loss. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[LoopWeaver: Weaving Feedback Loops into Hierarchical Generation under Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.04996)**  `arXiv:2603.04996`  `cs.CL`  
  _Yifan Zhu, Guanting Chen, Bing Wei, Haoran Luo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models face a multi-objective optimization problem in constrained long-text generation, balancing global structure, local coherence, and constraint satisfaction. We propose LoopWeaver, a hierarchical workflow with two sequential offline preference-optimization stages. Constraint-aware planning produces structured plans, feasibility filtering removes invalid plans, and a shared deterministic reward constructs preference pairs for plan-level and generation-level DPO. At inference time, LoopWeaver remains feed-forward: it generates and revises a complete plan, filters the revised plan, and then generates segments conditioned on the current sub-plan and running narrative. We evaluate the method on four LongGenBench-style structured scenarios across multiple backbones. Results show improvements in both constraint accuracy and text quality across all four open-source backbones. Our software and data are publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Steer2Edit: From Activation Steering to Component-Level Editing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.09870)**  `arXiv:2602.09870`  `cs.CL`  
  _Chung-En Sun, Ge Yan, Zimo Wang, Tsui-Wei Weng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Steering methods influence Large Language Model behavior by identifying semantic directions in hidden representations, but are typically realized through inference-time activation interventions that apply a fixed, global modification to the model's internal states. While effective, such interventions often induce unfavorable attribute-utility trade-offs under strong control, as they ignore the fact that many behaviors are governed by a small and heterogeneous subset of model components. We propose Steer2Edit, a theoretically grounded, training-free framework that transforms steering vectors from inference-time control signals into diagnostic signals for component-level rank-1 weight editing. Instead of uniformly injecting a steering direction during generation, Steer2Edit selectively redistributes behavioral influence across individual attention heads and MLP neurons, yielding interpretable edits that preserve the standard forward pass and remain compatible with optimized parallel inference. Across safety alignment, hallucination mitigation, and reasoning efficiency, Steer2Edit consistently achieves more favorable attribute-utility trade-offs: at matched downstream performance, it improves safety by up to 17.2%, increases truthfulness by 9.8%, and reduces reasoning length by 12.2% on average. Overall, Steer2Edit provides a principled bridge between representation steering and weight editing by translating steering signals into interpretable, training-free parameter updates. Our code is available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[DAWN: Dependency-Aware Fast Inference for Diffusion LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.06953)**  `arXiv:2602.06953`  `cs.CL`  
  _Lizhuo Luo, Zhuoran Shi, Jiajun Luo, Zhi Wang, Shen Ren, Wenya Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion large language models (dLLMs) have shown advantages in text generation, particularly due to their inherent ability for parallel decoding. However, constrained by the quality--speed trade-off, existing inference solutions adopt conservative parallel strategies, leaving substantial efficiency potential underexplored. A core challenge is that parallel decoding assumes each position can be filled independently, but tokens are often semantically coupled. Thus, the correct choice at one position constrains valid choices at others. Without modeling these positional dependencies, parallel strategies produce deteriorated outputs. Motivated by this insight, we propose DAWN, a training-free, dependency-aware decoding method for fast dLLM inference. DAWN extracts dependency relations and leverages two key observations: (1) masked positions dependent on unmasked certain positions become more reliable; (2) coupled masked positions strongly influence each other's predictions. Given those findings, DAWN leverages a dependency graph to select more reliable unmasking positions at each iteration, achieving high parallelism with negligible loss in generation quality. Extensive experiments across multiple models and datasets demonstrate that DAWN speedups the inference by 1.80-8.06x over baselines while preserving the generation quality. Code is released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Fix the Structural Bottleneck: Context Compression via Explicit Information Transmission](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.03784)**  `arXiv:2602.03784`  `cs.CL`  
  _Jiangnan Ye, Hanqi Yan, Zhenyi Shen, Heng Chang, Ye Mao, Yulan He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context LLM agents often struggle with growing token, memory, and latency costs, making efficient context compression essential for practical deployment. Existing LLM-as-a-compressor methods remain noticeably inferior to using the full context. We find that this gap partly stems from their inability to preserve contextual information effectively. In this work, we revisit context compression from a structural perspective and identify two key bottlenecks in standard LLM-based compressors: limited coordination among compression tokens during information aggregation, and layerwise dilution that weakens useful signals from intermediate hidden states. To address these limitations, we propose ComprExIT, a new context compression framework based on explicit information transmission. ComprExIT adaptively selects features across frozen LLM layers, then allocates information from anchors to compression slots through a globally coordinated transport plan. Experiments on 12 datasets show that ComprExIT consistently outperforms strong soft-compression baselines, improving average F1 by up to 18.5%, while adding only ~1% trainable parameters and achieving more than 2x faster compression than the fastest baselines. The code will be released upon acceptance.
  </details>

- **[HAPS: Hierarchical LLM Routing with Joint Architecture and Parameter Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.05903)**  `arXiv:2601.05903`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zihang Tian, Rui Li, Jingsen Zhang, Xiaohe Bo, Wei Huo, Xu Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) routing aims to exploit the specialized strengths of different LLMs for diverse tasks. However, existing approaches typically focus only on selecting LLM architectures, treating candidate models as fixed while overlooking parameter adaptation, which can substantially affect task performance. In this paper, we introduce HAPS, a hierarchical LLM routing framework that jointly searches over model architectures and parameters. Specifically, a high-level router selects among candidate LLM architectures, while a low-level router generates input-conditioned LoRA parameters for the selected architecture, expanding each candidate from a single fixed behavior into a region of reachable behaviors. The low-level router is built on the backbone of the high-level router, so that architecture selection and parameter generation share a common representation of the prompt. We further optimize the whole framework with a reinforcement learning objective. Extensive experiments show that HAPS consistently outperforms strong routing baselines across reasoning- and knowledge-intensive benchmarks, with analyses of its components, design choices, scalability, cost, and mixed open- and closed-source deployment further demonstrating the robustness of these gains. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Latent Refinement Decoding: Enhancing Diffusion-Based Language Models by Refining Belief States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2510.11052)**  `arXiv:2510.11052`  `cs.CL`  
  _Qinglin Zhu, Yizhen Yao, Runcong Zhao, Yanzheng Xiang, Siya Qi, Amrutha Saseendran, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autoregressive (AR) models remain the dominant paradigm for natural language generation, but their strictly sequential decoding process leads to high inference latency. Recent diffusion-inspired language models, such as LLaDA and Dream, alleviate this issue through parallel generation, yet they still face two key limitations: information loss, since predictive distributions over non-finalised tokens are discarded at each step, and unstable commitment dynamics, where local token decisions are not sufficiently coordinated at the global level. We propose Latent Refinement Decoding (LRD), a two-stage decoding framework consisting of Latent Refinement and a Predictive Feedback Loop. In the first stage, LRD keeps masked positions as distributional mixtures of predicted tokens and the mask embedding, enabling the model to form more globally consistent beliefs before committing tokens. In the second stage, LRD progressively finalises confident tokens while preserving uncertain ones for further iterative refinement, with KL-divergence dynamics serving as a stable criterion for convergence and early stopping. Experiments show that LRD improves performance on both coding tasks, including HumanEval by 6.3 and MBPP by 2.6, and reasoning tasks, including GSM8K by 2.9 and MATH500 by 3.8, while achieving up to 10.6x overall speedup. Moreover, LRD is compatible with single-token decoding, multi-token threshold commitment, and system-level accelerators; when combined with Fast-dLLM, it reaches up to 18.4x speedup over vanilla decoding while further improving accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Stabilizing Knowledge, Promoting Reasoning: Dual-Token Constraints for RLVR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.15778)**  `arXiv:2507.15778`  `cs.CL`  
  _Runze Liu, Jiakang Wang, Fuzheng Zhang, Xiu Li, Guorui Zhou, Kun Gai, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has become an effective post-training method for improving the reasoning abilities of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, existing methods mainly apply uniform optimization constraints across all tokens, ignoring their heterogeneous roles. Prior work shows that high-entropy tokens are closely tied to reasoning, while low-entropy tokens primarily encode factual knowledge, and recent approaches attempt to exploit this distinction by isolating token updates via masking or asynchronous training. We argue that retaining training signals across token types can lead to more effective learning. To address this, we propose Archer, an entropy-aware RLVR framework with dual-token constraints that preserves joint optimization while modulating update strength across token types. Our method introduces response-level entropy normalization for stable token classification and applies differentiated clipping ranges and KL regularization to encourage exploration on reasoning tokens while preserving knowledge tokens. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and code generation benchmarks show that Archer consistently outperforms strong baselines across multiple model scales, improving both pass@1 and pass@K performance. These results highlight the importance of respecting sequence-level dependencies when designing fine-grained RL optimization strategies for LLMs. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[HARDTESTGEN: A High-Quality RL Verifier Generation Pipeline for LLM Algorithmic Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24098)**  `arXiv:2505.24098`  `cs.CL`  
  _Zhongmou He, Yee Man Choi, Kexun Zhang, Jiabao Ji, Junting Zhou, Dejia Xu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Verifiers provide important reward signals for reinforcement learning of large language models (LLMs). However, it is challenging to develop or create reliable verifiers, especially for code generation tasks. A well-disguised wrong solution program may only be detected by carefully human-written edge cases that are difficult to synthesize automatically. To address this issue, we propose HARDTESTGEN, an approach to synthesize high-quality test cases for algorithmic coding problems. We curate a comprehensive algorithmic programming dataset HARDTESTS with 26.6k problems and high-quality synthetic tests. Compared with existing tests, HARDTESTGEN tests demonstrate significantly higher accuracy in verifying LLM-generated code (+11.22 percentage points in precision, the percentage of actually correct code within the predicted correct ones). We also show that downstream post-training -- including rejection sampling and reinforcement learning (RL) -- using HARDTESTS verifier results in improved performance of LLM code generation. We open-source our dataset and synthesis pipeline atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SCALE: Small-Model-First Concept Abstraction with LLM-Guided Experience Internalization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12120)**  `arXiv:2611.12120`  `cs.AI` `cs.RO`  
  _Hong Su_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous agents must continually acquire reusable knowledge from experience without relying on costly large language model (LLM) reasoning for every decision. However, existing experience-based approaches often struggle to transform accumulated observations and reasoning into compact knowledge that lightweight models can independently apply. This paper proposes SCALE, a small-model-first framework that integrates selective LLM reasoning, experience-driven concept abstraction, and knowledge internalization. A lightweight model handles routine predictions, while prediction errors and accumulated cross-episode evidence selectively trigger LLM reflection. The LLM analyzes relevant historical experiences, generates positive and negative counterfactual examples, and proposes reusable concept hypotheses. Independently validated concepts are progressively incorporated into hierarchical neural representations, enabling lightweight models to exploit increasingly abstract knowledge. Meanwhile, mastered experiences are excluded from active memory while their original records remain available in a complete archive. Experiments across ten random seeds show that historical recall improves held-out accuracy from 87.11% to 98.28%, hierarchical concept knowledge increases difficult-case accuracy from 55.33% to 66.47%, and abstract archiving reduces active experience records from 6,000 to 1,528.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Reasoning: RL-Policy Guided LLM Inference for Efficient Strategy in Liar's Poker](https://arxiv.org/abs/2511.03724)**  `arXiv:2511.03724`  `cs.AI` `cs.MA`  
  _Richard Dewey, Janos Botyanszki, Ciamac C. Moallemi, Andrew T. Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI researchers have long focused on poker-like games as a testbed for environments characterized by multi-player dynamics, imperfect information, and reasoning under uncertainty. While recent breakthroughs have matched elite human play at no-limit Texas hold'em, the multi-player dynamics are subdued: most hands converge quickly with only two players engaged through multiple rounds of bidding. In this paper, we present Solly, the first AI agent to achieve elite human play in reduced-format Liar's Poker, a game characterized by extensive multi-player engagement. We trained Solly using self-play with a model-free, actor-critic, deep reinforcement learning algorithm. Solly played at an elite human level as measured by win rate (won over 50% of hands) and equity (money won) in heads-up and multi-player Liar's Poker. Solly also outperformed large language models (LLMs), including those with reasoning abilities, on the same metrics. Solly developed novel bidding strategies, randomized play effectively, and was not easily exploitable by world-class human players. In one extension, when Solly's policies were provided to LLMs as a domain-specific guide for inference, LLM performance improved and token costs decreased. This result suggests that low-cost RL agents can be used to boost LLM efficiency and reasoning capabilities.
  </details>

- **[AutoBuddy: Evaluating Proactive, Always-On Agents on Task Discovery and Execution in a Simulated Workplace](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12393)**  `arXiv:2611.12393`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Shiqi Chen, Daniel Madrigal Diaz, Wei-Ning Chen, Gbola Afonja, Fangkai Yang, Rujia Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM agents become more autonomous, they are increasingly expected to run as always-on assistants on a user's behalf, identifying work to be done and completing it without being asked. Yet existing benchmarks usually give the agent its task, whereas in knowledge work the requests sit in the user's workspace and the agent must first recognise which obligations exist. We study proactive task discovery and execution, in which an agent must infer a user's pending tasks from their workspace and complete them in the systems where the work lives. This setting poses two challenges. For the agent, the tasks are buried in a large volume of mail, chat, documents and records. For evaluation, a benchmark needs a simulated organisation whose people, events and documents are mutually consistent, so that tasks arise realistically and their correct outcomes are known. We introduce AutoBuddy-Bench, a simulator that addresses these challenges. It models an organisation's employees, projects, business systems and event history as a temporal graph, renders it into realistic workspace artefacts, derives ground-truth tasks from the events that create obligations, and computes each task's outcome from the world state; execution is judged by the agent's changes to the workspace. To evaluate current LLMs, we build AutoBuddy, a reference agent with interchangeable backbone LLMs: it identifies tasks from the workspace, grounding each in its source records, and executes each in a separate session with write access. Varying only the backbone, we find a systematic shift in where tasks are lost: weaker models fail mainly at discovery, intermediate models identify tasks but often stop short of carrying them out, and the strongest models fail mainly during execution, most often on business-system operations. Discovery precision is only weakly related to execution quality, suggesting the two are distinct capabilities.
  </details>

- **[SkillDuet: Co-Evolving Models and Skills through Response Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12109)**  `arXiv:2611.12109`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xingguang Ji, Ziqiang Dong, Yubo Zhang, Xi Leng, Yang Yang, Guanjun Jiang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  During reinforcement learning, experience can be both internalized in LLM parameters and retained externally as reusable textual skills. Parameter updates alter the model's behavior and the interaction experience from which new skills are extracted. These skills, in turn, guide the model's subsequent interactions and parameter updates. As the model's capabilities evolve, previously beneficial skills may become redundant or detrimental. We present SkillDuet, a framework that coordinates parameter updates and skill evolution through model response feedback. Skills extracted from on-policy trajectories guide parameter updates through OPD at every step. A response-based score, Strength, ranks candidate skills by the magnitude of the shift they induce in the current model's responses, and the highest-scoring skills are periodically consolidated into a persistent global skill set (Global). The updated model and Global jointly guide subsequent interactions, generating experience for further parameter and skill updates. With Qwen3-4B, SkillDuet achieves success rates of 98.57% on ALFWorld and 92.97% on WebShop, exceeding the matched SEED-API baseline by 2.86 and 5.47 percentage points, respectively. Ablations show that response-based skill selection and Global consolidation improve task performance. Further experiments show that the benefits of retained skills depend on both their content and their compatibility with the model. SkillDuet provides a concrete mechanism for model-skill co-evolution and skill-level recursive self-improvement.
  </details>

- **[Weight-Space Programming of Policies and Their Verification via Model Checking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11604)**  `arXiv:2611.11604`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Dennis Gross_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can write the weights of small neural networks directly, without gradient descent. This weight-space programming creates policies for sequential decision-making from a task description alone, but nothing guarantees that a written policy behaves as intended. We combine weight-space programming with probabilistic model checking. Given an environment modeled as a Markov decision process and a requirement in probabilistic computation tree logic (PCTL), an LLM writes all weights of a policy network, optionally repairs them on a dataset of optimal actions, and receives model-checking feedback until the requirement holds or a round limit is reached. The model checker Storm computes, rather than estimates from simulations, the probability that the policy satisfies the requirement, so every returned policy is verified. With two LLMs on three environments, the created policy of each of the 18 runs attains the optimal probability, before any dataset or model-checking feedback. Four of six policies programmed for two transporter jobs also complete three jobs with probability 1, so they generalize to a task that the LLM never saw. The pipeline also programs a policy that satisfies a complex safety requirement expressed with the PCTL until operator in all six runs. In one of them, dataset repair raised the accuracy to 98.4\% but broke the requirement, and one round of model-checking feedback restored it.
  </details>

- **[DiSARM: Diverse and Surprising Association Rule Mining with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10445)**  `arXiv:2611.10445`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lijia Liu, Hisashi Kashima_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Exploratory Data Analysis (EDA) aims to discover informative patterns in large datasets. For transactional data, Association Rule Mining (ARM) is an established approach that facilitates EDA by efficiently generating candidate patterns. Beyond efficient mining, identifying informative rules has also been extensively studied through both objective and subjective interestingness measures. Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer new opportunities for assessing subjective interestingness using world knowledge. Nevertheless, relying solely on LLMs raises concerns about scalability and statistical reliability. Building on prior ARM research, we propose a hybrid framework, DiSARM, that leverages the world knowledge of LLMs to assess the subjective interestingness of rules, while maintaining statistical reliability and promoting diversity among the selected rules. Across three transactional datasets, experiments demonstrate that DiSARM effectively combines statistical reliability with surprise and diversity, offering a promising approach to ADE from transactional data.
  </details>

- **[Restoring General and Improving Domain-Specific Safety in Fine-Tuned LLMs Without Retraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10279)**  `arXiv:2611.10279`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Vishnu Kabir Chhabra, Ding Zhu, Zhihui Zhu, Xueru Zhang, Mohammad Mahdi Khalili_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Safety-aligned language models are known to suffer degradation in refusal behavior following domain-specific fine-tuning, even when the fine-tuning data is entirely benign. The predominant mitigation strategy, preference optimization on the fine-tuned model, is computationally expensive and requires domain-specific preference data that is often unavailable in practice. We propose SPECTRA, a training-free, weight-level method that restores refusal behavior by transferring the base model's refusal direction into the fine-tuned model's weights via an activation-aware, low-rank update, requiring no gradient-based optimization or domain-specific safety data. The low-rank design restricts the intervention to the subspace most causally active on harmful inputs, leaving benign behavior largely undisturbed by construction. Empirically, SPECTRA substantially improves refusal behavior across a comprehensive safety evaluation, while preserving general coherence, domain-specific knowledge, and producing negligible change in false refusal rates.
  </details>

- **[Same Beliefs, Different Policies: Learning Reusable Representations for Early Stopping in LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30450)**  `arXiv:2610.30450`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Jingwei Ni, Dmitry Abulkhanov, Vladislav Smirnov, Markus Leippold, Elliott Ash, Timothy Baldwin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning LLMs often continue reasoning after reaching a correct answer, wasting computation and sometimes replacing it with an incorrect one. Deciding when to stop is a metareasoning problem: the model must weigh what further reasoning could achieve against its cost, given what it can already answer. Direct utility training learns a stopping representation at a chosen token price; supporting new prices can require additional controller training. We introduce BELT (BELief-based Termination), which separates outcome prediction from stopping control. It learns beliefs about current and future answers from a frozen LLM's hidden states, then fits a linear stopping policy on the frozen features. The prediction targets are independent of token price, so one probe supports different accuracy-cost trade-offs through CPU policy refitting or threshold adjustment. Averaged over five out-of-distribution (OOD) math benchmarks, BELT reduces generated tokens by 41% on Qwen3-1.7B and 51% on OLMo-3-7B-Think without loss of accuracy. The same math-trained BELT transfers to coding and agentic tasks without training on code or agentic data. In all three domains, its accuracy-cost trade-offs are competitive with or better than those of end-to-end controllers. On OLMo3, its probe has 21.2x fewer parameters than one checkpoint of the strongest baseline, OS-Pruner, and needs 55x fewer training GPU-hours than OS-Pruner's five price-specific checkpoints.
  </details>

- **[Towards Reliable, Generalizable, and Specific In-Context Knowledge Editing via Multi-Objective Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.25100)**  `arXiv:2608.25100`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Xuzhong Wang, Maiqi Jiang, Tejal Nair, Girija Bhusal, Yanfu Zhang, Haipeng Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are powerful but limited by static parametric knowledge that becomes outdated once pretraining ends. Knowledge editing addresses this problem by updating model behavior on target facts without full retraining. In particular, in-context knowledge editing has gained attention because it is training-free and readily applicable to black-box LLMs. Recent reinforcement learning (RL)-based approaches improve over fixed retrieval strategies by adapting prompt construction to the quantity-quality trade-off. Despite initial success, they fail to model the prompt as a structured entity under the distinct and often competing objectives of reliability, generality, and specificity. Previous methods largely optimize a single objective and make decisions over only part of the prompt construction process, thereby overlooking both the balance of different objectives and the global organization of demonstrations. We propose Multi-Objective In-context Knowledge Editing (MO-IKE), a multi-objective RL algorithm that formulates prompt construction for in-context knowledge editing as a Constrained Markov Decision Process. MO-IKE trains a dynamic retriever to optimize competing objectives in knowledge editing, enabling more balanced and globally coherent prompt construction. On Llama-3.2, MO-IKE improves edit success (reliability) from 85.0% to 92.0%, paraphrase consistency (generality) from 77% to 79%, while increasing retention rate (specificity) by 23.0% compared to prior RL-based methods.
  </details>

- **[Cooperative Coevolution for Resource-Constrained Agentic LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02391)**  `arXiv:2608.02391`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Zhiyuan Wang, Shengcai Liu, Jiahao Wu, Ning Lu, Hui Ouyang, Shaofeng Zhang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using large language model (LLM) agents produce long, multi-turn trajectories, making gradient-based post-training memory-intensive. Evolution strategies (ES) enable memory-efficient full-parameter post-training without backpropagation and can eventually match the performance of gradient-based reinforcement learning (RL). However, resource-constrained settings typically offer only a few GPUs, so the high GPU-hour requirements of ES translate into prohibitively long training times. To address this, we introduce Cooperative Parameter-subspace Evolution Strategy (CoPES), a cooperative coevolutionary method that decomposes the full parameter space into lower-dimensional subspaces and searches over them cooperatively to improve optimization efficiency. On Qwen3.5-4B math, CoPES recovers 92% of full-parameter GRPO's validation-accuracy gain within the GPU-hour budget of GRPO's best validation checkpoint, versus 67% for standard ES. Its theoretical GPU memory requirement is less than one-eighth that of full-parameter GRPO. It consistently outperforms standard ES and LoRA-based GRPO on all evaluated pass@k metrics across five math benchmarks. CoPES's gains extend to Qwen3.5-9B with unchanged optimization hyperparameters and to multi-hop QA. These results demonstrate an improved trade-off between memory requirements and training time for agentic LLM post-training under resource constraints. The code is open-sourced inthis https URL
  </details>

- **[PEARL: Solver-in-the-Loop Interactive Optimization Modeling from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18256)**  `arXiv:2607.18256`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Hongliang Lu, Zhong Li, Yuxuan Chen, Yuan Lan, Fan Zhang, Zaiwen Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Optimization modeling is the process of translating real-world decision problems, often described in natural language, into formal mathematical formulations and executable solver code. While recent advances in large language models have shown promise in automating this process, many approaches focus on one-shot generation or rely on predefined execution-and-repair workflows, without explicitly learning a policy for tool use and revision. This stands in sharp contrast to real-world optimization modeling, which is inherently interactive and proceeds through repeated solve-debug-revise cycles. We introduce PEARL, a system for multi-turn solver-interactive optimization modeling that uses Python execution and mathematical programming solvers inside this loop. Rather than relying on a fixed repair workflow, PEARL learns when to test partial models, how to revise from solver diagnostics, and when to stop. It operates in a multi-turn tool-integrated setting where intermediate execution results, feasibility signals, and solution checks are used to improve both formulations and solver code before finalization. Across diverse optimization benchmarks, PEARL substantially improves executable objective agreement over the same-scale base model and is competitive with strong one-shot and tool-augmented baselines. Notably, PEARL-Qwen3-4B outperforms the much larger DeepSeek-V3.2-685B baseline under direct prompting on both macro- and micro-averaged Pass@1. Code and datasets are provided atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SCARCE: Scalable Cascade Analysis for Rare-event Characterisation via Embeddings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29623)**  `arXiv:2606.29623`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Yingjie Wang, Yi Dong, Edmund Lau, Jie Meng, Taylor T Johnson, Xiaowei Huang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Rare events govern the safety profile of modern AI systems, yet their probabilities are extremely difficult to estimate: direct Monte Carlo requires prohibitive sample budgets. Subset Simulation (SS) addresses this by decomposing a rare-event probability into moderate conditional probabilities over nested intermediate events. However, classical SS requires a handcrafted scalar performance function whose sublevel sets define those events, demanding detailed knowledge of the failure geometry and limiting transfer to new domains. We propose SCARCE (Scalable Cascade Analysis for Rare-event Characterisation via Embeddings), which replaces the performance function with learned latent representations and geometric rulers that score proximity to failure regions. Adaptive thresholding constructs nested intermediate events directly from data. We formalise SCARCE through a non-negative supermartingale, yielding a high-probability upper envelope that remains valid under early stopping. On MNIST misclassification, where dense Monte Carlo provides ground truth, SCARCE achieves approximately 400--500 times lower mean absolute error than grid-searched traditional SS while eliminating systematic over-counting. We then study PAIR-style LLM jailbreaks under a fleet-level threat model with adversarial fraction $\eta$. On Llama-Guard-3-8B hidden states, a PCA-based ruler attains 2.6% mean relative error for $\eta \geq 10^{-3}$ against finite-sample references whose average bootstrap relative half-width is 27.9%, and transfers to a GCG-style corpus with 2.93% relative error after recalibration. A directional criterion $\mathrm{KL}(p_{\mathrm{good}}\,\|\,p_{\mathrm{bad}})$ ranks rulers consistently with estimation error (Spearman $\rho=0.83$).
  </details>

- **[Interpreting Neural Combinatorial Optimization via Evolving Programmatic Bottlenecks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.19741)**  `arXiv:2606.19741`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Haocheng Duan, Yuxin Guo, Jieyi Bi, Anqi Xie, Sirui Li, Yining Ma, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Neural Combinatorial Optimization (NCO) achieves strong performance, its interpretability remains an open question. In this paper, we introduce Evolving Programmatic Bottlenecks (EPB), the first post-hoc, surrogate-based interpretability method for NCO, which explains a black-box policy by distilling it into an interpretable behavior surrogate. A natural surrogate is a Concept Bottleneck Model (CBM), yet standard concepts are input attributes, whereas NCO is better explained by decision rules that are hard to predefine. EPB thus uses LLMs to evolve a bank of programmatic decision rules as concepts, each producing an action distribution, where a learned state-dependent router then mixes them to form the surrogate action. We optimize EPB through an iterative framework: Block I fixes the bank size and introduces the hybrid textual-numerical gradient descent to jointly optimize the textual programs and the numerical router; Block II adapts the bank size via failure-targeted expansion and redundancy pruning. Extensive experiments demonstrate that the EPB surrogate closely matches the teacher's behavior and solution quality. We then analyze the distilled rules, reveal several NCO decision patterns, and verify the rules' faithfulness to the surrogate's decisions. Moreover, the surrogate allows targeted program edits that improve its generalization beyond the teacher. Our work advances interpretable NCO and establishes EPB as a promising direction for interpreting complex learned decision-making policies.
  </details>

- **[Evolutionary System Prompt Learning for Reinforcement Learning in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.14697)**  `arXiv:2602.14697`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Lunjun Zhang, Ryan Chen, Bradly C. Stadie_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building agentic systems that can autonomously self-improve from experience is a longstanding goal of AI. Large language models (LLMs) today primarily self-improve via two mechanisms: self-reflection for context updates, and reinforcement learning (RL) for weight updates. In this work, we propose Evolutionary System Prompt Learning (E-SPL), a method for jointly improving a model's experiential context and its weights. In each RL iteration, E-SPL samples trajectories under multiple system prompts in parallel, then uses them to jointly update the weights with RL and evolve the system prompts with self-reflection.
