E-SPL encourages a natural division between procedural knowledge in weights and interpretable declarative knowledge in system prompts. Most notably, in an easy-to-hard generalization setting (AIME $\rightarrow$ BeyondAIME), E-SPL improves RL success rate from 38.8% $\rightarrow$ 45.1%. E-SPL also improves RL on AIME 2025 (56.3% $\rightarrow$ 60.6%), HMMT 2025 (50.0% $\rightarrow$ 52.7%), and agentic search (44.2% $\rightarrow$ 48.6% on gpt-oss-120b). Across all settings, E-SPL outperforms both RL-only and evolution-only baselines, demonstrating that weight updates and context updates are deeply synergistic and can together yield gains in generalization that neither achieves alone. Code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Rule Forest: Interpretable Ensembles of LLM-Generated Questions for Rare-Outcome Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.24622)**  `arXiv:2505.24622`  `cs.AI` `cs.LG`  
  _Ben Griffin, Aaron Ontoyin Yin, Diego Vidaurre, Ugur Koyluoglu, Joseph Ternasky, Fuat Alican, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many high-stakes screening decisions in finance amount to predicting a rare outcome from unstructured text, such as whether a founder will successfully build a large company, or which crowdfunded loan will fail to attract funding. When positives are this rare, simple models may be preferable to more complex ones. For example, a model that learns a weight for each feature may have too little signal to estimate those weights reliably, and also inspecting the model means reading a list of fitted coefficients rather than a simple rule that can be checked by eye. We introduce Rule Forest, which uses a large language model to generate simple YES/NO questions, rather than using it to directly predict outcomes, and combines the strongest questions into an equal-weight "green-flags" scorecard. This scorecard is as accurate as an equivalent model that learns a weight per question, within noise, and its advantage appears largest when positives are rarest. The result is an interpretable list of rules that a domain expert can edit and extend. When predicting founder success, the fraction of shortlisted founders who succeed increases from about 1 in 50 to 1 in 10, and to about 1 in 6 with a handful of expert-written questions. A second dataset which focuses on identifying failed loans, Kiva microloans, shows the same qualitative pattern. Across both tasks, the lesson is the same: a language model is most useful not as the predictor but as a generator of features a person can read, edit, and improve, and when positives are scarce that interpretability costs no measurable accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Seeing Isn't Enough: Unlocking Visual Reasoning by Bridging the Reasoning-Access Gap with TIGER](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10552)**  `arXiv:2611.10552`  `cs.AI` `cs.CV` `cs.LG`  
  _Gregory Kang Ruey Lau, Minh Huynh Nguyen, Bryan Kian Hsiang Low_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) inherit strong textual reasoning capabilities from their language backbones and text pretraining, but often fail to apply the same level of competency to visual inputs. We show that this degradation cannot be attributed to only perception failures, by evaluating models on semantically-equivalent text and rendered-text image inputs: models can accurately read text in the images and recover near-text performance when provided their own transcriptions, but just cannot reason well on the images directly. We characterize this discrepancy as a modality reasoning-access gap, where models fail to route perceived visual content into existing reasoning machinery accessible with text. To bridge this, we propose TIGER (Text-to-Image Gap-targeted training for Enhanced Reasoning), which first mines synthetic modality counterfactuals where a model succeeds on a text problem but fails when provided the corresponding rendered image, and post-trains the model on the image input via Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), along with SFT and DPO variants. Importantly, although trained exclusively on synthetic rendered-text images with no multimodal annotations, TIGER results in model performance improvements across diverse real-world visual benchmarks including geometric diagrams (MathVerse), multi-disciplinary sciences (EMMA, MMMU), complex charts (ChartQAPro), and embodied spatial reasoning (RoboSpatial-Home). TIGER also complements existing state-of-the-art RLVR visual reasoning methods, yielding further performance gains. Analysis involving reasoning-subspace activation and activation patching provide support indicating that TIGER helps to align visual-to-reasoning pathways toward text reasoning states. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Measuring What Tool-Using LLM Agents Actually Do: Structured Validation, Causal Error Propagation, and Fabricated Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.16706)**  `arXiv:2604.16706`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.MA`  
  _Bhaskar Gurram_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-using agents built on large language models (LLMs) are usually scored by comparing the final answer with a reference text, using a string heuristic or an LLM judge. This is unreliable when the references describe the task rather than its answer. We replace it with a deterministic validator that checks the tools called, the executed parameters, the resulting state, and whether the values in the answer are supported by the correct tool observations. We re-scored 33,583 traces from 13 agents on simulated calendar, weather, medical and retail tools. The simulator reproduced all 10,396 logged observations, and the gold observations supported the validator in 16 of 18 disagreements with a three-annotator majority (the annotators in none). The instrument changes conclusions: on the same 306 traces, Gemini-2.0-Flash scores 63.4% under the substring heuristic and 5.6% under the validator. In a paired design (same task and model, with and without one schema-valid wrong argument injected into the first tool call), the error rate rises from 52.4% to 74.5% (+22.0 percentage points, 95% CI 18.4-25.5; 1,085 pairs, 7 models), and by 44.7 points (38.4-51.0) where the corrupted call executed without errors. An LLM-judge ensemble estimates +2.5 points on the same pairs. Agents also report tool results without executing any call, in 0-42.5% of traces depending on the model (rule-based lower bounds), largely because the harness accepts an action and a final answer in one completion. A rule-based runtime monitor costs about 7 microseconds per trace but reduces wrong answers mainly by abstaining.
  </details>

- **[ODSA: Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11621)**  `arXiv:2611.11621`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG` `cs.MA`  
  _Macardle Meng, Haomin Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Test-time scaling improves the accuracy of a language model's answers by spending more computation at inference time. Established methods do so either sequentially, revising a single solution over several passes, or in parallel, generating several independent solutions and selecting among them. We present ODSA (Orchestrated Discovery and Self-Aggregation), an LLM-directed scaling framework in which an orchestrator LLM is given existing test-time scaling methods as actions --- discovery of a new independent solution, comparison of existing solutions to build a better one, and acceptance of a proposed answer, which triggers its verification --- and directs stateless workers to carry them out. The orchestrator does not solve the problem itself: it allocates computation, feeds the results of earlier actions into later ones, and takes every action from a single context, so that each decision is informed by everything tried before. On Humanity's Last Exam, ODSA improves accuracy by 4.7 to 18.0 points across three models over a single direct call to the model with no test-time scaling. On LiveCodeBench, where a single direct call already solves most problems, it improves accuracy by 0.6 to 14.0 points.
  </details>

- **[The Value of Not Knowing: How Fresh Eyes through Naive LLMs Fuel Creativity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11725)**  `arXiv:2611.11725`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _David B. D'Ambrosio, Alon Albalak, Joel Lehman, Kenneth O. Stanley_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many major scientific advances come from approaching a problem with a new or novel perspective. While large language models (LLMs) with internet-scale knowledge seem poised to take such perspectives, their consistent generation remains elusive. Instead, models are drawn to dominant views and preexisting, in-field bias. To explore the potential of fresh eyes to increase creativity, this paper proposes naive LLMs, models that are made intentionally ignorant of a specific topic. Through deprivation in training or intentional forgetting, naive models are unburdened by existing dogma, allowing them to be free to make new associations and conjectures. These naive models can then be paired with a stronger mentor model that can refine their unique perspectives into concrete proposals. For the 1B and 7B models tested in this paper, naive models consistently produce seed ideas that are more novel and useful than baseline counterparts, and mentor refinement improves the ideas even further. Thus, contrary to the prevailing tradition of scaling up training data, we demonstrate a case where less information is actually more powerful, in particular when the aim is to discover genuinely outside-the-box solutions.
  </details>

- **[In Search of Lost Time: Temporal Competence in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10536)**  `arXiv:2611.10536`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Ryan Liu, David Lee, Icey Siyi Ai, Marwa Abdulhai, Rachit Dubey, Thomas L. Griffiths_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Today's large language models (LLMs) increasingly interact with time: AI agents schedule tasks, coordinate with people and other AI systems, and even decide how long to think. Yet LLMs only ever encounter time secondhand---in descriptions rather than experience. Does this hinder their reasoning and decisions that involve time? Our paper traces this question from a model's knowledge to its actions through four successive evaluations. We find that models know the rules of time, which can be read, but struggle to predict durations, which must be lived; while they are highly accurate (92.0-97.5\%) on a collection of questions built from the theorems of various time-related literatures, they underestimate their own completion times by up to 99.5\% and fail to identify which tasks will take them longer to run. And while they are better at estimating durations of human tasks, their duration judgments change based on how an event is worded, magnifying similar human biases by up to 1.3$\times$. Moving from estimation to action, we find that some LLMs disregard time costs completely when answering user queries, ignoring a 1,200-fold increase in wait time entirely. Together, our experiments reveal new challenges posed to LLMs as they navigate a dimension they cannot directly perceive, and highlight how their blind spots can even be simple cognitive abilities that humans take for granted.
  </details>

- **[AVCORP: Identifying and Resisting Deception via Communication Theory and Theory of Mind](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10176)**  `arXiv:2611.10176`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Fardin Saad, Zhe Zhang, Sanjana Cheerla, Amanul Haque, Munindar P. Singh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Today's LLM-based agents are vulnerable to deception by users and other agents. Defending against deception requires Theory of Mind (ToM) reasoning to infer the beliefs and goals of others as well as modeling tactics based on communication theory. To enable training models to detect deception, we adopt the well-known deception game, Avalon. We present a corpus, AVCORP, of 5,000 annotated utterances from 250 games of Avalon. AVCORP includes (1) 37 tactics mapped to a 4x4 behavior matrix, and (2) reasoning traces capturing abductive hypotheses, suspicion levels, and nested ToM beliefs. We train Qwen3-8B on AVCORP and evaluate it on 25 held-out games and 20 games played by humans. On held-out games, our best trained detector outperforms its untrained baseline and frontier models (GPT-5.6 Sol, Gemini 3.1 Pro, DeepSeek-V4-Pro), matching Claude Opus 5.5. On human games, every trained detector exceeds the untrained model in F1 score across all rounds.
  </details>

- **[PolicyGuide: From Guarding One Action to Guiding the Whole Workflow for Policy-Compliant LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19861)**  `arXiv:2608.19861`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Seongjae Kang, Taehyung Yu, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Customer-service LLM agents must follow organizational policy when acting on a user's behalf. Compliance failures arise from either forbidden actions, such as granting an ineligible change, or omitted procedural requirements, such as identification or confirmation. Runtime safeguards can intervene on risky actions, but action-local checks do not guide an agent through a multi-step procedure. Workflow-following systems support prescribed process execution, but primarily target workflow completion rather than safeguarding agent behavior. PolicyGuide instead compiles each domain policy into a workflow graph and invokes a proactive verifier at user-turn boundaries. From persisted graph state, the verifier reconciles open requests and returns step-specific remediation along a policy-compliant path. Across the $\tau^2$-bench airline, retail, and telecom domains with a GPT 5.4 agent and verifier, PolicyGuide raises mean $\mathrm{Pass}^4$ from $0.42$ to $0.62$, with the largest gain on telecom ($0.19$ to $0.61$), the most workflow-structured domain. The same workflows transfer to Claude Sonnet 4.6 and Gemini 2.5 Pro agents. Complementary evaluations find the lowest observed attack-success rate under adversarial users and the strongest procedural compliance in an independently validated workflow-level audit.
  </details>

- **[Weakly Supervised Distillation of Hallucination Signals into Transformer Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.06277)**  `arXiv:2604.06277`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.LG`  
  _Shoaib Sadiq Salehmohamed, Jinal Prashant Thakkar, Hansika Aredla, Shaik Mohammed Omar, Naveen Bera, Pulijala Sai Nikhila, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing hallucination detection methods for large language models (LLMs) rely on external verification at inference time, requiring gold answers, retrieval systems, or auxiliary judge models. We ask whether this external supervision can instead be distilled into the model's own representations during training, enabling hallucination detection from internal activations alone at inference time.We introduce a weak supervision framework that combines three complementary grounding signals: substring matching, sentence embedding similarity, and an LLM as a judge verdict to label generated responses as grounded or hallucinated without human annotation. Using this framework, we construct a 15000-sample dataset from SQuAD v2 (10500 train/development samples and a separate 5000-sample test set), where each example pairs a LLaMA-2-7B generated answer with its full per-layer hidden states and structured hallucination labels.We then train five probing classifiers: ProbeMLP (M0), LayerWiseMLP (M1), CrossLayerTransformer (M2), HierarchicalTransformer (M3), and CrossLayerAttentionTransformerV2 (M4), directly on these hidden states, treating external grounding signals as training-time supervision only. Our central hypothesis is that hallucination detection signals can be distilled into transformer representations, enabling internal detection without any external verification at inference time.Results support this hypothesis. Transformer-based probes achieve the strongest discrimination, with M2 performing best on 5-fold average AUC/F1, and M3 performing best on both single-fold validation and held-out test evaluation. We also benchmark inference efficiency: probe latency ranges from 0.15 to 5.62 ms (batched) and 1.55 to 6.66 ms (single sample), while end-to-end generation plus probe throughput remains approximately 0.231 queries per second, indicating negligible practical overhead.
  </details>

- **[Web World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.23676)**  `arXiv:2512.23676`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL` `cs.CV`  
  _Jichen Feng, Yifan Zhang, Chenggong Zhang, Yifu Lu, Shilong Liu, Mengdi Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language agents increasingly require persistent worlds in which they can act, remember, and learn. Existing approaches sit at two extremes: conventional web frameworks provide reliable but fixed contexts backed by databases, while fully generative world models aim for unlimited environments, but the world is constructed primarily through generation, making it harder to maintain a fixed, deterministic global framework and reducing controllability. In this work, we introduce the Web World Model (WWM), a middle ground where world state and ``physics'' are implemented in ordinary web code to ensure logical consistency, while large language models generate context, narratives, and high-level decisions on top of this structured latent state. We build a suite of WWMs on a realistic web stack, including an infinite travel atlas grounded in real geography, fictional galaxy explorers, web-scale encyclopedic and narrative worlds, and simulation- and game-like environments. Across these systems, we identify practical design principles for WWMs: separating code-defined rules from model-driven imagination, representing latent state as typed web interfaces, and utilizing deterministic generation to achieve unlimited but structured exploration. Our results suggest that web stacks themselves can serve as a scalable substrate for world models, enabling controllable yet open-ended environments.
  </details>

- **[OGDA: Ontology-Grounded Data Agents for Reliable Analysis over Heterogeneous Data Lakes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12114)**  `arXiv:2611.12114`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Boyi Zhang, Hong Ting Tsang, Yisen Gao, Qiao Xiao, LI Yufei, Haoyu Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Full-pipeline data analysis over real-world data lakes requires LLM agents to locate and integrate information across large collections of tables with different schemas. Existing data agents follow task-specific exploration, which exhausts the context window in massive lakes. As the context fills, the agent's reasoning degrades, leading it to locate and process incorrect data files. These errors propagate into the final answer. To overcome these limitations, we introduce Ontology-Grounded Data Agent (OGDA), which utilizes a compact ontology to reduce exploration overhead and constrain data processing. OGDA constructs the ontology through agentic hypothesis testing. At each step, a proposer agent induces schemas, concepts, and relations from raw sources, and a verifier checks each proposal against the underlying data. During task execution, the ontology serves as a representation of the data lake for the agent to explore. Every data-processing action is constrained by a task graph, which the agent can extend only after observing the semantic meaning of the data. Across KramaBench and DiscoveryBench with three LLM backbones, OGDA outperforms the strongest baselines by up to 10.82 points. These results suggest that explicitly modeling the data lake is a key step toward reliable end-to-end data agents.
  </details>

- **[FinCutBench: A Continually Updated Benchmark for Measuring the Temporal Boundaries of LLM Financial Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11588)**  `arXiv:2611.11588`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Siyuan Li, Ke Wan, Boyong Hou, Yujie Deng, Zixuan Yuan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In financial backtesting and other point-in-time tasks, external inputs can be restricted by date, but model parameters may retain financial facts disclosed later, introducing parametric look-ahead bias. We introduce FinCutBench, a continually updated monthly financial knowledge benchmark for measuring how far a fixed model's financial knowledge extends over calendar time. The current dataset contains 9,000 Chinese and English four-option multiple-choice questions from January 2023 to September 2026, covering Chinese and U.S. markets and four categories of financial information. We construct monthly knowledge profiles using the approximate months in which the tested facts became public. After a profile passes the decline test and knot checks, we define the FinCut Boundary as the first month when the fitted profile reaches its lower level, and report the stability of the estimated date under resampling. Boundary dates and localization precision vary across models. Using the independently measured Transition Start and Boundary as fixed references for downstream sample grouping, we find clear relative declines after the Boundary in U.S. asset ranking and earnings recall for some models, while Chinese-market ranking estimates vary across models and all eight disclosure-period-paired forecast-error comparisons have unadjusted $p$ values above 0.10. The FinCut Boundary thus provides an empirical reference for the temporal scope of parametric knowledge in point-in-time financial evaluation, supporting evaluation-window selection and result interpretation.
  </details>

- **[CoRA-Bench: Evaluating Evidence Preservation by Context Selectors Under Token Budgets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10238)**  `arXiv:2611.10238`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Huzaifa Pardawala, Siddhant Sukhani, Sudheer Chava, Agam Shah_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Context selection can reduce input length during long-input inference but can remove evidence required to answer a query. We introduce CoRA-Bench, a benchmark for evaluating evidence preservation under shared token budgets. We compare head and tail truncation, BM25, dense retrieval, dense retrieval with reranking, LLMLingua-2, and Provence across BABILong, LOONG Financial Spotlight, a custom NoLiMa diagnostic, and LongMemEval. Evidence-preservation results reveal task-dependent tradeoffs: BM25 achieves 0.921 supporting-fact recall on BABILong at 128K, dense retrieval and dense reranking each achieve 0.908 needle recall on NoLiMa at 64K, and dense retrieval achieves 0.993 answer-bearing-turn recall on LongMemEval at 64K. Evidence, token retention, and complementary reader evaluations use the same selected contexts. Reader evaluations use dataset-native answer metrics and three runs on the same questions, with paired bootstrap confidence intervals. At a 32K budget, BM25 achieves 46.20% BABILong answer accuracy and dense retrieval achieves 71.13% LongMemEval accuracy. The results show that method rankings vary across evidence types and between evidence recall and downstream accuracy.
  </details>

- **[DeepResearch RewardBench: Evaluating Reward Models for Deep Research Report](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10156)**  `arXiv:2611.10156`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Shudong Liu, Yuting Zhong, Guanhua Chen, Xinyi Yang, Junnan Liu, Lidia S. Chao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Empowered by Large Language Models (LLMs), deep research systems integrate online information into research reports through multi-step web searching and information synthesis. Evaluating these reports presents a significant challenge due to their long context, high information density, and domain specificity, necessitating the use of AI evaluation as a substitute for prohibitively expensive human assessment. However, few studies have examined the effectiveness of existing reward models or LLMs in understanding and evaluating such research reports. To bridge this gap, we introduce DeepResearch RewardBench, the first benchmark dedicated to evaluating reward modeling for research reports. We collect diverse report candidates from multiple agents or tool-augmented LLMs. These candidates are scored using query-specific golden rubrics, and we ensure data quality and difficulty through a combination of multi-round filtering and human verification. Our benchmark comprises preference datasets formatted as pairs, triplets, and quadruplets to test ranking capabilities across varying levels of difficulty. Extensive experiments demonstrate that while current reward models handle general coherence effectively, their performance significantly degrades when evaluating research reports. Generative LLMs acting as judges demonstrate comprehensive superiority in this domain, with comparative evaluation proving more effective than direct scoring methods. We anticipate that this benchmark will serve as a vital testbed for the evaluation and advancement of research agents.
  </details>

- **[PolicyGuard: A Dialogue-Grounded Sub-Agent Verifier for Policy Adherence in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.29225)**  `arXiv:2606.29225`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Seongjae Kang, Taehyung Yu, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents handle user requests on behalf of organizations through tool calls and must follow the company policies stated in their system prompts. Prior work approaches this as a safeguarding problem -- external checks that block non-compliant agent actions. We argue that policy adherence is broader: real workflows unfold across many turns, require explicit user confirmation and prerequisite reads, and hinge on dialogue rather than on any single argument value. We introduce PolicyGuard, a sub-agent verifier that shares the agent's view of the dialogue, reasons over the policy in context, and returns actionable remediation. On $\tau^2$-bench airline across GPT 5.4, Claude Sonnet 4.6, and Gemini 2.5 Pro, PolicyGuard improves $\mathrm{Pass}^4$, the fraction of tasks successful in all four trials, by +12.0 / +6.0 / +12.0 pp; pooled paired tests support an overall cross-vendor effect. We further evaluate PolicyGuard on the full retail and telecom base splits, covering all three $\tau^2$-bench customer-service domains. Further analyses show that PolicyGuard improves reliability beyond agent self-checking, catches more policy violations with fewer blocks than argument-level guards, and preserves its refusal accuracy under three adversarial probes.
  </details>

- **[PersonaDrive: Human-Style Retrieval-Augmented VLA Agents for Closed-Loop Driving Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.12616)**  `arXiv:2606.12616`  `cs.AI` `cs.CL`  
  _Mahmoud Srewa, Praneetsai Vasu Iddamsetty, Mohammad Abdullah Al Faruque, Salma Elmalaki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Closed-loop driving simulators mostly fill their environments with traffic agents that behave alike, whether they are rule-based or learned. Recent methods that control driving style learn it from labels inferred from existing driving recordings, from synthetic data generated to reflect each style, or from reward weights estimated by a large language model (LLM). All of these are indirect estimates of a style, not recordings of people who were instructed to drive in it. We introduce PersonaDrive, a vision-language-action (VLA) driving agent that takes its style from retrieved human demonstrations. The demonstrations come from a new dataset in which 15 participants drove CARLA routes on a driving simulator under conservative, neutral, and aggressive instructions. PersonaDrive trains a lightweight retrieval head on features from a frozen image encoder and on control signals over per-style databases, and fine-tunes one VLA backbone over the retrieved demonstrations. Switching style only means searching a different FAISS index, with no retraining. On the Bench2Drive benchmark without style conditioning, PersonaDrive improves the Driving Score by 4.6% over SimLingo and by 2.5% over HiP-AD, a gain that fine-tuning without retrieval does not reproduce. With style conditioning, PersonaDrive outperforms the strongest baseline, Drive My Way, in every style, with a Driving Score 6.7% to 11.6% higher. Its driving also follows the instruction, since average speed rises by 19.8% from the conservative to the aggressive style. Each traffic agent is evaluated individually as a driving policy. The dataset and model weights are available on the project page:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Automated Knowledge Acquisition Pipeline for Disassembly Instructions: A Comparison of NLP and Generative AI Frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12816)**  `arXiv:2611.12816`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tim Schnieders, Tareq Maximilian Gabriel Ahrari, Oliver Petrovic, Christian Brecher_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The push towards circularity and Re-X principles presents a challenge to SMEs, particularly due to increasing product complexity that impedes the adoption of disassembly automation. This paper introduces and compares two frameworks for extracting disassembly instructions from unstructured information sources, such as disassembly manuals. Our goal is to transform natural language text into a structured, curated and machine-readable format. We implemented and tested a rule-based Natural Language Processing (NLP) framework against a generative AI framework leveraging LLMs. The performance of both approaches was compared using real-world disassembly manuals. This work provides the necessary data foundation for developing and automating future disassembly processes.
  </details>

- **[LogCDM: Interpretable Log Anomaly Detection via Behavioral Graph Drift and Adaptive Signal Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12775)**  `arXiv:2611.12775`  `cs.AI`  
  _Suryansh Talukdar, Taha Sarwat, Jin Soung Yoo_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Log-based anomaly detection is increasingly dominated by large pretrained models, from BERT-scale encoders to 8B-parameter language models, which require GPUs at inference time and are difficult to deploy on the monitoring nodes where logs are produced. We present LogCDM, a compact detector that scores each log session using three complementary signals: the next-event surprise of a small causal Transformer, the distance between a graph-attention embedding of the session's own transition graph and that of a normal reference graph, and a transition-drift statistic that we show is exactly a frequency-weighted chi-squared divergence between transition kernels. The divergence view yields an asymptotic null distribution, a length-standardized score, and a per-edge decomposition that explains each alarm. A sigmoid gate fuses the three signals; under a shared-covariance Gaussian model, this gate is the Bayes posterior, and its learned weights indicate which signal each dataset relies on. With 8.1-8.8M parameters and no GPU required at inference, LogCDM reaches F1 scores of 0.984 on HDFS, 0.870 on chronologically split BGL, and 0.850 on Thunderbird, within 0.013-0.12 of an 8.1B-parameter LLM detector evaluated under the same protocol and above all compact baselines on BGL. It scores a session in 5.5-10.5 ms on a CPU. Ablations show that the structural signals add 0.025-0.030 F1 on BGL and Thunderbird but almost nothing on HDFS, consistent with the learned fusion weights.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Sunny Skies: Using Large Language Models to Predict Urban Traffic in Adverse Weather](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12635)**  `arXiv:2611.12635`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhu Xiao, Kuan Feng, Tong Li, Chenxi Liu, Gang Wang, Zhuo Tang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Urban traffic flow prediction is a cornerstone of intelligent transportation systems, yet accurate forecasting under adverse weather conditions remains a significant challenge. Existing data-driven models, while effective for routine patterns, often struggle with the sparse data and complex non-linear shifts associated with rare meteorological events. These methods typically treat environmental contexts as shallow numerical features, failing to capture the underlying semantic reasoning-why and how specific adverse conditions alter mobility behaviors across regions with different functional compositions. To address this limitation, we propose Multi-STG, a unified framework that integrates Large Language Model (LLM)-derived semantic priors with graph-based spatiotemporal learning. Multi-STG introduces an LLM-empowered semantic enhancement module that transforms comprehensive context descriptions such as weather, POIs, and holidays into high-level semantic embeddings, effectively providing a knowledge-driven complement to data scarcity. Furthermore, we design a multimodal attentional fusion mechanism to dynamically weigh heterogeneous inputs and a semantic-enhanced prediction head with gated residual calibration to robustly adjust forecasts based on semantic guidance. Extensive experiments on real-world multi-city private car datasets demonstrate that Multi-STG outperforms state-of-the-art baselines, reducing MAE by 11.82\% on average relative to the strongest baseline across three cities, while maintaining stable performance under sparse and non-stationary conditions.
  </details>

- **[SciMARS: Multi-Agent Recursive Self-Improvement for Closed-Loop Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12552)**  `arXiv:2611.12552`  `cs.AI`  
  _Weimin Wu, Han Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose SciMARS, a framework for recursive task-local adaptation of multi-agent harnesses in closed-loop scientific discovery. Multi-agent systems coordinate analysis, experimental design, and verification as scientific evidence evolves. However, two problems limit their effectiveness: (i) without shared memory, agents lose observations and repeat work; (ii) fixed role procedures, handoffs, and memory policies perpetuate mistakes despite retained evidence. We address these problems with a shared evidence graph and separate component editors. SciMARS adapts roles and coordination; SciMARS + Memory adds a third editor for how agents record, retrieve, and receive evidence. Candidate probes guide composition and selection of the next harness. We evaluate scientific discovery through experimental-data replay and a digital twin. SciMARS + Memory resolves 12/18 GPT-6-Astra and 2/18 GLM-5.3 attempts on Autonomous Science Bench, compared with 8/18 and 1/18 for the base multi-agent team. On ScienceAgentBench and AppWorld, it solves 14/38 and 11/20 tasks, compared with 11/38 and 5/20 for the base team. These results support combining shared memory with harness adaptation across scientific and interactive tasks.
  </details>

- **[AMSchem: Automated Schematic Visualization of AMS Circuits via Verifiable Agentic Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12333)**  `arXiv:2611.12333`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yichen Shi, Zhiyuan Wang, Jianing Cui, Yao Lai, Jinting Zhang, Zhuofu Tao, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For decades, the netlist-to-schematic gap has remained a persistent challenge in design automation, especially for analog and mixed-signal (AMS) circuits. Netlists encode connectivity, not visual presentation, making their manual conversion into readable schematics time-consuming and dependent on expert conventions. The same gap leaves LLM-based design tools without structured visual feedback, forcing them to iterate on circuits they cannot see. We present AMSchem, a training-free, verifier-guided agentic framework that automatically generates human-readable schematics from AMS netlists. AMSchem separates circuit-aware visual planning (DrawingIntent) from executable schematic geometry (SchematicIR), and applies localized visual feedback with bounded, revalidated repair patches. Deterministic validators enforce device preservation, pin-net consistency, and geometric correctness throughout. For evaluation, we construct AMSchem-Bench comprising 64 circuits of 12 types. On the 40-circuit schematic subset, AMSchem with gpt-5.5 achieves 85.0\% validity (vs. 32.5\% direct generation). When integrated as a visual feedback module in LLM-based analog design, AMSchem resolves all 13 previously failed cases from AnalogCoder benchmark across five models, substantially improving the success rate. Our dataset and benchmark are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Depth as a Candidate Source: Portfolio Allocation for Recurrent Test-Time Scaling in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12293)**  `arXiv:2611.12293`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chaoyuan Hao, Wentao Yue, Yanyan Wu, Boyuan Gao, Tianyou Lai, Maolin Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recurrent latent reasoning in large lanugage models (LLMs) scales test-time computation by increasing recurrent depth, naturally framing inference as a problem of deciding when to stop. We identify a previously overlooked limitation of this single-depth view through systematic preliminary analysis: deeper recurrence can lose correct solutions found at shallower depths, while depth-specific success sets remain broadly non-nested even after controlling for marginal accuracy. This suggests that depth determines not only computation cost, but also access to distinct candidate solutions. Motivated by this finding, we propose Depth Portfolio Allocation (DPA), which reformulates recurrent test-time scaling as budgeted allocation across depth-specific candidate sources. DPA supports both fixed portfolios and feedback-guided sequential allocation under a shared inference budget. Experiments on GSM8K, SVAMP, MultiArith, and MBPP show that mixed-depth portfolios expand oracle coverage under matched candidate counts and nominal costs, while feedback-guided allocation generally matches or improves uniform allocation and can reduce computation in high-budget settings. Code and data are avaible at :this https URL.
  </details>

- **[PARIS: A multi-agent LLM framework for end-to-end, PRISMA-guided systematic literature reviews with pre-synthesis corpus quality assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12222)**  `arXiv:2611.12222`  `cs.AI`  
  _Christophe Cruz, Hussam Ghanem, Samir Jabbar, Ait Kbir, Hocine Cherifi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Systematic literature reviews (SRs) remain slow and labor-intensive, and most automation tools address single stages of the workflow. We present PARIS (PRISMA Agentic Research Intelligence System), an open-source framework in which a conductor agent running in Claude Code orchestrates 15 specialized large language model (LLM) agents, from scoping a research question to exporting a manuscript draft. Agents are specified declaratively, coordinate exclusively through persisted artifacts that the conductor validates before every transition, and delegate retrieval, bibliometric analysis and scoring to deterministic Python utilities. Three human checkpoints gate study inclusion, corpus adequacy and the synthesis outline. We also introduce QualityScore, a pre-synthesis index that aggregates normalized network, bibliometric, semantic and process sub-scores into a 0-100 grade. Two case studies illustrate both: AI chatbots and university students' learning (388 records, 100 included) and AI-assisted SR methods (131 records, 123 included, QualityScore 69.1 recomputed with a proxy network sub-score). They show end-to-end feasibility across domains and the diagnostic use of QualityScore: in the first run, corpus improvements chosen at the corpus-adequacy checkpoint raised the score from 74.6 to 81.9 and the number of pre-2023 studies from 1 to 19. Audits of both runs exposed failure modes: incomplete retrieval, counts that drifted between agents, unrecorded or delegated checkpoint decisions and a grade that depends on how citation data are handled. PARIS fully supports 8 of the 27 PRISMA 2020 items and partially supports 11. Output quality has so far been judged only by an internal reviewer agent; independent validation against human reviews is the next step.
  </details>

- **[Toward Sustainable Agentic AI: Carbon Tradeoffs Across Iterative Self-Refinement and Tool-Augmented Coding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12128)**  `arXiv:2611.12128`  `cs.AI`  
  _Boris Ruf, Adrien de Mailly Nesle, Marcin Detyniecki_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Agentic language model systems can improve performance either by iterating on their own outputs or by using external tools in grounded environments. While these strategies can reduce error rates and substitute for larger models in some settings, their environmental cost remains relatively underexplored. We present two complementary empirical studies that estimate the energy and carbon footprint of agentic AI across settings of increasing complexity. Study~1 evaluates iterative verbal self-refinement: GPT-4.1-mini at $k = 1$--$5$ steps versus a GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline on two MATH benchmark splits. Study~2 evaluates tool-augmented agentic coding: GPT-4.1-mini, Claude Opus~4.6, and GPT-5.3-codex on SWE-bench Lite, where agents operate inside Docker-isolated environments with access to code execution and file editing tools. In Study~1, $k=3$ refinements close a 16-percentage-point accuracy gap to within 1~point, but nearly triple the carbon footprint relative to the cheap single-pass baseline while remaining below the GPT-4.1 single-pass baseline under the current token-based carbon scenario. In Study~2, GPT-5.3-codex achieves the highest resolution rate in this sample (81.2\%), ahead of Claude Opus~4.6 (68.9\%) and GPT-4.1-mini (25.0\%), but resolved-task-normalized efficiency remains mixed: GPT-4.1-mini is lowest on estimated carbon and cost per resolved task, while GPT-5.3-codex delivers substantially higher task success. All carbon estimates cover output-token inference only; prompt tokens, which exceed 99\% of tokens in Study~2, and tool execution are not charged, so the figures are lower bounds on the full agentic footprint.
  </details>

- **[System Optimizations for Cost-Efficient Agent Harnesses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12122)**  `arXiv:2611.12122`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jongseok Park, Jiaxiang Yu, Eric Zhou, Tony Hong, Sunga Kim, Yusuf Afifi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI agent harnesses play the critical role of providing LLMs with an interface to a computing environment. Recent studies show that AI agents' task-execution accuracy depends mostly on the LLM used, while harness-side variances such as system prompts or tools have minimal impact. Rather, such harness-side variances show a larger impact on the cost of agent executions, achieving cost reductions up to 2.6 times on the same model and task. To further explore these harness-side cost optimization opportunities, we analyze the cost accumulation patterns across various agent traces and identify system opportunities to reduce token use and LLM calls without modifying the model or affecting task accuracy. Based on this analysis, we design Agency, a harness interposition platform that provides a unified abstraction and allows easy and portable implementation of harness-side system optimizations on existing off-the-shelf harnesses such as Claude Code or OpenAI Codex. Using Agency, we implement proof-of-concept optimization techniques based on our analysis and achieve cost reductions up to 35% across various real-world benchmarks on existing harnesses without compromising accuracy.
  </details>

- **[From Fluent Text to Ontology-Compatible Definitions: A Quality-Control Agentic Workflow](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12095)**  `arXiv:2611.12095`  `cs.AI`  
  _Kun Feng, Chenchen Chu, Yan Liu, Haojie Sun, Jijun Tang, Chen Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biomedical ontologies are structured knowledge systems that must evolve continuously to represent newly emerging knowledge, but their manual maintenance remains time-consuming and laborintensive. Textual definitions are especially costly because they must be readable to humans and compatible with controlled entities, relations, and logical constraints. Large language models (LLMs) offer a promising way to assist ontology engineering tasks, including definition generation. However, textual plausibility does not guarantee knowledge-system compatibility. A generated definition may be fluent and close to a reference definition, yet still fail to ground its implied entities or violate ontology-level constraints. Directly inserting such definitions into ontology libraries may therefore introduce entity-level hallucinations and logical inconsistencies. To address this problem, we propose two structured-knowledge incompatibility metrics, Entity Error Rate (EER) and Logical Inconsistency Rate (LIR), to quantify entity grounding and logical consistency for LLM-generated ontology definitions. We further develop an agentic quality-control (QC) workflow that treats definition generation as an iterative process rather than a direct decoding step: it generates definitions, extracts axioms, checks entity existence and logical consistency, and uses the resulting diagnostics to drive repair loops. Comprehensive experiments on Disease Ontology, Uberon Ontology, and Uberon-ConstraintStress demonstrate that EER and LIR complement conventional reference-based metrics by evaluating whether generated content can be grounded and checked inside the target ontology. The workflow effectively decreases EER while maintaining strong conventional text-generation performance. LIR further exposes sparse ontology-level logical failures that are invisible to text similarity metrics, although their repair remains limited.
  </details>

- **[COMPASS: Composable Policy-Amortized Structured Search for LLM-Based Optimization Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12060)**  `arXiv:2611.12060`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nguyen Le Minh Hoang, Van Duc Cuong, Bui Trong Duc, Huynh Thi Thanh Binh_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models offer a promising technique for translating natural-language optimization problems into solver code, but their reliability remains constrained by the quality of template retrieval. While recent structured methods improve over flat prompting through hierarchical taxonomy search, their traversal rules remain fixed or LLM-driven at inference time, leaving retrieval unable to improve from past successes and failures. This motivates treating template retrieval as a policy that can be learned from modeling experience, rather than as a static inference-time rule. We propose COMPASS, a policy-amortized framework for retrieving optimization templates in LLM-based solver modeling, which represents the template library as a directed acyclic graph and formulates retrieval as a sequential decision process over template nodes. A deep Q-network learns a reusable traversal policy from template-level judgments and execution feedback, reducing reliance on fixed heuristic traversal through feedback-driven graph navigation. To support extensible retrieval, COMPASS embeds problem descriptions and template nodes in a shared semantic space, allowing newly inserted templates to be scored from their text descriptions without redefining the action space. Experiments across multiple LLM backbones and optimization-modeling benchmarks demonstrate that COMPASS consistently improves over heuristic structured retrieval, with the largest gains on complex and mixed-category problems.
  </details>

- **[Evaluating AI Agents for Industrial-Level Chip Floorplanning: A Framework and Empirical Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12048)**  `arXiv:2611.12048`  `cs.AI`  
  _Nova-Silicon Team_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Floorplanning is an early step in turning a logical circuit into a physical chip: it positions large circuit modules and constrains the space available for subsequent logic placement and wiring. These decisions affect congestion and timing, but their physical consequences emerge through downstream implementation. For AI agents, the task requires translating engineering constraints into spatial decisions whose quality cannot be judged by successful tool execution alone. We present an evaluation framework that connects submitted layouts to a prescribed downstream implementation flow, checks geometric validity, measures physical quality, and reports resource use. On one industrial design whose 99 macros occupy approximately 71\% of a fixed L-shaped layout region, we compare our specialized system Nova-SI with general-purpose engineering agents and agents restricted to file access, obtaining complete physical measurements for 39 layout outputs. Under our 0--100 composite quality score, Nova-SI achieves a mean of 57.97 without online feedback from the prescribed power-grid and placement evaluation, compared with 31.17 for the strongest measured general-purpose configuration, GPT-6 Astra. The difference is supported by raw congestion and timing measurements and persists across scoring-sensitivity checks. Layout-level analysis further shows that similar global utilization can conceal substantially different local congestion. Together, the protocol and case study assess agents through the physical consequences of their delivered layouts and provide evidence about system performance on this industrial instance.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Multi-Step Chemical Mechanism Prediction with Graph-Guided Preference Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.12013)**  `arXiv:2611.12013`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yu Zhang, Ruijie Yu, Xuelin Zhao, Jinming Yang, Feng Zhu, Xiaokang Yang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predicting multi-step chemical reaction mechanisms requires identifying plausible intermediates and correctly ordering the elementary reaction steps. Existing computational methods focus on single-step elementary reaction prediction, limiting their ability to generalize to unseen reaction spaces. We introduce a graph-guided framework for multi-step chemical mechanism prediction that leverages a chemical mechanism knowledge graph (ChemMechKG) to guide large language models (LLMs). To improve pathway prediction, we develop a graph-aligned reaction mechanism predictor (GRMP) that uses graph neural networks to generate synthetic candidate pathways for pathway optimization. During inference, we design a relation retrieval (RR) module to extract relevant graph evidence from the KG for pathway refinement. Experiments show that GRMP achieves the highest pathway accuracy among all compared methods on ChemMechQA and oMe-Gold, reaching 48.1% and 57.9%, respectively. On a fixed sample of 110 out-of-distribution reactions from ChemMechQA-Hard, GRMP achieves 36.4% top-1 pathway accuracy. LLM-as-Judge and human assessment further demonstrate its ability to generalize to unseen reactions.
  </details>

- **[AutoQS: Automated Quantization Strategy Design with Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11975)**  `arXiv:2611.11975`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhen Liu, Zhiqin Yang, Yonggang Zhang, Jianyi Liu, Jinjun Wang, Jingwen Fu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantization reduces LLM deployment costs, but effective strategies must balance model quality and storage. LLM-driven search can automate strategy design through repeated proposal and evaluation. This process must identify useful strategies within a limited evaluation budget. The key challenge is that \textit{actual quantization is expensive, while inexpensive proxies may not reflect quantized-model performance.} To address this challenge, we present AutoQS, an \textbf{Auto}mated \textbf{Q}uantization \textbf{S}trategy Design with LLMs. AutoQS keeps weight precision fixed and searches group-size allocations across layers and operators under deployment constraints. Evaluation history guides coordinated LLM proposals, while deterministic validation ensures compatible allocations. An inexpensive quality proxy evaluates candidates, and an candidate repository retains alternative allocations to inform subsequent proposals. Periodic actual quantization complements these proxy evaluations with measured quality and checkpoint storage, providing feedback for further search within the evaluation budget. Experiments on Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct show that AutoQS improves seven-task average accuracy over uniform-group GPTQ and AWQ by 0.70 and 1.12 percentage points, respectively, at four-bit weight precision.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Agents Organize Their Own Memory?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11929)**  `arXiv:2611.11929`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zichuan Fu, Junjia Qi, Wenlin Zhang, Yimin Deng, Shirong Wang, Jinyang Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In most LLM agents, weights are fixed after deployment, so new information must be retained in external memory. Existing approaches often rely on predefined records and fixed organizational rules, which cannot readily accommodate evolving tasks and interactions. This raises the question: can LLM agents organize their own memory using ordinary documents? We introduce self-organized memory (SoMem), a framework that stores memory in linked documents. The agent decides what to summarize, how to group related information, and when to revise existing topics, and every summary it produces stays linked to the original interactions as evidence. The runtime validates updates and automatically derives temporal, relational, and recent-memory views from the organized content. At query time, the agent chooses what to search and read, following document links and inspecting original evidence as needed. On LoCoMo, BEAM-100K, and LongMemEval-S, SoMem outperforms the strongest baseline by 2.66, 3.10, and 2.00 percentage points, respectively. The constructed memory can be reused across models, and additional reasoning effort can further improve answer quality. These findings demonstrate the feasibility of agents organizing and using their own persistent memory through ordinary documents. Code is available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Layer Roles Matter: Mitigating Modality Imbalance in Multimodal Emotion Reasoning via Adaptive Layer Profiling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11907)**  `arXiv:2611.11907`  `cs.AI`  
  _Pengyu Huang, Peipei Song, Beier Zhu, Zhiyuan Han, Jinpeng Hu, Xun Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Emotion Reasoning (MER) requires perceiving and integrating emotion cues from multiple modalities. Recent advances increasingly leverage Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) to enhance reasoning capacity. However, due to the inherent heterogeneity in conveying emotions across modalities, these models often suffer from modality imbalance and confront flawed emotion understanding. In this paper, we conduct an in-depth analysis on MER models and uncover two key empirical insights. (i) In MER, modality imbalance manifests two distinct failure modes: unimodal over-reliance and insufficient cross-modal utilization, which call for different intervention strategies. (ii) MLLM layers exhibit implicit functional hierarchies in multimodal emotion processing, playing separable roles in stabilizing unimodal representations and integrating cross-modal information. Motivated by these observations, we propose a Layer-Aware Modality Rebalancing (LAMR) framework by explicitly aligning intervention strategies with layer roles. Specifically, we first introduce two quantized metrics for adaptive layer role profiling. In each layer, Modality Entropy (ME) measures the dispersion of modality contributions, and Cross-modal Intensity (CMI) captures the strength of cross-modal interactions. Then, we respectively enhance modality equilibrium in stabilization layers and cross-modal propagation in integration layers. Extensive experiments on two comprehensive MER benchmarks demonstrate that LAMR effectively mitigates modality imbalance and improves emotion reasoning across diverse MLLM backbones, without additional training.
  </details>

- **[MOTIF: LLM-Defined Material Taxonomies for OOD Property Prediction via Invariant Feature Selection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11846)**  `arXiv:2611.11846`  `cs.AI`  
  _Gwangseon Jang, Sieun Yun, Dong Ho Lee, Kyong-Ha Lee, Chanuk Lim, Jin Woo Choi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization remains a central challenge in materials property prediction, where models must extrapolate to novel chemistries beyond the training distribution. While large language models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong scientific reasoning, their direct use for quantitative prediction remains limited. To address this, we systematically evaluate multiple roles for LLM assistance, finding that only taxonomy-based grouping consistently improves OOD generalization over a strong kNN+GBT baseline. Building on this, we here propose MOTIF, a framework that leverages LLMs not as predictors, but as generators of candidate taxonomies that partition materials into chemically meaningful environments. These taxonomies are evaluated via training-only pseudo-OOD validation, and only those that improve generalization are retained. Invariant feature selection is then applied within each accepted taxonomy, followed by gated aggregation across taxonomies. We evaluate on 2D halide perovskites, where bandgap prediction must generalize across distinct organic spacer families. On this benchmark, MOTIF reduces mean OOD MAE from 0.122 to 0.111 eV. Under a frozen dev/test protocol, the deployable configuration further improves errors from 0.123 to 0.117 eV (electronic bandgap) and from 0.181 to 0.173 eV (optical bandgap). Beyond this primary task, we validate transferability across additional materials datasets, including 3D perovskite solar cell efficiencies and Matbench benchmarks with structure-based splits. Across domains, MOTIF consistently improves robustness under distribution shift, outperforming expert-defined groupings, rule-based heuristics, and clustering baselines, while remaining competitive with strong classical selection methods. These results demonstrate that LLMs are most effective not as predictors, but as tools for discovering structure in data.
  </details>

- **[State Media at Retrieval Time: A Large-Scale Audit of Russian State-Aligned Sources in AI Chatbot Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11727)**  `arXiv:2611.11727`  `cs.AI`  
  _Laura Kurek, Elijah Covert, Rafael I Bonilla, Eric Gilbert, Ceren Budak_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The Pravda Network is a Russian state-aligned media aggregator, with over 110 websites and more than 15 million articles published since 2023. Given its prolific and repetitive output, emerging scholarship argues that the Pravda Network was built to influence AI systems. We conduct a large-scale audit of leading AI chatbots to understand: (1) the retrieval rates of Pravda Network articles, (2) the conditions of retrieval, and (3) whether chatbot responses affirm or caveat this content. We build a 10,000 prompt corpus from sampled Pravda Network headlines varied along two dimensions (headline rewording and user skepticism) and query 11 AI chatbots via the web interface (n=104,997 completed responses). Overall, we find susceptibility to Pravda Network retrieval to be infrequent---appearing in 7.3% of responses---and highly platform dependent---with three chatbots responsible for 94% of retrievals (this http URL, Claude, and Copilot). Among the frontier models, Claude is an outlier: ChatGPT, Gemini, and Meta AI retrieved no Pravda Network articles, likely indicative of a domain-level filtering policy. All 11 chatbots, however, retrieved other Russian state-aligned media to some degree (median=13.56% of responses). When Pravda Network articles are surfaced, caveat rates vary widely by platform (from 5.3% to 76.2%), withthis http URLproviding the fewest caveats. We also identify the tactic of headline washing: where 9.92% of sampled Pravda Network headlines attribute their reporting to other, often more credible, media outlets, while the articles themselves are republished from Russian state-aligned media. We discuss headline washing's potential to launder state media as independent reporting. With this audit, we provide a systematic analysis of AI chatbots' susceptibility to Russia's latest attempt to manipulate the information environment.
  </details>

- **[CASCoDe: A Closed-Loop Agentic Framework for Analog Sizing and Layout Co-Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11710)**  `arXiv:2611.11710`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziming Wei, Bingyang Liu, David Z. Pan_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Expert analog layouts differ from auto-generated ones in density and regularity, not only in legality: experts make a succession of discrete, hierarchical physical decisions and revisit each after extracted electrical results, while most existing automated flows act on a physical search space fixed before the controller runs. We present CASCoDe, a closed-loop LLM agent framework for analog netlist-to-layout design that formalizes those expert decisions as a hierarchical discrete action space-sizing selection, structural floorplan, local layout refinement, routing-attempt selection-on which the agent runs feedback-driven tree search from a circuit netlist to a verified GDS. The agent authors the circuit-specific floorplans and scripts, and an execution layer of interface adaptations over adapted tools make every action land at physical implementation and be judged by DRC, LVS, and extracted-RC simulation. On 20 SKY130 amplifiers spanning single-, two-, and three-stage topologies, CASCoDe is the only flow that delivers a DRC- and LVS-clean routed layout when meeting design specifications for every circuit. It reduces area by 59-85% (mean 71%) against a conventional P&R flow on identical PCells, raises mean device density from 0.39 to 0.74 against APPlace, MAGICAL, ALIGN and ICPI. A layout that beats every baseline in area and legality appears after 11 minutes of agent time on average, and ablations show that the structured action space and the feedback-guided tree search each reduce area and search cost.
  </details>

- **[Self-Indexing Latent Memories for Access-Aware Continual Instruction Tuning of LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11684)**  `arXiv:2611.11684`  `cs.AI`  
  _Dhruv Dixit, Paritosh Pandey, Snehalkumar 'Neil' S. Gaikwad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Continual instruction tuning often preserves old skills with task-private modules. Inference still requires selecting the right module from the prompt alone. We introduce Task-Isolated Latent Adapter Continual Training (TILA-CT), a replay-free method that makes each task module self-indexing. Each task receives a latent memory and adapter bank in a frozen LLM. During adaptation, the memory gathers causal-prefix information and injects task-conditioned context. For access, its persistent state provides the routing prototype used by a shared prompt-only router. On the 8-task CT-Pipeline stream, TILA-CT achieves 65.19 acquisition and 63.98 final macro score, with 1.38 points of average forgetting. A middle-reversed order gives 65.38 acquisition and 64.17 final macro score. Across 6 backbones, final scores remain within 1.5 points of acquisition. Ablations support memory-derived routing keys and causal gathering. Final macro routing accuracy is 93.9\%, and oracle routing recovers 1.21 final macro points. These results separate preserved task computation from learned access to it.
  </details>

- **[RADE: Risk-Adjusted Deployment Efficiency, A Framework for Routing, Memory, and Escalation in Safe LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11659)**  `arXiv:2611.11659`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chaymaa Abbas, Kareem Hassani, Mariette Awad_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A deployed language model does not merely answer questions. It spends computation, consumes time, consults evidence, exposes memory, invites or avoids human review, and sometimes makes mistakes whose consequences are radically unequal. Yet LLM systems are still commonly compared as if the relevant object were a single model and the relevant outcome were a single benchmark score. This paper argues that the unit of analysis should instead be the deployment policy: the mechanism that decides when to retrieve, when to remember, when to call a larger model, when to verify, and when not to answer at all. We introduce risk-adjusted deployment efficiency (RADE), a system-level objective that scores the utility a deployment delivers net of inference, retry, oversight, and priced expected failure cost, while treating harms that cannot be priced as hard budgets alongside constraints on latency, privacy, and resources. The framework brings selective routing, retrieval and governed memory, efficient specialization, uncertainty estimation, abstention, and human escalation into a common account. We then examine five deployment regimes in which the same nominal LLM capability leads to different rational architectures, and propose a reporting protocol based on risk-coverage behavior, end-to-end cost, update drift, memory exposure, evidence quality, and escalation load. The central claim is modest but consequential: frontier capability should be treated as a resource to allocate, not a default state to maintain. A safe and sustainable system is not the one that always uses the strongest model; it is the one that spends capability where the reduction in risk or increase in utility justifies the cost.
  </details>

- **[Inside the Trading Loop: Markets, Models, and Moves with LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11657)**  `arXiv:2611.11657`  `cs.AI`  
  _Renee Jia, Di Mu, Yao Yu, Tianrui Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We built a hybrid multi-agent trading system that combines large language model (LLM) analysts with momentum-based portfolio construction and rule-based execution. Company-news and macroeconomic agents return structured reports to a coordinator that sets stock weights and cash reserves. We evaluate the portfolio engine using its broker-recorded paper account, which operated with deterministic fallbacks, and compare LLM-enabled configurations through retrospective replays. From March 11 to August 28, 2026, the account returned 34.5%, compared with 14.4% for the S&P 500 fund SPY over the same dates. This raw return advantage came with a larger maximum drawdown. Reconstructed policies also finish above matched SPY and QQQ references when assessments are used in the same session or deferred to the next. Design comparisons examine cash authority, selective invocation, and response contracts. A constant-cash control gives a return estimate close to stance-based sizing, and shorter report contracts improve observed parsing but can change recommendations.
  </details>

- **[LiveAsset: Learning Intent Assets from Corrective Interactions for Agent Runtime Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11560)**  `arXiv:2611.11560`  `cs.AI`  
  _Guanming Liu, Weihang Wang, Peng Zhang, Meng Wu, Kainan Tu, Jiahao Liu, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents acting across web applications and online services often need multi-turn corrections to align their work with users' underspecified goals and preferences. In practice, users may identify problems in the current outcome without specifying the desired result, so learning user intentions requires interpreting each correction against the agent's preceding and revised outcomes. These expectations are often connected to a particular context, making it hard to know when they apply and how to ensure the agent later acts as the user expects. To this end, we propose LiveAsset, an intent harness that learns reusable intent assets from corrective interactions and uses them for runtime control of agent execution. LiveAsset jointly interprets each correction with the agent's original and revised results to infer user intent, incrementally updating the assets while preserving earlier requirements that remain applicable. The learned requirements guide agent execution from task entry and provide criteria for runtime verification, which uses the explanations to assess applicability before each reply is delivered and directs the agent to revise the result to address unmet requirements while preserving those already satisfied. Following a learning phase in which simulated users progressively reveal requirements through multi-turn corrections, we evaluate LiveAsset using same-task replay of the original requests on {\pi}-Bench, PERMA, and Vita and cross-task transfer on PAST-Bench and SkillEvolBench. Experiments show that LiveAsset better fulfills requirements learned from corrective interactions and improves performance on these benchmarks compared with memory, skill, and rule-based harness baselines. Code is available atthis http URL.
  </details>

- **[InfraRSI: Harnessing Recursive Self-Improvement for Training Infrastructure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11558)**  `arXiv:2611.11558`  `cs.AI`  
  _InfraRSI Team, Yuchuan Tian, Haocheng Mei, Ye Yuan, Chao Xu, Boxun Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) accelerates isolated algorithm and kernel synthesis, distributed LLM training depends on tightly coupled launch settings, operator kernels, and communication schedules, where single edits often hit out-of-memory (OOM) boundaries, compiler conflicts, measurement jitter, or silent numerical drift. We present InfraRSI, the agentic harness that turns open-ended human commands into verified training optimizations via a five-stage workflow: init locks editable scope and a fixed task contract over workload, loss, and timing invariants to prevent reward hacking, doctor profiles baseline phases and calibrates cluster noise, run co-optimizes launch knobs and code patches before re-verifying top candidates against the baseline and logging findings to a cross-hardware experience bank, and report and apply audit source paths, track failed-trial costs, and commit promoted changes. Rather than stepping greedily from a single incumbent, InfraRSI initializes from diverse experience-seeded configurations and fits global execution-time and censored peak-memory models across all trials, including OOMs. Using these models, it prices per-setting memory costs, revises miscalibrated LLM priors from live runs, and pairs memory-hungry optimizations with memory savers and verified code patches. Across four single- and multi-node dense and Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) LLM workloads, InfraRSI achieves a speedup of up to $2.75\times$ and lifts mean Model FLOPs Utilization (MFU) from $17.4\%$ to $30.0\%$, outperforming existing tuners and coding agents while transferring experience across GPUs. As it improves training infrastructure efficiency, InfraRSI forms a two-level Recursive Self-Improvement loop---revising priors online within runs and distilling experience across clusters---to push foundation model scaling.
  </details>

- **[GOPR-CAD: Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning for CAD Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11470)**  `arXiv:2611.11470`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yitian Pang, Yifan Li, Xiaowei Yue, Li Zheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generating accurate CAD models requires substantial engineering expertise and careful control of geometry, topology, and mechanical constraints. Text-to-CAD aims to simplify this process, but current LLM-based methods often generate CAD programs directly. Language models remain unreliable when producing long code while coordinating coupled geometric, topological, and dimensional constraints. Consequently, executable programs may still produce structurally incorrect models, which execution rate and rendered appearance may fail to reveal. To address these limitations, we propose GOPR-CAD, an agentic Text-to-CAD pipeline based on Graph-of-Operations Progressive Reasoning. A language model acts as a decision agent over encapsulated CAD operations and progressively constructs an executable operation graph, which a deterministic compiler converts into the final CAD model. This separation requires no additional training and supports different language models. To evaluate structural fidelity, we construct MHCAD, a benchmark of 300 standard mechanical part requirements derived from Machinery's Handbook. Their explicit structures enable reference-free STEP/B-rep evaluation. GOPR-CAD achieves 82.7% Structural Yield on MHCAD, and 87.2% Structural Yield on an external CADPrompt subset.
  </details>

- **[BiVerify: Closing the Loop Between Backward and Forward Verification for Mathematical Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11368)**  `arXiv:2611.11368`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Xin Zhao, Mengqi Wei, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have made substantial progress in mathematical reasoning but still produce incorrect solutions to complex problems, indicating the need for more reliable self-correction mechanisms. Mathematical self-correction typically identifies errors through verification, which can proceed backward or forward. The two directions provide complementary verification capabilities. Backward verification checks a candidate answer against the original constraints, bypassing complex intermediate reasoning, but cannot ensure correct reasoning or objective completeness. Forward verification follows the problem conditions and current solution, enabling it to locate reasoning errors and assess objective completeness, but may overlook key constraints in complex solution chains. We propose BiVerify, a dynamically scheduled framework for bidirectional mathematical verification. BiVerify begins with backward verification to screen the answer against the original constraints, followed by forward verification of the reasoning process; subsequent direction switching, state resetting, and re-verification are governed by verification outcomes. Upon failure, the system revises the candidate or verification flow until the same candidate passes both directions. We also introduce shared mathematical tools that separate logical reasoning from precise computation. We systematically evaluate BiVerify on five challenging mathematical reasoning benchmarks using multiple mainstream DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini models. BiVerify exceeds the strongest representative public baseline evaluated in this work by 13.5 percentage points in average accuracy, with the margin reaching 31.2 percentage points on GPT. Further analysis shows that BiVerify's overall performance exceeds that of either verification direction alone.
  </details>

- **[Separation of Skilling Context for Standard Operating Procedure-based Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11348)**  `arXiv:2611.11348`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mitsuhiro Nakayama, Toshiaki Sota, Takehiko Yamaguchi, Michiaki Tatsubori_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Standard Operating Procedures (SOPs) are kept concise for human readability, so LLM agents that generate executable skills from them lack the tacit knowledge and implementation details needed for automation. Human intervention fills these gaps, but must be repeated as SOPs evolve---a problem we call \textit{SOP-skill drift}. We propose managing SOPs and Contextual Knowledge (CK) separately, together with a \textit{contract-aware} evaluation that distinguishes genuine violations from CK-resolved differences. On eight Indeed-derived workflows (single LLM), it raises the functional-equivalence rate from 12\% to 58\%. Under three SOP-revision perturbations, previously extracted CK already covers 79.5\% of newly introduced automation gaps.
  </details>

- **[Verify Before You Fetch: Reliable KV Cache Compression for LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.11319)**  `arXiv:2611.11319`  `cs.AI`  
  _Devanshu Jha, Gaurav Kumar, Agniva Chowdhury, Kumar Krishna Agrawal, Aditya Desai_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-context applications, long-lived agents, and multi-agent systems require large language models (LLMs) to process and retain extensive information. In these settings, the KV cache can exceed GPU high-bandwidth memory (HBM) capacity, necessitating offloading to host memory, where CPU-to-GPU transfers can dominate inference latency. While KV cache compression can reduce data movement, query-agnostic compression that guarantees accurate attention for arbitrary queries is extremely challenging, if not impossible. We introduce VerKV, a hybrid approach that provides $(\epsilon, \delta)$ guarantees for approximating attention on any given query. VerKV maintains a compact set of query-agnostic pivotal tokens on the GPU, together with a token-level sketch of the full KV cache, while retaining the complete cache in CPU memory. We introduce a novel stochastic sampling procedure, PoissonComp, to select the pivotal tokens. At inference time, our proposed KVFetch algorithm uses the pivotal tokens and the sketch to estimate the relative error of the resulting attention computation. If the estimate satisfies a user-specified $(\epsilon, \delta)$ tolerance, inference proceeds without accessing host memory. Otherwise, VerKV performs query-aware retrieval to fetch additional tokens from the CPU, refining the attention computation to meet the required tolerance. Thus, VerKV enables attention with bounded approximation error while minimizing CPU-to-GPU transfers. We show that PoissonComp outperforms state-of-the-art approaches to KV cache compression. Combined with KVFetch, it achieves near-dense performance (90-100% of dense quality at 32K context length, averaged over RULER and LOFT), using Qwen3-4B, Qwen3.5-9B and Ministral-3-8B, while reducing the GPU memory footprint by 8-32x and keeping CPU-to-GPU memory fetches within 2-23% of the cache per generated token, depending on the model.
  </details>

- **[Keeping LLM Agents on Procedure: Executable Finite-State Control for Long-Horizon Instruction Fidelity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10884)**  `arXiv:2611.10884`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yutao Sun, Haokun Li, Mingshuai Chen, Guanyan Li, Zhongyi Wang, Shengchao Qin, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Much of the automation handed to language-model agents is procedural: each step needs little intelligence, but a long unattended run must follow a skill or SOP stated once at the start. We show that agents drift from such procedures over long runs, and that restating the instructions or resuming a stopped agent only moves the drift to another shortcut. We study an executable finite-state machine (FSM) that keeps the workflow state outside the model's context and enforces it at three points: tool calls, state transitions and the end of the agent's turn. In a pre-registered experiment on 200-account SOP-Bench batches, Claude Haiku 4.5 with the FSM decided on average 139 accounts correctly and by the procedure, against 48 for the strongest baseline (95% CI of the difference [46, 130]), and kept this rate to the end; across three models, the gain tracked how much the plain agent drifted. Because the runtime, not the model, supplies this long-horizon reliability, the FSM can let small models carry procedural work: Haiku 4.5 with it averaged more such decisions than plain Claude Sonnet 4.6 at 38% of the cost. Analyses on SWE-bench Verified, $\tau^2$-bench, and staged and cross-context workflows further suggest that reliable long-horizon automation can come from stronger execution control, not only stronger models.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal Models Can See, But Can They Think? A Study of Turkish Visual Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10831)**  `arXiv:2611.10831`  `cs.AI`
