  _Berke Bunyamin Sule, Yavuz Selim Aygan, Ahmed Zeer, Elif Ince, Ali Mert Temizsoy, Ozgur Agar, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language Models have had massive leaps in recent years. Yet, when tested on high-level cognitive tasks like pattern completion, abstract logic, and relational reasoning, they still fall short. This limitation stems from the models' lack of ability to find out underlying rules and maintain constant intermediate steps, rather than merely deciding on the correct option. To research these shortcomings, this study analyzes visual reasoning performance throughout a multilingual evaluation framework encompassing the BİLSEM, MM-IQ, Visual Puzzles, and PuzzleVQA datasets. We evaluate various data mixing strategies and supervised fine-tuning (SFT) methods. We observed that a correct answer does not always mean the reasoning chain is valid. Because of this, we adopt an LLM-as-a-Judge approach to assess the quality of reasoning beyond final answer accuracy. Our experimental results reveal that baseline models often exhibit more stable reasoning compared to fine-tuned counterparts. Most importantly, we observe that SFT can lead to model collapse, manifesting as infinite text loops or empty outputs. The study concludes that current VLMs still struggle with abstract visual reasoning.
  </details>

- **[BlenderFORGE: Framework for Optimizing Reactive 3D-Graphics Editing Ability of MLLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10768)**  `arXiv:2611.10768`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zilin Guo, Tianrui Zhang, Yi Rong, Yichen Liu, Yuxin Guo, Jingcheng Ni, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have shown promise as interactive agents, yet precise, programmatic 3D graphics editing remains difficult to train because existing systems largely rely on closed-source model APIs and lack deterministic, state-verifiable execution environments. We present BlenderFORGE, a trainable data-environment-optimization framework for open-weight MLLM agents that edit explicit Blender scenes through Blender Python (bpy) code generation. BlenderFORGE targets four fundamental state-verifiable editing task families: object placement, shape-key editing, lighting adjustment, and material modification. It integrates three components: (1) a scalable perturbation-based data generation pipeline built from curated assets and large-scale 3D scene repositories; (2) an isolated Blender Sandbox for deterministic code execution, structured state export, and closed-loop reward computation; and (3) a two-stage training pipeline that uses offline teacher trajectories for cold-start Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT) and sandbox-computed rewards for multi-turn tool-agentic Reinforcement Learning (AgentLoop-RL). Experiments across the four task families show that the resulting open-weight Qwen3-VL-8B and Qwen3.5-9B BlenderFORGE agents substantially improve over their base models and achieve competitive performance with several zero-shot proprietary MLLM baselines on selected metrics. Additional cross-benchmark evaluations and camera-reframing experiments provide evidence of transfer beyond the original training setting.
  </details>

- **[Hallucination-Guided Unlearning: Using Hallucination Traces to Reveal Overfitted Memories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10697)**  `arXiv:2611.10697`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jiayu Zhang, Ziqi Zhong, Yuliang Gai, Canran Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Selective unlearning is increasingly important for the safety and compliance of large language models. Harmful or memorized content is often entangled with useful knowledge and difficult to enumerate. We use the model's own failures to identify candidates for unlearning. We propose Hallucination-Guided Unlearning (HGU), which uses hallucinations as diagnostic signals. HGU localizes unsupported spans via evidence and internal consensus, traces a sparse set of high-responsibility MLP neurons, and edits them with compact gates under a utility-preserving KL constraint. On TOFU, HGU achieves the best MU-FE trade-off, with Avg scores of 0.7221 (forget05) and 0.7699 (forget10), and the highest SepS H-Avg (0.4382/0.4715) under mixed forget/retain prompts. On the WMDP auxiliary task, HGU yields the strongest forgetting (e.g., 14.2 on Economics) while improving Retain (52.7) and MMLU (57.2). These results suggest that tracing hallucinations enables targeted, post-hoc unlearning that better balances forgetting efficacy and general utility without full retraining.
  </details>

- **[Efficient LLM Adaptation with Forward-Only Passes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10610)**  `arXiv:2611.10610`  `cs.AI`  
  _Baichuan Huang, Ananth Balashankar, Amir Aminifar_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) inference serving is undergoing rapid growth and large-scale deployment, motivating us to rethink how the inference process itself can be leveraged to enable efficient task-specific LLM adaptation. In this paper, we propose a forward-only approach for efficient LLM adaptation with forward-only passes in LLM inference serving. We exploit the angle concentration of activations induced by each singular value decomposition (SVD) component to measure its contribution-dispersed or concentrated-to the angle concentration of the hidden states. Based on this, our forward-only search then efficiently identifies the weight matrix with the highest dispersion that merits rank reduction. We then selectively remove the higher-order components and retain the lower-order components in SVD. Empirical results across diverse datasets demonstrate the competitive accuracy of our forward-only approach, while theoretical analysis shows lower peak memory and greater speedup than the gradient-based approach and greater speedup than the exhaustive search approach. The experiments further present the robustness and generalization of our forward-only approach to various LLMs with up to 57B parameters. The code is included in the supplementary material.
  </details>

- **[Sampling Is Not Curiosity: Why LLM Agents Should Investigate](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10501)**  `arXiv:2611.10501`  `cs.AI`  
  _Alfonso Amayuelas, Piotr Piękos, Xin Eric Wang, William Yang Wang, Jürgen Schmidhuber, Alex Pentland_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly used for open-ended tasks, very common in research scenarios where they are required to explore, investigate, or act curiously. At inference time, an LLM is a fixed map from context to next-token distribution, the pretrained weights determine the function, and only its conditioning changes as context accumulates. Mechanisms like temperature, best-of-N, tree search, or RAG vary how the distribution is sampled or what it is conditioned on, but do not update the agent's model of the environment. We argue that current LLM agents do not explore curiously; they explore stochastically. From how curiosity is understood in the RL literature, it requires a predictive structure over how the environment responds to the agent's actions, which improves online from current interaction, drives action selection through its predicted improvement, and persists beyond the context window. Additionally, in its open-ended form, the agent should intrinsically originate what to investigate. Thus, we propose a framework defining curiosity in LLM agents, reserving the term for systems that satisfy it. In doing so, we identify the research directions its implementation opens up.
  </details>

- **[Represented but Not Used: Value-Policy Dissociation in LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10457)**  `arXiv:2611.10457`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yuzhang Luo, Mohamed Aghzal, Liangming Pan, Ziyu Yao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents need to choose actions whose consequences unfold over multiple future steps, yet poor decisions do not reveal whether the model failed to represent those consequences or failed to use them. Mechanistic interpretability has studied internal representations of environment state and plans, but sequential decision making also requires evaluating how candidate actions change future outcomes. Reinforcement learning formalizes this as action value. We study this computation in Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct, Qwen2.5-32B-Instruct, and GPT-OSS-20B across a partially observed Grid environment and WebShop, a simulated web shopping environment. We find that the relative values of candidate actions are linearly decodable from intermediate hidden states, including at states where the policy prefers a worse action. We call this gap between value representation and policy choice value-policy dissociation. To investigate how represented value relates to action selection, we compare direct interventions along the value readout direction with Natural Value Transport (NVT), a transformation learned from hidden state changes that naturally result from differences in action value. While readout-direction interventions have limited and inconsistent effects, NVT more consistently influences action preferences, particularly at later layers. We also test a closed-loop controller that uses the value readout as a sensor and NVT as an actuator, observing improvements in task outcomes. These results suggest that LLM agents can represent useful prospective value without reliably translating it into policy choice.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Query Similarity: Safety-Conditioned Cache Routing for LLM Response Reuse](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10418)**  `arXiv:2611.10418`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shilong Pan, Zhiliang Tian, Weiguo Chen, Zihan Chen, DongSheng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantic response caching reuses a cached response for a semantically similar query to improve LLM inference efficiency. However, query similarity alone can lead to unsafe reuse when safety conditions change. Existing approaches improve cache safety through stricter similarity checks, context-aware validation, or response verification, yet they may still miss safety conflicts between the cached response and the new request or introduce unnecessary verification overhead. We argue that safe and efficient semantic caching requires a reuse policy that makes the cached response's safety conditions explicit and uses them to guide the reuse decision with a balance between safety and utility. In this paper, we propose SafeCache, a safety-aware framework that separates candidate retrieval from safety approval and formulates semantic response reuse as a safety-conditioned routing problem. SafeCache matches each cached-response condition against the new request and aggregates the per-condition mismatches into an overall risk score. A separate contradiction guard prevents explicit conflicts from being diluted by strong matches. It further estimates matching uncertainty and calibrates the routing policy under a required unsafe-reuse target and a verifier-call budget to choose among direct reuse, selective verification, or regeneration. Experiments show that SafeCache achieves the better safety-utility trade-off among the evaluated baselines, enabling safer response reuse at comparable cache utility.
  </details>

- **[TurnFlow: Benchmarking User-State Reasoning in Multi-Turn LLM Agents via Scene-Guided User Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10412)**  `arXiv:2611.10412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhixun Chen, Ming Li, Anjie Liu, Xiaoyuan Zhang, Quanyu Dai, Huangxun Chen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed through conversational interfaces, yet strong single-turn performance does not imply reliability in multi-turn settings. Multi-turn assistance requires a model to maintain an evolving representation of user requirements and to reason over it before complying with later requests. Failures can therefore be subtle: models may fail to recognize a changed constraint or hidden conflict, or recognize it without enforcing it in the final response. Existing benchmarks primarily test fully specified prompts, long-context recall, or controlled domain environments, leaving open-domain requirement reconciliation undermeasured. We introduce TurnFlow, an interactive benchmark for evaluating whether models preserve, revise, and act on user requirements across turns. TurnFlow combines adaptive user simulation with verifiable judging, allowing conversations to unfold naturally while keeping hidden requirements and pass/fail criteria controlled. In our evaluation, nine LLMs achieve overall pass rates from 42.86% to 59.59% on 490 scenarios. Performance varies substantially across challenge types: models perform well on Stateful Multi-Constraint Following but remain considerably less reliable on Scope Creep and Goal Shift. These results reveal persistent limitations in reasoning over evolving user requirements across turns.
  </details>

- **[Tail-risk-aware Agent Safety: Constraining Tail Risks Across Multi-step LLM Agent Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10411)**  `arXiv:2611.10411`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shilong Pan, Zhiliang Tian, Zihan Chen, Wentao Zou, Weiguo Chen, DongSheng Li_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based agents extend LLMs with tool calls, memory, planning, and retrieval to act in external environments, but they are vulnerable to attacks at different workflow steps. Existing defenses, including prompt engineering and post-training alignment, primarily improve LLM-only safety and often overlook risks that arise from multi-step execution. External verification adds guardrails to check each step separately, but it typically ignores how risks can propagate across steps. We argue that we should consider risk transitions across dependent steps through the agent workflow, rather than separate verification on a single step. In this paper, we propose \textbf{T}ail-risk-aware \textbf{A}gent \textbf{S}afety (TAS), a tail-risk-aware safety framework that models agent workflow safety as a sequence of dependent safety states and constrains the rare but extreme risks (i.e., tail risk) with a shared budget. TAS first models safety-state transitions sequentially along the workflow as an MDP to trace risk propagation, then measures a tail-sensitive risk to target rare but high-cost risks (i.e., tail risk), and constrains the risk with a shared budget to derive a step-wise safety strategy. By constraining tail risk rather than the overall average, TAS suppresses rare high-cost failures while preserving utility in normal cases. Experiments show that TAS achieves better performance compared with eleven baselines across three benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[SIRIS: Can LLMs Tell When Simulation Errors Matter in AI-Driven Systems Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10329)**  `arXiv:2611.10329`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jinwoo Hwang, Sukmin Cho, Tao Zhang, Soheil Abbasloo, Jongse Park, Peng Cheng_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents increasingly drive systems research and rely on simulators to evaluate candidate designs at scale. Simulators are imperfect by design: their modeling limitations can distort predictions yet leave the recommended design correct, or change which design should be selected. We introduce Siris to test whether LLMs can tell these cases apart, judging from their systems understanding when a limitation invalidates the simulator's recommendation. By perturbing a simulator, Siris constructs workload pairs with the same candidates, limitation, and simulator recommendation. The perturbation changes predictions on both workloads, yet a reference simulator supports the recommendation on only one. Evaluating thirteen frontier LLMs on LLM serving design problems, we find that identifying a limitation does not ensure a correct choice, and even the strongest models sometimes abandon valid recommendations. Self-correction through a review turn helps these models restore valid choices but introduces new errors in several others. These findings motivate evaluating systems understanding through design decisions under imperfect simulators.
  </details>

- **[TripleGrader: A Web-Based Platform Supporting Collaborative Triple Annotation with Quality Grading and Inter-Rater Agreement Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10317)**  `arXiv:2611.10317`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rajan Lamichhane, Songhui Yue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge graphs need validated relational triples, and gold benchmarks of such triples are often built by several annotators. Most text annotation platforms focus on span tagging and compute agreement on categorical labels. They are not designed for ordinal quality grading of triples that have already been extracted. We present TripleGrader, an open-source web platform for grading subject-predicate-object triples on a 0 to 10 ordinal scale. In TripleGrader, each triple is scored independently, and peer scores stay hidden until the grader has rated it. Graders then discuss entries in threaded comments and propose revisions, which the session owner approves and publishes as new versions. For agreement, the platform computes quadratic-weighted Cohen's kappa for two raters and a quadratic-weighted Fleiss-type kappa for three or more. Graded sessions and the final benchmark are exported in common formats. We evaluated the platform in three ways. We ran 72 automated tests, including 18 workflow scenarios through the REST API. We also injected 10 error types to check whether prescribed corrections could be applied while preserving unaffected triples. On two small corpora with existing gold benchmarks, we then used the platform to curate the triples extracted by four large language models into each benchmark. Only 10% to 32% of the candidate triples could be kept unchanged, and 53% to 71% needed corrections, partly to match the benchmarks' naming conventions. Per model, 11 to 29 missing triples had to be added. On these corpora, the extracted triples therefore needed substantial human review. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Conformal Fair Decoding for Large Language Model Recommenders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10285)**  `arXiv:2611.10285`  `cs.AI`  
  _Ziwei Wu, Jingrui He_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) can generate personalized recommendations without task-specific training, but changing a protected attribute while holding interaction history fixed can alter the semantic content of recommended items. We study fairness control when model weights and full-vocabulary logits are unavailable. We propose Fair- Rec, an item-level decoding framework that uses an offline-calibrated semantic-disparity threshold and controls candidate selection as recommendation lists are generated. A hard variant filters candidates against the threshold; a soft variant trades off candidate likelihood and disparity while adapting a penalty using observed violations. Under an explicit uniform enforceability assumption, we establish a pathwise O(1/T) upper bound on the excess empirical violation rate of the soft decoder. Experiments on MovieLens-1M and Amazon Movies & TV using Qwen3-8B and Llama3.1-8B-Instruct show that the soft variant lowers measured counterfactual disparity relative to zero- =shot recommendation, UP5, and FACTER, while retaining competitive HR@10 and NDCG@10.
  </details>

- **[Adversarial Evaluation of Large Language Models for Patient-Friendly Radiology Report Summarization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10277)**  `arXiv:2611.10277`  `cs.AI`  
  _Magdalini Paschali, Asad Aali, Arogya Koirala, Andrew Johnston, Katherine McNamara, Richard Gaus, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: To develop and validate a scalable adversarial evaluation framework for assessing the robustness of large language models (LLMs) that generate patient-friendly summaries of radiology reports.Methods: We introduced a clinically grounded adversarial framework that uses controlled term substitutions and impression removals across intensity levels. Using a radiologist-validated adversarial dictionary, we generated adversarial variants of CT and MRI reports. Six LLMs summarized original and adversarial impressions using 2 prompt styles, yielding 18,000 evaluation pairs. Robustness was evaluated with an automated LLM-as-a-judge framework scoring factual faithfulness, sensitivity to adversarial edits, certainty fidelity, and hallucination rate. Failure mode analysis quantified vulnerabilities in incorporating clinical modifiers. Agreement with two radiologists was assessed on 105 cases.Results: GPT-5-mini achieved the highest factual faithfulness and certainty fidelity under both adversarial edits. Under term substitution, hallucinations occurred in 28.1-30.1% of GPT-5-mini summaries, compared with 35.3-36.4% for Claude Sonnet 4.5 and 41.4-41.9% for MedGemma. Impression-removal edits were handled more robustly than term substitutions. Even for the highest-scoring summarizer, substitutions of anatomical terms were not reflected in 23.7% of summaries, diagnostic certainty in 22.3%, and descriptor terms (e.g., "substantial") in 21.2%. Judge-radiologist agreement (macro-F1: 0.55-0.56) was comparable to inter-radiologist agreement (0.53).Conclusions: Adversarial evaluation exposes clinically meaningful failure modes, including missed anatomical modifiers, altered diagnostic certainty, and omitted severity descriptors.
  </details>

- **[Ensemble Routing for Prompt-Adaptive Cost-Aware LLM Inference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10193)**  `arXiv:2611.10193`  `cs.AI`  
  _Saul Urso, Emanuele Carlini, Patrizio Dazzi, Matteo Mordacchini_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly support Web-based services that must balance response quality and inference cost across heterogeneous requests. Conventional routing selects one model per prompt, while fixed ensembles cannot adapt their composition and cost to individual requests. We introduce PISA, a prompt-adaptive, cost-aware single-model router, and PISA-E, its extension to ensemble routing. At the core of both methods, PISA combines soft cluster memberships with model-specific predictors to estimate individual model capabilities. Building on these estimates, PISA-E jointly selects ensemble composition and cardinality under an explicit cost--quality preference that can be adjusted at inference time without retraining. A tractable ensemble-success surrogate and efficient candidate selection enable this extension without ensemble-specific training. Across four LLM routing benchmarks under in-distribution (ID) and out-of-distribution (OOD) conditions, PISA achieves the highest macro-average cost--performance hypervolume among the evaluated single-model routers in both settings. Meanwhile, PISA-E achieves the highest macro-average task performance among the evaluated ensemble approaches when prioritizing response quality, surpassing the strongest competitors by 2.09 and 2.87 percentage points under ID and OOD conditions, respectively. At selected lower-cost operating points, it also exceeds the strongest ensemble competitors' macro-average task performance while reducing average inference cost by approximately 36% and 25%, respectively. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of prompt-adaptive capability estimation for both single-model routing and selective ensembling.
  </details>

- **[AutoEMatic: Runtime-Adaptive Mixed-Precision LLM Inference via Automatic Exponent-Mantissa Refinement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10165)**  `arXiv:2611.10165`  `cs.AI`  
  _Akshat Ramachandran, Reena Elangovan, Rangharajan Venkatesan, Charbel Sakr, Tijmen Blankevoort, Brucek Khailany, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing LLM post-training quantization techniques determine precision before inference begins, yet the precision a model requires varies dynamically across prompts, layers, and inference phases. We present AutoEMatic, a full-stack co-design that adapts the precision of weights, activations, and the KV cache at runtime. A lightweight policy selects each layer's precision at every prefill and decode step from two runtime signals: the entropy of the next-token distribution and the magnitude of each layer's update to its hidden states. This runtime adaptation is made practical by AutoFP, a multi-scale floating-point format that derives 2-, 4-, and 6-bit precisions from a single NVFP4 base format. We realize AutoEMatic on two targets: GPU kernels that execute AutoFP on NVFP4 Tensor Cores, and an accelerator whose vector MAC datapath executes all AutoFP formats natively. Across reasoning and agentic benchmarks, AutoEMatic improves accuracy by up to 21.7% over NVFP4 and matches group-scaled FP6 accuracy on most benchmarks, using 4.9 bits per weight versus 4.5 for NVFP4. On GB300 GPUs, AutoEMatic retains approximately 90-98% of NVFP4 decode throughput across the evaluated configurations. Furthermore, the AutoEMatic accelerator reduces latency by 1.6-2.3x and energy by 7-23% compared to an accelerator with NVFP4-only datapath at iso-accuracy.
  </details>

- **[The Infrastructure Divide: Analyzing Community Stance on Data Center Development via Reddit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10109)**  `arXiv:2611.10109`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yoko Koyama, Gongfan Chen, Mansi Saxena_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid expansion of AI-driven data center construction has outpaced public engagement: an estimated $64 billion in U.S. data center projects were blocked or delayed by local opposition between May 2024 and March 2025, yet the concerns driving this resistance remain poorly characterized. Prior engagement methods such as surveys and hearings are introduced late in the planning process, and no study has systematically mined social media to measure community stance at scale. To address this gap, this study presents an LLM-powered opinion-mining framework and applies it to 798 Reddit posts and 102,328 associated comments spanning a decade. An open-source large language model, guided by a Chain-of-Stance prompting framework and three domain-specific labeling rules, classified stance at 83% of the human inter-reviewer agreement ceiling. Opposition dominated discourse volume rather than disapproval: against posts outnumbered favor posts 2.6:1 and generated 8.5 times more comments, and this gap widened significantly over 86 weeks. Topic modeling of opposition arguments resolved into four reproducible clusters, resource consumption and scarcity (42.8%), contested siting processes and secrecy (34.2%), political opposition to data centers (13%), and political and corporate accountability (8%). Engagement analysis revealed that posts with emotional or provocative political framing attracted the most engagement, while key technical concerns (e.g., water, power) were dispersed across lower-engagement comment threads. These findings give local leaders, developers, and policy makers a quantified, continuously updatable basis for prioritizing concerns and policy levers, supporting a shift from opposition to negotiated, verifiable partnership for data center development.
  </details>

- **[META-IR: Meta-Introspective Reasoning for Adaptive Safety Alignment in LLM](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10089)**  `arXiv:2611.10089`  `cs.AI`  
  _Mahabubur Rahman Miraj, Hongyu Huang, Nankun Mu, Chao Chen, Shanqin Yi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to jailbreak attacks and harmful-content prompts, particularly under adversarial distribution shifts where static alignment pipelines fail to generalize. Although introspective methods such as STAIR strengthen refusal consistency, they depend on fixed self-evaluation policies that cannot rapidly adapt to evolving manipulation strategies. To overcome these limitations, this paper introduces META-IR, a meta-introspective reasoning framework that enhances safety alignment through meta-learning-driven adaptation. It employs a dual-loop architecture: an inner introspective loop that performs structured risk analysis, self-assessment, and reflective correction via SI-MCTS, and an outer meta-learning loop that optimizes the introspection policy across diverse jailbreak task families to enable rapid generalization to unseen attacks. A dynamic jailbreak simulation module further exposes the model to progressively harder adversarial prompts, enabling continual self-improvement without human supervision. Experiments across multiple models (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct and Qwen-2-7B-Instruct) and a broad suite of safety, helpfulness, generalization, and introspection metrics demonstrate that META-IR substantially improves adaptation speed, robustness, and reasoning stability compared with all baseline models. These results establish META-IR as an effective pathway toward building LLMs capable of sustained, adaptive safety alignment under dynamic and evolving threat environments.
  </details>

- **[AYO: A Safe and Privacy-Preserving Platform for Studying Children's Interaction with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10054)**  `arXiv:2611.10054`  `cs.AI`  
  _Huiwen Alex Yang, Rachel Skwersky, Bill D. Thompson, Celeste Kidd_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Children and adolescents are increasingly interacting with generative AI and large language models (LLMs), yet little is known about how these interactions may influence their development, beliefs, and behavior. Existing research has relied heavily on surveys, while direct study of children's interactions with LLMs presents substantial challenges related to safety, privacy, consent, and experimental control. We introduce AYO (AI Youth Open Science), an open-source platform designed for safe and systematic research on children's interactions with conversational AI. AYO provides a child-friendly interface, configurable age-based safety guardrails, researcher monitoring, and privacy-preserving data practices. These capabilities enable cross-sectional, longitudinal, and experimental research on how children use and respond to LLMs and how AI design may shape trust, epistemic judgment, curiosity, and other aspects of development. AYO provides the infrastructure to generate evidence that guides safer AI systems, educational practices, and policies for children growing up with AI.
  </details>

- **[LoRazor: Trimming Adapter Execution for Efficient Multi-LoRA Serving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10049)**  `arXiv:2611.10049`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiang Li, Pengcheng Wang, Azam Ikram, Saurabh Bagchi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LoRA offers a parameter-efficient adaptation strategy, supporting multi-user workloads via customized adapters on a shared backbone. However, serving concurrent requests with different adapters introduces substantial computation and memory-management overhead. We ask the research question: Do all requests need their adapters throughout the whole generation process? Our motivational study reveals that employing LoRA adapters at every decoding step is unnecessary, releasing them early has limited impact on accuracy while substantially accelerating generation. Although skipping adapters early offers theoretical workload reduction, translating these computational savings into concrete throughput gains requires tailored GPU kernels. We present LoRazor, a multi-LoRA serving framework that combines early adapter release with active-row aware GPU kernels. LoRazor determines when to permanently release a request's adapter and continue decoding with the backbone alone. By dynamically resizing LoRA kernel launches as adapters are released, LoRazor trims idle GPU blocks. Across two backbones and four tasks, LoRazor achieves up to 3.02x request throughput and 2.53x output-token throughput over vLLM multi-LoRA, with a mean relative task-accuracy decrease of 2.89%.
  </details>

- **[Veterans' Mental Health Crisis Severity Annotation: Do LLMs Agree More Than Human Experts?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10048)**  `arXiv:2611.10048`  `cs.AI`  
  _K M Sajjadul Islam, Pranav Yaramalla, Nadiyah Johnson, Mark Flower, Praveen Madiraju, Zeno Franco_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) offer a scalable approach for annotating mental health and crisis-related social media content, but their reliability in safety-critical tasks remains unclear. This study evaluates LLM-based crisis-severity annotation of veteran-related social media posts against annotations from eight Veteran Subject Matter Experts (SMEs). Using 149 tweets and five severity categories, we evaluate 15 open-source and 10 proprietary LLMs across eight prompt variants, examining annotation performance, inter-model agreement, prompt sensitivity, and high-severity crisis detection. Human reviewers showed higher ordinal than nominal agreement ({\alpha} = 0.494), indicating that disagreements often occurred between neighboring severity levels. In contrast, selected LLMs demonstrated substantially higher inter-model consistency, with ordinal agreement ({\alpha}) reaching 0.8336 under the baseline prompt. Proprietary models generally showed better agreement with expert annotations and greater stability than open-source models. GPT-5.6-Terra achieved the best overall performance, while the open-source Qwen2.5-7B showed the strongest performance in identifying Acute Crisis with Suicidal Ideation cases. However, higher model consistency did not necessarily correspond to agreement with human judgments or reliable detection of rare high-severity cases. Label-flipped demonstration experiments also revealed model-specific sensitivity to misleading in-context examples, while explanatory demonstrations did not consistently improve performance. Overall, LLMs show promise as scalable complements to expert annotation, but require careful evaluation before use in high-stakes mental health applications.
  </details>

- **[Don't Blame The Preferences: Examining The Emergence of Sycophancy in LLM Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.10045)**  `arXiv:2611.10045`  `cs.AI`  
  _Chao Peter Yang, Catherine Zehr, Pranav Bijith, Brandon Fain_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI sycophancy, the tendency of large language models (LLMs) to flatter users and affirm their beliefs, can undermine accuracy and contribute to misinformation, dependence, and overconfidence. Although prior work has benchmarked sycophancy in released models and proposed mitigations, its origins during training remain poorly understood. Preference alignment methods such as RLHF and DPO are often blamed, but their effects are rarely compared with earlier training stages. We investigate sycophancy across the training pipelines of three model families and three benchmarks. We find that sycophancy is largely established by the end of supervised fine-tuning (SFT), rather than introduced by preference alignment. Across all nine model benchmark combinations, SFT checkpoints attain at least 92% of preference aligned models' sycophancy scores and at least 85% of final models' scores; on average, they are about as sycophantic as final models. Preference alignment increases sycophancy in only four of nine cases, by an average of 4.2 percentage points when it does. To probe the underlying causes, we isolate model representations associated with sycophancy using mechanistic interpretability and measure their activation on training datasets from each stage. SFT datasets show roughly the same average activation as other training data but contribute substantially more examples, and the highest-scoring individual datasets all come from SFT. Our findings suggest that addressing sycophancy requires interventions throughout the training pipeline, particularly in data selection and augmentation for pre-training and SFT.
  </details>

- **[Zero-Shot Weight-Space Programming Across a Difficulty-Graded Ladder of Two-Player Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.09271)**  `arXiv:2611.09271`  `cs.AI`  
  _Eduardo C. Garrido-Merchán_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Weight-space programming sets every weight of a feed-forward network by hand, from an explicit specification of the desired computation, with no gradient descent and no training data anywhere in the construction. We ask how far a large language model can carry this practice, zero-shot, across games of deliberately increasing difficulty. We design a four-rung ladder (Nim, tic-tac-toe, two sizes of Connect Four, and a chess endgame) spanning four axes of difficulty, and build a second, independent network at each rung, using a structurally unrelated game at matched difficulty, to separate what a rung's finding says about the difficulty level from what it says about the one game used to measure it. Ten networks are built and independently verified. Six admit a complete, non-statistical proof of correctness against an independent oracle. The chess-endgame rung is exact where correctness is cheap, move legality and, in one network, an exact one-ply mate detector, and collapses where it is expensive, composing a multi-move mating plan, from 84.1-100% success at the one-ply tactics it was built to get right down to 0.57-9.13% in full adversarial play; its replicate shows this collapse is an order of magnitude less severe under a shorter required plan and exact rather than heuristic safety checks. The standard-sized rung confirms, by holding hand-set evaluation weights fixed and adding only shallow search, that the remaining gap is an architectural ceiling rather than a matter of engineering effort, and this exact collapse reproduces across two structurally unrelated games. We release all ten networks, their independent oracles, construction-cost measurements, and a cold-prompt protocol for a future cross-model comparison
  </details>

- **[PCBRouteAgent: How Close Are LLM Agents to Becoming PCB Routing Engineers?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.08175)**  `arXiv:2611.08175`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yue Zhang, Jilei Sun, Liqiang Jing, Yijie Wei, Ruohan Yang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automating printed circuit board (PCB) routing requires coordinating board-level connectivity decisions with geometric execution under strict design rules. Although recent agents can interact with native routing tools, reliable routing on complex boards remains challenging. We present PCBRouteAgent, a multimodal PCB routing agent inspired by how human engineers coordinate global strategy with local execution. A Global Routing Planner combines board images and structured electrical and geometric information to specify connection topology, routing order, corridors, and layer transitions. A Detailed Routing Executor realizes these guides through KiCad's native routing engine and performs bounded local recovery. Unresolved failures are returned as structured feedback, enabling targeted guide revisions while preserving accepted routes. We evaluate PCBRouteAgent on 247 synthetic and real-world boards from four PCBWorld-Bench test splits. It achieves clean-pass rates of 100%, 94.9%, 100%, and 40% on D2, D3-A, D3-B, and D3-C, respectively. On the three real-world subsets, PCBRouteAgent outperforms the best evaluated baseline in clean-pass rate by 13.9, 36, and 10 percentage points, respectively.
  </details>

- **[CogniLife: Continuous Cognitive Distillation for Deployed Multi-Expert Agent Marketplaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.04483)**  `arXiv:2611.04483`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xuwei Tan, Jingchun Huang, Ming Cai, Shuangquan Lyu, Tiange Wang, Yinzhi Wang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-expert agent marketplaces -- where creators publish domain-specific AI experts for end users to consult -- are an emerging deployment pattern for language technology. A published expert can receive many requests yet little evidence about its creator, who remains active elsewhere: the cold-expert problem. We frame continuous cognitive distillation as converting attributed creator observations into revisable decision rules, communication patterns, and domain procedures. CogniLife proposes a cross-expert attribution design for this problem, with Creator Profiles for transferable creator patterns and Expert Journals for listing-specific knowledge; this design is not yet implemented or evaluated. A deployed platform partially realizes the surrounding lifecycle through creator-authorized authoring, capability-owned proposal filtering, and reversible consolidation of eligible owner-scoped memory. On ProfyBench-v1, an 85-task offline benchmark built from replay seeds and controlled internal tests, the Profy Full configuration as a whole achieves a mean overall score of 4.65/5 from the primary LLM judge, compared with 4.01 for Raw LLM and 4.28 for Expert Prompt, with the largest gains in completeness, specificity, and missing-information tasks. These configuration-level scores do not test cold-expert mitigation, cross-expert transfer, or longitudinal distillation.
  </details>

- **[Training LLM Agents to Automate LLM Post-Training with RSI-Dojo](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03698)**  `arXiv:2611.03698`  `cs.AI`  
  _Shuzheng Gao, Zhaojian Yu, Penghao Yin, Yuling Shi, Kai Cai, Shilin He, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recursive Self-Improvement (RSI) aims to enable AI systems to autonomously build and improve AI systems themselves, including models, harnesses, and underlying infrastructure. Within this broader agenda, a central capability is enabling agents to train and improve base models. However, existing studies on automated model training primarily focused on workflow- or harness-level optimizations, rather than internalizing the ability to conduct post-training into the model itself. Developing this capability requires executable model training environments that can produce high-quality trajectories demonstrating how to select data, train models, diagnose failures, and iteratively improve results. In this paper, we introduce RSI-Dojo, a collection of executable model training environments designed to teach LLM agents to conduct post-training. To construct RSI-Dojo, we develop RSI-Craft, an automatic pipeline that converts human research artifacts into executable model training environments. RSI-Craft automatically collects research papers from top venues, instantiates datasets and metrics into a unified interface, and reproduces evaluation dependencies. It then filters tasks by data size, execution reliability, and discriminability, yielding RSI-Dojo with 281 seed model training tasks. Building on RSI-Dojo, we employ a trajectory collection method with an improved harness to collect high-quality model-training trajectories from frontier models, then retain examples through rubric-based and outcome-based filtering. We fine-tune Qwen3.5-9B and Qwen3.6-35B-A3B on these trajectories. The resulting RSI-Dojo-9B and RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B improve over their base models by 8.74 and 16.87 PostTrainBench points, respectively. RSI-Dojo-35B-A3B reaches 29.95 on PostTrainBench and 53.29 on PostTrainBench-Lite, matching the performance of frontier models such as GLM-5.2 and GPT 5.5.
  </details>

- **[DeliverBench: Benchmarking AI Agents on Delivering Realistic Multi-Stage Professional Projects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.03412)**  `arXiv:2611.03412`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yumou Liu, Mingju Chen, Zhoufutu Wen, Qianyu Yang, Zijie Wang, Sihan Chen, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are judged by what they deliver, and in professional work what gets delivered is a project, not a task. A project pays off only if the whole package holds together, and one weak part can limit the value of the rest. Yet existing benchmarks mostly test agents on one task at a time, leaving a gap between the capability we measure and the outcome that makes agents useful. To address this gap, we introduce DeliverBench, which tests whether agents can carry a project from brief to final delivery. Reconstructed from real materials with 13 domain experts, its 11 projects span settings such as financial due diligence, legal contract drafting, and drug safety reporting. They comprise 150 subtasks, with 2-15 subsequent releases of new materials and client feedback per project. Later subtasks build on the agent's own earlier deliverables, so unresolved errors can carry into downstream work. Expert rubrics assess each subtask submission and the final delivery package. Across seven model-harness systems, the best scores 65.0/100 on final delivery and passes 50.0% of subtasks at a 90-point threshold. Together, these results suggest that task-level capability does not simply compose into delivery; delivery also depends on keeping the work coherent as it evolves.
  </details>

- **[CHATDIFY: Automating End-to-End Dify Workflow Synthesis from Natural Language via Multi-Agent Collaboration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2611.01563)**  `arXiv:2611.01563`  `cs.AI`  
  _Tao Fan, Guoqiang Ma_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The proliferation of Large Language Models has catalyzed powerful application development platforms like Dify, which enable users to build sophisticated LLM-powered workflows through modular node orchestration. However, manually configuring these workflows requires deep platform expertise and remains time-consuming and error-prone, creating a significant barrier for non-expert users. To address this challenge, we propose \textsc{ChatDify}, a novel multi-agent framework that automates end-to-end Dify workflow synthesis from natural language descriptions. \textsc{ChatDify} employs an Actor-Critic architecture comprising three specialized agents: the \textit{Planner Agent} that decomposes user requests into structured execution plans, the \textit{Executor Agent} that performs context-aware parameter synthesis and schema-augmented DSL compilation, and the \textit{Reviewer Agent} that ensures reliability through hybrid verification with closed-loop refinement. We evaluate \textsc{ChatDify} on a curated benchmark of 100 production-inspired tasks across three state-of-the-art LLM backends, achieving 0.96, 0.91, and 0.75 success rates with Qwen-3.7, GLM-5.1, and DeepSeek-v4, respectively. These results demonstrate its robustness and practical applicability for lowering the technical barrier to creating sophisticated LLM applications.
  </details>

- **[P-ContextBench: From Personal Fact Recall to Personalized Agent Behavior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.40959)**  `arXiv:2610.40959`  `cs.AI`  
  _Grant Nasir Brown, Abdul Basir Bari, Anmol Tadikonda, Isabel Song_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for personal tasks, yet evaluating their safe use of personal context remains difficult because such contexts are private, fragmented, dynamic, and socially situated. We introduce P-ContextBench, a realistic, verifiable, and scalable benchmark for evaluating personal-context understanding. P-ContextBench constructs population-structured, life-story-grounded digital humans from demographic seeds reflecting the U.S. population. Rather than using explicit, static profiles, P-ContextBench embeds traits, routines, goals, and constraints as implicit evidence across fragmented, evolving synthetic mobile-app records. It evaluates five capabilities: single-source fact recovery, multi-source fact recovery, temporal state tracking, inference of higher-level personal patterns from routine behavior, and safe handling of requests conflicting with persona-specific constraints through compliance, warning, clarification, or refusal. A multi-stage validation pipeline checks persona plausibility, context recoverability, information leakage, evidence preservation, test-case grounding, and human-judge agreement. We instantiate P-ContextBench with 100 personas, 10,088 hidden facts, 18,261 app-trace fragments, and 4,810 verified test cases. The pipeline supports configurable generation across scales and demographic groups. Cost-effective state-of-the-art (SOTA) LLMs relevant to personal agents achieve below 60% accuracy on temporal tracking, higher-level pattern inference, and constraint-aware action-boundary decisions, with fewer than 10% of test cases answered correctly across all three repeated runs. Even a high-performing SOTA model remains inconsistent on these capabilities. P-ContextBench provides measurable targets for improving the accuracy, reliability, and safety of LLM-based personal agents.
  </details>

- **[DriveMind: Mind-Evolving Belief Tracking for Closed-Loop Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32069)**  `arXiv:2610.32069`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yan Shi, Kehua Chen, Jiarui Qi, Yihong Tang, Haicheng Liao, Yinhai Wang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can perceive a traffic scene, reason about it, and propose a driving action within a single model. However, existing MLLM-based drivers reason from scratch at each frame and commit to a single explanation, discarding earlier hypotheses about the scene. When the scene is ambiguous, the agent locks in on the wrong interpretation and acts on it before evidence accumulates. In this paper, we propose DriveMind, an MLLM-based driving framework that keeps several hypotheses about the scene alive at the same time and updates their weights as new frames arrive. Inspired by particle filtering, DriveMind propagates each hypothesis through a VLM, scores it against the current observation, and resamples to maintain diversity. On Bench2Drive, adding belief tracking to the same driving pipeline improves the Driving Score (DS) from 68.50 to 81.37 and the Success Rate (SR) from 41.36% to 54.09%. The largest gains occur on ambiguous scenarios such as unsignalized intersections (+20.0 DS) and oncoming-traffic interactions (+20.5 DS). Beyond the overall metrics, DriveMind also improves fine-grained interactive driving abilities such as merging, emergency braking, and give-way.
  </details>

- **[Large language models show systematic failures in incorporating evolving medical knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30631)**  `arXiv:2610.30631`  `cs.AI`  
  _Min Zeng, Zaifu Zhan, Kai Yu, Rui Zhang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical knowledge evolves, yet whether large language models (LLMs) faithfully incorporate revisions into clinical recommendations remains unclear. We introduce ChronoEvo, comprising 1,709 recommendation-level revisions across 12 forms of medical knowledge change and 275 guideline lineages, to distinguish recognition of revised guidance from its full incorporation. With updated guideline evidence explicitly provided, revision recognition exceeded full incorporation across 14 models, with a 20.1-percentage-point gap in the strongest model. For Qwen3-4B, 90.8% of responses recognized revisions, 80.2% were clinically correct, and 58.5% fully incorporated them, yielding a 32.3-percentage-point gap. Two clinicians' adjudicated assessment of 300 responses yielded a 40.3-percentage-point gap in this enriched validation sample; 61 of 144 responses with incomplete incorporation were clinically incorrect. Prompting Qwen3-4B to prioritize revised evidence reduced outdated-content retention and improved clinical correctness without significantly improving full incorporation. These findings identify faithful incorporation of revised guidance as a distinct challenge for clinical LLM reliability.
  </details>

- **[LG-Bench: A Graph-Structured Evaluation Benchmark for Life Sciences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26787)**  `arXiv:2610.26787`  `cs.AI`  
  _Lu Sun, Xiangyi Zhang, Xiangyang Zhu, Zijian Chen, Qiang Hu, Yuan Tian, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traditional evaluation benchmarks reduce inherently interconnected scientific knowledge in life sciences into flat lists of questions, disregarding the underlying topological structure of the knowledge. We introduce LG-Bench, the first graph-structured benchmark for life sciences, featuring over 10,000 high-quality multiple-choice questions across medicine, biology, and chemistry. Our approach constructs a weighted evaluation graph using bidirectional matching and semantic similarity algorithms, where nodes represent questions and edge weights capture their semantic relationships. Leveraging this graph topology, we design two novel evaluation metrics. The Global Coherence Score (GCS) measures a model's consistency within semantically related neighborhoods, while Knowledge Balance Score (KBS) analyzes how model errors are distributed across the graph to reveal conceptual blind spots. LG-Bench facilitates fine-grained comparison of LLMs by surfacing differences in conceptual coherence and patterns of knowledge organization across models. Our framework shifts the evaluation paradigm from flat accuracy metrics to structure-aware analysis, offering a new lens for diagnosing and improving LLM performance in the life sciences domain.
  </details>

- **[ACED-Bench: Evaluating How LLM Agents Acquire and Act on Causal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.14381)**  `arXiv:2610.14381`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yiwei Chen, Amila Weerasinghe, Siddharth Biswal, Utkrisht Rajkumar, Olcay Boz, Taylor Berg-Kirkpatrick, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used as scientific assistants, yet we still lack reliable ways to evaluate whether they can investigate a system rather than merely answer a prompt. A useful evaluation must let agents choose experiments, interpret finite evidence, and make causal decisions, while preserving objective ground truth. We introduce ACED (Active Causal Evidence and Decision Benchmark), a neuro-symbolic benchmark that separates the readable scientific surface from the formal causal world: natural-language scenarios describe the system, while a hidden causal model defines the data, the effect of every intervention, and the oracle answers. In ACED-Repair, agents diagnose a failing production system: within 15 experiments they must decide which controls to test, read confounded and filtered data, and recommend a fix, which we re-simulate on a hidden true objective. In ACED-Query, agents answer causal questions and make constrained causal decisions in worlds backed by categorical probabilistic graphical models. Acceptance on ACED-Repair ranges from 43% to 0% across 12 LLMs, while strong agents approach ceiling on ACED-Query's qualification questions. Tracing every experiment shows where investigations stop: most open-weight models test a true lever on its own in fewer than half of the worlds, the strongest models test the right levers but often miss the working dose or combination, and agents that do run a working fix almost always recommend it. No model reliably repairs systems with two independent causes or systems that need a subgroup-specific policy. These results suggest that reliable AI co-scientists need explicit support for choosing experiments, keeping track of evidence, and composing interventions. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AssemState: Manual and Physical-State-Guided Reasoning for Zero-shot Furniture Assembly](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.08446)**  `arXiv:2610.08446`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zhiyuan Qi, Jierui Li, Yifan Shen, Cheng Qian, Jiateng Liu_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) have made significant progress in visual understanding, but precise 3D spatial reasoning integrated with physical environment remains difficult. Furniture assembly requires not only recovering step-level operations from diagrammatic manuals, but also translating semantic attachment relations into 6D pose updates that enable parts to physically interact with the environment and previously assembled components. To study this problem, we propose AssemState, a zero-shot framework for manual and physical-state-guided furniture assembly. It firstly employs anchor-guided boundary assembly states to decompose manual pages into single-part operations and recover an assembly-tree. Then, it uses iterative after-state feedback refinement to guide successive (SE(3)) updates and corrections, and validates their physical plausibility through simulation-based release tests. Experiments show that compared with the strongest prior baseline, AssemState improves F1 from 38.58\% to 62.80\% and Tree Exact Match from 28.24\% to 53.92\% for assembly-tree recovery. On 243 independently evaluated part-level operations, our proposed iterative refinement improves judge-accepted operations from 0 to 5.3\% and reduces mean Chamfer distance from 5.4111 to 1.7744. However, visually plausible candidate poses may still suffer from collision, floating, mirror-orientation errors, incomplete seating, and wrong-side attachment. These results show that AssemState improves operation-structure recovery and selected local pose metrics, while MLLMs remain limited for spatial relationship reasoning.
  </details>

- **[From Dialogue to Diagnostic Hypotheses: Evaluating LLM Agents for Psychiatric Assessment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.07473)**  `arXiv:2610.07473`  `cs.AI`  
  _Milad Mohammadi, Fatemeh Akrami Shamsabadi, Zahra Mohseni, Amirhossein Safdarian, Malekfarhad Malek, Hadi Moradi, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although LLMs show promising diagnostic reasoning on supplied clinical evidence, their ability to elicit such evidence through free-form psychiatric interviews remains less understood. We evaluate interview quality and diagnostic hypotheses using simulated patients and human participants. For controlled simulation, we introduce PsyCPG, transforming clinical cases into structured patient profiles. On 30 paired cases, experts rate PsyCPG simulations higher in quality than raw-case role-play (4.50 versus 3.83/5). We introduce PsyCIDRA, a dual-agent framework: its interviewer agent uses a notepad tool, expert-written skills, and ICD-11 reference tools to guide interviews; its diagnostic agent analyzes the transcript and produces a report for expert review, ranking differential diagnoses with supporting, conflicting, and missing evidence. Across four models and 53 development cases, PsyCIDRA achieves higher observed diagnostic agreement than direct prompting. On 81 held-out simulated cases, exact-code rank-1 accuracy reaches 60.5% versus 51.9%. We conduct 101 interviews with human participants; two psychologists, blinded to interview condition and model reports, independently assess interview quality and transcript-supported hypotheses. In separate arms, PsyCIDRA agrees with experts on whether to issue a hypothesis in 79.6% of cases versus 65.4% for direct prompting. This study provides an empirical basis for understanding and evaluating LLM agents in free-form psychiatric interviewing.
  </details>

- **[LUMOS: Tracing Parametric Knowledge from Training Data to Behavioral Outputs in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02902)**  `arXiv:2610.02902`  `cs.AI`  
  _Seoyeon Ye, Gayoung Kim, Jiyoung Hong, Sookyung Kim, Hyunsoo Cho_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Current analyses of LLMs' parametric knowledge are largely output-centric, drawing conclusions about what a model knows without verifying what it was actually trained on. This leaves fundamental questions, such as whether a correct response reflects genuine generalization or rote memorization, grounded in speculation rather than evidence. To resolve these ambiguities, we introduce LUMOS, a diagnostic framework that traces knowledge across training-data exposure, internal representations, and behavioral outputs, leveraging OLMo 2 with its fully transparent training corpus. By grounding analysis in verified exposure, we reveal that models internally encode rare facts with high separability (84%) yet fail to express them behaviorally (54%), though this retrieval gap narrows with scale. Furthermore, when models are asked to self-reflect on their own answers, they perform reliably on trained content (83%) but drop to random-baseline levels (49%) on unseen content. This collapse persists even under chain-of-thought prompting, which inflates confidence signals rather than improving calibration. Collectively, these findings demonstrate that incorporating the training-data axis into LLM evaluation transforms speculative diagnoses into verifiable claims, and we advocate that this axis should be a standard component of knowledge assessment in LLMs.
  </details>

- **[Alleviating Hallucination in Reasoning Tasks with Training-Free Uncertainty-Guided Steering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.38962)**  `arXiv:2609.38962`  `cs.AI`  
  _Litian Liu, Qiqi Hou, Yubing Jian, Reza Pourreza, Mohammad Ghavamzadeh, Roland Memisevic, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent work on hallucination detection in large language models has shown that, for a fixed pre-trained model and reasoning task, it is possible to estimate the model's confidence in the correctness of its outputs. Such uncertainty estimates have primarily been used to improve truthfulness by detecting or filtering confabulations. In this work, we ask whether these signals can instead be used more proactively to directly improve the accuracy of model-generated answers. We propose USteer, a simple, training-free steering mechanism that adjusts a model's layer-wise activations during inference using the gradient of a confidence measure with respect to the activations. This procedure nudges generation toward outputs with lower uncertainty at inference time, without modifying model parameters or requiring additional supervision. We show that this approach consistently reduces hallucination across a range of reasoning tasks, demonstrating that confidence signals can be leveraged not only for detection, but also for effective inference-time control of model behavior.
  </details>

- **[AdaPath: Query-Adaptive Path-Finding via Path-Bank for Multi-Hop Implicit Biomedical KGQA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.30556)**  `arXiv:2608.30556`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jun Hyeong Kim, Dongki Kim, Yinhua Piao, Sung Ju Hwang_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Path-finding over knowledge graphs has become an effective way to ground LLM reasoning on multi-hop questions. However, biomedical QA introduces two distinct challenges that general-domain methods are not designed for: (i) queries do not expose intermediate reasoning and can be answered through multiple valid pathways, and (ii) biomedical knowledge graphs are densely connected, so path-finding methods easily take wrong turns. To address these challenges, we propose AdaPath, a path-finding framework that retrieves query-adaptive meta-paths from Path-Bank, which captures both query semantics and biomedical knowledge graph structure. AdaPath provides the missing cues in biomedical queries while effectively pruning dense knowledge graph neighborhoods during multi-hop reasoning. We further release BioStrat-QA, a biomedical KGQA benchmark that stratifies multi-hop queries by how much intermediate reasoning they expose. Across biomedical KGQA benchmarks, AdaPath consistently outperforms baselines, sustaining meaningful path-finding even when multi-hop queries expose less surface information. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Algorithmic Impact Reveals the Hidden Social Choice Structure of Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24046)**  `arXiv:2608.24046`  `cs.AI`  
  _Zachary Wojtowicz, Michelle Si, Finale Doshi-Velez, Ariel Procaccia_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When an AI algorithm makes decisions that affect more than one person, aligning it becomes a problem of social choice: how should people's divergent preferences about system behavior be reconciled and aggregated into a single coherent model? The standard approach to aligning frontier AI models - reinforcement learning from human feedback - largely sidesteps this question and has poor social choice guarantees. However, it remains unclear what alternative should replace it. We show that, when agent utilities can be expressed linearly in a fixed feature representation, the alignment problem can be reformulated as linear optimization over a convex impact space, which makes it amenable to the standard toolkit of welfare economics and mechanism design. This reformulation clarifies how alignment protocols translate into welfare consequences and, conversely, how a social planner's desired constraints on welfare consequences can be translated back into alignment protocols. We apply this transformation to show that voting-by-issues and random-dictatorship mechanisms are strategyproof and unanimous. Demonstrating the reverse direction, we also apply the impact representation to derive a family of alignment protocols that maximize utilitarian social welfare subject to various social desiderata, such as bounds on individual or group harm. We illustrate the welfare implications of these alignment protocols empirically using real human preferences over kidney allocation, charitable food distribution, LLM responses, and trolley problems.
  </details>

- **[Incorporating Cognitive Load and Knowledge Transfer for Multi-Domain Knowledge Tracing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24005)**  `arXiv:2608.24005`  `cs.AI`  
  _Haotian Zhang, Shucun Wang, Jinze Wu, Liang Ding, Shuochen Liu, Zhenya Huang, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge Tracing (KT) aims to assess students' dynamic knowledge states from their learning histories. While most existing KT methods focus on single-domain learning with notable success, real-world learning scenarios often involve multiple domains simultaneously, introducing two critical factors: 1) Cognitive load, arising from managing learning across domains in both temporal and knowledge dimensions. 2) Knowledge transfer, where knowledge states in one domain influence related states both within and across domains. In this paper, we focus on exploring these factors to improve students' knowledge state assessment in multi-domain learning scenarios and propose a novel method incorporating cognitive Load and knowledge Transfer for Multi-domain Knowledge Tracing (LT-MKT). Specifically, to bridge isolated domains, LT-MKT first integrates textual information from questions and their associated concepts to construct a Multi-domain Hierarchical Graph, leveraging the advanced representational capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Then, cross-domain features in both the temporal and knowledge dimensions are explicitly modeled to capture the effects of cognitive load. Additionally, a knowledge transfer module is designed to model the propagation of knowledge states within and across domains. By jointly modeling these factors, LT-MKT enables more accurate prediction of students' future performance. Finally, extensive experiments on real-world datasets demonstrate that our method achieves state-of-the-art performance.
  </details>

- **[Science Edge Evaluation: SEE the Missing Step Toward Real Scientific Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.06931)**  `arXiv:2608.06931`  `cs.AI`  
  _Taolin Han, Yuchen Zhang, Jinghang Wang, Yun Wu, Wai Yuet Chiu, Zhaohai Li, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly involved in scientific discovery, yet it remains unclear whether they can support complex real laboratory science. Here we introduce Science Edge Evaluation (SEE), a multimodal benchmark of expert-curated questions grounded in peer-reviewed literature and experimental practice in chemistry, biology, and materials science. Evaluation of 28 multimodal large language models (MLLMs) shows that even the best-performing model reaches only 54.9% accuracy. Moreover, general-purpose models outperform science-specialized models on average. In the visual-agent evaluation, the use of tools increases the best accuracy to 56.1%. Tool use can expand the information available to models, but more information does not necessarily lead to reliable scientific reasoning. The key challenge is whether models can manage tool-derived information within the boundaries of the original experimental evidence. Together, these findings reveal that current MLLMs still cannot reliably make justified and evidence-bounded inferences from experimental results, which is an essential capability in real scientific discovery. Bridging this gap requires MLLMs to transition from explaining established scientific concepts to deriving novel and evidence-based insights from experimental data.
  </details>

- **[CastFSR: Fast--Slow--Reflect Agentic Reasoning for Context-Aware Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.03031)**  `arXiv:2608.03031`  `cs.AI`  
  _Xiaoyu Tao, Mingyue Cheng, Bokai Pan, Hao Xue_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Time series forecasting is fundamental to temporal data mining, yet numerical models often struggle to account for changing contextual factors. A key challenge is determining when contextual evidence should guide forecast revisions while maintaining consistency with temporal patterns and domain knowledge. To address this, we propose CastFSR, a fast--slow--reflect agentic framework for context-aware time series forecasting. Fast-thinking forecasting analyzes numerical patterns, selects a suitable forecaster, and constructs a data-driven forecast prior. Slow deliberative reasoning incorporates relevant contextual evidence to selectively refine this prior, while reflective evaluation checks the revised forecast against temporal patterns, contextual evidence, and domain constraints. A shared state maintains forecasting priors, contextual evidence, intermediate forecasts, and feedback across stages. CastFSR supports both training-free LLM coordination and two-stage post-training for compact LLM coordinators. Workflow-aware trajectory distillation (WATD) learns state-conditioned tool use and forecasting decisions from teacher trajectories, while trajectory-level policy optimization (TLPO) further improves the coordinator through reinforcement learning guided by forecasting accuracy and workflow feedback. Experiments on multiple real-world datasets under an offline evaluation protocol using historical context demonstrate competitive performance against representative baselines.Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Sentence-Level Attribution for Black-Box Large Language Model Outputs via Energy-Based Surrogates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02879)**  `arXiv:2608.02879`  `cs.AI`  
  _Maryam Rezaee, Pooriya Safaei, Maryam Asgarinezhad, Fatemeh Seyyedsalehi_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The widespread adoption of proprietary Large Language Models (LLMs) via closed APIs poses an interpretability challenge. While white-box and gray-box (logit-dependent) attribution methods require internal model access, post-hoc attribution under a strictly black-box, text-only generative regime remains largely unexplored. To address this, we propose a model-agnostic, post-hoc attribution interpreter operating at the sentence level. We first train a transformer-based Energy-Based Model (EBM) as a surrogate to score the consistency between prompt and response sentences based on observed LLM generations. To prevent energy collapse and avoid a focus on superficial artifacts, we design contrastive negative samplers that evaluate partial counterfactual dependencies across prompt and response segments. This learned energy landscape then guides the training of a lightweight interpreter network. After model-specific training, the interpreter operates as a standalone tool; it estimates the influence of prompt sentences on a user-specified output sentence without requiring runtime API queries to the target LLM. Comprehensive experiments demonstrate that our EBM surrogate captures nuanced generative dependencies beyond surface-level heuristics, while the interpreter achieves competitive attribution quality with parameter-efficient, $\mathcal{O}(1)$ inference.
  </details>

- **[TrajWiki: Source-Grounded Memory Trajectories for Long-Horizon Dialogue Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.00967)**  `arXiv:2608.00967`  `cs.AI`  
  _Jingyu Sun, Yuyang Xue, Mingyang Li, Zhengtao Yao, Jiachen Li, Yang Cui, et al._
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents have shown strong capabilities in generating coherent and contextually appropriate responses, yet robust long-horizon dialogue remains limited by the lack of external memory that is traceable, updatable, and diagnostically transparent. Existing memory-augmented agents often store memories as isolated records or overwritable states, making it difficult to preserve how information originates, evolves, conflicts, or becomes obsolete over time. We propose \textbf{TrajWiki}, a trajectory-based memory framework for long-horizon conversational agents. Instead of treating memory as static entries, TrajWiki represents each memory as a source-grounded evolution trajectory, maintained through immutable episodic snapshots and claim-level operations such as \texttt{ADD}, \texttt{REVISE}, and \texttt{DEPRECATE}. To reduce fragmentation and retrieval cost, TrajWiki further introduces \textbf{Memory Wiki}, a persistent intermediate layer that incrementally compiles dialogue history into structured and interlinked wiki pages capturing salient entities, events, quantities, topics, and conflicts. At inference time, queries are routed hierarchically from relevant wiki pages to linked memory trajectories, then to corresponding snapshots and source messages for evidence-grounded answer synthesis. Experiments on LoCoMo and MedMT show that TrajWiki improves long-horizon dialogue performance across both open-source and closed-source LLM backbones, while providing greater interpretability and diagnostic visibility into memory evolution, retrieval failures, and answer generation.
  </details>

- **[AMTFV: Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification for LLM Self-Correction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29549)**  `arXiv:2607.29549`  `cs.AI`  
  _Rui Zou, Yutao Zhu, Mengqi Wei, Ji-Rong Wen_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models have demonstrated strong mathematical problem-solving capabilities, yet reliably verifying their candidate answers remains challenging. Existing representative methods mainly revise outputs through natural-language reflection or assist verification by directly generating verification programs; the former may not reliably support exact computation, whereas the latter prematurely couples mathematical modeling with low-level implementation. We propose AMTFV (Agentic Mathematical Tool-Flow Verification). By introducing Mathematical Tool Flow (MTF) as an interrupt--execute--resume interface, AMTFV decouples verification modeling from concrete execution and supports exact computation through a mathematical toolbox. Specifically, the verification agent first constructs a verification workflow, encodes the mathematical objects and computational intent requiring reliable execution in an MTF request, and sends it to the mathematical toolbox agent. The latter parses the request, generates executable calls, and dispatches them to the backend for exact computation. Tool outputs then support candidate-answer adjudication, answer revision, and verification-workflow revision. We evaluate AMTFV on five challenging mathematical reasoning datasets with seven model configurations from DeepSeek, GPT, and Gemini. Experimental results show that AMTFV outperforms the representative baselines evaluated in this study overall; under an individual model configuration, it improves average accuracy over the strongest baseline by up to 8.3 percentage points, with larger gains on samples of medium and high verification complexity.
  </details>

- **[OrigaMI: An LLM Serving System with First-Class Interpretability Apps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.22595)**  `arXiv:2607.22595`  `cs.AI`  
  _Michael Blum, Mark Silberstein, Yaniv David_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Mechanistic interpretability (MI) enables monitoring and steering LLMs by operating on their internal state during inference. MI applications remain difficult to develop, expensive to execute, and challenging to manage at runtime.We present OrigaMI, a system that integrates MI applications as first-class components of LLM serving, enabling simplified development, efficient execution, and flexible deployment. Guided by a comprehensive analysis of modern MI applications, OrigaMI streamlines the MI applications development via a simple Python-based Domain Specific Language (DSL) for accessing intra- and inter-layer runtime model state at well-defined trigger points during inference. OrigaMI enables efficient access to ephemeral attention scores via a novel FlashInspect kernel that retains the performance benefits of FlashAttention. OrigaMI's compiler integrates multiple MI applications into the performance-optimized inference execution flow while ensuring conflict-free coexistence. OrigaMI's runtime supports dynamic, per-request activation and deactivation of MI applications without service interruption or performance degradation when inactive, leveraging OrigaMI's dynamic memory management and CUDA graph-compatible execution.We integrate OrigaMI with vLLM and evaluate ten MI applications, including sparse autoencoders, steering, and jailbreak detection, across four state-of-the-art LLMs. OrigaMI incurs negligible execution overhead, up to two orders of magnitude lower than existing frameworks, while enabling per-request activation and deactivation up to 1,000 times faster than alternatives. These results demonstrate that OrigaMI makes MI applications practical for deployment in production LLM serving systems.
  </details>

- **[Contextualized Evaluation of Vision Language Models through Dynamic, Multi-turn Interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14499)**  `arXiv:2607.14499`  `cs.AI`  
  _Yijiang Li, Huiqi Zou, Bingyang Wang, Ziang Xiao_
  <details open><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-modal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have made substantial advances on benchmarks, yet their real-world effectiveness remains uncertain. This gap stems from the fundamental misalignment between benchmarks in controlled, static settings and the dynamic, interactive, and contextualized nature of real-world applications. To bridge this gap, we propose CEDI (Contextualized Evaluation of MLLMs through Dynamic, multi-round Interactions), a framework that recasts evaluation as a three-party interaction between an evaluatee model, an automated examiner, and a grader. The examiner conducts multi-turn, semi-structured conversations guided by a graph-based representation of the task. By navigating state-space transitions, CEDI deploys diverse strategies, from clarification requests to adversarial probes, to elicit performance evidence. We apply CEDI to visual hallucinations. Empirical results across multiple models, diverse settings, datasets, and domains show that contextualized, interactive evaluations reveal not only significantly more hallucinations than conventional static evaluation but also ones that more closely resemble those arising in practical use cases. We further show that hallucinations often accumulate over long contexts, through self-reinforcing dialogue history, and models are particularly vulnerable to questions requiring premise rejection or refusal. Together, these findings highlight CEDI as a step toward realistic, systematic, and ecologically valid assessments of MLLMs' capabilities. Code is available atthis http URL.
  </details>
